# Sūrah al-Muʾminūn (Chapter 23) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Blueprint of Spiritual Prosperity and the Throne of Grace**

Al-Muʾminūn, "The Believers," is the twenty-third chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 118 verses of mid-to-late Makkan revelation. It derives its title from its majestic opening proclamation: *“Certainly, the believers have succeeded (*qad aflaḥa al-mu'minūn*)”* (23:1). In prophetic tradition, the opening ten verses of this sūrah are celebrated as the definitive portrait of spiritual felicity: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb reported in Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī (3173) that when revelation descended upon the Prophet ﷺ, a sound like the buzzing of bees was heard near his face, and after a moment of profound ecstasy, he turned to the Companions and recited these opening verses, stating: *“Ten verses have been revealed to me; whoever establishes them will enter Paradise.”* Coming at a time when the early Muslims in Makkah were socially ostracized, economically marginalized, and physically threatened, Sūrah al-Muʾminūn performs a radical transvaluation of values: it declares that true success (*falāḥ*) is not measured by pagan wealth, tribal lineage, or political dominion, but by the cultivation of inward humility in prayer, moral purity, sexual integrity, fidelity to trusts, and steadfastness upon truth.

**The Illusion of Pagan Prosperity**

The historical context of Sūrah al-Muʾminūn was marked by the boastful arrogance of the Qurayshi aristocracy. The chieftains looked down upon the Prophet's early followers—slaves like Bilāl, impoverished youths like ʿAmmār, and disenfranchised outcasts—mocking that if their new religion possessed any value, the affluent elites would have been the first to receive it. Sūrah al-Muʾminūn addresses this psychological confrontation directly: it warns the wealthy deniers not to imagine that their abundant wealth and children are a sign of divine favor or a hastening of good (*“Do they think that what We extend to them of wealth and children is because We hasten for them good things? Nay, they perceive not!”*, 23:55–56). True ruin lies in complacency; worldly riches without spiritual consciousness are merely a slow divine testing (*istidrāj*) that leads to catastrophic bankruptcy on the Day of Judgment.

**The Structural Anatomy of the Sūrah**

The architecture of Sūrah al-Muʾminūn is organized around four clear thematic movements. The opening movement (1–22) establishes the six ethical pillars of the true believer: inward humility and awe in prayer (*khushūʿ*), turning away from futile and vain speech (*laghw*), active purification through zakat and charity, guarding sexual modesty except within lawful marital bonds, honoring trusts and covenants, and punctually guarding the regular prayers, promising that such souls shall inherit *al-Firdaws* (the highest terrace of Paradise). This ethical code is immediately grounded in the biological miracle of human embryogenesis: the clay extraction, the drop of semen (*nuṭfah*), the clinging clot (*ʿalaqah*), the lump of flesh (*muḍghah*), the clothing of bones with flesh, and the miraculous fashioning into another creation, concluding: *“So blessed is Allah, the best of creators”* (23:14), followed by the cosmic signs of the seven layered heavens, life-giving rain, and the blessed olive tree that springs from Mount Sinai producing oil and seasoning for the eaters. The second movement (23–50) reviews sacred history through the lens of moral confrontation: Noah building the Ark under the mocking eyes of the chieftains who branded him an ambitious social climber; the successive generation destroyed by the screaming blast (*al-ṣayḥah*); Moses and Aaron confronting Pharaoh's arrogant refusal; and the universal dietary and moral command given to all messengers: *“O messengers, eat from the good things and work righteousness”* (23:51). The third movement (51–92) exposes the splintering of humanity into sectarian factions, each rejoicing in what it possesses, contrasts the terrified pleas of the dying sinner against the serenity of the mindful, and stages the dramatic reality of the *Barzakh*—the impenetrable spiritual barrier behind deceased souls preventing them from returning to earth to rectify their wasted lives (23:99–100). The closing movement (93–118) provides the Prophet with defensive prayers against demonic whispers (*“My Lord, I seek refuge in You from the incitements of the devils,”* 23:97), portrays the horror of the deniers whose faces are scorched in Hell while the believers are rewarded for their patient endurance, and asks humanity the penetrating existential question of verse 115: *“Did you then think that We created you without purpose, and that you would not be returned to Us?”*, sealing the chapter with the glorious prayer: *“My Lord, forgive and have mercy, and You are the best of the merciful.”*

**The Interior Architecture of Khushūʿ**

The foundational insight of Sūrah al-Muʾminūn is that ethical action flows from the interior sanctuary of *Khushūʿ*—the quiet trembling, focused awe of the heart standing conscious before its Creator. When prayer becomes a mechanical, hurried exterior ritual, moral life degrades; but when the soul learns to stand in stillness before the Lord of the Throne, worldly distractions (*laghw*) lose their grip, trusts are honored instinctively, and sexual desires are disciplined by sacred limits. Sūrah al-Muʾminūn offers a comprehensive roadmap of human flourishing: beginning with the whisper of the heart in prayer and culminating in the eternal inheritance of Paradise.

---

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:1

> Successful indeed are the believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict Arrives Before the Qualifications**

The chapter opens with a finished statement rather than a command. *Qad aflaḥa al-muʾminūn* — success has already come to the believers. The particle *qad* before a perfect verb settles the matter: this is reported as accomplished, not offered as a possibility. Nothing is asked of the hearer here, no condition is attached, no threat is held back for later. A group is named, and a fact about that group is declared.

That order does real work. Moral instruction normally begins with the requirement and only then names the reward, so the hearer must accept the burden before learning what it buys. Here the gain is stated first, and the description filling verses two through nine arrives as identification rather than payment. Nobody is being sold anything. A result is announced, and the people who already hold it are then described. The eight marks that follow are not the price of success but its evidence.

**A Word Taken From the Plough**

The noun behind the verdict, *falāḥ*, does not begin life as an abstraction. Its root f-l-ḥ carries the plain physical sense of cleaving or splitting, and specifically of cutting open soil; from the same root comes *falāḥah*, the work of tilling land. The classical lexicons record both senses side by side, and the second follows from the first without strain: the farmer who opens the ground is labouring toward a yield that has not yet appeared.

That is why *falāḥ* is a stronger word than the English "success" it usually receives. English success can describe a lucky outcome. *Falāḥ* describes the condition of a thing that has been sown, cultivated, and brought to its harvest — growth that has finished its work. When the call to prayer says *ḥayya ʿalā al-falāḥ*, come to success, the summons goes out to the one act that cultivates instead of consuming. When this sūrah opens with the same word, it is claiming that belief is the seed and the believer's later life the crop: verses two to nine describe the cultivation, and verse eleven is the harvest itself.

**Success Declared Rather Than Claimed**

Across the Book, *falāḥ* is assigned by God instead of achieved by self-assessment. A community is told to raise up a group that calls to good and forbids evil, and the sentence closes: *"it is they who will be successful"* (3:104). The opening of the Cow describes the guided and lands on the same phrase: *"It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful."* (2:5). In both places the verdict belongs to God, and in both it settles on a described group rather than on a hope.

That reassignment would have been heard sharply in a city whose wealth came from trade. Meccan life had its own measure of flourishing — caravan, contract, standing among one's own — and this verse takes the commonest word for doing well and hands it to someone else. It does not argue with the merchants' arithmetic. It declares who has in fact thrived, and the people named are not the ones the market would have named.

**A Definite Group, Described Immediately After**

*Al-muʾminūn* carries the definite article, and a definite plural with nothing before it to point back to is unusual enough to notice. The hearers already knew who was meant: the small company around the Prophet, visible and unpopular. The verse does not invent a category and then fill it. It points at a known people and states what is true of them.

The specification follows at once, and that is what keeps the opening from turning into a slogan. Faith is not left as an interior claim that nobody can check. It is given eight outward descriptions — humility in prayer, silence about what does not matter, alms, guarded desire, kept trusts, guarded prayer — and a reader can ask which of them he recognises in himself. The same construction appears twice more, both times with the identical verdict word: *"Successful indeed are those who purify themselves,"* (87:14) and *"Successful indeed is the one who purifies their soul,"* (91:9). Each time the word is followed by the work.

**A Ten-Verse Guarantee With a Contested Chain**

A report from ʿUmar describes the moment this passage came down: the Prophet turned toward the qiblah, raised his hands, made a long supplication, and then said that ten verses had been revealed to him and that whoever adhered to them would enter Paradise — after which he recited from this opening until he had completed ten. The report is preserved in the collections of al-Tirmidhī, Aḥmad and al-Nasāʾī.

Its chain is disputed, and the dispute is worth stating rather than hiding. The isnād runs through Yūnus b. Sulaym al-Ṣanʿānī, and al-Tirmidhī himself observed that the wording is known only through him; al-Nasāʾī judged it *munkar*, unacceptable. Later scholars differ: some treat the defect as the dropping of an intermediate transmitter, Yūnus b. Yazīd, which would make the chain broken rather than false. What survives the criticism is the substance the verse already contains on its own — a short list of deeds with Paradise attached. The narration adds only the occasion, and the occasion is the weak part.

**Why the Certainty Is Not a Comfort**

Declaring success in the perfect tense is not the same as reassuring everyone. The verse names believers and nobody else, and the sūrah spends much of its later length describing what happens to those who stand outside the name. What the tense fixes is the standard. A real difference separates those who flourish from those who do not, and that difference is settled now, before any Day decides it, by whether the eight descriptions hold.

This is why praise does not follow the opening; a list does. A verdict without specification would be flattery, and a specification without a verdict would be a checklist. The sūrah gives both in a single sentence, then spends nine verses on the second half. Whoever wants the first half has to look at the second.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:2

> those who humble themselves in prayer

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Mark Is an Inner State**

The list begins where a person cannot be seen. Not attendance at prayer — the sūrah demands that separately, in its final item — but the condition of the one who is praying. The word is *khāshiʿūn*, and the quality it names is neither effort, nor length of standing, nor fluency in recitation. It is the collapse of the self's claim to importance while standing before God.

Placing this first is a judgement about what prayer actually is. A prayer correct in its movements and empty in the person performing it does not satisfy the description, and the sūrah knows the outward form is the easier half. The same emphasis runs through the Cow, where prayer is called a heavy thing *"except for the humble"* (2:45). The difficulty is not the rite. It is being small enough in one's own eyes to perform the rite as an address.

**A Root About Lowered Eyes**

The root kh-sh-ʿ describes physical lowering. Where the Qur'an applies its derivatives to the deniers on the Day of Judgment, the image is always the eyes: *"With eyes downcast, they will come forth from the graves as if they were swarming locusts,"* (54:7), and *"with eyes downcast, utterly covered with disgrace. That is the Day they have ˹always˺ been warned of."* (70:44). The root also carries the sense of something withered or subdued — land dried out and sunk low.

That range is what makes the word stronger than "reverent". Reverence can be a feeling about another. *Khushūʿ* is a change in the one who feels it: the head drops, the gaze stops travelling, the body gives up its claim to space. Applied to prayer, it names the posture of the soul that the posture of the limbs is meant to express. Where the two disagree, the sūrah is describing the second and calling it by the name of the first.

**The Report About Eyes Lifted to the Sky**

Anas b. Mālik reports that the Prophet asked what was wrong with people who raise their eyes toward the sky during prayer, and that his tone hardened as he said it: they must stop, or their sight would be taken away. The report stands in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, 750.

The severity is the informative part. The fault is not that a worshipper looked at something holy; the sky is not an improper object. It is that a wandering gaze belongs to a mind that has left the prayer while the body stayed in it. The correction given by the practice of the community is to fix the eyes on the place where the forehead will touch the ground, so that the visual field matches the act. Some schools let the gaze rest slightly forward and downward instead. Both solutions treat the eyes as the instrument by which attention is held, not as a detail of etiquette.

The warning attached to the report — that sight would be taken — is not a threat about optics. It says that the faculty used carelessly in the act of worship may be withdrawn from the worshipper altogether. A gaze that will not stay where the prayer puts it has been trained to go wherever it likes, and a faculty trained that way is no longer under its owner's command.

**Stillness Read Into the Same Word**

A second exegetical reading takes *khushūʿ* here to mean stillness in the movements of prayer: not hurrying through bowing and prostration, but settling in each position long enough for it to be performed rather than passed over. A third reading takes it as fear. The two are nearer to each other than they appear. Stillness is the bodily form of fear; a frightened man does not hurry.

Between them the inward reading is the stronger one, because the verse describes a quality of persons rather than a rule about limbs. Stillness belongs to valid prayer in the schools of law whether or not this verse is cited for it. Humility is what this list is made of — every other item in it names a disposition — and a list of dispositions should not open with a technicality. The stillness reading is best held as the visible sign of what the verse names, not as its meaning.

The fear reading has the same status. Fear in prayer is not terror of punishment; it is the awareness of standing in front of someone whose judgement matters, which is precisely what makes stillness possible. A man who believes nobody is watching moves quickly. All three readings describe one condition from three angles — the heart, the limbs, the emotion — and the verse needs none of them to exclude the others.

**Humility in Prayer Depends on the Life Outside It**

The spiritual tradition treats *khushūʿ* as something prepared before the prayer begins. A person who has spent his day in deception, in noise, in appetite does not arrive at the prayer mat neutral and then become humble on command. Practice of the virtues in the hours between prayers supplies the material of the humility inside them, and the sūrah makes the link structural: the first mark is humility in prayer, and the six that follow are conduct in the world.

The prophets are described with the same word: *"Indeed, they used to race in doing good, and call upon Us with hope and fear, totally humbling themselves before Us."* (21:90). The order there is instructive. Good deeds and supplication come first, and humility is named as the manner of both. Humility is not a state entered at the opening formula. It is the shape a life has already taken by the time it stands up to pray.

**The Same Word on the Day of Judgment**

Derivatives of this root return at the end of the Book as the description of those who refused to lower themselves here: *"with eyes downcast, totally covered with disgrace. For they were ˹always˺ called to prostrate ˹in the world˺ when they were fully capable ˹but they chose not to˺."* (68:43). The connection is explicit inside the verse — they were called to prostrate and did not. The lowered gaze of the worshipper and the lowered gaze of the condemned share one vocabulary. One is a choice made in time; the other is the same posture, imposed.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:3

> those who avoid idle talk

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Word Wider Than Sin**

*Al-laghw* is the word, and its range is wider than sinful speech. The exegetes divide over how much wider. Some restrict it to talk that is forbidden — slander, mockery, falsehood — and hold that believers are simply being told to leave off what is already unlawful. Others take it across three layers: the forbidden, the undesirable, and the merely permitted but pointless. On the wider reading a believer is asked to withdraw from conversations that harm nobody and achieve nothing.

The wider reading is the better one. If *laghw* meant only forbidden talk, the verse would add nothing to the general prohibition, and the list would be telling believers to avoid sin twice. The narrow reading is not absurd — it keeps the term inside the familiar category of the unlawful — but it renders the verse redundant, and redundancy in a list this compressed is unlikely. What the verse does is extend discipline past the point where law stops.

**The Evidence in the Cups of Paradise**

One verse settles the range of the word by setting it beside a different one. Describing the drink of Paradise, the Book says those there pass it around, *"a drink ˹of pure wine,˺ which leads to no idle talk or sinfulness."* (52:23). *Laghw* and *ithm*, sin, are named as two things. If the first simply meant the second, the sentence would have repeated one idea through two unrelated nouns joined by "or", which Arabic does not do for emphasis.

That distinction gives the word its content. *Laghw* is speech that goes nowhere: the argument producing no conclusion, the story told for effect, the joke at someone's expense, the opinion offered because silence felt awkward. It becomes sin when it injures, and it remains *laghw* whether or not it ever does. The believer described here has trained himself to hear the difference and to leave the second category alone — not because it will be counted against him, but because it is worth nothing.

The same verse supplies the positive contrast without stating it. What fills the cups of Paradise is conversation that means something, and the drink there produces no *laghw* because nothing in that company needs to be said for its own sake. The defect being removed is not intoxication but the speech intoxication produces.

**Turning Away Instead of Answering**

The verb is *muʿriḍūn*, turning aside. It does not describe someone who wins the argument, nor someone who corrects the speaker, nor someone who stays and disapproves in silence. It describes a person who leaves. What is praised is not superior argument but the refusal to enter.

The Book shows the action performed: *"When they hear slanderous talk, they turn away from it, saying, 'We are accountable for our deeds and you for yours. Peace ˹is our only response˺ to you! We want nothing to do with those who act ignorantly.'"* (28:55). Every element of that reply is a refusal to continue. It assigns responsibility without assigning blame, offers a greeting in place of a retort, and closes the exchange. Anyone who has been provoked knows what that restraint costs, and the sūrah is describing people who have made it habitual.

**Passing By Without Stopping**

A second description places the same behaviour among the servants of the Merciful: *"˹They are˺ those who do not bear false witness, and when they come across falsehood, they pass ˹it˺ by with dignity."* (25:72). The verb differs and is slightly stronger. They do not merely turn away; they move past the thing while remaining upright, neither joining it nor stooping to fight it.

The dignity in that verse is what turns avoidance into a virtue rather than a temperament. Many people stay clear of pointless talk because they cannot handle it, or because contempt keeps them out. The believer described in 25:72 stays intact — his bearing does not change — and simply declines to participate. That is why the item belongs in a list of the marks of success. Avoidance that leaves a person bitter has gained nothing; avoidance that leaves him whole has gained the thing this sūrah wants.

**The Absence That Defines Paradise**

Paradise is described negatively with this same word. *"There they will never hear any idle talk—only ˹greetings of˺ peace."* (19:62), and again of the reward: *"never to hear any idle talk or lying therein—"* (78:35). The felicity of the Garden is defined in part by what will not be heard inside it, and what is removed is exactly what the believer has already stopped producing.

That makes verse three a description of the Garden before it is a command. The believer is not being denied something enjoyable for a distant payment. He is being given a rehearsal. The talk that fills most human company is the one thing the Garden excludes, and whoever has left it behind here has already adjusted to the conditions of there. The other marks on this list are efforts. This one is a subtraction, and it is the subtraction the reward repeats.

**Speech Placed Between Worship and Wealth**

The order of the list is not arbitrary. Prayer comes first, then the tongue, then the pocket, then desire, then trusts, then prayer again. Between the two poles of worship and wealth sits the thing that costs nothing and consumes the most: talk. It is listed where it belongs — after the discipline of attention, before the discipline of possessions — because a person who cannot govern his speech will not govern his money either.

Every item after this one is harder to fake. Alms leave the hand, chastity governs the body, trusts show up in whether property comes back. Speech is the item nobody audits, and the sūrah places it second. That placement is a claim about scale: the tongue is not a small province of the moral life with a small effect on the rest. It is where the habits of restraint or indulgence are formed, and the remainder of the list follows from it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:4

> those who pay alms-tax

**Expanded Commentary**

**Zakāh Means Growth Before It Means Giving**

The term used here, *zakāh*, is built on a root whose primary sense is increase. To say that something *zakā* is to say that it grew, and that it grew clean — the two ideas are not separated in the root. Agricultural usage keeps this visible: the same family of words describes a crop that comes up well. When the Qur'an speaks of zakāh as something a person gives away, it is naming the part of wealth that must be removed for the remainder to increase.

That is not a figure of speech wrapped around a tax. It is a claim about how property behaves. Wealth held entire does not stay whole; it hardens the holder, and the sūrah's later portrait of the affluent who reject the messengers depends on exactly that. The payment described here is small by design, and its effect is not arithmetic but moral: it keeps the rest of a person's possessions in the category of things he holds rather than things that hold him.

**A Due, Not a Generosity**

By the time Islamic law took shape, *zakāh* named a specific obligation: an annual payment calculated on accumulated wealth above a fixed threshold, at a rate set by the texts rather than by the payer's mood. That technical meaning developed later — the obligation with its defined threshold and rates was legislated in the second year after the Hijrah, in Medina — and this sūrah is Meccan. Here the word still carries its wider sense of giving that purifies.

The distinction matters for reading the verse honestly. It is not claiming that the first Muslims of Mecca were calculating annual percentages on liquid assets. It is claiming that a believer's wealth is not entirely his, and that the recognition takes the form of regular, deliberate transfer rather than occasional kindness. When the rates and categories were fixed afterwards, they gave the Meccan principle a shape that could be audited. The verse states the principle; the later legislation supplied the arithmetic.

**The Same Root Inside the Same Verdict**

Two other places in the Book join this root to the identical verdict word that opened this sūrah. *"Successful indeed are those who purify themselves,"* (87:14) uses *tazakkā*, the same root in its reflexive form. *"Successful indeed is the one who purifies their soul,"* (91:9) uses *zakkāhā*. In all three sentences the formula is the same: *qad aflaḥa*, success has come, followed by a verbal noun from z-k-w.

The coincidence is too exact to be accidental. The Book is treating purification and prosperity as one phenomenon seen from two ends. A thing that is purified is a thing from which something has been removed so that what remains can grow — and that is the whole doctrine of zakāh in a single mechanism. The believer who pays is not making a donation. He is performing on his wealth the operation the sūrah has just declared to be the definition of success.

**The Purification Runs Both Ways**

The direction of the benefit is easy to misread. Because alms reach the poor, it is natural to suppose that the recipient is the one being purified. The Qur'an says otherwise in a verse addressed to the Prophet about the collection itself: *"Take from their wealth ˹O Prophet˺ charity to purify and bless them, and pray for them— surely your prayer is a source of comfort for them."* (9:103). The object of the purification is the payer.

That reversal explains why the payment is obligatory rather than voluntary. A gift the giver chooses to make can be adjusted downward whenever it begins to cost him something, and a virtue that stops where discomfort starts is not a virtue. A fixed due removes the negotiation. The payer is not deciding, in the moment, whether he is generous; he is being made generous on a schedule, and the making is the point. The comfort mentioned at the end of 9:103 is not the receipt of money but the knowledge that the transfer is not a favour.

**Why Wealth Comes Third**

Position in this list is meaningful. Prayer governs attention, silence about what does not matter governs the tongue, and now the hand is reached. The order moves from the interior outward, and each stage costs more to fake than the one before. A person can perform the movements of prayer while absent from them; he can stay silent without effort; but alms leave the estate and can be counted by others.

Placing wealth third, before chastity and before trusts, also makes a claim about which appetite is most general. Not every person is tested in desire, and not everyone is given a trust to keep. Everyone who owns anything is tested by owning it. The sūrah therefore puts the item that applies to almost every adult believer in the third position, where the list is still gathering momentum, and treats the refusal to part with property as a failure of the same order as irreverence in prayer.

**The Eight Recipients Named in One Verse**

The Meccan generality of this verse was later given precision. *"Alms-tax is only for the poor and the needy, for those employed to administer it, for those whose hearts are attracted ˹to the faith˺, for ˹freeing˺ slaves, for those in debt, for Allah's cause, and for ˹needy˺ travellers. ˹This is˺ an obligation from Allah. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise."* (9:60). The list is exhaustive in form — "only for" — and it closes off the payer's discretion over destination as well as amount. Four of the eight categories are people in a temporary bind rather than permanent poverty: the indebted, the stranded traveller, the administrator, the one being freed. Zakāh in the Qur'an is thus not merely relief for the destitute but a standing mechanism for returning people to independence.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:5

> those who guard their chastity

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Thing Guarded Is a Thing Under Attack**

The verb is *ḥāfiẓūn*, guarding, and the noun is *furūj*, the plural of *farj*, the private part. The pairing is worth holding on to. Nobody guards a thing that is safe by nature. Guarding implies a target exposed to pressure, an attack expected, and a defence maintained over time rather than settled once. The verse does not describe people who lack desire or who find temptation easy. It describes people who hold a position.

The word choice also refuses abstraction. English translations render the phrase as guarding "their chastity", which sounds like a quality. The Arabic names a part of the body. What is being described is not a mood of purity but a boundary kept at a specific place, and the sūrah will define that boundary in the next two verses with legal precision. The list of the believer's marks reaches here into the one province of life where most people concede defeat, and it describes the believer simply as someone who has not.

**The Guard Begins at the Eyes**

The Qur'an sets out the order of this defence explicitly elsewhere, and the order runs from the gaze to the part: *"˹O Prophet!˺ Tell the believing men to lower their gaze and guard their chastity. That is purer for them. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what they do."* (24:30). Two commands, in that sequence, and the first is the precondition of the second.

This is a psychological claim stated as legislation. Desire is not usually confronted at the point of action; it is assembled earlier, from what a person allows himself to look at and to keep looking at. A defence mounted at the last stage is defending against something already built. The verse in an-Nūr therefore puts the earliest available intervention first, and this verse in al-Muʾminūn describes the result — a guard that is still standing — without needing to repeat the method. Read together, the two verses say that chastity is maintained upstream, in the small unobserved decisions about attention.

**The Requirement Named for Both**

Nothing in this verse is limited to men, and the Book states the parallel requirement in terms that leave no room to argue. A long list of paired qualities ends with the same phrase applied to both sexes: *"and men and women who guard their chastity, and men and women who remember Allah often—for ˹all of˺ them Allah has prepared forgiveness and a great reward."* (33:35).

The grammatical form of 23:5 is masculine plural, which in Arabic includes both men and women unless something restricts it. The explicit pairing in al-Aḥzāb removes any doubt about the intention. It also removes a common evasion — the assumption that a text addressed in the masculine is legislating for men while leaving women's conduct to a separate, private code. Here the same duty, the same word, and the same reward are extended across the list, item by item, to both.

**A General Prohibition With One Exception**

The verse states the rule in absolute form and stops. The exception is held back for the verse that follows, where it is given with two named categories and the phrase "free from blame". Structurally this is the way law is written when the permitted area is narrow: state the prohibition without qualification, then define the boundary, then declare everything outside it unlawful.

The effect on the reader is deliberate. A person who encounters the prohibition first registers its scope before learning how small the exception is. Had the exception come first, the verse would have read as a permission with a condition attached, which is the opposite of what the sūrah wants. The moral shape of the passage is that the default is restraint and the lawful is the carved-out space, not that the default is freedom and the unlawful is the forbidden corner.

**The Fourth Appetite in a List of Appetites**

Read in sequence, the marks on this list are one discipline applied to four faculties. Attention in prayer, the tongue in company, wealth in the hand, and desire in the body. Each is an appetite with a natural direction and no natural limit, and in each case the sūrah describes not suppression but governance — humility rather than self-loathing, silence rather than muteness, payment rather than poverty, a guarded boundary rather than the absence of desire.

That is why the item sits where it does. A reader who has followed the list this far has been told that prayer requires presence, that speech requires restraint, and that property requires release. The fourth item is the hardest of the four for most people, and the sūrah places it after three successes rather than before them. The ordering teaches that the government of desire is not an isolated achievement. It rests on habits already formed elsewhere, and it fails in most lives at the point where those other habits were never built.

There is also a cost argument running underneath. Prayer, silence and alms can all be resumed after a lapse, and the lapse costs what it costs. A breach of this boundary commonly produces consequences that fall on other people — children without fathers, reputations, marriages — and the sūrah's later legal material in an-Nūr treats those consequences, not the appetite, as the reason the matter is legislated at all.

**The Same List in Sūrat al-Maʿārij**

Nearly the whole sequence reappears later in the Book, in the same order and often in the same words. *"and those who guard their chastity"* (70:29), followed by the identical exception and the identical phrase about transgression, followed by *"˹The faithful are˺ also those who are true to their trusts and covenants;"* (70:32) and *"and who are ˹properly˺ observant of their prayers."* (70:34). The portrait is fixed enough to be recognised as a portrait. Two sūrahs, separated in revelation, describe the believer with one set of features, and the stability of the description is the argument: this is not a mood of a particular moment in the Prophet's mission but a standing definition.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:6

> except with their wives or those ˹bondwomen˺ in their possession, for then they are free from blame

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception Names Two Legal States, Not Two Moods**

The carve-out lists two categories and no more: wives, and those whom the right hand possesses. Both are defined by law rather than by feeling. A wife is a woman joined to a man by a contract with conditions — a dowry, witnesses, rights and duties that survive the marriage and bind his heirs. The second category is a status created by the law of war in the Arabia of the seventh century, and it too carried obligations: maintenance, protection, and the prohibition of coercion that another verse makes explicit.

Naming both, and only both, is what gives the prohibition its edge. The verse does not say "with those you love" or "with those who consent". It says: within these two defined relations. Consent, affection and social approval are all left out of the definition, which means a relation possessing all three but lacking the legal form is not inside the exception. The sūrah is legislating form, and it does so because form is what can be checked, inherited from, and held people to.

**"Free From Blame" Is a Legal Finding, Not Praise**

The phrase *ghayru malūmīn*, not blameworthy, is precise. It removes reproach; it does not confer distinction. Nobody is called virtuous for being inside the exception, and the verse does not grade the two categories against each other or against anything else. It states that no charge lies against a person who keeps within them.

That distinction between permission and commendation runs through the whole legal material of the Qur'an. Something can be lawful without being the best available choice, and the Book regularly distinguishes the two — what is permitted, and what is better. Here the sūrah is doing the minimum necessary work: closing the field so that the next verse can name everyone outside it. The believer's excellence, in this passage, lies in the guarding described in the verse before, not in the permission described here.

The formulation also protects the parties. A blame attached to a lawful relation would push that relation underground, where its conditions cannot be enforced and the woman's rights cannot be claimed. By declaring the permitted field free of reproach, the verse keeps it in the open, where contracts are witnessed, obligations are known, and children have a recognised father. Blame is reserved for what happens outside the law, and that is where the law cannot reach.

**What "Those in Their Possession" Referred To**

The second category needs stating plainly rather than glossing over. Captives taken in war entered the household as bondwomen, and the Qur'an legislated for that existing institution instead of pretending it away. The ruling in an-Nisāʾ shows how the category worked in practice: married women are forbidden *"except ˹female˺ captives in your possession"* (4:24), which means the law recognised the captivity and regulated its consequences rather than leaving them to the owner.

One of those regulations is recorded in a report about the captives of Awṭās. Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī relates the Prophet's instruction: do not approach a pregnant woman until she has given birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until she has menstruated once. The report is in Sunan Abī Dāwūd, 2157, and al-Albānī graded it ṣaḥīḥ in Irwāʾ al-Ghalīl, 187. The rule establishes lineage and prevents a woman from being treated as available the moment she is captured — a limit imposed on the owner, in his own household, by a law he did not write.

Regulation is not endorsement, and reading the verse as endorsement mistakes the genre. The text addresses a practice it found in place and imposes limits, duties and exits on it. The alternative — silence — would have left the institution exactly as it was. What the Qur'an chose was to make the woman's position a matter of law with named rights, and to make her release a religiously rewarded act.

**The Text's Direction Is Toward Freeing**

The institution described in this verse is set inside a Book that repeatedly pushes the other way. Emancipation is named as the steep path: *"It is to free a slave,"* (90:13). A master asked to release a bondsman is commanded to facilitate it and to contribute from his own wealth: *"And if any of those ˹bondspeople˺ in your possession desires a deed of emancipation, make it possible for them, if you find goodness in them."* (24:33). One of the eight categories entitled to alms-tax is precisely those being freed from bondage (9:60).

Read against that background, the mention in 23:6 is a statement of the present legal reality, not a recommendation of it. The direction of travel in the Book is toward a condition in which the second category no longer exists. The exception remains in the text because the text legislates for the world as it was revealed into; the pressure inside the text runs toward the world in which only the first category remains.

**A Bounded Permission Keeps the Rule Intact**

The logic of these two verses is the logic of any law with a defined allowance. State the prohibition without qualification, then give the exception in full, then declare the remainder unlawful. A prohibition with a vague exception is no prohibition at all, because every claimant will locate himself inside the blur.

That is why the exception is stated so dryly. There is no rhetoric in it, no appeal to the naturalness of desire, no softening. Two categories, one legal finding, and a comma. The dryness is the discipline: the reader is given exactly the boundary and nothing to argue with. Verse seven then does the only work left to do, which is to name whoever stands outside it.

**The Prophet's Own Case Was Treated Differently**

The sūrah of al-Aḥzāb lists the Prophet's permissions at length and then closes them with a sentence that shows how exceptions function in this legal system: *"˹this is˺ exclusively for you, not for the rest of the believers."* (33:50). A permission granted to one person by name is marked as his alone, and the general rule for everyone else is stated separately in the same verse — *"We know well what ˹rulings˺ We have ordained for the believers in relation to their wives and those ˹bondwomen˺ in their possession."* (33:50). The contrast makes the shape of 23:6 clearer. Where the Book intends a particular allowance, it says so and restricts it; where it states a rule for believers, it leaves the list closed.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:7

> but whoever seeks beyond that are the transgressors

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word Describes Crossing a Line**

*ʿĀdūn* comes from the root ʿ-d-w, whose basic sense is to pass over, to go beyond, to cross from one side of something to the other. A transgressor in this vocabulary is not primarily a sinner in the sense of one who has failed a standard of excellence. He is a man who was told where the boundary ran and stepped over it. The word contains its own geography: there is a line, and he is now on the wrong side of it.

That is why the sentence is stronger than a moral disapproval. Disapproval can be weighed against other considerations and traded off. A boundary either holds or it does not. The verse has just drawn one with unusual clarity in the two before it, and this verse supplies the only remaining question: what about the rest? The answer is that the rest is transgression, and the people in it are named by the act rather than by their intentions.

**No Third Category Is Left**

The force of the sentence comes from what it excludes structurally. Verse five states the rule, verse six gives the exception, and verse seven declares everyone else outside. There is no intermediate status, no tolerated grey zone, no category of the regrettable but permitted.

Most real moral discussion takes place in the space this verse closes. People rarely defend outright prohibition-breaking; they argue that their case is different, that the category does not quite fit, that a lesser evil is being avoided. The verse removes the ground for all of it by making the exception exhaustively short. Two categories were named, both legal, both defined by contract or status. Anything proposed in addition is not a hard case at the edge of the permission. It is on the other side of the line.

The wording is worth pausing on. Whoever *seeks* beyond that — *ibtaghā*, from the same root as seeking and desiring — is named a transgressor. The verse does not wait for the act. The search itself, the deliberate looking for a route outside the two categories, is what the sentence describes. That is consistent with the verse two places earlier in this list, where the guard is maintained at the level of the gaze. Both verses locate the moral event upstream of the deed, in the decision to look for something the law has not provided.

**A Permission Is Defined by Its Limit**

The pairing of verses six and seven states a general principle about how permissions work in this Book. An allowance whose boundary is not stated is not an allowance but an invitation, and the Qur'an's legislative style is to state the limit in the same breath as the grant.

Consider the structure. The prohibition is unqualified: guard your private parts. The exception is enumerated: wives, and those in possession. The consequence is a legal finding: no blame. Then the remainder is named: transgressors. Four moves, and not one of them is rhetorical. A reader who wants to know where he stands does not have to interpret anything; he has to check which category his situation falls into, and if it falls into neither, the verse has already answered him.

**Where the Jurists Took This Verse**

These two verses became a standard proof-text in a dispute about temporary marriage. Jurists of the mainstream schools argued that a fixed-term contract does not make a woman a wife in the sense 23:6 requires, since the relation is not intended to be permanent and does not carry the full set of marital rights; on that reading, seeking such a relation is seeking "beyond that". Imāmī jurisprudence disagrees, holding that a fixed-term contract is a genuine marriage contract and reading the relevant permission in 4:24 as covering it.

The disagreement is worth reporting with its grounds rather than as a list of names. The first side is arguing from this verse and from the nature of the marital bond the Qur'an describes: a bond meant to be lasting, carrying inheritance, maintenance and lineage, none of which a contract that expires on a date can support. The second side answers that 4:24 speaks of seeking women with wealth in a legal marriage and that the earliest community practised fixed-term marriage before it was prohibited, so the question is what 23:6-7 actually excludes. What both sides accept is the premise this verse establishes: the permitted field is closed, and anything claimed inside it must be shown to belong to a named category.

That shared premise is the verse's lasting contribution. Every later discussion of sexual ethics in Islamic law begins from the assumption stated here — that the default is prohibition and that any claimed permission must be located in the text. The argument is never about whether a line exists. It is about where the line was drawn, and this verse is the one that drew it.

**Why the Sentence Is So Short**

Five Arabic words carry the whole verdict. There is no explanation of why transgression is wrong, no description of consequences, no appeal to shame. The brevity is proportionate to the work being done: the verse is not persuading anyone. It is labelling.

Short declarative sentences of this kind appear where the Qur'an considers a matter settled. Length is spent elsewhere in the sūrah — on the stages of creation, on the stories of the messengers, on the arguments with the deniers. Nothing is spent here, because the reader who has accepted the two verses before this one does not need anything further, and the reader who has not accepted them will not be moved by a longer sentence. The list of the believer's marks resumes immediately afterward with trusts and covenants, as though nothing had needed saying.

**The Same Formula in al-Maʿārij**

The three-verse unit reappears almost word for word in the sūrah of the Ascending Ways: the guarding, the exception for wives and those in possession with the phrase about being free from blame, and then the identical verdict on whoever seeks beyond it. The repetition is the argument. A formula used twice in the Book, with the same terms and the same order, is not a local ruling for one situation; it is the settled statement of a boundary. The stability of the wording across two sūrahs tells the reader that the line drawn here is not going to move, and that the definition of who stands outside it has already been given.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:8

> ˹the believers are also˺ those who are true to their trusts and covenants

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Words That Cover Everything Owed**

The verse names two things: *amānāt*, trusts, and *ʿahd*, covenant. Between them they take in the whole of what a person owes. A trust is a thing placed in your hands by someone else — property, a secret, a duty, a person, a sum of money — which you are holding and not owning. A covenant is a word placed on your tongue: a promise, a treaty, a condition accepted at a contract, an undertaking given to God.

The first is about things; the second is about speech. Both are breaches that can be committed invisibly. Nobody audits a deposit, and nobody hears a promise once it has been made. The sūrah has just described believers as people who guard a part of the body that no one else can see, and now it describes them as people who return what no one can force them to return. The list is consistently about the private interior of conduct, the part of a life that produces no witnesses.

**The Trust the Heavens Declined**

The Qur'an sets out the weight of the word *amānah* in a verse that is deliberately strange: *"Indeed, We offered the trust to the heavens and the earth and the mountains, but they ˹all˺ declined to bear it, being fearful of it. But humanity assumed it, ˹for˺ they are truly wrongful ˹to themselves˺ and ignorant ˹of the consequences˺,"* (33:72). The greatest structures in creation refused the offer out of fear, and the human being accepted it.

Whatever precisely the trust in that verse denotes, its scale is stated: this is not a small obligation that happens to be binding. It is the one the cosmos would not take. Read against it, the verse here is describing people who are carrying what the mountains refused, and carrying it honestly. The description is not about administrative tidiness. It is about a creature who accepted a responsibility beyond his natural capacity and has not quietly set it down.

**Trusts Are Owed to People, Not Only to God**

The legal material in the Book makes the horizontal application explicit. *"Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners; and when you judge between people, judge with fairness. What a noble commandment from Allah to you!"* (4:58). The command to return what has been placed with you is issued alongside the command to judge honestly, and both are addressed to the dealings of people with each other.

The warning side is just as direct: *"O believers! Do not betray Allah and the Messenger, nor betray your trusts knowingly."* (8:27). The verse places betrayal of a trust beside betrayal of God and His Messenger in a single prohibition, which is not a rhetorical escalation but an equation of seriousness. A deposit kept back, a confidence sold, a duty quietly neglected — these are not small failures of character in the Qur'an's accounting. They are breaches of the same order as apostasy in kind, if not in degree.

**A Covenant Is a Word That Binds the Speaker**

The second term, *ʿahd*, covers every undertaking a person has taken on: an oath, a treaty, a condition agreed in a sale or a marriage, a promise made to God in a moment of sincerity, and the primordial undertaking of the creature toward his Creator. Related words — *mīthāq*, a firm pact, and *mīʿād*, an appointed tryst — extend the same idea through the Book's language about the relation between God and human beings.

What makes a covenant different from an intention is that it has been externalised. An intention can be revised without wronging anyone; a promise has already changed the position of the person who received it, who has planned on it and may have acted on it. That is why the Qur'an treats keeping one as a matter of justice rather than of good manners. The word has left the speaker and is now load-bearing in someone else's life.

**The Same Pair in the Same Order Elsewhere**

The identical formulation appears in the sūrah of the Ascending Ways: *"˹The faithful are˺ also those who are true to their trusts and covenants;"* (70:32). Same two nouns, same order, same verb. Two sūrahs describe the believer with the same phrase, and the phrase is not a general commendation of honesty — it names the two specific categories of owed things.

The stability of the formulation is the point. Where the Qur'an repeats a description in the same words, it is marking that description as a fixed feature of the portrait rather than an incidental detail of one passage. Trusts and covenants belong to the definition of a believer in the same way that humility in prayer does, and a person who is careless about deposits and promises has failed one of the marks on the list, not merely one of the courtesies.

**The Question That Comes With the Pledge**

The Book attaches an audit to the second term. Of the orphan's property and of undertakings generally: *"And honour ˹your˺ pledges, for you will surely be accountable for them."* (17:34). Accountability is attached to the pledge itself, not only to its performance — a promise made lightly will be asked about as a promise made lightly.

That closes the loop with this verse. A trust can be returned or embezzled, and the record is kept either way; a covenant can be honoured or broken, and the word spoken will be produced again. The believer described here is someone who lives with both records open, and who therefore does not accept what he cannot carry. The sūrah's later verses will describe people who ask to be sent back in order to act differently; this verse describes the ones who did not need to ask.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:9

> and those who are ˹properly˺ observant of their prayers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The List Opens and Closes With Prayer**

The second verse described believers as humble in their prayers; this one describes them as guarding them. Prayer is the frame of the whole list. Everything between — silence about what does not matter, alms, guarded desire, kept trusts — sits inside a structure whose first and last items are the same act of worship.

That framing is a claim about where the rest of the list comes from. The intervening virtues are not independent achievements that happen to be found in religious people. They are what a person becomes when the prayer is maintained, and the sūrah's arrangement says so by putting prayer at both ends. Take away the frame and the middle items lose their support: silence becomes temperament, alms become generosity, kept trusts become reputation. Inside the frame they are the fruit of a discipline performed five times a day.

The closing position is the stronger of the two. A list that began with prayer and ended with property would be moving from the sacred to the ordinary. This one moves the other way: it works through the conduct of the world and returns to the act of worship, as though the conduct had been undertaken for the sake of the return. The believer is described as someone whose day is bracketed, and whose brackets are the same act.

**Guarding Means Keeping the Appointed Times**

*Yuḥāfiẓūn*, from ḥ-f-ẓ, means to keep watch over a thing so that it is not lost. Applied to prayer, the exegetes read it first of all as punctuality: each prayer performed inside its appointed window, not deferred until the next one absorbs it. The Book states the rule directly: *"When the prayers are over, remember Allah—whether you are standing, sitting, or lying down. But when you are secure, establish regular prayers. Indeed, performing prayers is a duty on the believers at the appointed times."* (4:103).

The emphasis on the time is not bureaucratic. A prayer made up afterwards is not the same act as a prayer made on time, because the time is part of what is being given. Anyone who has watched his own day will recognise the mechanism: a prayer pushed to its last moments is a prayer the day has already consumed, and the person who arrives at it arrives late in every sense. The command in the Cow is put in the same terms — *"Observe the ˹five obligatory˺ prayers—especially the middle prayer—and stand in true devotion to Allah."* (2:238) — with observation and standing in devotion joined.

**The Difference Between This Verse and Verse Two**

The two prayers in this list are not redundant. Verse two describes the quality inside the prayer; verse nine describes the continuity of the practice. A person can be present when he prays and irregular in praying. Another can be punctual for decades and never once truly there.

The sūrah requires both, and it separates them deliberately. Humility without constancy is an occasional experience; constancy without humility is a routine. Neither is the believer being described. By placing the inner quality first and the outward maintenance last, the list implies an order of difficulty and an order of dependence: presence is the harder achievement, but persistence is the one that keeps the harder achievement possible, because the person who prays only when he feels present will soon not pray at all.

**Guarding Presupposes Something That Slips**

The verb carries an assumption worth naming. Nobody guards a thing that stays put on its own. The word is used of watchmen, of stored goods, of memorised text — all of which tend toward loss and require attention to remain held. Prayer is placed in that category.

That is a realistic description rather than a pessimistic one. The prayer does not maintain itself; it is displaced by work, by sleep, by company, by the ordinary pressure of the day, and the displacement is never dramatic. No believer decides to abandon the prayer. He defers it, and then defers the deferral, and the habit erodes without a decision ever being taken. The verse describes people who have recognised this and set themselves against it, and the word it uses is the word for a man standing watch.

The same verb is used elsewhere in the Book for keeping a thing against loss, and the object is always something valuable and perishable. A watchman does not guard because the thing under guard is under active attack; he guards because leaving it unattended is itself enough to lose it. The prayer is placed in that category, and the description quietly concedes what every practising believer knows: the danger to the prayer is not persecution or doubt but inattention.

**The Same Word in al-Maʿārij**

The closing item of this list reappears verbatim in the sūrah of the Ascending Ways: *"and who are ˹properly˺ observant of their prayers."* (70:34). The same word, the same position at the end of the sequence, the same bracketing of trusts and covenants before it.

Two independent sūrahs arranging the believer's marks in the same order, with prayer closing both, is a strong indication that the description was fixed in the Prophet's teaching rather than composed for a local occasion. It also tells the reader how to weigh the items: if prayer were merely one virtue among seven, it would not be given the first and last positions twice. The sūrah is saying that the whole portrait stands or falls with the maintenance of this act.

**A Middle Prayer Left Unnamed**

The command in the Cow singles out one prayer for extra emphasis — *"especially the middle prayer"* (2:238) — and does not say which of the five it is. The exegetes have argued for the dawn prayer, the afternoon prayer, and others, and no reading has settled the matter.

The unresolved specification has an obvious effect on practice. A believer who does not know which prayer is the middle one cannot afford to be careless about any of them, and the ambiguity distributes the emphasis across all five. That is precisely the discipline this verse describes. Guarding the prayers is not a matter of protecting the ones that feel important and letting the others slide; it is treating every window as watched, because the one that matters most has not been identified.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:10

> These are the ones who will be awarded

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Pointer That Gathers Eight Descriptions**

*Ulāʾika hum* — these are the ones. The verse contains no new content at all. It is a demonstrative pointing back at the eight descriptions just completed, and it does the work of collection: eight separate qualities, each stated in its own verse, are now gathered into a single company and given a single outcome.

That collecting move matters more than it looks. A list of virtues can be read as a menu, from which a person takes the items he manages and leaves the rest. The demonstrative refuses that reading. It does not say "whoever does some of these"; it says *these* — the whole set, as one description of one kind of person. The reader who has been ticking items off is now told that the ticking was the wrong activity. What is being described is a shape of life, and the reward attaches to the shape.

The demonstrative also does something to time. Eight verses have described behaviour spread across a lifetime — prayers at their hours, wealth given out year after year, trusts returned whenever they come due. The pointer gathers all of that duration into a single present company. Whoever has been living this way is standing in it now, and the verse is not promising that he will one day qualify. The qualification is the life already lived.

**The Reward Is Framed as an Inheritance**

The word that follows is *wārithūn*, heirs, and the verse is left unfinished so that the object arrives only in the next one. The framing is legal rather than commercial. An heir does not earn an estate; he receives it by belonging to a household, and the transfer is fixed by relation rather than negotiated by performance.

The choice of this word is striking in a passage that has just listed effort. Humility in prayer, silence, alms, guarded desire, kept trusts, maintained prayer — all of these are work. Yet the reward is described as something inherited, which is to say as something that could not have been purchased at that price. The eight marks qualify a person for the household; they do not pay for the estate.

**Inheritance Is Not a Wage**

The Book uses both frames, and the difference between them is the whole difference between two ways of understanding the Hereafter. Elsewhere the same reward is described as earned: *"This is Paradise awarded to you for what you used to do."* (7:43). Here it is inherited.

Both statements are true on their own terms, and holding them together is not a contradiction. The wage language insists that the reward is not arbitrary — it corresponds to a life actually lived, and nobody enters the Garden without having done something. The inheritance language insists that the correspondence is not arithmetic — no quantity of prayer or alms equals a garden, and a person paid strictly for his deeds would be paid very little. The sūrah chooses the second frame at the moment of award, and the choice tells the reader how much of the outcome is grace.

There is a further implication in the legal image. An inheritance can be lost by an heir who repudiates the household, and it can be squandered by one who takes it for granted. The word carries obligation as well as entitlement. The believer described in the eight verses before this one is not a passive recipient of a bequest; he is a member of a household whose standing depends on how he has carried himself, and the sūrah has spent nine verses describing what that carrying looks like.

**A Sentence That Stops Before Its Object**

The verse ends on the verb. What is being inherited is withheld until the next verse names it. In recitation the pause is audible, and the effect is to hold the listener in the gap between the company and its estate.

Suspension of this kind is not decoration. It forces the hearer to complete the sentence himself, and what he supplies from his own expectation is then confirmed or corrected by the next line. A reader who expects a general reward — forgiveness, mercy, escape from the Fire — is given something more specific and more concrete: a place, owned, permanent. The delay makes the naming of that place land with more weight than a single verse containing both halves would have carried.

**The Definiteness of the Claim**

The structure is emphatic: a demonstrative, then a separated pronoun, then a definite noun. *These — they — are the heirs.* Arabic builds emphasis by exactly this kind of doubling, and the effect is exclusivity as well as certainty. Not "they will be among the heirs", but they are the heirs.

The exclusivity is not meant to exclude the merciful treatment of others; the Book is full of forgiveness extended to people who fall well short of this portrait. What it excludes is the assumption that the estate is general. Inheritance belongs to a household, and a household is defined. The verse is telling the reader that there is a specific company being described here, that it has identifiable marks, and that the estate goes to it — which returns him to the eight descriptions with a sharper question than the one he started with.

**The Same Promise Given to the Righteous**

The language of inheritance is not local to this passage. A decree recorded in the Scriptures is quoted: *"Surely, following the ˹heavenly˺ Record, We decreed in the Scriptures: 'My righteous servants shall inherit the land.'"* (21:105). And the righteous themselves will say on the Day: *"Praise be to Allah Who has fulfilled His promise to us, and made us inherit the ˹everlasting˺ land to settle in Paradise wherever we please."* (39:74). The second of these is spoken from inside the reward, and it uses the same verb this verse uses. The promise made here is the promise those verses describe as already kept — which is what the perfect tense of an inheritance always implies: the estate has been settled, and the only question left is who turns up to take possession.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:11

> Paradise as their own. They will be there forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**Firdaws: The Word Behind the English "Paradise"**

The Arabic word used here is *firdaws*, and it is not an ordinary word for garden. Arabic already has *jannah* for that, a term built on the idea of something covered and hidden, which is why it fits a garden dense with foliage. *Firdaws* is a borrowing, and its history is shared with the English word "paradise". Both descend from an Old Persian compound, *pairi-daēza*, meaning a walled enclosure — a park enclosed by a wall, which passed into Greek as *paradeisos* and into Arabic in this form. Lexicographers of Qur'anic vocabulary have long traced the word along that route, and the older Arabic authorities agreed that it denotes a garden while differing over what kind.

The etymology carries meaning into the verse. A walled enclosure is a place with an inside and an outside, and admission to it is a matter of the gate rather than of the landscape. The word chosen for the believers' estate is the word for the garden that has a boundary. The eight marks in the verses before this one are the description of who is inside the wall, and the sūrah has been precise about them because the enclosure is.

**The Highest Garden, Named in a Ḥadīth**

The Prophet gave the word a location in the geography of the Garden. He said that Paradise has a hundred grades prepared for those who strive in God's cause, that the distance between two grades is like the distance between heaven and earth, and then: when you ask God, ask Him for al-Firdaws, for it is the best and highest part of Paradise — the sub-narrator adding that he believed the Prophet also said that above it is the Throne of the Most Merciful, and from it the rivers of Paradise flow. The report is in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, 2790.

Three details in that report bear on this verse. Firdaws is not one garden among many but the summit; the rivers of the Garden originate there, so every other garden is downstream of it; and the Throne stands above it, making it the nearest place in the reward to God. When the sūrah names the estate of the believers with this word rather than the generic one, it is naming the top of the structure. The eight marks are not the price of a modest plot.

**The Object That Was Withheld**

The previous verse ended on the verb, and this one supplies what was missing. The heirs inherit *al-Firdaws* — a specific estate, named, and described as their own. The syntax of possession is worth noting: the garden is theirs in the way an inheritance is a person's, not in the way a rented room is his.

That is a large claim made in three words. The reward promised in most religious language is relief — escape from punishment, admission to a place of safety. What is promised here is ownership of the highest garden in existence, held permanently. The sūrah has moved from the believer's humility in prayer to his possession of Paradise without any change of register, and the sequence implies that the second follows from the first by the ordinary logic of the household rather than by an extraordinary favour.

**Eternity Stated in a Single Phrase**

*Fīhā khālidūn* — in it, abiding. Three Arabic words carry the permanence, and they are the same words the Book uses for the inhabitants of the Fire. The symmetry is exact and it is not softened anywhere: whatever the two places differ in, they do not differ in duration.

The pairing does two things at once. It removes the consolatory reading of punishment as temporary correction, which the Qur'an nowhere offers. And it removes the assumption that eternity is automatic on the side of reward. Permanence is a property of both estates, and the sūrah is telling the reader that the only variable within his control is which of them he is being described in. The eight marks were given in detail precisely because the outcome has no middle term and no later appeal.

**Eight Marks and One Estate**

The opening section of the sūrah is now complete, and its architecture is visible. A verdict, eight descriptions, a collection of the described into a company, a transfer, and a permanence. Nothing in the structure is decorative, and nothing is repeated: each of the eleven verses does one job.

That compression is unusual. Most of the Qur'an's descriptions of the reward are expansive, lingering on rivers, garments, couches and shade. Here the reward is given in five words and the specification of who receives it is given in eight verses. The proportion is the message. The sūrah is far more interested in the description of the believers than in the description of what they get, and a reader who skips to the end of the passage to see what is promised has read the part the sūrah considered obvious and missed the part it considered difficult.

**The Garden Promised to the Devout**

The same estate is promised elsewhere in the Book with the same economy, and the condition is stated in the same register of inner disposition: *"That is Paradise, which We will grant to whoever is devout among Our servants."* (19:63). Devotion there and humility in prayer here name the same orientation of a person, and both are followed by a list of outward conduct rather than a list of feelings.

The parallel confirms what this passage has been doing from its first verse. The Garden is not awarded to a state of mind. It is awarded to the people whose state of mind has produced a describable life, and the description is always given — never left to the reader's imagination about his own sincerity.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:12

> And indeed, We created humankind from an extract of clay

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Turns From Conduct to Origin**

Eleven verses have described a kind of person and awarded him an estate. Verse twelve abandons the subject entirely and begins an account of how a human being is made. The turn is abrupt enough to look like a new topic, and it is not.

The sūrah is arguing about resurrection, and the argument it chooses is not philosophical but observational. Before it can tell the denier that he will be raised, it has to establish that he has already been made once, by someone else, out of material that gave no indication of what it would become. The opening section supplied the moral stake — there is a reward and people are qualified or disqualified for it. This section supplies the physical premise. A creature who did not assemble himself from an extract of clay has no grounds for declaring that the one who did it cannot do it again.

**Sulālah: Something Drawn Out and Refined**

The word for the material, *sulālah*, is not the ordinary word for clay. It is formed from a root meaning to draw something out, to extract it from a larger mass — the way a sword is drawn from a scabbard or a thread pulled from a bundle. What the verse describes is not raw material scooped up but a portion separated from the rest and refined.

That nuance is easy to miss and it matters. An extract is a selected thing, and selection implies purpose at the very first stage. The human being is not made from the first material to hand; he is made from what was taken out of it. The sūrah will go on to describe a sequence in which each stage produces something the previous one did not contain, and it begins that sequence with a word that already implies the sequence: something drawn out, in order to become something else.

**Clay, and What the Book Says About It**

Elsewhere the same origin is described with more specificity, and the variations are informative rather than contradictory. Human beings are made *"from sounding clay moulded from black mud"* (15:26), and *"from ˹sounding˺ clay like pottery,"* (55:14). The creation is *"originate[d]... from clay"* (32:7). Elsewhere it is simply clay, or dust, or a base fluid.

Clay is the significant choice among these. It is not one element but two — earth and water — already combined, and the combination is what makes it workable. Dust alone cannot be shaped; mud alone cannot hold a shape. Clay is the point at which the mixture becomes capable of receiving a form, and the Qur'an's repeated use of the word puts the origin of the human being at precisely that point. The other descriptions add texture: dried, sounding when struck, black, altered by fire. They describe the same material at different stages, and together they insist that the body is earthenware — made, formed, and perishable.

**Adam, or Every Human Being**

The exegetes divide over the referent of "humankind" here. Some take it as Adam alone, the first man formed directly from clay, and read the following verses as a shift to his descendants. Others take it as the species, in which case the clay is the remote origin of every person. A third position, recorded by al-Rāzī, holds that the clay is a reference to Adam himself rather than to the material.

The general reading is the stronger, and the reason is structural. The verse that follows describes a drop placed in a secure lodging, which is not how Adam was made but how every human being since has been made. If verse twelve were only about Adam, the sequence from twelve to sixteen would switch subjects mid-sentence without warning. Reading it as the species makes the whole passage one continuous account: the extract of clay in the first man, then the same origin repeated in every womb, then death, then rising. The particular creation is the origin of the general pattern.

**The Material Answer to an Old Objection**

The objection this origin invites is recorded in the Book in the mouth of Iblīs: *"Should I prostrate to the one You have created from mud?"* (17:61). The argument is not new to the Qur'an's Meccan audience; it is the argument of the first objector, and it takes the same form as the argument against resurrection — that a thing made from low material cannot be worth much, and certainly cannot be raised to anything.

The verse answers it by owning the premise. There is no attempt to elevate the material or to deny that it is mud. The human being is from an extract of clay, and the honour lies not in the clay but in what was done with it. That is the same answer the sūrah will give about resurrection: the material is not the issue, because the material never made anything by itself. Whoever drew the extract out of the earth and made it into a person who could deny Him can take the same substance back and make it again.

**Dust, Clay, Mud and Pottery**

The Book returns to this origin with a persistence that suggests the point is being pressed rather than illustrated. *"He is the One Who created you from clay, then appointed a term ˹for your death˺ and another known only to Him ˹for your resurrection˺ —yet you continue to doubt!"* (6:2), where the material and the two appointed times are joined in one sentence. And again in the address to the angels: *"I am going to create a human being from clay."* (38:71). The pairing in 6:2 is the one that matters here: origin, term, return, and then the accusation of doubt. That is exactly the four-part structure this sūrah is now building, verse by verse.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:13

> then placed each ˹human˺ as a sperm-drop in a secure place

**Expanded Commentary**

**Nuṭfah: The Smallest Word for a Whole Person**

The word is *nuṭfah*, a drop — specifically a drop of seminal fluid. Arabic uses the term for any small quantity of liquid, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly for the beginning of a human being. The choice is deliberate in its diminishment: the account of human origins does not begin with anything impressive, and the word chosen is the word for a quantity too small to notice.

The Book makes the diminishment explicit wherever it wants to argue from it. *"He created humans from a sperm-drop, then—behold!—they openly challenge ˹Him˺."* (16:4). And in the sūrah of the Rising: *"Were they not ˹once˺ a sperm-drop emitted?"* (75:37). Both verses set the smallness of the beginning beside the arrogance of the later creature, and both use the exclamation to mark the disproportion. The man arguing with his Maker is a man who began as a drop, and the argument he is making is about his own importance.

**A Fixed and Fortified Lodging**

The place is described with two words: *qarār*, a settled place of rest, and *makīn*, firmly established, from a root meaning to be firmly placed and to have standing. Together they describe more than a container. A *qarār* is where a thing comes to rest and stays; a *makīn* place is one that holds its position and cannot be easily disturbed.

That is an accurate description of the womb in its function. It is enclosed, protected, supplied, and fixed within a body that moves while the thing inside does not. The verse in the Pilgrimage uses the same idea with a different emphasis: *"˹Then˺ We settle whatever ˹embryo˺ We will in the womb for an appointed term, then bring you forth as infants, so that you may reach your prime."* (22:5). There the settling is joined to a term, and the emphasis falls on the fact that the secure place is secure for a measured period and no longer.

**From One Origin to Every Womb**

The sequence moves here from the species to the individual. The extract of clay was one origin; the drop in a secure place is an origin repeated in every person. The verse is careful about this: what was placed there is a human being, not a part of one.
