That move is the hinge of the whole passage. If the account stopped at the first man, it would be a story about the distant past with no bearing on the listener. By making the second stage an event in every life, the sūrah puts its argument inside the experience of everyone hearing it. The listener is not being told about Adam's formation. He is being told about his own, and about a process he has watched operate in his own household, among his own children, without ever having arranged any of it.

**Why the Argument Begins With Something Small**

Rhetorically the sūrah is doing something unusual. Most arguments for the resurrection in the Qur'an begin with the earth: the dead land revived by rain, the seed producing a plant, the visible demonstration that what seems finished is not. This passage begins instead with the smallest possible instance of a beginning, and it keeps the scale small for three verses.

The strategy is to make the denier's objection unusable. The man who doubts that he will be raised is usually doubting because the claim sounds large. So the sūrah gives him a claim that sounds small — a drop, a clot, a lump — and lets him watch it become a person. Once he accepts that, the size of the resurrection stops being the obstacle it appeared to be. *"Do people not see that We have created them from a sperm-drop, then— behold!—they openly challenge ˹Us˺?"* (36:77). The challenge and the drop are put in the same sentence, and the disproportion is the argument.

**Secure, But Not Permanent**

The two adjectives describing the place are about stability, not about duration, and the verse that follows supplies the duration. A secure lodging is one you will leave. The sūrah does not dwell on the point here, but the sequence makes it unavoidable: the drop becomes something else, that becomes something else, and the whole progression runs toward a birth and then a death.

Elsewhere the Book spells out the term attached to the process: *"then He brings you forth as infants, so that you may reach your prime, and become old— though some of you ˹may˺ die sooner—reaching an appointed time, so perhaps you may understand ˹Allah's power˺."* (40:67). The security of the womb is the first of a series of provisions, each of which ends. The verse is not sentimental about the beginning of life; it is using it to establish that the whole career of a human being is a sequence of placements made by someone else, each with a limit attached.

The phrase "so perhaps you may understand" at the end of that verse states the purpose of the account. The stages are not being narrated for their interest. They are being narrated so that the person who has been carried through every one of them, without deciding anything about any of them, will draw the obvious conclusion about who is doing the carrying.

**The Same Sequence Repeated Seven Times**

This account is not a local detail. The stages from drop to birth appear in the Pilgrimage, in Fāṭir, in the Believer, in the Rising, in the Sent Forth, in ʿAbasa, and in al-Insān — seven places, with variations of emphasis but never a contradiction. The Sent Forth asks: *"Did We not create you from a humble fluid,"* (77:20), and ʿAbasa states: *"He created them from a sperm-drop, and ordained their development."* (80:19). A theme returned to that often, in that many different registers of argument, is not ornament. It is the Qur'an's standing evidence for the claim that the one who began this can begin again.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:14

> then We developed the drop into a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then developed the clot into a lump ˹of flesh˺, then developed the lump into bones, then clothed the bones with flesh, then We brought it into being as a new creation. So Blessed is Allah, the Best of Creators

**Expanded Commentary**

**Six Movements in a Single Sentence**

The verse runs through six stages joined by *thumma*, then — a particle that carries sequence and, in most readings, some interval between the steps. Drop, clot, lump, bones, flesh laid over bones, and finally a new creation. Five of the six are physical transformations of the same material; the sixth is of a different order and will need separate handling.

The syntax is as important as the content. Each stage takes the previous one as its object: the drop is made into a clot, the clot into a lump, the lump into bones. Nothing is discarded and nothing is added from outside except at the last step. What the verse describes is one continuous work on one piece of material, and the accumulation of *thumma* clauses gives the reader the sense of time passing — of a process that takes place while nobody is attending to it.

**ʿAlaqah: The Clinging Thing**

The second stage is *ʿalaqah*, from a root meaning to cling, to hang, to be suspended. The word is used for a leech and for a blood clot, and the two senses are connected by the idea of attachment. The verse in the Pilgrimage gives the fuller description: *"then ˹developed you˺ into a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then a lump of flesh —fully formed or unformed"* (22:5), where the addition "fully formed or unformed" acknowledges that this stage may or may not proceed.

That qualification is significant. The Qur'an does not describe an automatic process running to completion every time. It describes a sequence that can stop, and it says so in the one verse that sets the stages out for the express purpose of answering doubt. The Rising states the sequence more briefly and adds a making and a perfecting: *"Then they became a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then He developed and perfected their form,"* (75:38). Between the two accounts the reader has both the stages and the fact that they are governed.

**Muḍghah: A Chewed Lump**

The third stage is *muḍghah*, a morsel — the amount a mouth would take, or the appearance of something that has been chewed. The word is chosen for its look rather than its substance. At this stage the developing thing is a small mass of tissue whose surface is uneven and indented, and the Arabic names it by the resemblance.

Two features of the term deserve notice. It is a word of quantity as well as appearance — a *muḍghah* is small, and the verse is still insisting on the smallness of the material. And it is a word with no dignity in it. There is no attempt to make the stage sound impressive. The Qur'an's account of human origins runs from clay to a drop to a clot to a chewed morsel, and the accumulation of undignified words is doing argumentative work: the person who will later deny his Maker began as a thing that would not have been looked at twice.

**Bones, Then the Flesh That Covers Them**

The fourth and fifth stages reverse the usual expectation about what supports what. Bones are formed first, and the flesh is then laid over them as a covering — the verb is *kasawnā*, we clothed, the word used for putting a garment on a body. The frame precedes the covering.

The Believer gives the same order in a longer account: *"then from a sperm-drop, then ˹developed you into˺ a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then He brings you forth as infants"* (40:67), compressing the middle stages but keeping the sequence intact. The clothing image is the important element here. A garment is put on something that already exists and has a shape; it is not generated from it. The verse is describing a construction in which the structure comes first and the visible body is fitted to it afterwards — which is exactly the order that makes the human form possible, and exactly the order no one arranges.

**"Another Creation": Three Readings Weighed**

*Thumma anshaʾnāhu khalqan ākhar* — then We brought him into being as another creation. The exegetes divide three ways. Some read it as the inbreathing of the spirit into what had until then been only a form. Others take it as birth. A third group, including al-Rāzī, understands it as development into maturity and the full use of the faculties.

The first reading is the strongest, for a reason internal to the verse. The five preceding stages are all transformations of matter, and the phrase "another creation" marks a break in kind rather than a further step in the same series; if it meant merely another stage of growth, the word *ākhar* would be doing nothing. The Book describes the inbreathing elsewhere in exactly these terms: *"then He fashioned them and had a spirit of His Own ˹creation˺ breathed into them. And He gave you hearing, sight, and intellect."* (32:9). The second and third readings are not excluded by this; birth and maturity are further instances of the same break. But the primary sense is the moment at which the thing in the womb stopped being tissue and became someone.

**The Exclamation That Ends the Sentence**

The verse does not end with its description. It ends with *tabāraka Allāh*, Blessed is Allah — a form of praise built on a root meaning to be exalted and abundant in good, used elsewhere for God alone — followed by *aḥsan al-khāliqīn*, the Best of creators.

That final phrase has been much discussed, because it appears to concede that there are other creators. The Arabic usage is straightforward: the word *khāliq* covers making, devising and estimating, and human beings make things. The superlative does not grant them equality; it locates them on a scale whose top is occupied by the one who made them out of a clot. The exclamation is placed where it is for a reason. After six stages of undramatic material transformation, the verse breaks into praise, and the praise is the point of the passage: the description was given so that this sentence could be said about it.

**The Same Stages Set Out for the Doubter**

The Pilgrimage states the purpose of the sequence openly: *"O humanity! If you are in doubt about the Resurrection, then ˹know that˺ We did create you from dust, then from a sperm-drop..."* (22:5), and closes the argument with *"That is because Allah ˹alone˺ is the Truth, He ˹alone˺ gives life to the dead, and He ˹alone˺ is Most Capable of everything."* (22:6). The stages are evidence, not anatomy. This sūrah uses them the same way, and the reader who treats the passage as a description of embryology alone has missed the argument it was written to make.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:15

> After that you will surely die

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address Turns to You**

The three verses before this one spoke about "him" — the drop, the clot, the thing brought into being as another creation. This verse turns and speaks to the listener directly: you will die. The shift from third person to second is not a stylistic variation. It moves the account out of the category of information and into the category of address.

Arabic grammar makes the certainty heavier than the English shows. The sentence carries both *inna*, the particle of emphasis, and the emphatic *lām* attached to the active participle — a construction that leaves no room for a hedge. It is not "you will probably die" or "death comes to everyone". It is a statement about you, personally, in the strongest form the language provides. The man who has been following the stages of his own formation is now told what the sequence ends in, and he is told it as a fact about himself rather than as a general truth.

**The Flattest Sentence in the Passage**

Nothing is added. There is no comfort, no explanation of why, no reminder of what comes after — the next verse handles that, and this one is left bare. The sūrah could have written "you will die and then be raised" in a single breath, and instead it separates the two, letting the first stand alone for a moment.

The Book elsewhere refuses to soften the same statement. *"You ˹O Prophet˺ will certainly die, and they will die too."* (39:30), addressed to the one person whose death the hearers least wanted to face, and paired with theirs in the same sentence. *"Every soul will taste death. And We test you ˹O humanity˺ with good and evil as a trial, then to Us you will ˹all˺ be returned."* (21:35). The word "taste" is the notable one in that verse: death is not something that happens around a person but something he experiences, in the first person, once.

**Death Placed Between Making and Raising**

The position of this verse inside the sequence is the argument. Creation, death, resurrection — three stages, in that order, with nothing else inserted. The sūrah of the Heights puts the same three in God's mouth as a decree about the human being: *"Therein you shall live, and therein you shall die, and thence shall you be brought forth."* (7:25).

Death is therefore not an interruption in the Qur'an's account of a human life. It is the second of three appointed events, and the fact that it is the only one the listener has no doubt about is what makes it useful as a premise. Everyone concedes the first stage, because he exists. Everyone concedes the second, because he watches it happen. The dispute is only ever about the third, and the sūrah's method is to place the third immediately after two things nobody denies, so that the denial has to explain why the sequence stops.

**The End of the Extract, Not of the Making**

What dies is the thing that was made from an extract of clay. The verse does not describe death as annihilation or as the disappearance of a person; it describes it as the termination of the body's term. The material goes back, and the sequence that built it out of a drop is over.

That framing matters for what follows. If death were the end of the person, resurrection would be the creation of a new person who happens to resemble the old one, and the promise of reward and punishment would lose its point. The sūrah avoids that reading by keeping the three stages continuous: the same "you" who was made, who dies, and who is raised. The body's term ends; the person's account does not. What the verse removes is the assumption that the body's persistence was ever guaranteed, and with it the assumption that a body which has been dissolved cannot be assembled again by whoever assembled it the first time.

**Certainty That Needs No Support**

Four Arabic words carry the whole statement, and no evidence is offered for it. The sūrah has just spent three verses building a detailed case about origins, and here it argues for the most certain fact in human experience by simply asserting it.

The economy is itself the point. A claim that requires support is a claim somebody might reasonably doubt. Death does not require support, and the sūrah treats it accordingly — no proof, no illustration, no appeal to observation, because the listener is already convinced. What the verse is doing is not establishing a fact but placing one. It takes the certainty the reader already holds and installs it in the middle of the sequence, where it will do work in the verse that follows.

That placement is what lets the verse be brief without being thin. Nothing new is taught; something known is positioned, and the positioning is the whole of the verse's contribution.

**The Reward Held Back Until the Day**

The bareness of this verse is answered by a statement elsewhere about when the account is settled: *"Every soul will taste death. And you will only receive your full reward on the Day of Judgment. Whoever is spared from the Fire and is admitted into Paradise will ˹indeed˺ triumph, whereas the life of this world is no more than the delusion of enjoyment."* (3:185). Death is not the payment. It is the closing of the period during which anything could still be changed.

That is why the sūrah lets the verse stand alone. Between this statement and the next one lies the whole of a person's expectation, and the reader who reads on immediately discovers that the sequence does not stop. The verse is a door, and its flatness is what makes the room beyond it legible.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:16

> then on the Day of Judgment you will be resurrected

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Passive Verb: Raised by Someone Else**

*Tubʿathūn* is passive. You will be raised — not you will rise. The verb removes the agent from the sentence, and the absence is the claim: whatever happens at the end of the sequence will not be done by the one it happens to.

That single grammatical feature answers the most common objection to resurrection, which is not really about possibility but about agency. A person who says "how can the dead rise?" is usually saying that he cannot imagine a mechanism. The verse does not offer one. It states the outcome in a form that assigns it to another, and it leaves the mechanism where the sūrah has left it all along — with the one who made the drop into a person in the first place. The same passivity runs through the three verses before this: We created, We placed, We developed, We clothed, We brought into being. The raising is the last item in a series of acts the listener did not perform.

**The Day Named for Standing**

*Yawm al-qiyāmah* — the Day of Rising, or of Standing. The noun comes from the same root as the verb in this verse, and the two are deliberately matched: the day takes its name from what happens on it. It is not called the Day of Judgement here, though judgement is what occurs; it is called the Day of Standing Up.

The naming is a physical description before it is a theological one. The image is of bodies that were horizontal becoming vertical, of a scene in which everyone is on their feet at once. That image does more work than the abstract word "judgement" would, because it is continuous with the account the sūrah has just given. The same power that stood the drop up into a person stands the dissolved body up again, and the day is named for the standing rather than for the verdict that follows it.

Standing also implies exposure. A person lying down is covered, contained, out of sight; a person standing is visible, and standing before someone is the posture of answering. The day takes its name from that posture, and the sūrah's later verses will return to it when they describe people asking to be sent back. What happens on the Day is described throughout the Book in terms of standing, being shown, and being unable to leave.

**The Claim the Denier Actually Makes**

The verse is short because the position it answers is short. The Book records the denial in its plainest form: *"They insisted, 'There is nothing beyond this worldly life and we will never be resurrected.'"* (6:29). Two claims, and both are answered by the sequence in verses twelve to sixteen without a word of argument.

There is nothing beyond this life — but the life in question began as a drop, which was not this life, and became something else. We will never be resurrected — but you have already been assembled once, by an agent you did not choose, from material that contained no instruction about becoming you. The sūrah does not refute the denier with logic. It describes his own history in five verses and lets the description do the refuting, which is why the answer here can be as brief as the denial.

**Two Verses as One Argument**

Verses fifteen and sixteen are a pair that cannot be separated without losing the point. Death alone is a fact about biology; resurrection alone is a claim about the future. Joined, they form the third term of a sequence whose first two terms nobody disputes.

The sūrah's method throughout this section is to build from the conceded to the contested. You were made — conceded, you exist. You will die — conceded, you watch it happen. You will be raised — disputed. But the disputed term is not independent of the other two; it is the completion of the same series, performed by the same agent, on the same material. To deny the third while accepting the first two is not to hold a different opinion about evidence. It is to stop the sequence at an arbitrary point because the next step is unwelcome.

**What Is Raised Is What Died**

The verse keeps the second person from the verse before it. The same "you" that dies is the "you" that is raised, and the sūrah does not introduce a new subject or a new body. That continuity is what makes the promise meaningful.

If the person raised were merely someone similar to the person who died, the reward and the punishment described elsewhere in the sūrah would attach to a stranger. The Qur'an's insistence on the identity of the two is what turns resurrection from a speculation about matter into a statement about responsibility. The one who kept his trusts and guarded his prayer is the one who will be raised, and the account will be his. The verse is therefore not only a claim about the future but a claim about the present: the person reading it is the subject of the sentence, and the sentence has not yet reached its verb.

**The Return to the True Master**

Elsewhere the Book describes the same event as a return rather than a beginning: *"Then and there every soul will face ˹the consequences of˺ what it had done. They all will be returned to Allah—their True Master. And whatever ˹gods˺ they fabricated will fail them."* (10:30). The word for returned implies that the soul was somewhere before, and belonged somewhere.

That framing completes the three-stage structure of this passage. Made, killed, brought back — and the last stage is described as a homecoming to an owner rather than as an arrival at a court. The fabricated gods failing at that moment is the verse's sharpest detail: everything a person relied on besides the one who made him from a drop will be unavailable at the point where it is needed, and what remains is the original relation.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:17

> And indeed, We created above you seven levels ˹of heaven˺. We are never unmindful of ˹Our˺ creation

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṭarāʾiq: Layers Laid One Over Another**

The word translated as levels is *ṭarāʾiq*, the plural of *ṭarīqah*. Most readers meet this word as meaning paths or ways, and that is one of its senses. But the same root carries the idea of laying one thing on top of another: the verb *ṭāraqa* means to superimpose, and a *ṭarīqah* is a layer, a stratum, a band laid over what is beneath it. The commentators who read the verse as describing the seven heavens take the word in this second sense.

That reading is supported by the way the Qur'an describes the heavens elsewhere. Nūḥ asks his people: *"Do you not see how Allah created seven heavens, one above the other,"* (71:15), using a phrase that means literally in layers, one upon one. The sūrah of al-Mulk says the same: *"˹He is the One˺ Who created seven heavens, one above the other. You will never see any imperfection in the creation of the Most Compassionate."* (67:3). If the heavens are described as stacked, then a word meaning layers is the natural description of them here.

**The Other Reading: The Paths of the Planets**

A second exegetical position takes *ṭarāʾiq* in its commoner sense of paths and understands them as the courses of the seven luminaries visible to the naked eye — the five planets, plus the sun and the moon. A related version identifies them with the celestial spheres in which those bodies were traditionally held to move.

The grounds for this reading are the ordinary meaning of the word and the traditional cosmology, in which each planet had its own sphere. The grounds against it are that the verse says these things were created "above you", which fits a stacked structure better than a set of orbital tracks, and that the Qur'an's own parallel descriptions consistently emphasise layering rather than motion. The layered reading is the stronger. The planetary reading is not a rival account of the same reality so much as an older cosmological vocabulary applied to the same verses, and it survives in the commentaries because the commentators were careful not to discard a meaning the word could bear.

**Seven Heavens as a Standing Description**

The number is fixed across the Book and never varies. *"He is the One Who created everything in the earth for you. Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens."* (2:29). *"So He formed the heaven into seven heavens in two Days, assigning to each its mandate."* (41:12). *"Allah is the One Who created seven heavens ˹in layers˺, and likewise for the earth."* (65:12).

Two features of these parallels matter for this verse. The first is that the seven are always described as made, never as pre-existing, and always by the same agent. The second is the phrase in al-Fuṣṣilat — "assigning to each its mandate" — which says that each layer has a function allotted to it. The cosmology in the Qur'an is not a map of the sky for its own sake. It is a description of an ordered structure in which every level has been given a task, and the reader is meant to see order where he might otherwise see emptiness.

**The Second Sentence Carries the Point**

The verse could have ended with the seven. It does not: *"We are never unmindful of ˹Our˺ creation."* That clause is the reason the cosmology is here at all.

The statement answers a specific worry, which is the worry of anyone who has looked at the size of the sky and then at his own life. A structure of that scale invites the inference that its maker is occupied elsewhere. The verse refuses the inference in the plainest possible terms — not unmindful, in the sense of a person who has ceased to attend to something he once knew about. The negation is stronger than "God knows about creation"; it denies the specific possibility of neglect. Whatever the seven levels are, and however large they are, nothing in them and nothing beneath them has slipped out of attention.

**A Spiritual Reading of the Seven**

The mystical commentators read the seven as the veils over the heart — layers of ignorance and vice laid one on another, which the discipline of the spiritual life has to remove in order for the rain of the next verse to reach the ground.

The reading is worth reporting because it is coherent with the sequence and because it does not compete with the literal sense. The verses that follow describe water sent down in measure and soaking into the earth, and a reader who takes the earth of verse eighteen as the receptive heart will naturally take the layers above it as what stands between the water and the soil. That is an application of the verse rather than an alternative to it, and it depends on the literal claim: the allegory works because the physical description is true. A reader who keeps only the allegory loses the argument the sūrah is making about the one who attends to everything.

**Never Unaware: A Repeated Assurance**

The phrase is not unique to this place. After describing hardening of the heart, the Cow closes: *"And Allah is never unaware of what you do."* (2:74). Hūd closes with the same assurance directed at the Prophet himself: *"And to Him all matters are returned. So worship Him and put your trust in Him. And your Lord is never unaware of what you do."* (11:123).

The repetition shows what the phrase is for. It appears where a person might reasonably suppose that his situation has been overlooked — a hardening community, a prophet opposed by his own people, an individual believer inside a structure of seven heavens. The assurance is not information about God's knowledge in the abstract. It is a specific denial that anything is too small, too distant or too long-delayed to have been noticed.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:18

> We send down rain from the sky in perfect measure, causing it to soak into the earth. And We are surely able to take it away

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bi-Qadar: A Measured Descent**

The rain comes *bi-qadar*, by measure. The word *qadar* covers quantity, proportion and appointed degree, and the Qur'an uses it as a general principle of creation: *"Indeed, We have created everything, perfectly preordained."* (54:49). Applied to rainfall it says something precise: the water that falls is not a random quantity but a calibrated one.

The parallel verses make the calibration explicit and give it a moral reason. *"And ˹He is the One˺ Who sends down rain from the sky in perfect measure, with which We give life to a lifeless land. And so will you be brought forth ˹from the grave˺."* (43:11), where the measured rain is made the image of resurrection. And in the Consultation the measure is explained: *"Had Allah given abundant provisions to ˹all˺ His servants, they would have certainly transgressed throughout the land. But He sends down whatever He wills in perfect measure."* (42:27). The restraint is not a limitation on generosity. It is the form the generosity takes.

**Water Stored in the Ground, Not Just Falling on It**

The second clause is easily passed over: the water is caused to soak into the earth. The verse does not stop at precipitation. It follows the water after it lands, into the soil, where it is held.

That is a specific and unusual thing for a seventh-century text to single out. Rain that runs off the surface is visible and immediate; rain that infiltrates is invisible and delayed, and it is the second kind that sustains springs, wells and roots through the dry season. The verse names the hidden part of the process as the important part. The sūrah of al-Ḥijr makes the same point by denying human control over it: *"We send fertilizing winds, and bring down rain from the sky for you to drink. It is not you who hold its reserves."* (15:22). The storage is the provision, and the provision is not in anyone's hands but His.

**The Capacity of the Valleys**

Another verse describes the same descent with attention to what receives it: *"He sends down rain from the sky, causing the valleys to flow, each according to its capacity."* (13:17). The measure is in the sending and the capacity is in the receiving, and the two are matched.

The verse then turns the image into a parable about truth and falsehood, using the foam that rides on the flood and the useful residue that stays in the ground. The move is characteristic: a physical process is described accurately, and the accuracy is what makes it usable as an illustration. For this verse the relevant detail is the phrase "each according to its capacity" — the same rain falls everywhere, and the amount each place holds depends on the shape of the place. That is a description of water, and it is also the sūrah's standing image for how guidance is received.

**The Threat in the Last Clause**

The verse ends by naming a capacity rather than a fact: *"And We are surely able to take it away."* Nothing has been withdrawn. The rain is still falling, the ground is still holding it, and the sentence says only that removal is possible.

Two other passages put the same possibility as a question. *"Say, 'Consider this: if your water were to sink ˹into the earth˺, then who ˹else˺ could bring you flowing water?'"* (67:30). And: *"Have you considered the water you drink? Is it you who bring it down from the clouds, or is it We Who do so? If We willed, We could make it salty. Will you not then give thanks?"* (56:68-70). All three do the same work: they take the most taken-for-granted provision in human life and show that it is a provision rather than a property. The clause is placed at the end of a verse about blessing, where it changes the reader's relation to the blessing without changing the blessing.

**The Allegorical Reading and What It Depends On**

Commentators read the verse spiritually: the earth as the receptive heart, the water as knowledge and insight, the measure as the amount each heart can hold at a time. The reading fits the sequence, since the next verses describe what the water brings forth.

It is worth stating what the allegory requires. The spiritual reading has force only because the physical claim is true — because water really is sent in a quantity that is not arbitrary, and really does soak into ground that receives it according to its capacity. Take away the literal fact and the illustration collapses into a metaphor about nothing. The exegetes who read the verse this way were not replacing its meaning with a hidden one. They were extending it, and the extension stands on the description.

**Blessed Rain and the Grain**

The same descent is described elsewhere with its outcome attached: *"And We send down blessed rain from the sky, bringing forth gardens and grains for harvest,"* (50:9). The word for blessed there is the same family of words as the exclamation that closed verse fourteen, and the connection is exact — the making of the human body and the making of his food are described with the same vocabulary of blessing.

That is the function of this verse inside the sūrah's argument. Verses twelve to sixteen described a person made and then killed and then raised. This verse describes the provision that keeps him alive in between, sent in measure, stored in the ground, and revocable at will. The one who measured the water is the one who assembled the drop.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:19

> With it We produce for you gardens of palm trees and grapevines, in which there are abundant fruits, and from which you may eat

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Trees Named Before All the Rest**

The water described in the verse before produces *jannāt*, gardens, and two plants are named: the date palm and the vine. Both were the staples of the Ḥijāz, and both were what a garden in that landscape was measured by. A plot with palms and vines was a productive holding; a plot without them was land.

The Book names the same pair repeatedly when it wants to describe cultivated bounty. *"And We have placed in it gardens of palm trees and grapevines, and caused springs to gush forth in it,"* (36:34). *"And on the earth there are ˹different˺ neighbouring tracts, gardens of grapevines, ˹various˺ crops, palm trees—some stemming from the same root, others standing alone. They are all irrigated with the same water, yet We make some taste better than others."* (13:4). The pairing is not botanical taxonomy. It is shorthand for a settled, irrigated, productive life — the thing a nomadic society aspired to and a mercantile one traded in.

**The Tree the Prophet Compared to the Believer**

The palm carries a further weight in the tradition. Ibn ʿUmar reports that the Prophet once said there is a tree among trees whose leaves do not fall and which is like a Muslim, and asked the company to name it. People began guessing at the trees of the desert; the answer occurred to Ibn ʿUmar, but he was the youngest present and held his tongue while Abū Bakr and ʿUmar stayed silent. When nobody answered, the Prophet said: it is the date palm. The report is in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, 61, and in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, whose wording supplies the detail about the two elders.

The comparison rests on the palm's characteristics: it fruits in every season, its leaves do not shed, and no part of it is without a use — fruit, fibre, wood, shade, fodder from the kernel. A tree that gives in every season and wastes nothing is what the Prophet chose as the image of a believer, and the sūrah's decision to name the palm first in its list of what the measured rain produces belongs to the same economy of thought. The tree is listed first because it does the most.

**Fruits Beyond the Two Named**

Between the two trees and the closing clause the verse inserts a general statement: in them there are many fruits. The garden is not restricted to what was named, and the two plants are given as the frame rather than the inventory.

That structure is deliberate. A list of every crop would be a catalogue and would date itself to one region and one climate. Naming the two that mattered to the hearers, then opening the category, does two things at once: it makes the description concrete enough to be recognised, and it prevents the reader from treating the blessing as local. The verse is describing the productive earth as such, using the two plants by which its first audience recognised production. The same method appears in the Bee, where the list is longer and the closing clause is the point: *"With it He produces for you ˹various˺ crops, olives, palm trees, grapevines, and every type of fruit. Surely in this is a sign for those who reflect."* (16:11).

**A Garden That Ends in Eating**

The verse closes on a purpose clause: and from which you may eat. The garden is not described as scenery, as a sign of wealth, or as a place of rest. It is described as food, and the final word of the verse is the act of consumption.

That closing is what separates this description from the Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden of the Hereafter. Here the eating is a necessity repeated daily; there it is a pleasure with no need behind it. By ending the verse on the ordinary act, the sūrah keeps the blessing inside the reader's actual day. He is not being shown a paradise he cannot reach. He is being shown the mechanism by which his next meal exists — measured rain, soaked ground, a palm, a vine, a hand reaching for the fruit — and being asked to notice that no part of the mechanism is his.

**The Blessing That Is Also a Test**

The same two trees appear in a parable about loss. *"Would any of you wish to have a garden with palm trees, grapevines, and all kinds of fruits with rivers flowing underneath and as they grow very old with dependent children, a fiery whirlwind hits the garden, burning it all up?"* (2:266). The parable is about charity ruined by ostentation, and it chooses this garden as the image of everything a person has built up.

The choice is not accidental. The palm takes years to fruit and the vine needs constant tending; a garden of these two is the accumulated work of a lifetime. Its destruction in the parable is total, and the timing is the cruellest part — at the moment when the owner is old and has children depending on him. The sūrah that lists the garden among the blessings has a counterpart in the sūrah that uses it to show how fast a blessing can be withdrawn, and both descriptions depend on the same two trees.

**The Same Two Trees in the Reward**

The reward repeats the bounty in compressed form. Of the successful: *"Gardens, vineyards,"* (78:32), and of what the earth will bring forth for them, *"as well as grapes and greens,"* (80:28). The vocabulary of provision here and of reward there is the same vocabulary.

That continuity makes the verse's blessing harder to dismiss. The believer is not asked to despise the garden in order to want the Garden. He is asked to notice that the fruit in his hand comes from the same arrangement — measured water, receptive ground, a Creator who is not unmindful — and that the arrangement has a second, permanent version.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:20

> as well as ˹olive˺ trees which grow at Mount Sinai, providing oil and a condiment to eat

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Tree Named by Its Mountain**

The list moves from a category of gardens to a single tree with a location attached. The olive is identified by the place it grows — Mount Sinai — and the identification is the verse's most striking feature. Nothing else in this passage is tied to a geography.

The reason for the tie is that the mountain carries memory. It is where Mūsā received the law, and the Qur'an swears by it: *"and Mount Sinai,"* (95:2). A tree described as growing there is not being given a botanical habitat so much as a lineage. The sūrah has been listing provisions — measured rain, stored water, palms, vines — and here it lists one that arrives with a covenant attached to it. The olive of Sinai is the same tree whose oil lit the lamp in the parable of light, and the hearers who knew the story of Mūsā would have heard the name and thought of the law before they thought of the fruit.

**Saynāʾ: A Place Name or an Adjective**

The exegetes disagree about the word itself. Some held that *saynāʾ* is not the proper name of the mountain but an adjective meaning blessed or beautiful, so that the verse would describe a tree growing on a blessed mountain rather than naming Sinai.

Al-Ṭabarī rejected that reading on a linguistic ground: there is no such adjective in Arabic. His objection is the stronger of the two, and it is decisive on the point it addresses — if the word cannot function as an Arabic adjective, the adjectival reading has nothing to stand on. The defenders of the reading are not inventing; the mountain is called blessed elsewhere and the sense fits. But a meaning that the language cannot carry is not a meaning, and the plain reading remains: the tree grows at the mountain of Mūsā, and the name is the name of a place the audience knew.

**Oil and a Relish**

Two products are named. *Duhn* is the oil — the pressed oil of the olive, used for lighting, for anointing, and for cooking. *Ṣibgh* is the condiment, and the word is related by root to dyeing; in usage it means something a morsel is dipped into, a liquid relish eaten with bread. Olive oil and vinegar were both *ṣibgh* in the ordinary speech of the time.

The pairing is precise about how the tree is used. Oil is a raw material with many applications, and the verse names it without qualification. The condiment is the finished use — the thing on the table. Between them they cover the whole range of what the olive provides, from lamp fuel to a meal, and the verse's economy is typical of this passage: it does not praise the tree, it lists what it gives.

**The Olive Elsewhere in the Book**

The tree appears in one of the most quoted verses in the Qur'an, in a parable about divine light. The lamp in the niche is *"lit from ˹the oil of˺ a blessed olive tree, ˹located˺ neither to the east nor the west, whose oil would almost glow, even without being touched by fire."* (24:35). The olive there is called blessed, and the oil is described as so pure that it seems to shine on its own.

It appears too in the list of what the measured rain produces: *"With it He produces for you ˹various˺ crops, olives, palm trees, grapevines, and every type of fruit."* (16:11), where it is named before the palm. The two uses are different in kind — one is provision, the other is an image — and together they explain why this sūrah gives the olive a verse of its own. It is the only plant in the Book that serves both as food and as the standard illustration of light.

**Why One Tree Gets a Location**

The structure of verses nineteen and twenty is worth reading closely. The first describes gardens in general, with palms and vines named as the two principal plants and other fruits left open. The second describes one tree, and attaches a place name to it. The movement is from the general to the specific, and the specificity is not there for botanical completeness.

A place name in a list of provisions does something a plant name cannot: it locates the blessing in history. The garden is anyone's garden; the olive of Sinai belongs to a story the audience already knew, involving a prophet, a mountain, and a law given on it. The sūrah is about to spend a long section narrating the messengers — Nūḥ first, then Mūsā — and it places this tree here, at the end of the passage on provision, as the hinge between the two. The blessings of the earth are listed, and the last of them points toward the mountain where the commandments came.

**A Blessed Tree Neither East Nor West**

The parable in an-Nūr adds a detail that has been read for centuries as the key to the image: the tree is *"neither to the east nor the west"* (24:35). Commentators have taken this to mean a tree on open ground, unshaded by anything, receiving the sun from its rising to its setting, so that its oil is at its purest.

Whatever the physical sense, the detail belongs to the same thought as this verse. The olive of Sinai is a tree of a specific place, and the olive of the parable is a tree of no particular direction — open to all of the sky. Both descriptions end in oil, and both use the oil to make a point about provision: the first that it feeds a person, the second that it lights him. The sūrah that lists the tree among the blessings has already told its audience what the blessing is for.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:21

> And there is certainly a lesson for you in cattle, from whose bellies We give you ˹milk˺ to drink, and in them are many other benefits for you, and from them you may eat

**Expanded Commentary**

**Anʿām: The Eight Animals Named Elsewhere**

The word *anʿām* covers the grazing livestock of Arabia — camels, cattle, sheep and goats — and the Book identifies them precisely when it wants to. An-Niʿām sets out the pairs: *"˹Allah has created˺ four pairs: a pair of sheep and a pair of goats"* (6:143), and then *"a pair of camels and a pair of oxen"* (6:144). Eight animals, four kinds, male and female of each.

That specificity matters for reading this verse. *Anʿām* is not a poetic word for animals in general; it names the herd, the property a household's survival rested on. The verse is therefore not making a general observation about nature. It is addressing the largest single asset its hearers owned, and telling them that what comes out of it did not come from them. The lesson is placed where the wealth is.

**A Drink Taken From Between Two Other Things**

The sūrah of the Bee describes the same provision with a physiological detail: *"And there is certainly a lesson for you in cattle: We give you to drink of what is in their bellies, from between digested food and blood: pure milk, pleasant to drink."* (16:66). Milk appears between two things that are not drinkable, and comes out as neither.

That is the observation the verse is drawing attention to. An animal eats dry fodder and produces blood; from between those two a third substance emerges that is neither, and it is fit to drink. The verse does not explain the mechanism — it has no interest in that — but it insists on the strangeness of the result. Anyone who keeps animals has watched this happen daily and stopped seeing it. The verse restores the sight.

**The Supplication Taught for Milk**

Milk is the one food for which the Prophet gave a distinct supplication. Ibn ʿAbbās reports him saying: whoever is given food by God should ask Him to bless it and to feed him better; and whoever is given a drink of milk should ask Him to bless it and give him more of it — adding that nothing takes the place of both food and drink except milk. The report is in Sunan al-Tirmidhī, 3455.

The asymmetry is the informative part. For food the request is for something better; for milk it is simply for more. The reasoning given is that milk already does the work of both sustenance and drink, so there is nothing better to ask for in its place. A seventh-century instruction about a desert staple has turned out to be a fair description of its nutritional profile, and the sūrah's decision to single out the drink among the cattle's benefits is the same judgement made in a different register.

**"Many Other Benefits" Left Unitemised**

The middle clause is deliberately open: in them are many benefits. Nothing is listed. The hearers knew what the list contained — wool, hair and leather; dung for fuel; horns and bone; the animal's labour in the field — and the verse leaves them to supply it.

The openness has a rhetorical function. A list would have been an inventory, and an inventory invites the reply that some items matter and others do not. An open clause invites the hearer to run through his own household and count. The same method appears in the sūrah of the Forgiver: *"It is Allah Who made cattle for you so that you may ride some and eat others. Also, you find in them ˹other˺ benefits."* (40:79-80). The unspecified remainder is where the gratitude is supposed to be generated, because it is the part the hearer has to notice for himself.

**Drink, Then Benefits, Then Meat**

The order of the three clauses is not casual. Milk comes first, then the unspecified benefits, and meat last. In a pastoral economy that is the order of value: milk is daily, the by-products are continuous, and slaughter is occasional, since an animal killed for meat stops producing both of the others.

The verse therefore lists the provisions in the order a herder would rank them, and it puts the least costly at the top. Nothing has to die for the first benefit. The structure quietly makes a point about how the provision is arranged: the most reliable part of what the animal gives is the part that costs it nothing, and the part that costs it its life comes last. A reader who has eaten meat without thinking about the sequence has read the verse in the wrong order.

The same ordering appears in the Forgiver's account, which puts riding and eating first and the unspecified benefits after (40:79). The two verses do not contradict each other; they select different items from the same animal for different purposes. What al-Muʾminūn selects is the provision that recurs every day, and it places that at the head of the list.

**Warmth, Food and a Sight at Evening**

The sūrah of the Bee opens its account of the same animals with the two benefits a desert household would have named first: *"And He created the cattle for you as a source of warmth, food, and ˹many other˺ benefits."* (16:5), and then adds a benefit with no use at all — *"They are also pleasing to you when you bring them home and when you take them out to graze."* (16:6).

That last clause is worth keeping beside this verse. The provision is not only useful; it is agreeable to look at, and the Book counts the pleasure of it as part of the gift. The lesson this verse points to is not simply that the cattle are useful but that they are useful, productive and beautiful, and none of the three was arranged by their owner.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:22

> And you are carried upon ˹some of˺ them and upon ships

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Carriers Named in a Single Clause**

The verse joins two things that have nothing in common except their function. An animal and a ship; a living creature that feeds itself and a built object that does not move without wind or muscle. Both are described with the same passive verb: you are carried.

The pairing does more than catalogue transport. It puts a domesticated beast and a piece of carpentry in one category, which means the category is not "animals" or "technology" but something else — the set of things that hold a human being above the ground he would otherwise have to cover on foot. The sūrah has just listed what the cattle give in food and material; this verse adds movement, and adds the ship to the same sentence, so that the reader cannot treat either as self-evident.

The passive construction is doing the work. The verse does not say that you ride them or that you sail ships; it says you are carried upon them and upon ships. The human being is the cargo in both cases. What differs is only the size of the vehicle and the medium it moves through, and neither of those changes who is doing the carrying.

**The Ship as a Standing Sign**

The Qur'an returns to ships more often than to almost any other human artefact, and always in the same role. The long list of signs in the Cow includes *"the ships that sail the sea for the benefit of humanity"* (2:164) beside the alternation of night and day and the reviving rain. Luqmān puts it as a question: *"Do you not see that the ships sail ˹smoothly˺ through the sea by the grace of Allah so that He may show you some of His signs? Surely in this are signs for whoever is steadfast, grateful."* (31:31).

The reason for the recurrence is that a ship is a visible demonstration of dependency. A building sits on the ground; a ship is held up by something that could stop holding it at any moment, and everyone aboard knows this. The sūrah of ar-Rūm adds the commercial motive — *"and that ships may sail by His command, and that you may seek His bounty"* (30:46) — so the sign is not only physical but economic: the trade the hearers lived by ran on a medium they did not control.

**The Prayer That Only Works in a Storm**

Yūnus describes the moment when the sign stops being an observation and becomes a fact: *"And it so happens that you are on ships, sailing with a favourable wind, to the passengers' delight. Suddenly, the ships are overcome by a gale wind and those on board are overwhelmed by waves from every side, and they assume they are doomed. They cry out to Allah ˹alone˺ in sincere devotion, 'If You save us from this, we will certainly be grateful.'"* (10:22).

That verse explains why this one exists. The blessing of being carried is easy to forget while the carrying continues; the storm restores the knowledge for as long as it lasts. The sūrah's argument is not that people are ignorant of God but that they are careless of Him in proportion to their comfort, and the ship is the illustration because it is the one provision that makes the dependency impossible to ignore when the weather turns.

**A Moment Set Aside for Remembrance**

Az-Zukhruf turns the same scene into an instruction: *"so that you may sit firmly on their backs, and remember your Lord's blessings once you are settled on them, saying, 'Glory be to the One Who has subjected these for us, for we could have never done so ˹on our own˺.'"* (43:13). The verse names the exact moment for the remembrance — once settled, when the danger of mounting is past and the journey has begun.

The formula it gives is short and precise: these have been subjected to us, and we could not have done it. That is the whole content of the blessing stated in this verse. Being carried is not an achievement; it is a subjection arranged by someone else, and the words are designed to be said at the point where a traveller is most likely to feel self-sufficient.

**Why Movement Comes Last in the Provision**

The sūrah's sequence has been running through provision: measured rain, gardens, palms, vines, olives, then the cattle and their milk. Movement is the final item, and its position is the point. Food keeps a person alive; movement is what he does with the life.

Placing the ship beside the animal at the end of the list also extends the argument's reach. Everything before this verse was produced from the earth — water, plants, livestock. The ship is made by human hands out of cut timber, and it is included in the same catalogue of what God provides. The verse is saying that the artefact belongs to the provision as much as the animal does, because the wood, the sea, the wind and the knowledge of how to shape a hull that floats are none of them invented by the shipwright.

**Ships Like Mountains**

Ar-Raḥmān gives the image its fullest form: *"To Him belong the ships with raised sails, sailing through the seas like mountains."* (55:24). The comparison is of scale — a vessel that looks like a piece of landscape moving across water — and the verse opens with the possessive: they belong to Him.

That is the theological content of this verse in a single phrase. The ships are not neutral instruments that happen to be useful; they are His, and their sailing is under His command. The traveller sitting on the deck of something the size of a mountain is being carried, and the sūrah's word for it — *tuḥmalūn*, you are carried — puts him in the passive, where he belongs.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:23

> Indeed, We sent Noah to his people. He declared, “O my people! Worship Allah ˹alone˺. You have no god other than Him. Will you not then fear ˹Him˺?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Every Messenger Repeats**

The sūrah turns from provision to history with the first of its prophetic narratives, and the first words it puts in the messenger's mouth are words the Book uses again and again. *"Indeed, We sent Noah to his people. He said, 'O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him.'"* (7:59). Hūd says it to ʿĀd: *"O my people! Worship Allah. You have no god other than Him."* (11:50). Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb say it in the same sūrah, in the same words, to their own peoples.

The repetition is not a formula filling space. It records what every messenger was actually sent to say, and the identity of the wording across different peoples and centuries is the argument: the message did not change with the audience. The sūrah of al-Aʿrāf sets the four messengers out in sequence with this refrain, and this sūrah echoes that arrangement — a point its own later verse makes explicit when it says the messengers were sent in succession.

**Nine Hundred and Fifty Years of It**

The length of Nūḥ's mission is stated elsewhere with unusual precision: *"Indeed, We sent Noah to his people, and he remained among them for a thousand years, less fifty. Then the Flood overtook them, while they persisted in wrongdoing."* (29:14). Nine hundred and fifty years, and the verse closes on the fact that they persisted.

That number reframes the address in this verse. A man who spent nine and a half centuries saying the same sentence to the same community, and who watched generation after generation refuse it, is not a figure of rhetorical patience. The sūrah is describing the longest sustained rejection recorded in the Book, and it opens the story with the sentence that was said throughout. The reader meets the words before he learns how long they were said, and the length makes them heavier.

**Three Moves in the Address**

The speech has three parts and no more. An address — *my people*. A command — worship God. A question — will you not then fear Him. There is no argument in it, no evidence offered, no threat detailed, no appeal to consequence.

The economy is worth noticing. A messenger opening his mission to a community that has never heard him might be expected to establish his credentials first. Nūḥ does none of that. He names the relationship between himself and his hearers, states the single demand, and asks whether they will respond to it. Everything else in the story — the chiefs' objections, the accusation of madness, the ark, the flood — follows from those three moves, and none of it changes them.

The opening words, *yā qawm*, are the smallest of the three and the easiest to miss. He does not say "you people" or "O community of such-and-such"; he claims membership in the group he is addressing. The messenger who is about to be told that he wants to rule over them begins by identifying himself as one of them, and the charge in the next verse is a direct inversion of that opening.

**One Demand, Stated Twice**

"Worship Allah" is followed immediately by its explanation: "You have no god other than Him." The second sentence is not additional information. It is the first sentence unpacked, and the unpacking is negative.

The reason the negation is needed is that the hearers were not atheists. They acknowledged a supreme deity and associated others with Him, which is the situation the Qur'an addresses throughout its Meccan material. A command to worship God would have been accepted by them without changing anything in their practice. What made the demand intolerable was the exclusion: no other. The verse therefore contains the whole content of the mission in its second clause, and the rest of the story is the record of a community refusing that one word.

**The Question at the End Is an Invitation**

*Afalā tattaqūn* — will you not then be reverent? The verse ends on an interrogative, and the form of it is not a challenge but a prompt. The particle *fa-* makes the fear a consequence: given what has just been said, will you not respond?

Ending the address with a question rather than a warning does specific work. A threat closes a conversation; a question leaves it open, and it leaves the hearer holding the next move. The sūrah is showing what a messenger's opening actually sounds like — not a denunciation but an appeal that expects an answer. Everything that follows in this narrative is the community's reply, and the reply is given in the next two verses at some length, by people who were not asked to speak.

The word for fear here is *taqwā*, the same family of words the Book uses for the reverence that keeps a person away from what harms him. Nūḥ is not asking for terror. He is asking whether they will act like people who expect to be answerable, and the question is put to a community that has just been told there is no god but one.

**The Same Sentence From a Different Mouth**

Hūd's version of the refrain carries one addition that shows how stable the core is: *"O my people! Worship Allah. You have no god other than Him. You do nothing but fabricate lies ˹against Allah˺."* (11:50). The first two sentences are identical to Nūḥ's; the third is a charge against his particular people's practice.

The pattern is consistent across the Book: the unchanging core of the message, then the specific error of the audience addressed. What every messenger says is the same. What each adds is aimed at the local idolatry. This verse gives the core with nothing added, and it is the first thing the sūrah puts on record after its account of the world's provision.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:24

> But the disbelieving chiefs of his people said ˹to the masses˺, “This is only a human like you, who wants to be superior to you. Had Allah willed, He could have easily sent down angels ˹instead˺. We have never heard of this in ˹the history of˺ our forefathers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chiefs Answer Before the People Do**

The reply does not come from Nūḥ's people. It comes from *al-malaʾ* — the notables, the assembly of leading men — and the verse specifies that they are speaking to the masses. The crowd has not yet said anything; its position is being formed for it by the people whose standing the message disturbs.

The word *malaʾ* is used throughout the Book for exactly this group: the chiefs of Pharaoh's court, the council of the Queen of Sheba who are summoned with the words *"O chiefs! Indeed, a noble letter has been delivered to me."* (27:29), the notables of Quraysh who say *"Carry on, and stand firm in devotion to your gods."* (38:6), the king's advisers in the story of Yūsuf addressed as *"O chiefs! Tell me the meaning of my dream"* (12:43). It is a term of office rather than of character, and the sūrah puts the rejection in their mouths first because that is where the rejection originates.

**"A Human Like You"**

The first objection is about the messenger's species. Hūd's people put it the same way: *"We see you only as a human being like ourselves."* (11:27). The messengers as a group are answered with it: *"You are no more than humans like us!"* (14:10). And of the Prophet himself: *"What kind of messenger is this who eats food and goes about in market-places ˹for a living˺?"* (25:7).

The objection sounds like a complaint about evidence and is not. Nobody argues that a human being is incapable of telling the truth. The claim is that a man who eats, trades and walks in the market cannot occupy the position of speaking for God — an argument about status rather than about content. It is also an argument the objectors cannot win, since every messenger the Book records was human, and the sūrah's audience knew the histories.

**The Real Fear: Superiority**

The verse states the motive openly: he wants to be superior to you. The chiefs are not confused about the message. They have understood it correctly and identified what it does to them.

A call to worship one God transfers ultimate authority from the people who currently hold it. In a tribal society, religious standing was political standing: the guardians of the shrine, the arbiters of custom, the men whose opinion settled disputes. A messenger who says there is no god but God removes the ground from all of that, and the chiefs' response is not a theological position but an accurate reading of their own situation. The verse records them saying the quiet part aloud, and it is the only place in the objection where they are being honest.

**"Had Allah Willed, He Could Have Sent Angels"**

The second objection is theological in form. If God had wanted to send a messenger, He would have sent an angel; He did not, so He has not. The demand appears repeatedly in the Book: *"If only an angel had been sent down with him to be his co-warner,"* (25:7), and the same request put to the Prophet in 6:8 and elsewhere.

The argument fails on its own terms, and the Book says why. An angel would not be a messenger to human beings in any sense they could act on; a being of a different order, unable to be imitated, would establish nothing about how a person should live. The sūrah of al-Anʿām answers the request directly: if an angel were sent and the matter decided, they would be given no respite (6:8). The chiefs are proposing a test whose result they have not thought about, and the objection is a way of refusing the message while appearing to defer to God's power.

**"We Never Heard of This From Our Forefathers"**

The third objection is the shortest and the most revealing. Nothing is claimed about the truth of the message; the claim is that it is unprecedented. Custom is offered as evidence.

This is the argument of every settled religious establishment, and the Qur'an treats it as the weakest of the three. An-Nisāʾ's account of the same scene has the chiefs adding that no one follows the messenger except the lowliest and most thoughtless (11:27) — a sociological observation offered as a refutation. Precedent is not a criterion of truth; it is a description of what happened to be believed. The sūrah reports the objection without answering it because it does not require an answer, and its placement last suggests the order in which the objections actually arise in a mind: first the status of the speaker, then the shape of the claim, then the comfort of never having heard it before.

**The Demand for an Angel, Answered**

The Book answers the angel objection in a way that turns it into an argument for the messenger's humanity. The messengers themselves say to their peoples: *"Is there any doubt about Allah, the Originator of the heavens and the earth? He is inviting you in order to forgive your sins, and delay your end until your appointed term."* (14:10) — and then report the reply: *"You are no more than humans like us!"* The messengers do not deny being human. They treat it as beside the point.

The sūrah of al-Furqān records the same exchange and answers it by describing what the Prophet is: a warner who eats and trades, sent to a people who need a warner they can follow. The objection in this verse is the standard one, and the standard answer is that a messenger from among you is the only kind that could ever reach you.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:25

> He is simply insane, so bear with him for a while.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Word That Means Both Possessed and Mad**

The Arabic is *bihi jinnah* — in him is a *jinnah*, from the same root as *jinn* and related to *majnūn*, the common word for mad. Both terms carry two senses at once: possession by an unseen being, and insanity with no supernatural explanation. Classical usage does not force a choice between them, and the Qur'an uses the family of words for both.

The ambiguity is useful to the speakers. An accusation of possession places the man outside the community's moral order — he is not wrong, he is inhabited, and nothing he says needs to be answered on its merits. An accusation of insanity does the same work by a different route. The chiefs need only one of the two senses, and the word supplies both. That is presumably why the charge is so common in the accounts of the messengers: it disposes of an argument without engaging it.

**The Charge Put to Every Messenger**

Sabaʾ reports the same accusation in a disjunction that exposes its function: *"Has he fabricated a lie against Allah or is he insane?"* (34:8). The two options are alternatives offered by people who have not decided which they believe, and the verse comments on the speakers rather than on the charge: those who do not believe in the Hereafter are bound for torment and have strayed furthest from the truth.

Ad-Dukhān records the version directed at Mūsā: *"then they turned away from him, saying, 'A madman, taught by others!'"* (44:14) — madness and instruction by someone else, two charges that do not sit well together. At-Ṭūr dismisses both against the Prophet: *"For you, by the grace of your Lord, are not a fortune-teller or a madman."* (52:29). The consistency across these passages is the point. Every messenger is met with the same word, and the word is never supported by anything.

**"Bear With Him for a While" Is a Strategy**

The second half of the verse is advice, and it is the most revealing part of the chiefs' speech. They do not propose refuting Nūḥ, imprisoning him, or expelling him. They propose waiting.

The policy assumes that the movement will die with the man. Give him time, let him talk himself out, and the community will return to what it was. It is the response of people who believe they are on the side of the settled order and that the disturbance is temporary. The verse records the strategy without commenting on it, and the story then supplies the commentary: what the waiting produced was the flood. The sūrah does not need to say that the plan failed. It shows the plan and then shows what came after it.

**The Diagnosis That Requires No Argument**

Notice what the verse does not contain. No point of Nūḥ's message is addressed. His claim that there is no god but God is not examined, and his call to fear God is not answered. The man is categorised and the discussion is closed.

This is the mechanism the verse exists to expose. Once a speaker is placed in a category — mad, possessed, self-interested, foreign — his statements no longer have to be read. The accusation is not a rival argument; it is a device for not having one. The sūrah reports the charge in the chiefs' own words precisely so that a reader can see how little work it does, and the Qur'an's habit of recording the accusations against its messengers verbatim is part of how it answers them.

There is also a social mechanism at work. The chiefs are addressing the masses, and the charge is calibrated for that audience. A crowd cannot evaluate a theological claim about divine unity, but it can accept that a man is disturbed. The label transfers the decision from the many who cannot judge the argument to the few who have already decided it, and it does so in a single sentence.

**A Verdict Reached Before Hearing the Case**

The two verses together give the full shape of the rejection. Verse twenty-four offers three objections; verse twenty-five abandons all three and offers a diagnosis instead. The movement from argument to label is worth attending to, because it is the ordinary course of a refusal.

When the objections are answerable, they are eventually answered. When they are not, what remains is a judgement about the person. The chiefs began with the claim that Nūḥ wanted superiority, moved to the claim that God would have sent an angel, and ended by saying he was mad. Each step is weaker than the one before, and the last one requires nothing from the speaker except a word. The verse is a short sentence, and it is the point at which the community stopped listening.

The two verses are also unequal in length, and the disproportion is the commentary. Six lines of reasoning in the first, four words in the second. What the sūrah shows is not that the chiefs had nothing to say but that what they had did not survive examination, and what replaced it was shorter than the message it was meant to dispose of.

**The Accusation Reversed**

Sabaʾ turns the charge back on those who make it. After recording the two options — fabricated lie, or insanity — the verse continues: *"In fact, those who do not believe in the Hereafter are bound for torment and have strayed farthest ˹from the truth˺."* (34:8).

The reversal is not a counter-insult. It identifies the actual defect in the reasoning: a person who has closed the question of what comes after this life cannot evaluate a messenger whose whole message is about it. The chiefs are not mad. They are, in the sūrah's assessment, further from the truth than the man they have just diagnosed, and the distance is measured by the very thing they refuse to consider.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:26

> Noah prayed, “My Lord! Help me, because they have denied ˹me˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address Turns From People to God**

For three verses Nūḥ has been speaking to his community, and they have been speaking about him to each other. This verse changes the direction of the speech. He stops addressing the people and addresses his Lord.

The turn marks the end of an attempt. Nothing in the verse says the mission has been declared a failure or that persuasion has been formally abandoned; it records the point at which a man who has said everything he was sent to say has nothing left to say to those who will not hear. The structure of the narrative is exact: the messenger's address, the chiefs' reply, and then the messenger's prayer. What happens next is no longer a conversation.

**The Shortest Prayer in the Story**

The prayer is five words in Arabic. There is no praise, no confession, no list of grievances, no request for a specific outcome. My Lord, help me, for they deny me.

The brevity is not stylistic. A longer prayer would have been about Nūḥ — his suffering, his endurance, his righteousness. This one is about a fact: the denial. He states the situation and asks for help with it, and leaves the rest to the one he is asking. The sūrah of al-Qamar records a version of the same cry that is even more stripped: *"So he cried out to his Lord, 'I am helpless, so help ˹me˺!'"* (54:10). Between the two accounts the reader has the prayer in its plainest form — a statement of inability and a request.

**Help, Not Vengeance**

The word used is a request for aid, not for punishment. Nūḥ does not ask that his people be destroyed, and he does not ask for vindication in front of them. He asks to be helped.

That distinction matters, because the flood follows. The verse does not record a messenger calling down destruction on his community; it records a man asking for support against people who have rejected him, and the destruction that comes afterwards is decided elsewhere. The sūrah of al-Qamar makes the sequence explicit: the cry of helplessness, then the opening of the gates of the sky (54:10-11). The prayer and the punishment are adjacent in the narrative but they are not the same act, and the messenger's request is the smaller of the two.

**"They Have Denied Me": The Object Is the Messenger**

The ground given for the prayer is *fa-innahum kadhdhabūn* — they have denied me. Not they have denied You, though that is also true; the object of the denial is the man.

That is the correct description of what has happened. The chiefs never engaged the content of the message; they engaged the messenger's species, his motives and his sanity. The denial was directed at him from the start, and his prayer names it accurately. It also does something else: by making the denial the reason for his request, he places the matter in the hands of the one who sent him. A messenger whose rejection is treated as a personal failure has nothing to appeal to. A messenger whose rejection is a fact about his mission has somewhere to take it.

**What a Messenger Does When Persuasion Ends**

The verse records a transition that the rest of the Book treats as normal rather than exceptional. Messengers argue, warn, invite and answer objections; at some point they stop, and what they do then is pray. Yūnus preserves the same moment in Nūḥ's own words, addressed to the people who are about to kill him: *"If my presence and my reminders to you of Allah's signs are unbearable to you, then ˹know that˺ I have put my trust in Allah. So devise a plot along with your associate-gods—and you do not have to be secretive about your plot—then carry it out against me without delay!"* (10:71). He adds that he has never asked them for payment, and that his reward is only from God (10:72).

The point of the transition is that the messenger's task was never to produce belief. He was sent to deliver a message clearly, and the outcome is not his to manufacture. Nūḥ's prayer is not an admission of defeat; it is the recognition that the remaining part of the matter belongs to someone else. The sūrah will show what that someone else does about it, beginning with the next verse, and none of it is Nūḥ's doing.

There is a further element in the Yūnus account worth setting beside this verse. Nūḥ states that he asked for nothing in return, which removes the chiefs' accusation of self-advancement at its root. The man they said wanted to be superior to them had spent centuries working for free, and the sūrah records the charge and the answer in different places without ever connecting them explicitly.

**"I Am Helpless, So Help Me"**

The parallel in al-Qamar gives the prayer its fullest form and its context: *"So he cried out to his Lord, 'I am helpless, so help ˹me˺!' So We opened the gates of the sky with pouring rain, and caused the earth to burst with springs, so the waters met for a fate already set."* (54:10-12). The words *mughtaẓ*, overcome, describe a man at the end of his resources.

Read beside this verse, the two accounts show the same shape: the messenger reduced to a single sentence, and the response arriving as an event rather than an argument. The sūrah of al-Qamar adds the closing comment — *"We certainly left this as a sign. So is there anyone who will be mindful?"* (54:15) — which is the purpose of the whole narrative, and the reason this short prayer is preserved in the text at all.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:27

> So We inspired him: “Build the Ark under Our ˹watchful˺ Eyes and directions. Then when Our command comes and the oven bursts ˹with water˺, take on board a pair from every species along with your family—except those against whom the decree ˹to drown˺ has already been passed. And do not plead with Me for those who have done wrong, for they will surely be drowned.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Built Under Watchful Eyes**

The answer to the prayer is an instruction, and the instruction begins with a phrase that appears again in the account: *bi-aʿyuninā*, under Our eyes. Hūd gives the same formula — *"And build the Ark under Our ˹watchful˺ Eyes and directions"* (11:37) — and al-Qamar describes the finished vessel as *"sailing under Our ˹watchful˺ Eyes"* (54:14).

The phrase does two things. It tells Nūḥ that he is not working alone, which matters for a man about to construct something enormous in front of a community that has just called him mad. And it tells the reader that the construction was supervised — the dimensions, the materials and the method were given, not invented. The words "and directions" make that explicit: the building is a revealed act, not an engineering project.

**The Oven: Three Readings Weighed**

The sign of the flood is the strangest detail in the narrative. *Fāra at-tannūr* — the *tannūr* gushed or boiled over. The word ordinarily means an oven, and the exegetes divide over what it refers to here. Some read it as a literal oven, and take the gushing of water from it as the signal that the flood has begun. Others take *tannūr* to mean the highest and best place on the earth, so that the sign is water appearing where water never appears. A third reading takes it as the break of dawn.

The literal reading is the strongest, for a reason internal to the narrative. A sign is only a sign if the person waiting for it can recognise it, and a named object in a named place is recognisable in a way that "the dawn" is not — every dawn looks the same. Hūd records the same event with the same word (11:40), and neither account explains it, which suggests the hearers knew what was meant.
