Water coming out of a bread oven is exactly the kind of impossible event that would function as a signal to the man who had been told to watch for it.

**Two of Every Kind**

The instruction is to take aboard a pair — *ithnayn*, two — from every kind. The number is specified and the category is broad, and the verse does not explain how the collection was made or where the animals came from.

The silence is characteristic of Qur'anic narrative, which omits the mechanics and keeps the commands. What the reader is given is the shape of the instruction: the preservation of kinds, in pairs, so that what survives can reproduce. The sūrah of Hūd adds the community of believers to the cargo and notes how few there were: *"But none believed with him except for a few."* (11:40). Between the animals and the handful of people, the ark carries everything that will continue.

The pairing also answers an objection the verse does not raise. A single surviving animal cannot found anything; a pair can. The instruction is framed in the terms of what comes after the water, which means the order to build was given by someone who had already decided what the world would look like when it drained.

**The Exception Inside the Rescue**

The family is included, and then subtracted: except those against whom the decree has already been passed. The verse does not name them. Hūd does, later, when Nūḥ calls to a son standing apart from the others and the son refuses to board (11:42).

The exception is placed before the reader knows who it refers to, which is a deliberate arrangement. The reader learns first that kinship will not save anyone, and only afterwards discovers whose kinship was at issue. When Nūḥ then asks about his son, the answer is a correction rather than a comfort: *"O Noah! He is certainly not of your family—he was entirely of unrighteous conduct. So do not ask Me about what you have no knowledge of!"* (11:46). The category that matters is not blood.

**The Prohibition on Intercession**

The last clause forbids a prayer: do not plead with Me for those who have done wrong. The instruction is given before Nūḥ makes the plea, and it is repeated in Hūd at the same point (11:37).

The prohibition has a limit built into it that the Book states elsewhere. After it became clear that the polytheists were bound for the Fire, the Prophet and the believers were told it was not proper to seek forgiveness for them, even close relatives (9:113); and Ibrāhīm's prayer for his father is explained as the keeping of a promise, withdrawn once the enmity was plain (9:114). The principle across these verses is not that compassion is a fault but that intercession has a boundary, and the boundary is drawn by the decision of the person being interceded for. Nūḥ's people had made theirs over nine centuries.

**Planks and Nails**

Al-Qamar describes the vessel in the plainest terms available: *"We carried him on that ˹Ark made˺ of planks and nails,"* (54:13). Two materials, no ornament, no dimensions.

The description belongs beside this verse because it is the answer to the instruction. Nūḥ was told to build under supervision, and what he built was a wooden box fastened with nails — the simplest thing that floats. The sūrah then closes its own account of the event with the reason the story is told at all: *"We certainly left this as a sign. So is there anyone who will be mindful?"* (54:15). The narrative is preserved not for its detail but for the question at the end of it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:28

> Then when you and those with you have settled in the Ark, say, “All praise is for Allah, Who saved us from the wrongdoing people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Words Spoken in Safety**

The verse dictates what Nūḥ is to say at a precise moment: once he and his company are settled aboard. Not when the command came, not when the last animal was loaded, not when the water receded. The words are assigned to the interval between boarding and departure, when the escape is complete in fact and not yet tested by the journey.

That is the moment when a rescued person is most likely to feel relief without direction. The danger behind is known and the danger ahead is not yet visible. The verse fills the interval with a formula, and in doing so it teaches that gratitude has a place in the sequence of events rather than being a mood that arrives when it likes.

The timing also gathers the community. Nūḥ is told to say it, and those with him are included in the wording of the praise — *who saved us*, plural. The first act of the saved people, before they have travelled anywhere, is a shared statement in the first person plural. They become a community in the sentence, and the sentence is given to them rather than composed by them.

**A Formula Given Before the Event**

The instruction is delivered before the rescue happens. Nūḥ is told what he will say before he has anything to say it about, and the words include a completed act — *Who saved us* — spoken by a man who is still on dry land.

The arrangement is worth attending to. A prayer composed after deliverance is a natural response; a prayer dictated in advance is a discipline. It commits the speaker to a reading of what is about to happen before the outcome is visible, and it fixes that reading in language he did not choose. The Book does this repeatedly with the words of the believers in Paradise and the words of the damned in the Fire; here it does it with the words of a man boarding a boat in front of a hostile town.

**Saved From a People, Not From Water**

The object of the salvation in the formula is not the flood. It is *al-qawm aẓ-ẓālimīn*, the wrongdoing people. Nūḥ is told to praise God for being delivered from a community, and the water is not mentioned.

That is an accurate statement of what the rescue was. The flood was the instrument; the thing being escaped was nine centuries of refusal, mockery and a standing accusation of madness. A man rescued from a burning house praises God for being taken out of the house, not for being taken out of the smoke. The formula names the real burden — the people — and the sūrah's choice to put that word in the mouth of the rescued is a judgement about where the harm lay.

**Praise Placed at the Moment of Settling**

The verb is *istawayta*, you have settled, established yourself, taken your place. It is the word for arriving at rest on something, and the verse makes the praise conditional on it. Not while climbing aboard; once settled.

The choice excludes haste. A person still in motion is not yet in a position to take stock, and the formula asks for stock-taking. It also implies that the settled moment is brief: the ark is about to move, the water is about to rise, and there is a window in which the rescued are together, safe and stationary. The instruction places gratitude in that window, which is where it can actually be performed rather than merely intended.

Gratitude offered afterwards is a memory of the rescue. Gratitude offered inside the window is a response to it, and the response is what the formula asks for.

**What the Formula Assumes**

Saying "Who saved us from the wrongdoing people" commits the speaker to a description of those he has just left. It calls them wrongdoers, in a sentence of praise, and it does so at the moment when the charge is being vindicated rather than argued.

The formula therefore does more than thank. It settles a question that had been open for nine centuries and that no argument had closed. The chiefs said Nūḥ was mad; the formula says they were the wrongdoers. The sūrah does not stage a debate and declare a winner. It puts the verdict in the mouth of the rescued man as the first thing he says, and leaves the reader to notice that the verdict was not reached by discussion.

There is a further function in the timing. Nūḥ has just been forbidden to intercede for the wrongdoers; the next thing he is told to say names them as wrongdoers in an act of praise. The two instructions belong together. The prohibition closes a door, and the formula occupies the space behind it with something the prophet can actually do, so that the obedience is not left as a bare refusal.

**The Rescue Described in al-Aʿrāf**

The sūrah of the Heights records the outcome in a single sentence: *"But they rejected him, so We saved him and those with him in the Ark, and drowned those who rejected Our signs. They were certainly a blind people."* (7:64).

The parallel is instructive in what it omits. There is no description of the flood, no account of the boarding, no detail of the voyage — only the rejection, the rescue, the drowning, and a final word about blindness. The whole narrative is compressed into the fact that some were saved and others were not, and the reason given for the division is the same one this verse puts in Nūḥ's mouth: they were the wrongdoing people.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:29

> And pray, “My Lord! Allow me a blessed landing, for You are the best accommodator.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Prayer, About the Destination**

Two prayers are dictated in these two verses, and they cover different ground. The first thanks God for the escape; this one asks about the arrival. Nūḥ is told to request a landing, and to describe the landing he wants.

The specificity is notable. He is not told to ask for safety, or for provision, or for the destruction of his enemies. He is told to ask for a place to come down, and to ask that the place be blessed. A man who has just been taken out of a century-long confrontation is instructed to pray about geography, and the instruction treats where a person lands as a matter serious enough to be requested from God rather than accepted as it comes.

The two prayers also divide time between them. The first looks back at what has been done; this one looks forward to what has not. Between them there is no request about the present moment, which is the moment the ark is afloat and nothing can be done about it. The liturgy leaves the unmanageable interval unaddressed, and puts speech at the two points where speech has an object.

**The Root of Landing**

The word for landing comes from n-z-l, the root of *nuzūl*, descent, and of *manzil*, a place where one alights or is lodged. The same root gives the word for a guest's provision and for a lodging arranged by a host. The request is therefore not simply "let me come down safely" but "give me a place, and let the place be good".

That is a larger request than it first appears. A landing is where a journey ends and a life resumes: where the survivors will disembark, build, plant and become a people again. The verse asks for that future to be blessed at the moment it is least visible — before the water has risen, while the destination is unknown. The root's sense of hospitality is part of what makes the closing phrase work.

The same root runs through the Book's vocabulary for descent, including the descent of revelation itself: the Qur'an is repeatedly described as sent down, using this family of words. A prayer that asks God for a blessed place of descent is therefore asking in the vocabulary of what God characteristically does. He brings things down from above and settles them where they belong, and the request assumes the pattern rather than interrupting it.

That association gives the prayer a larger scope than a safe landing. The survivors are asking to be placed, and the word they are given to use is the word the Book uses for everything God places — water on the earth, a message in a community, a people in a land. The request is small in its occasion and large in its vocabulary, and the sūrah chooses the vocabulary deliberately.

**Rescue Is Not Complete at the Rescue**

The arrangement of the two verses makes a claim about how deliverance works. Being taken out of danger is the first half; being placed somewhere is the second, and the second requires its own request.

The point is not theoretical. A person removed from a bad situation and set down nowhere has not been delivered, and the sūrah's instruction recognises this by giving the arrival its own prayer. The ark saves Nūḥ from drowning; it does not house him. Between the two lies the question this verse raises, and the answer is given later in the Book when the vessel comes to rest.

**"The Best of Those Who Give a Place"**

The closing phrase names God as the best of those who provide a landing — *khayr al-munzilīn*, the best of those who cause to descend or lodge. The superlative implies that others do it: hosts lodge guests, cities receive travellers, families take in relatives.

The comparison is doing quiet work. Every human lodging is temporary, conditional and limited by the resources of the one who offers it. The verse places the request for a place inside that scale and puts God at the top of it, which is a way of saying that the request is being made to the only party who can guarantee the outcome. A traveller asking a host for a bed asks for something the host may not be able to give. Nūḥ is asking the one who arranged the ark, the water and the land.

**Why Both Prayers Are Dictated**

Neither prayer is spontaneous. Both are given word for word, in advance, to a prophet who was perfectly capable of composing his own. The dictation is the instruction.

What is being taught is the shape of a rescued person's speech: praise for what has been done, then a request for what is still needed, in that order. The formula does not permit the request to come first, and it does not permit the praise to be skipped on the grounds that the danger is not over. The two prayers together form a small liturgy for deliverance, and the Book preserves it in full because it is meant to be used.

**The Landing Named in Hūd**

The answer to this prayer is recorded in the sūrah that tells the same story: *"And it was said, 'O earth! Swallow up your water. And O sky! Withhold ˹your rain˺.' The floodwater receded and the decree was carried out. The Ark rested on Mount Judi, and it was said, 'Away with the wrongdoing people!'"* (11:44).

The verse gives the landing a name. Whatever al-Jūdī is precisely — a mountain in the region of the upper Tigris, in the traditional identification — the text specifies a place, and that specification is the fulfilment of the request made here. The prayer asked for a blessed lodging; the narrative reports where the lodging was, and closes with the same phrase about the wrongdoers that the prayer of praise had used.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:30

> Surely in this are lessons. And We ˹always˺ put ˹people˺ to the test

**Expanded Commentary**

**Signs Placed in a Story**

The narrative stops and comments on itself. Two clauses, and neither of them continues the account: there are signs in this, and We test. The story of Nūḥ is not told for its interest, and the verse says so before the reader has a chance to treat it as a tale.

Al-Qamar makes the same move with more force: *"We certainly left this as a sign. So is there anyone who will be mindful?"* (54:15). The question at the end of that verse is what the comment in this one exists to raise. A sign is not a proof that compels; it is a thing left in place for someone to notice, and the noticing is the response being asked for. The verse therefore addresses the reader directly, in the middle of a story about people long dead, and treats the reader as the one under examination.

**Who Was Tested**

The second clause is ambiguous in a productive way. *Mubtalūn*, being tested, has no stated object in the Arabic, and the exegetes supply it two ways. One reading takes the test as historical: God tested the peoples before, and the statement is there to show the Prophet that other messengers went through what he is going through. The other takes it as general: testing is what God does with human beings, and the audience is included.

Both readings are supported by the verse's position. It closes a narrative about an earlier community, which favours the historical sense, and it is addressed to a sūrah whose first audience is a Meccan community undergoing its own pressure, which favours the general one. The stronger reading holds both together: the test that destroyed Nūḥ's people is the same test now being applied to theirs, and the story is evidence that the test has a result.

**Testing as Part of the Design**

The Book states the principle without reference to any particular people: *"˹He is the One˺ Who created death and life in order to test which of you is best in deeds. And He is the Almighty, All-Forgiving."* (67:2). Testing is not an emergency measure or a response to human failure; it is given as the purpose of the arrangement in which life and death exist at all.

Al-ʿAnkabūt puts the same principle as a rebuke to an expectation: *"Do people think once they say, 'We believe,' that they will be left without being put to the test? We certainly tested those before them. And ˹in this way˺ Allah will clearly distinguish between those who are truthful and those who are liars."* (29:2-3). The second verse of that pair repeats exactly what this verse says — the testing of those before — and supplies the reason: to separate the truthful from the liars. A test with no distinction in its results would not be a test.

**The Test Is Not the Punishment**

The distinction between the two is easy to lose, and the verse keeps them apart by putting the test before the story's ending rather than after it. The flood was a punishment; the nine centuries before it were a test. Both belong to the same account, and only one of them was avoidable at the end.

The test is the part in which the outcome is open. While Nūḥ was speaking, his people could have answered differently, and the verse's placement says that the open period is the significant one. Once the water came, nothing was being decided any more. A reader who takes the story as a warning about punishment has read the wrong half of it; the test is the half he is inside.

Hūd makes the open period explicit in the narrative itself: while the ark was being built, the chiefs walked past and mocked (11:38). The mockery is recorded because it happened during the test, when a different response was still available, and the sūrah keeps the two phases distinct for the same reason this verse does.

**Why the Verse Closes the Account**

The comment arrives at the end rather than the beginning, which changes how the story has been read. A reader who was told in advance that there were signs in the account would have been hunting for them; told afterwards, he has to go back over what he has just read.

That backward look is the point of the arrangement. The verses just completed contain a messenger, a message, an objection based on status, an accusation of madness, a prayer, an instruction, a rescue and a drowning. The comment says: all of that is a sign, and you are being tested by it. The sūrah is about to begin the same sequence again with a different people, and the reader now knows what to do with it.

**Tested With Fear and Famine**

The Cow describes what the test consists of in concrete terms: *"We will certainly test you with a touch of fear and famine and loss of property, life, and crops. Give good news to those who patiently endure—who, when faced with a disaster, say, 'Surely to Allah we belong and to Him we will ˹all˺ return.'"* (2:155-156).

That description belongs beside this verse because it defines the test in the material terms the sūrah has just been using. The preceding verses were about provision — rain, gardens, livestock, transport — and the test is conducted with the same currency: the withdrawal or the threat of withdrawal of exactly those things. The formula the tested are told to say is the same one Nūḥ was told to say, in a different form: a statement of belonging, made before the outcome is known.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:31

> Then We raised another generation after them

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qarn: A Generation, and a Measure of Time**

The word is *qarn*, which carries both senses at once: a generation of people, and the span of time they occupy. Arabic uses the word for the people and for the century together, and the double sense is not a coincidence — a generation is a people defined by the time it holds the earth.

The verse uses the word in a single clause and gets both meanings out of it. A people has been destroyed and another has been raised in its place, and the interval between them is measured in the same unit as the people themselves. The sūrah is not describing a succession of individuals but a succession of occupants, and the word it chooses makes the succession sound like the turning of a page rather than the passing of an age.

**The Word That Measures History**

*Qarn* is one of the Qur'an's standard units for talking about the past. It appears in a long series of places — in al-Anʿām, in Yūnus, in Hūd, in Maryam, in al-Qaṣaṣ, in al-Aḥqāf, in Qāf — almost always in the same construction: how many generations We destroyed before them. The word does the work that a list of names would do in another literature.

That usage tells the reader how the Book thinks about history. The past is not a sequence of events to be recorded but a sequence of tenancies to be evaluated. Every *qarn* received something, did something with it, and was replaced. The verse here is the shortest possible statement of the pattern: one generation ends, another is raised, and the pattern continues whether or not the second has learned anything from the first.

**Which People? The Verse Does Not Say**

The commentators have tried to identify them. Some say ʿĀd, others Thamūd, and al-Qurṭubī notes the obvious difficulty: the verse gives no identification, and the next verse describes a messenger sent "from among themselves" without naming him either.

The attempts are natural and the failure to settle them is not a defect in the text. The sūrah of al-Qaṣaṣ does the same thing with a different people: *"Indeed, We gave Moses the Scripture—after destroying earlier nations—as an insight for the people, a guide, and mercy so perhaps they would be mindful."* (28:43). The earlier nations are unnamed there too. What the account needs is not the name of the people but the shape of their story, and the shape is given in the verses that follow.

A name would have narrowed the lesson to one nation. Leaving it out lets the shape apply to any people who were warned, refused, and were remembered afterwards only as a warning.

**The Silence Is the Point**

An unnamed generation has a use that a named one does not. If the verse said "then We raised ʿĀd", the reader would file the account under a particular history and treat it as finished business about people he is not.

Left unnamed, the generation stands for any generation, including the one listening. The sūrah's Meccan audience knew the stories of ʿĀd and Thamūd by name from other passages; here they are given a people with no name, whose spokesman has no name, and whose objection is the objection their own chiefs are making. The anonymity is a device for recognition, and it works because the objection in the verses that follow is quoted at length.

Al-Qurṭubī's observation is the right way to hold the question. The verse does not identify the people, and a commentator who supplies a name is adding information the text withholds. What is withheld is not accidental: the sūrah is building a pattern, and a pattern is legible only when the particular names are removed from it.

**Destruction Followed by Replacement**

The verse states a fact that the Book treats as a standing feature of the world rather than an event. Civilisations do not merely end; they are succeeded, and the succession is described with a verb of raising — *anshaʾnā*, We brought into being, We raised — which is the same verb used for bringing something into existence from nothing.

That verb choice is significant. The generation that follows is not what remains after the destruction; it is a new act. The earth is not left empty and gradually repopulated by survivors. It is given new occupants, and the sūrah of al-Isrāʾ states the principle with Nūḥ named explicitly: how many generations We destroyed after Nūḥ. The replacement is part of the pattern, and its regularity is what makes the pattern available as a warning.

The same verse in al-Isrāʾ adds that God is sufficient, concerning the sins of His servants, as Aware and Seeing — which puts the replacement in its proper frame. The raising of a new generation is not a fresh start granted to a world that has forgotten. Nothing has been forgotten; the record stands, and the new occupants are being given what the old ones had.

**How Many Generations After Nūḥ**

The sūrah of the Night Journey puts the number in terms of quantity rather than names: *"How many a generation have We destroyed after Noah! And sufficient is your Lord, concerning the sins of His servants, as Aware and Seeing."* (17:17). The verse follows an account of the Israelites' two periods of corruption, and it invokes Nūḥ as the fixed point from which the count runs.

That is the function of this verse inside al-Muʾminūn. Nūḥ's story has just been told and closed with a comment about signs and testing; the next verse raises another generation, and the reader understands that he is watching a cycle that has turned many times since. The sūrah does not need to name the second people because the first one has already established what happens to them.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:32

> and sent to them a messenger from among themselves, ˹declaring,˺ “Worship Allah ˹alone˺. You have no god other than Him. Will you not then fear ˹Him˺?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Messenger From Among Themselves**

The phrase *min anfusihim*, from among themselves, is the whole of the verse's new material. Everything else in it repeats what Nūḥ said. The sūrah chooses to specify the messenger's origin at precisely the point where the previous narrative showed that origin being used as an objection — a human like you, who eats what you eat.

The specification is an answer given in advance. Before the new people raise the objection, the verse states that their messenger was one of them, and it states it as a fact about God's arrangement rather than as a concession. The Book treats a messenger from among the people as the normal form of messengership, not as a defect in it, and it says so here in four words that the following verses will spend themselves attacking.

There is a practical dimension to the phrase as well. A messenger from among the people speaks their language, knows their customs and can be checked against what he was like before the message came. He is not an outsider with no history. That is what makes the accusation of fabrication in the verses below so serious, and so easily made: the man's whole life is public property.

**The Same Sentence, Word for Word**

The quoted speech is identical to the one Nūḥ spoke in the earlier account: the call to worship, the exclusion of every other god, and the closing question about fear. Not a word has been adjusted for the new audience, the new time or the new circumstances.

That identity is the verse's argument. A message that changed with its audience would be a negotiation; a message delivered in the same words across an interval measured in generations is a fixed thing. The sūrah is showing that the demand placed on this people is not a local arrangement or a tribal cult but the same demand that was placed on the people who were drowned, and that its terms have not been revised because the first audience refused them.

**Why the Refrain Is Repeated in Full**

The sūrah could have written "and he said to them what Nūḥ had said". It quotes the sentence again instead, and the repetition is deliberate.

A summary would have made the second account derivative. A full quotation makes it independent — this people is being addressed directly, in its own narrative, with the complete demand in front of it, and its reply will be its own. The Book's habit of repeating the refrain across the sūrahs of al-Aʿrāf, Hūd and this one produces the effect of hearing the same voice from different mouths, which is what the later verse in this sūrah says explicitly when it describes the messengers as sent in succession.

The repetition also protects the messenger. A reader who met the demand once might treat it as Nūḥ's particular teaching. Meeting it again, unchanged, in the mouth of a man from a different time and place, the reader has to consider that the sentence is not the property of any prophet. That is the point the sūrah will make later, when it describes the divisions people made among themselves despite having received the same instruction.

**The Unchanged Core Across the Messengers**

The core appears in the same form everywhere it is recorded. *"Indeed, We sent Noah to his people. He said, 'O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him.'"* (7:59). Hūd to ʿĀd: *"O my people! Worship Allah. You have no god other than Him."* (11:50). The wording is stable enough that a reader can recognise it as a formula on first hearing and know what is coming.

The stability has a consequence for the objections. If the message is always the same, then the objections to it are always the same too, and the sūrah is about to show that they are — a human like you, we never heard this from our fathers. The refrain is what makes the pattern visible. Without the repeated core, each narrative would be a separate quarrel; with it, the quarrels are recognisably one quarrel conducted over centuries.

**The Question Again**

The verse ends, as the earlier one did, on an invitation: will you not then fear Him. Nothing has been added to make the appeal harder to refuse, and nothing has been dropped.

The persistence of the interrogative form across the whole sequence of messengers is worth noting. None of them closes his opening address with a threat. Each one asks a question that expects an answer, and each one is answered by an accusation instead. The sūrah preserves the question in full each time because the question is where the hearer's responsibility lies — and the following verses will record a community that never answers it, arguing instead about the messenger's diet.

**The Addition Each Messenger Makes**

The refrain has a fixed core and a variable part, and the variable part is aimed at the local error. Hūd's version appends a charge: *"You do nothing but fabricate lies ˹against Allah˺."* (11:50). Ṣāliḥ's and Shuʿayb's versions, in al-Aʿrāf and Hūd, carry their own specific accusations against their peoples' practice.

This verse gives the core alone, with nothing added, which suits an unnamed people whose specific idolatry the sūrah does not describe. What the reader is shown is the irreducible content of every mission — one God, no other, and a question about whether that changes how you live. Everything else in the prophetic record is commentary on that sentence.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:33

> But the chiefs of his people—who disbelieved, denied the meeting ˹with Allah˺ in the Hereafter, and were spoiled by the worldly luxuries We had provided for them—said ˹to the masses˺, “This is only a human like you. He eats what you eat, and drinks what you drink

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Descriptions Before the Speech**

The verse does not simply report what the chiefs said. It interrupts its own sentence to describe them, and the description has three parts: they disbelieved, they denied the meeting of the Hereafter, and they had been given luxury in this life. Only after all three does the quotation begin.

The arrangement tells the reader how to hear the speech. The argument that follows is not being offered as an argument; it is being offered as the output of three stated conditions. A reader who met the quotation first would have to evaluate it on its merits. Met after the description, it arrives already diagnosed. The sūrah uses this technique rarely, and it uses it here because the objection is going to sound plausible to anyone who has not been told who is making it.

**Luxury as a Cause, Not a Context**

The third description is a causal clause: those to whom We had given luxury in this life. Their ease is presented as one of the reasons they said what they said, not as an incidental fact about them.

The Book makes the same connection elsewhere. Al-Isrāʾ describes how destruction reaches a society through its elite: *"Whenever We intend to destroy a society, We command its elite ˹to obey Allah˺ but they act rebelliously in it. So the decree ˹of punishment˺ is justified, and We destroy it utterly."* (17:16). Hūd says of the destroyed peoples that the wrongdoers pursued their pleasures and became wicked (11:116). The claim is not that wealth makes a person wicked. It is that a settled, comfortable position gives a person a reason to want nothing to change, and a message about being answerable is a message about change.

**"A Human Like You" Again, With a New Detail**

The objection repeats the one Nūḥ's chiefs made, and then supplies evidence for it: he eats what you eat and drinks what you drink. The addition is the verse's contribution. The earlier account said the messenger was human and wanted superiority; this one says he is human and shares your appetites.

The same objection is recorded against the Prophet in more detail: *"What kind of messenger is this who eats food and goes about in market-places ˹for a living˺? If only an angel had been sent down with him to be his co-warner,"* (25:7). The market and the meal are the two proofs offered in both cases, and both are facts that no one disputes. The argument does not depend on denying anything about the messenger. It depends on treating ordinary human life as incompatible with carrying a message.

The detail about drinking is new in this account, and it sharpens the objection in a specific way. Eating can be read as a necessity; drinking water beside a man you have known all your life is a piece of ordinary intimacy. The chiefs are pointing at the most unremarkable facts of the prophet's daily existence and offering them as disqualifications. The ordinary is being used as evidence against the extraordinary, and the sūrah records the move without answering it because it does not need an answer.

**The Argument From Appetite**

Why should eating be an objection? The reasoning is not stated, and reconstructing it is the only way to see what is wrong with it.

The implied premise is that a being who needs food is a being with wants, and a being with wants cannot be a disinterested channel of divine speech — he will be, at bottom, a man seeking something. The premise confuses two different things. Needing food makes a person dependent; it does not make him dishonest. Every prophet in the Book ate, and the Qur'an never treats this as a difficulty to be explained away. It treats it as the reason a prophet can be followed at all, since a messenger who did not share the condition of his people could not tell them how to live inside it.

**The Chiefs Speak to the Masses Again**

As in the earlier narrative, the speech is directed at the crowd rather than at the messenger. The verse says they said it *to the masses*, and the messenger is referred to in the third person throughout: this is a human, he eats, he drinks.

The grammar is a description of the strategy. The chiefs are not in conversation with the prophet; they are managing an audience. Nothing in their speech is addressed to him, and nothing in it asks him a question. The sūrah preserves the third-person construction because it shows the shape of the refusal — a discussion conducted over the messenger's head, in which the person who made the claim is never spoken to.

**The Luxurious Are Named First in Destruction**

The pairing of luxury with rejection runs through the sūrah's later material and through the Book generally. Al-Anbiyāʾ records the taunt directed at a destroyed people: *"Do not run away! Return to your luxuries and your homes, so you may be questioned ˹about your fate˺."* (21:13). The homes and the comforts are named as the things they will be asked about.

That verse belongs beside this one because it identifies the same two items in the same order. The chiefs here are described as given luxury, and their speech is about preserving the arrangement that luxury depends on. The question the destroyed are told to return and answer is the question the chiefs refused to hear, and the sūrah's account of who refuses it is consistent: the people with the most to lose.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:34

> And if you ˹ever˺ obey a human like yourselves, then you would certainly be losers

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Conditional Threat Aimed at the Crowd**

The verse is addressed to the people, not to the messenger, and its form is a warning about consequences: if you obey him, you will be losers. The chiefs have finished describing the prophet and have moved on to what will happen to anyone who listens to him.

The conditional is worth examining, because it is not an argument about truth. Nobody is claiming that the message is false here. The claim is that obedience to it produces loss. That is a different kind of statement, and it belongs to the register of a politician rather than a theologian: not "he is wrong" but "following him will cost you". The shift from the previous verse's description of the man to this verse's prediction about his followers marks the point where persuasion becomes pressure.

**Loss as the Chiefs' Currency**

The word is *khāsirūn*, those who come out of a transaction with less than they put in — traders who have lost on a deal, not people who have been punished or misled. The vocabulary is commercial, and the audience is a mercantile one.

The choice of idiom tells the reader what the chiefs are appealing to. They are not arguing about the afterlife; they have already denied it in the verse before. They are arguing about advantage, and the advantage in question is present and material. Obey a man who tells you there is no god but God, and you lose your standing, your shrine, your trade routes and your customs. The warning is a calculation, and it is offered to people who think in calculations.

There is also a hidden admission in it. The chiefs do not tell the crowd that the messenger is lying; they tell them that obeying him is costly. A speaker who believed his opponent was simply wrong would say so. The switch from truth to consequence suggests that the truth of the message is not something the chiefs are confident about, and the sūrah lets the switch stand as evidence.

**Who Actually Loses**

The verse states a prediction that the sūrah will reverse. The people the chiefs describe as losers if they obey are, in the Book's accounting, the only people who do not lose; and the ones who refuse are the ones described with the same word.

Al-Kahf puts the reversal in the form of a question: *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Shall we inform you of who will lose the most deeds? ˹They are˺ those whose efforts are in vain in this worldly life, while they think they are doing good!'"* (18:103-104). The description fits the chiefs exactly: they are working hard, they believe they are protecting their people, and the work comes to nothing. The verse in al-Muʾminūn records the charge being made, and the Book elsewhere identifies who it actually applies to.

**The Argument's Hidden Premise**

Beneath the warning sits a claim that is never stated: that obeying an equal is degrading. The emphasis in the verse falls on "a human like yourselves", and the loss being threatened is not poverty but subordination.

That premise explains the whole sequence of objections. The chiefs could not accept a messenger from among themselves because a messenger has authority over those he addresses, and authority held by a peer is authority they do not control. An angel would have been tolerable, since an angel is not a competitor. A man from their own number is intolerable, because his elevation is their reduction. The verse is the clearest statement of the motive in the entire narrative, and it is spoken by the people who have it.

The premise also explains why the chiefs address the crowd rather than the prophet. A man whose objection is about status cannot win by argument, because the messenger's claim does not rest on status. What the chiefs can do is persuade the crowd that following him is a demotion for everyone, and that is precisely the form the warning takes. The verse is a piece of social management dressed as a prediction.

**The Word for Losers**

*Khāsir* and its plural appear throughout the Qur'an as the standard description of those who misjudge the terms of their own existence. It is used of the people whose scale is light on the Day: *"But those whose scale is light, they have doomed themselves for wrongfully denying Our signs."* (7:9) — the verb there is from the same root, and it is reflexive: they have lost themselves.

That reflexive use is the answer to this verse. The loss the chiefs threaten comes from outside — obey him and you will forfeit something. The loss the Book describes is self-inflicted and total. The two uses of one word set the chiefs' calculation against the Qur'an's, and the difference between them is the difference between losing property and losing the self that owned it.

**The Real Losers Named Elsewhere**

The sūrah of al-Kahf's question has an answer attached to it, and the answer is not about the afterlife alone. Those who lose most are the ones whose effort in this life was wasted while they believed they were doing well (18:104). The loss is realised here, in the uselessness of the work, before it is realised anywhere else.

That is the sharpest reversal available of the chiefs' warning. They told the masses that obedience would cost them; the Book says the refusal cost them everything they were trying to protect, and cost them the awareness that it had. The verse records the threat in their words and leaves the outcome to be read in the verses that follow, where the destruction arrives without warning and the mockery stops.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:35

> Does he promise you that once you are dead and reduced to dust and bones, you will be brought forth ˹alive˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Objection Is About the Hereafter**

The chiefs move from the messenger's person to his content, and the content they choose is resurrection. The objection is put as a question addressed to the crowd: does he really promise you this?

The same question appears throughout the Book in nearly identical words. Al-Isrāʾ records it as mockery: *"When we are reduced to bones and ashes, will we really be raised as a new creation?"* (17:49). Al-Wāqiʿah preserves the fuller form: *"When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected?"* (56:47). An-Nāziʿāt has it in its shortest version: *"even after we have been reduced to decayed bones?"* (79:11). The formula is stable enough to be recognisable, and this verse is one instance of a standing objection rather than a local quibble.

**"Brought Forth": A Word About Leaving the Earth**

The Arabic behind the translation is *mukhrajūn*, caused to come out. The commentators note that the literal sense is being made to leave the place one is in — the earth in which the body lies.

The word choice is precise about what resurrection means in the Qur'an. It is not the survival of a soul or the continuation of a reputation; it is emergence from a location. The dead are somewhere, in the ground, and the claim being mocked is that they will be made to leave it. That concreteness is what makes the objection possible in the first place: a claim about the survival of the spirit would be harder to ridicule, while a claim about bodies coming out of soil can be met with the observation that bodies turn to dust.

**Dust and Bones: The Two Stages Named**

The verse names two states, and they are not redundant. Dust is what remains when the body has completely disintegrated; bones are what remain while it has not. Between them they cover the whole process of decay, from the fresh corpse to the unidentifiable powder.

Naming both is a rhetorical device. The objector is not describing a particular corpse but the full range of what happens to corpses, and the range is what makes the promise seem absurd. A body that is still recognisable might conceivably be restored; powder cannot. The objection takes the worst case and presents it as the general case, which is what an effective objection does. The Book's answer, given elsewhere, is that the one who produced them the first time knows every created being (36:79) — an answer aimed at the powder rather than at the bones.

The two words also map onto the sūrah's own account of origins. Verses twelve to fourteen described the human being assembled from an extract of clay and a drop; dust and bones are the same materials in reverse order. The objection is therefore built out of the same inventory the sūrah has already used for the making, and the reader who has followed the earlier verses holds the answer before the chiefs finish asking the question.

**The Question Posed as Ridicule**

The form matters. The chiefs do not say the promise is false; they ask whether he really promises it, in a tone that expects the crowd to laugh. Al-Isrāʾ marks the tone explicitly with the word "mockingly" (17:49), and al-Wāqiʿah does the same (56:47).

Ridicule is a different instrument from argument, and it is more efficient. An argument has to be answered; a laugh only has to be joined. The verse shows the chiefs deploying it on an audience, and the sūrah preserves the mocking form because the form is part of the record. What is being described is not a disagreement about metaphysics but a social performance, in which the crowd's amusement is the intended result and the resurrection is only the occasion for it.

The efficiency of ridicule is also its limitation. A crowd that has been made to laugh has not been convinced of anything, and the position it holds is unstable — it lasts only as long as the mood does. The sūrah knows this, which is why the narrative moves straight from the mockery to the creed: underneath the performance there is an actual belief, and the belief is what the following verses have to answer.

**Why Resurrection Is the Harder Claim**

Of everything the messenger said, this is the part that carries a cost. Worship one God changes a practice; belief in being raised changes the meaning of every action a person has already performed.

That is presumably why the chiefs select it. The call to worship could be accommodated — most polytheistic societies can absorb another deity or a higher one. A promise that the dead will be brought out and held to account cannot be accommodated at all, because it makes the present life provisional. The objection in this verse is aimed at the one element of the message that has no compromise available, and the sūrah's own opening section, with its description of an estate inherited by the believers, is the thing the chiefs are refusing.

**The Same Question From the Quraysh**

The parallel verses are not about ancient peoples. They record the objection as it was put to the Prophet in Mecca, in the same words and with the same tone: *"And they argue with Us— forgetting they were created—saying, 'Who will give life to decayed bones?'"* (36:78). The clause "forgetting they were created" is the Book's answer in miniature.

That clause is what the chiefs in this verse have also forgotten. Their objection is built entirely on what happens at the end of a body's history, and it never mentions the beginning. The sūrah has already answered it eleven verses earlier, in its account of the drop, the clot and the lump; the narrative of the messengers then shows the objection being made anyway, by people who had the same evidence available to them.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:36

> Impossible, simply impossible is what you are promised

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hayhāta Hayhāta: A Word Said Twice**

The Arabic is *hayhāta hayhāta* — an interjection meaning "far away!", used to reject something as remote from possibility. It is not a sentence with a subject and a predicate. It is an exclamation, and the verse reproduces it exactly as it would have been shouted.

The Qur'an rarely records the sound of a refusal rather than its content, and when it does, the sound is the content. The chiefs are not arguing here. They have already argued, in the verse before, and this verse is what the argument became: a word flung at a claim. The preservation of the interjection in the text is a deliberate choice, and it tells the reader that the narrative has reached the point where reasons have run out.

**An Interjection, Not an Argument**

The verse contains no evidence, no reasoning and no counter-claim. It does not say why the promise is impossible or offer an alternative account of what happens to the dead. It says the promise is far away, twice.

That absence is what makes the verse worth its place in the sūrah. A reader who follows the sequence of the chiefs' speeches — a human like you, he eats what you eat, obey him and you lose, does he really promise you this — will notice that each step contains less reasoning than the one before. This verse is the floor. It is the shape a rejection takes when it has exhausted everything except itself, and the Qur'an records it without commentary because the shape is the commentary.

There is a further reason the exclamation is preserved. It shows the audience what refusal sounds like at its end, so that a reader who recognises the sound in his own company can identify where he is standing. The Qur'an does not only report the arguments of the deniers; it records the noise they make when the arguments are gone.

**The Distance the Word Names**

*Hayhāta* is a word about space. It means far, remote, out of reach, and it is used of something that will not come near. The rejection is therefore framed not as falsehood but as remoteness: what you are promised is so far off that it is not worth accounting for.

That framing is psychologically precise. Most people who reject the Hereafter do not claim to have proved its impossibility; they treat it as too distant to organise a life around. The word captures the actual posture, which is not denial so much as dismissal. The sūrah's answer to that posture is not an argument about distance but a statement about time — soon, they will be regretful — which converts the remote into the imminent without changing anything about it.

The word also carries an implication the speakers may not have noticed. Something that is far away is not something that does not exist; it is something that has not arrived. The strongest form of the objection would have been a denial, and the chiefs did not make one. Their chosen word concedes the possibility of the promise while dismissing its urgency, and the sūrah's reply addresses exactly that concession.

**What the Repetition Does**

The word is doubled, and Arabic doubles interjections for force rather than for information. One *hayhāta* would have carried the same meaning; two carry contempt.

The doubling also produces a rhythm, and rhythm is what a crowd responds to. A phrase repeated twice is a chant, and the verse is recording something said in public to an audience. The sūrah has been careful throughout to note that the chiefs speak to the masses; here it preserves the form their speech took in front of them. The doubled word is not a theological position. It is a performance, and the sūrah lets it stand on the page exactly as it was delivered.

That performance has an audience function worth naming. A crowd that has laughed cannot easily go back to considering the claim seriously, because the laughter has already placed the claim outside the range of things under discussion. The chiefs are not persuading the masses with the interjection; they are closing off the discussion in front of them, and the doubled word is the door shutting.

**Where the Verse Sits in the Sequence**

The four verses of the chiefs' speech form a descending order, and their arrangement is worth reading as a unit. Status: he is a human like you. Motive: obey him and you lose. Content: does he promise you resurrection? And then this: impossible.

The progression moves from an observation about the messenger, to a calculation about the audience, to a question about the message, and finally to a word about nothing in particular. Each stage is less answerable than the last because each stage makes less of a claim. By the time the sūrah reaches this verse, the chiefs have stopped asserting anything that could be examined, and the narrative can move on. The next verse states their creed in full, which is the only place in the sequence where they say something with content.

**The Creed Behind the Exclamation**

An interjection of this kind has a belief underneath it, and the verse that follows supplies it: there is nothing beyond this life, people die and others live, and nobody is resurrected. The shout and the creed belong together.

Reading them in that order shows how the two work. The creed is not argued for either, but at least it states a position; the exclamation is the position stripped down to a noise. The sūrah preserves both because both are needed to describe a refusal — the thing people believe and the thing they say when the belief is challenged in public. A reader who has only the creed will underestimate the social force of the denial; a reader who has only the shout will not know what is being denied.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:37

> There is nothing beyond our worldly life. We die, others are born, and none will be resurrected

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Complete Metaphysics in One Sentence**

After four verses of objection, the chiefs finally state a position. It is short, and it is the most consequential statement in the whole exchange: there is nothing but this life.

The verse is not a mood or a preference. It is a metaphysics — a claim about what exists — and everything else the chiefs said follows from it. Their objection to a human messenger, their calculation about loss, their mockery of the resurrection: all of it is downstream of this sentence. The sūrah lets the narrative run for four verses before producing the creed because the creed is what a reader needs in order to understand the rest, and because a refusal that begins with the details looks reasonable until the premise appears.

**"We Die and Others Live"**

The middle clause is compressed, and the exegetes read it two ways. One reading takes it as describing the succession of the species: we die, and others live after us — the world continues without us, and that continuation is all there is. The other takes it as a statement about the alternation itself: some die while others are alive, and the pattern exhausts what happens.

Both readings end in the same place, and the difference between them is not important. What the clause asserts is that life and death are a closed circuit inside this world. The generation that goes is replaced by the generation that comes, and the replacement is the whole of the story. That is precisely what the verse before this narrative described — We raised another generation after them — so the chiefs are taking a fact the sūrah has just stated and drawing from it the conclusion the sūrah does not draw.

**The Same Words in the Mouths of the Quraysh**

Al-Anʿām records the identical creed, with no middle clause: *"They insisted, 'There is nothing beyond this worldly life and we will never be resurrected.'"* (6:29). The word translated "insisted" carries the sense of holding a position against evidence, and the two halves of the sentence are the same two halves given here.

The parallel is what makes this verse more than a historical detail. The chiefs of an unnamed ancient people and the chiefs of seventh-century Mecca are recorded saying the same sentence, and the sūrah's audience knew which group they belonged to. The narrative is arranged so that the ancient creed is quoted in full before the reader is reminded that it is also his own community's creed, and the reminder comes in the verses that follow when the messenger's prayer is answered.

**What the Creed Makes Impossible**

The consequences of the sentence are not drawn out in the verse, and they do not need to be. If there is nothing beyond this life, then no action has a consequence that outlives the actor, no injustice is ever settled, and no promise can be kept after the one who made it dies.

It also removes the ground under the messenger entirely. A man claiming to speak for God about a Day that does not exist is not a difficult case; he is a fabricator, which is what the next verse calls him. The creed and the accusation belong together, and the sūrah places them in that order so that the reader sees the accusation as a conclusion rather than as an insult.

The sūrah's opening section is the direct contradiction of all three. It described people who guard trusts and covenants, and it awarded them an estate — a transaction completed after the parties are gone. The chiefs' creed makes that transaction meaningless, which is why the two statements cannot coexist and why the argument between them is not a technical disagreement. The whole of the sūrah's first eleven verses stands or falls on the sentence in this one.

That is also why the narrative is placed where it is. Twenty verses of provision and origin have gone by, and eleven verses of moral description before them. The sūrah has built its case out of observable fact, and then it produces the creed that denies the case can mean anything. The reader is asked to hold both, and the tension between them is what the rest of the sūrah will work through.

**A Creed That Cannot Be Argued With on Its Own Terms**

Notice what the verse does not do. It offers no evidence for the claim that nothing exists beyond this life, and none is available; the claim is not the result of an investigation but the absence of one. The chiefs have not examined the question and found an answer. They have declined to admit the question.

That is why the Book answers it the way it does. Rather than arguing for the afterlife, it points at the beginning: you were a drop, and you are arguing. The sūrah of Yā-Sīn puts the answer in one sentence — *"They will be revived by the One Who produced them the first time, for He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of every created being."* (36:79) — and the answer works only on someone who concedes that he was produced. The chiefs' creed removes that concession, which is why the sūrah spends twelve verses establishing it before it reaches this point.

**The Closed Circuit and the Open One**

The chiefs' account of the world is a circle: people live, people die, others replace them, and the circle turns without reference to anything outside itself. The sūrah's account is the same circle with one line added — a beginning and an end that are not part of the rotation.

Everything the sūrah has said so far belongs to that added line. The measured rain, the gardens, the cattle and the ships are the provisions of the circle; the drop, the clot, the death and the raising are the line. The chiefs have described the circle accurately, and their error is not in the description but in the claim that the circle is all there is. The verse records the error in their own words, which is the most economical way of stating it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:38

> He is no more than a man who has fabricated a lie about Allah, and we will never believe in him.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Charge: Fabrication**

The chiefs' speech ends with an accusation about the messenger's honesty. Having dismissed the message in the verse before, they now account for it: he has made it up and attributed it to God.

The charge is the standard one across the Book. Hūd's people are told by their own prophet that they do nothing but fabricate lies against God (11:50), and Sabaʾ reports it as one of the two options offered about a messenger: *"Has he fabricated a lie against Allah or is he insane?"* (34:8). Between those two accusations the objectors cover the whole field — the man is either dishonest or unwell — and neither option requires them to engage with what he said.

**A Lie About God, Not About People**

The specificity of the charge matters. The accusation is not that the messenger is mistaken, or ambitious, or confused about something he believes. It is that he has spoken falsehood and attached God's name to it, which is the gravest category of lie the Qur'an recognises.

That gravity works in two directions. The chiefs intend it as the strongest charge available, and it is. But it also means that the alternative — that he is telling the truth — carries a weight the verse does not state. A man who fabricates a lie about God is not merely wrong; he has committed the specific wrong the Book treats as unforgivable when it is persisted in. The sūrah does not soften the accusation, and a reader who takes it seriously has to take the messenger's claim seriously too. There is no middle position in which the man is sincere and mistaken.

The charge also raises the stakes for the accusers. If the messenger is a fabricator, he has done the worst thing a person can do with God's name, and leaving him alive and unopposed is itself a failure. The chiefs' next step in the narrative is therefore not debate but destruction, which is the standard outcome of this accusation across the Book. The verse is the last thing said before the plotting begins.

**"We Will Never Believe"**

The verse closes with a decision stated in the strongest available form: *wa-mā naḥnu lahu bi-muʾminīn*, and we are not, for him, believers — with the negation and the emphasis that make it final. It is not "we are not convinced" or "we will see". It is a permanent refusal, announced in advance.

The announcement is the significant part. A person who has genuinely examined a claim and rejected it does not usually publish the permanence of his rejection; he states the rejection and moves on. The declaration of never is a commitment made to an audience, and it functions as a lock on future reconsideration. The chiefs are binding themselves in front of the masses, which means that any later change of mind would cost them their standing as well as their position.

The lock has an effect on the people listening too. A crowd that hears its leaders swear they will never believe has been told what believing would cost them socially, and the cost is separation from the men who hold the community together. The verse records a decision and, in the same breath, the pressure that makes the decision stick.

**The Sequence of the Three Charges**

The chiefs have now made three accusations across four verses, and they do not sit well together. He wants to be superior to you; he is insane; he has fabricated a lie against God. The first is a claim about ambition, the second about his mind, the third about his honesty.

A man who is insane cannot be a calculating fabricator, and a man fabricating for advantage is not out of his mind. The incompatibility is a reliable indicator that the accusations are not conclusions but reflexes, produced by the need to reject rather than by examination. The sūrah records all three without pointing out the contradiction, and the reader is left to notice it — which is a more effective way of discrediting the objection than any answer would have been.

**Belief in God Without the Hereafter**

The creed in the verse before explains how the accusation is possible. These are not atheists. They acknowledge God, and their objection to the messenger is that he has invented something about Him.

That combination is what the Qur'an addresses throughout its Meccan material: people who affirm a supreme power and deny that it has anything to do with how they will end up. A god who does not raise the dead is a god who imposes no obligations, and a messenger who claims otherwise is, on that assumption, necessarily a liar. The accusation in this verse is therefore sincere. It follows from the creed, and the creed is where the sūrah locates the fault.

**The Charge Answered by the Message Itself**

The Book answers the fabrication charge by pointing at the character of what was said. A man inventing a religion for advantage does not produce a message whose first demand is that he holds no authority, that he asks no payment, and that the outcome belongs to God alone — and Nūḥ states exactly that in Yūnus: he has never demanded a reward, and his reward is only from God (10:72).

The answer is not a proof, and the sūrah does not offer it as one. It is an observation about the shape of the message: it costs the messenger everything and gives him nothing, which is an unusual arrangement for a fraud. The chiefs' accusation is recorded in full, and the refutation is left standing in the other sūrahs where the messenger's own words are preserved.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:39

> The messenger prayed, “My Lord! Help me, because they have denied ˹me˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Sentence Recorded Twice**

This verse is word for word the verse Nūḥ prayed earlier in the sūrah. The same address, the same request, the same reason given. Nothing has been varied to suit a different prophet, a different people or a different century.

The repetition is the verse's whole content. A sūrah that has just shown the same message delivered in identical words to two peoples now shows the same prayer offered in identical words by two messengers. The symmetry is exact, and it is the structural argument of the passage: what a messenger says to his people does not change, and what he says to God when they refuse does not change either. The two fixed points bracket every prophetic narrative in the Book.

**What the Repetition Establishes**

If the prayer appeared once, a reader could treat it as Nūḥ's personal response to a personal situation — the reaction of a man worn down by nine centuries of refusal. Recorded a second time, in the mouth of a messenger whose name is not given and whose circumstances are barely described, it becomes something else.

It becomes the standard response. The verse tells the reader what a prophet does when the message has been delivered and refused, and it tells him that this is what every prophet did. There is no variety in the record: no prophet in this sequence argues further, threatens on his own account, or abandons the mission. They pray the same sentence, and the outcome is then decided elsewhere.

The repetition also protects the reader from a common misreading. A single account of a prophet praying for help could be taken as a moment of personal exhaustion, and the mission could then be read as something the prophet carried on his own nerves. Two accounts, centuries apart, in identical words, describe an office rather than a temperament. The man praying is replaceable; the prayer is not.

**An Unnamed Messenger Praying**

The sūrah has not told the reader who this messenger is. The generation is unnamed, the people are unnamed, and the man is referred to only as a messenger from among themselves. His prayer is recorded in the first person without a name attached to it.

The anonymity is doing work here that it did not do earlier. When Nūḥ prayed, the reader knew his history and could feel the weight of the centuries behind the sentence. This prayer has no history behind it, and what remains is the sentence itself — a man addressing his Lord, asking for help, and stating the reason. The verse reduces the prophetic prayer to its minimum, and the minimum turns out to be identical to the maximum.

The anonymity also removes the possibility of treating the prayer as exceptional. A named prophet in a famous story is easy to admire from a distance. An unnamed man saying an ordinary sentence in an unrecorded century cannot be admired from anywhere; he can only be recognised, and recognition is the response the sūrah is after.

**The Prayer as the Turning Point of Every Narrative**

In both accounts the prayer marks the same structural position: the end of speech between the messenger and his people, and the beginning of action between God and the people. Nothing further is said to the chiefs after it. In Nūḥ's case the next verse is the instruction to build; here the next verse is the answer about regret.

The pattern holds across the Book's prophetic narratives. Yūnus records Nūḥ's prayer in fuller form — he is rejected, so he calls for judgement between himself and them, and asks to be saved with the believers (26:117-118) — and the outcome follows immediately. The prayer is not a pause in the narrative. It is the mechanism by which the narrative passes out of human hands, and this verse occupies that position for the second time in one sūrah.

**The Distance Between the Two Prayers**

Between the first prayer and this one, generations have passed. The sūrah has moved from Nūḥ's flood to a people raised afterwards, and the interval is measured in the word for generation used at the start of the account.

That distance is what makes the identical wording significant. If two men in the same city said the same sentence, it would prove nothing. Two men separated by an unknown number of generations, addressing different peoples who made the same objections in the same words, arrive at the same prayer — and the sūrah treats the arrival as evidence that the position they are in is one position, not two. The prophets are not independently reaching similar conclusions. They are doing the same job.

For the Prophet hearing this sūrah revealed, the repetition had a further use. He was in the same position: a messenger from among his own people, told that he was a human like them, that he ate and drank, and that he had fabricated a lie. The verse hands him the sentence his predecessor prayed, and the sūrah does not need to tell him to use it.

**The Prayer Before the Answer**

Ash-Shuʿarāʾ preserves Nūḥ's version with its second half: *"Noah prayed, 'My Lord! My people have truly rejected me. So judge between me and them decisively, and save me and the believers with me.'"* (26:117-118). The request has two parts — a judgement and a rescue — and both are granted in the narrative that follows.

This verse keeps only the first element: help. The fuller version in ash-Shuʿarāʾ shows what is inside the request when it is unpacked, and the compressed version here shows what remains when it is not. Between them the reader has the prayer in both registers, and the sūrah's answer in the next verse is addressed to the compressed one.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:40

> Allah responded, “Soon they will be truly regretful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Is Not What Was Asked For**

The messenger asked for help, and the reply does not mention help. It states an outcome: in a little while they will be regretful. Nothing is promised to the prophet directly, and nothing is threatened to the people in the form of a command.

The shape of the answer is worth attending to. A request for assistance receives a prediction, which means the assistance consists in what is going to happen rather than in anything the messenger is asked to do. The verse removes him from the equation at the moment he asked to be put back into it. What follows in the narrative is a destruction that has no human agent, and the answer prepares the reader for it by refusing to describe it as an act of the prophet.

The mismatch between request and reply is also a description of how prayer works in the Qur'an. The answer is not obliged to take the form the asker imagined. Nūḥ asked to be helped against people who denied him, and what he is given is the knowledge that their denial will be reversed in their own understanding. That is help, but not of the kind he pictured, and the sūrah leaves the difference unexplained.

**"In a Little While"**

*ʿAn qalīl* — after a little. The phrase is the whole of the answer's temporal content, and it is doing a great deal of work. To the chiefs, the promise of resurrection was *hayhāta*, far away, remote beyond reckoning. To God, the interval is short.

The two descriptions of time are the real disagreement in this passage. The creed in the verse before this one was built on distance: what is promised will not arrive, or will arrive too far off to matter. The answer does not argue with the creed. It re-describes the distance, and in doing so it takes the chiefs' own word for remoteness and applies it to their position instead. What seemed far is near; what seemed settled is about to end.

**Regret as the Predicted State**

The word is *nādimūn*, those who regret — and the choice of state rather than event is deliberate. The answer does not say they will be destroyed, or seized, or punished, though the narrative will describe all of that. It says they will regret.

Regret is the specific suffering of a person who has understood something too late to act on it. It is not pain inflicted from outside; it is the internal consequence of a decision, arriving after the decision can no longer be revised. The Qur'an uses the word for the man who killed his brother and then watched a crow show him what he should have done: *"He cried, 'Alas! Have I ˹even˺ failed to be like this crow and bury the corpse of my brother?' So he became regretful."* (5:31). The regret there is total and useless, and that is the state this verse predicts.

The two cases have the same structure. In both, the person acted, and the understanding arrived afterwards, delivered by something smaller than himself — a crow in one case, an event in the other. Neither man was prevented from knowing earlier. Both were told, and both found out later.

**Regret Without Effect**

The prediction contains an implicit clause that the verse does not state: the regret will change nothing. A regret that could alter the outcome would not be regret but reconsideration, and reconsideration was available throughout the centuries the messenger spent speaking.

That is what makes the answer severe rather than merely ominous. The chiefs are not being told that they will suffer; they are being told that they will understand. The understanding arrives at the point where it has no function, and the sūrah's later material returns to exactly this state in the mouths of those who ask to be sent back. The answer in this verse is the first appearance of a theme the sūrah will develop at length.

**The Shortest Answer in the Narrative**

The reply is six words in Arabic. It contains no explanation, no description of what is coming, no instruction to the messenger and no comfort for him. It is the most economical divine speech in the passage.

The economy matches the situation. Nothing needs explaining to a reader who has just been given the chiefs' creed in their own words, and nothing needs deciding — the decision was made centuries earlier, when the messenger began speaking and they began refusing. The verse records the closure of a long argument in a single clause, and the closure is not a verdict delivered at the end of a trial. It is the statement of what the parties have already chosen, with the date attached.

**The Regret That Comes Too Late**

The sūrah of ash-Shuʿarāʾ records the same outcome for a different people in one word: *"But they killed her, becoming regretful."* (26:157). The sequence there is compressed to its elements — the act, and then the regret — with nothing between them, and nothing after the regret.

That compression is the pattern this verse announces. The people who mocked the promise of resurrection are told that the thing they will eventually feel is the feeling of having been wrong at a moment when being wrong can no longer be undone. The answer to the messenger's request for help is therefore given to him in the only form that matters to him: the assurance that the matter is not unresolved, and that the refusal he has been living with for his whole life will one day be understood by the people who made it.

The verse closes a unit that began with the raising of a new generation. A people was brought into being, a messenger was sent from among them, the chiefs answered with four verses of objection, the messenger prayed one sentence, and the reply was six words. The sūrah gives the whole exchange that much space, and the disproportion between the length of the refusal and the brevity of the answer is the last thing the reader is left holding.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:41

> Then the ˹mighty˺ blast overtook them with justice, and We reduced them to rubble. So away with the wrongdoing people

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣayḥah: The Cry, the Blast, the Punishment**

The word for what overtook them is *ṣayḥah*, and it means a shout or a cry — a single loud sound. The exegetes read it three ways. One takes it as the shout of the angel, a voice that carries the punishment. Another takes it as a blast of wind or a thunderclap, the sound being the vehicle rather than the agent. A third treats it as the punishment itself, named after its most striking feature.

The three readings are closer than they look. In every one of them the destruction arrives as a sound, instantaneously, with no siege, no battle and no warning interval. That is what the word is doing in the verse: it is describing a punishment that does not behave like a war. Al-Ḥijr uses the same word twice for the same kind of event, and in both places it attaches a time of day rather than a mechanism, which suggests the narrative tradition cared about the suddenness more than the cause.

**"With Justice" — the Adverb That Does the Work**

The blast overtook them *bi-l-ḥaqq*, with truth or with justice, and the adverb is the most important word in the clause. A sudden destruction could be read as arbitrary — a catastrophe that falls on whoever happens to be there. The verse refuses that reading before it can form.

The justice named here is not retributive in the narrow sense of a punishment calibrated to a crime. It is the justice of a sequence that has been announced. The sūrah has just spent several verses on messengers who came, were denied, warned, and were ignored; the destruction arrives at the end of that sequence and not in the middle of an ordinary afternoon. The adverb tells the reader that what happened was earned by a long and fully documented refusal, and it tells him this at the exact moment when the sheer scale of the event might otherwise obscure the point.

**Ghuthāʾ: Dross, or Flood-Wreck**

The verb translated as reduced them to rubble comes from *ghuthāʾ*, and the exegetes give it two senses. One is the scum and debris a flood leaves behind — the useless matter deposited when the water withdraws. The other is dried plant rubbish, the kind blown about a deserted place.

Both images carry the same implication, which is worth stating plainly because it is harsher than the translation suggests. A community reduced to *ghuthāʾ* has not been defeated or conquered. It has been made into refuse. The word is used elsewhere for what a river casts up, and its sense is of material that has lost every property that made it useful. The verse is describing the end of a people in terms usually reserved for waste, and the choice of the word is a judgement on what they became rather than merely on what happened to them.
