The image is also final. Refuse is not a state a thing recovers from, and the sūrah's next verse moves on to the generations raised afterwards without returning to the ones reduced to it.

**"So Away With the Wrongdoing People"**

The verse ends with an exclamation: *fa-buʿdan li-l-qawmi ẓ-ẓālimīn*, away with the wrongdoing people. This is the second time the sūrah has used this formula; the first came after the flood of Nūḥ, and there it named the wrongdoing people as those who disbelieved.

The repetition is not padding. It is a refrain, and refrains in this sūrah mark the closing of a cycle. The formula appears where a narrative ends and the reader is meant to register the shape of what he has just read: messengers came, the people refused, the term arrived, and the community became refuse. The exclamation puts the reader's own response into the text, supplying the sentence a person would naturally say at the end of such an account, and in doing so it invites him to say it — and to notice that the same sentence will be available at the end of his own community's story.

**The Hour Was Specified**

Al-Ḥijr gives the destruction a time of day. Of one people: *"So the ˹mighty˺ blast overtook them at sunrise."* (15:73). Of another: *"But the ˹mighty˺ blast overtook them in the morning,"* (15:83). The punishment arrived at the start of a day, while people were beginning their ordinary business.

The detail is not decoration. A destruction that comes at sunrise is one nobody saw coming, because the day looked exactly like the day before it. The sūrah has already described the mechanism elsewhere: prosperity arrives, the warnings lose their urgency, and the silence of heaven is mistaken for approval.

The timing here is that mechanism at its end — the last morning of a community that had decided nothing was going to happen, and that was right up to the moment it was not. Nothing in the account suggests the people were doing anything unusual that morning, and the ordinariness is the point.

**Four Punishments, Four Peoples**

The sūrah has now described four judgements in sequence: the drowning of the people of Nūḥ, the blast that took the people of the cry, and the destructions named around them. Each has its own mechanism and its own word, and each ends with the same shape of outcome.

That variety is deliberate. A reader who sees one punishment repeated four times concludes that the account is a formula. A reader who sees four different punishments, each arriving in a different way and each preceded by the same refusal, concludes that the mechanism is irrelevant and the refusal is the whole of the matter. The verse contributes the second of the four, and it contributes it with an adverb about justice and a noun meaning refuse, which between them state both halves of the sūrah's argument: the destruction was deserved, and it left nothing behind.

The variety of mechanism also answers an argument from providence. A reader who sees the same disaster repeated might suppose the world is simply dangerous. Four different disasters, each preceded by the same refusal, point somewhere else.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:42

> Then We raised other generations after them

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse of One Clause**

The verse is a single sentence with no elaboration, and its brevity is itself the point. No time, no place, no names, no cause, no description of who the new generations were or where they came from. A reader looking for history will find nothing here, and a reader looking for the shape of history will find the whole of it.

The verse does one thing: it says that after the destruction, life continued. Another people came. The sūrah then moves immediately to what happened to them, and the reader who has just read two full accounts of messengers and refusals already knows the answer before it is given.

The compression is not a shortcut taken because the material is unimportant. It is a compression achieved because the material is now familiar, and familiarity is exactly what the sūrah has been building toward across its middle section. A narrative that has been told twice in full does not need a third full telling; it needs the first clause, and the reader supplies the rest.

**The Same Verb the Sūrah Has Used Twice**

The word for raised is from the root n-sh-ʾ, and the sūrah uses it earlier for the raising of the generations after Nūḥ and for the stages of a human being's formation. In both of those uses the verb describes something brought into being that was not there before, and in both the emphasis falls on the bringing rather than on the thing brought.

The recurrence matters for how the verse should be read. These generations were not survivors who carried on; they were raised, which is to say they were made. The verb belongs to the same vocabulary as the sūrah's account of creation, and its use here places the succession of communities inside the same order as the succession of the stages of a single life.

That placement is a claim rather than a stylistic choice. A people is a creature with a beginning and an end, formed and then finished, and the word chosen to describe its arrival is the word used for a human being's arrival. The sūrah opened with an account of how a person is made in stages, and it now describes communities with the same verb, which suggests that the pattern it is tracking operates at both scales.

**The Anonymity Is the Argument**

Nobody remembers these generations, and the verse gives them one clause. The communities that were destroyed are known because their destruction was recorded; the communities that came after are known only as the ones who came after.

That asymmetry is the sūrah's account of how history is actually kept. What survives of a people is what it did with its messenger. A community that receives a message and lives quietly inside it leaves no trace, because there is nothing in its story that anyone needs to retell, and no reader who encounters it will be changed by knowing its name.

The verse is not disparaging the survivors. It is noting that the record of the world is written in refusals, and that a community which does not refuse appears in that record only as a date between two disasters. The sūrah itself is part of that record, and it devotes its length to the refusals for the same reason: they are the only parts of the story that carry a lesson.

**What the Reader Has to Supply**

Everything that makes the verse meaningful is absent from it, and the reader supplies it from what he has just read. A generation is raised, and a reader who has followed the sūrah so far knows what follows a raising: a messenger comes, the chiefs speak, the people refuse, and the term arrives.

The sūrah does not narrate that again, because it does not need to. It supplies the first clause and lets the pattern supply the rest, which is a way of handing the pattern to the reader rather than continuing to spell it out. A reader who can complete the sentence has learned the thing the narratives were teaching.

The next verse makes the handover explicit by stating the rule that governs the interval, and the two verses together form a single unit: a people is raised, and the time given to it is fixed at both ends. Between the raising and the rule, the reader's own knowledge of the pattern does the work that a third full narrative would otherwise have to do.

**The Pattern Is Now the Reader's**

There is a further consequence of the compression, and it applies to the sūrah's first audience directly. The pattern has been demonstrated twice in full, and the audience is living inside a third instance of it.

They have a messenger. They have chiefs who have already spoken. They have an interval whose length they cannot know. The verse tells them that a generation was raised, and they are that generation — which means the account is no longer something being told to them about other people.

The sūrah has moved from narrative to address without changing its register, and the shift is possible only because the pattern has been established twice before. A reader who has not read the earlier verses cannot make the move; a reader who has cannot avoid it. The verse is short because the argument it makes has already been paid for.

**Given the Scripture After the Earlier Nations**

The succession described here has a further stage in it, and the sūrah names it shortly afterward. After the destroyed communities and the raised generations comes a people given a Book, and the giving is described with a purpose attached.

That arrangement places the Scripture inside the same sequence as the punishments. It is not a separate dispensation arriving from outside the pattern; it is the next item in it, given at a particular point to a particular people inside an interval that has already been described as fixed.

The verse establishes the mechanism by which one people follows another, and the verses that follow show what the new people were given. A reader who has followed the sūrah this far can predict the shape of what comes next, and the prediction is the measure of how completely the pattern has been installed.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:43

> No people can advance their doom, nor can they delay it

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ajal: The Term Fixed at Both Ends**

The word is *ajal*, an appointed term, and the verse states that it cannot be moved in either direction. The two negatives are not a rhetorical doubling. Each excludes something different, and each excludes something a community in that position would plausibly attempt.

Advancing the term is what a people does when it wants the matter settled — when it calls for the punishment to be brought, as this sūrah's own opponents do elsewhere in the Qur'an. Delaying it is what a people does when it wants more time, and the desire for more time is the more common of the two.

The verse closes both exits in a single sentence, and the symmetry of the construction makes the closure unmistakable. There is no version of the interval in which the people inside it control its length, and there is no negotiation available at either end of it. The sentence is short enough to be memorised and general enough to apply to any reader at any time.

**The Interval Is the Only Variable**

If the term cannot be shortened or lengthened, then everything a person or a people can affect lies inside it. The verse, read strictly, reduces the field of human agency to a single quantity: what is done with the time already allotted.

That is why the sūrah spends so much of its length on speeches. The words of the chiefs are recorded at length because words are what the interval is made of. Nothing they said could move their term by an hour; everything they said determined what the term would be worth at its end.

The verse is a statement about limits, and the narrative surrounding it is a demonstration of what remains once the limits are accepted. What remains is not nothing, but it is confined entirely to conduct, and the sūrah's insistence on recording conduct — what the chiefs said, how the messengers answered, what the people chose — follows directly from that confinement.

**A Rule, Not a Threat**

The verse is not addressed to anyone in particular and contains no imperative. It states a rule of the arrangement, in the same register as a law of nature, and it states it immediately after the raising of the new generations.

That placement gives it its force. The generations just described have been given their term, and the verse tells the reader what is true of that term before describing what the generations did with it. The rule is therefore not a warning issued at the moment of refusal; it is a condition of the situation the refusal happens inside.

The distinction matters for how the verse should be read. The people who denied their messengers were not threatened with the fixed term and then broke it. They were living inside it the whole time, and the only thing that changed over the course of their story was what they had done with it.

**The Same Statement Elsewhere**

The Qur'an states the rule in other places, and the statements agree. Al-Aʿrāf: *"And every nation has ˹its˺ ordained term. So when their term arrives, they cannot delay it for a moment, nor can they advance it."* (7:34). The wording is nearly identical, and the context is the same — the succession of communities and the messengers sent to them.

The parallel establishes that this is a fixed formulation rather than a local observation about one destroyed people. A rule stated twice in the same words, in the same context, in two different sūrahs, is being presented as part of the structure of things rather than as a comment on a particular event.

Al-Anfāl adds the historical dimension, describing the nations destroyed before as destroyed when their term came, and stating that God does not withdraw a favour from a people until they change what is in themselves (8:53). Between them the verses describe a term that is fixed and a conduct that is not, which is precisely the distinction this verse draws.

**Communal and Individual**

The commentators note that the term here is the term of a community, and that individual lifespans are described elsewhere in similar language. The two are not the same thing, and the verse is about the first of them.

A community's term is the period during which its messenger's call stands open. When it ends, the community is dealt with as a community, and the sūrah has just described four such endings in sequence.

The verse is therefore making a claim about collective history rather than about individual death, and the claim is that the period in which a people can respond has a length the people cannot alter. What it does inside that period is the whole of what is left to it, and the sūrah's four narratives are four demonstrations of that remainder being spent badly.

**The Appointed Term in Other Places**

Yūnus states the rule in the context of a challenge: *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Have you considered: if His torment were to overtake you by night or by day, what ˹part˺ of it would the wicked be ˹eager˺ to hasten?'"* (10:50). The question assumes that the hastening is something people ask for, and the answer is that the asking changes nothing.

That verse belongs beside this one because it supplies the motive the rule excludes. A community that wants the matter resolved will ask for the punishment to come, and a community that wants more time will ask for delay. Both requests are addressed by the same sentence, and both are refused by it.

The verse in al-Muʾminūn states the rule flatly, without the question, and its flatness is what makes it apply to every reader rather than only to the people who happen to be asking. It is the sūrah's clearest statement of the frame within which all of its narratives take place.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:44

> Then We sent Our messengers in succession: whenever a messenger came to his people, they denied him. So We destroyed them, one after the other, reducing them to ˹cautionary˺ tales. So away with the people who refuse to believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tatrā: One After Another, With Space Between**

The word for in succession is *tatrā*, and it carries an interval as well as an order. The messengers did not come as a crowd or in a single generation; they came spaced out, each to his own people, with time between one and the next.

That spacing is part of the sūrah's answer to the complaint that its audience had never heard such a thing. The message was not new. It was old, and it had been delivered repeatedly at intervals long enough for each generation to forget the last one and to suppose that its own situation was unprecedented.

The intervals are what make forgetting possible, and the sūrah's own habit of retelling the stories is what exists to close the gap. A reader who meets the same account three times in one sūrah is not being told something new; he is being prevented from doing the thing the intervals permit, which is to treat a warning as belonging to somebody else's century.

**Aḥādīth: The Word for Stories**

The verse says they were reduced to *aḥādīth*, and the word means accounts, narratives, things that are told. A destroyed people becomes material for a story, and the story is told by people who were not there.

The choice of word is exact and unforgiving. A community that becomes *aḥādīth* has ceased to be a people and has become content. Its decisions, its chiefs, its arguments and its interval have all been converted into a narrative that other people recount, usually as a warning and usually in a sentence or two.

The verse states this as the outcome of the destruction rather than as a separate consequence. What is left after the punishment is not a ruin to visit but a story to repeat, and the sūrah itself is one of the places the repetition happens. The reader holding this verse is holding one of the *aḥādīth*.

There is a second edge to the word. A story is told by the survivors, and the survivors are the people who came after — the ones the previous verse described as raised. The destroyed community does not tell its own account, and the account it gets is shaped by the fact that it is not there to give one.

**A Sentence That Summarises the Whole Book**

The verse is, in effect, a summary of the Qur'an's narrative material. Most of the sūrahs that tell stories tell this story: a messenger, a people, a refusal, an outcome. The verse states the pattern once, in a single sentence, and the rest of the Book fills it in.

That is why the compression is not a shortcut but a thesis. A reader who holds this verse in mind can read any prophetic narrative and know where he is inside it, which means he can also recognise the stage his own community has reached.

The sūrah's audience had a messenger among them and a set of objections already on record. The verse tells them that the sequence they are standing inside has been completed many times before, and that its last two steps are not optional. The pattern is described in the past tense, and the audience is inside it in the present.

**"Whenever a Messenger Came, They Denied Him"**

The construction is generalising: *kullamā*, whenever. Not some of them, not most of them — whenever. The verse states the rule without exception, and the exceptionlessness is doing argumentative work.

An account in which some peoples accepted would leave the reader with a question about what made the difference. An account in which the refusal is universal removes that question and replaces it with a different one, which is the one the sūrah actually wants answered: not why some peoples believed, but what the refusal consists in.

The following verses supply the answer in the form of speeches, and the speeches are remarkably consistent across centuries — always about the messenger's humanity, his social standing, or his sanity. The consistency is the evidence for the rule, and the rule is what makes the sūrah's repetition of the narratives necessary rather than merely decorative.

It also removes the most comfortable explanation available to the audience. A refusal caused by a particular messenger's manner, or by a particular people's history, could be treated as a local accident. A refusal that takes the same three forms across four unrelated communities cannot.

**The Third Exclamation**

The verse closes with the formula the sūrah has now used three times: away with the people who refuse to believe. Each occurrence marks the end of a cycle, and the three together form a refrain that the reader learns to expect.

The third occurrence names the people differently from the first two. The earlier ones named wrongdoing and disbelief; this one names the refusal to believe, using a verb of active rejection rather than a state of being.

The shift is small but real, and it fits the verse it closes — a verse about a pattern repeated across many peoples, where what recurs is not a condition but an act performed by each generation in turn. The refrain has moved from describing what the people were to describing what the people did.

That movement matters for the reader standing outside the account. Wrongdoing is something a person is; refusal is something a person does, and doing can be stopped. The third exclamation is the harshest of the three, and it is also the one that leaves the most open.

**The Example in Those Destroyed Before**

Al-Anfāl draws the same lesson from the same material, describing the destruction of earlier peoples as the precedent for what follows, and stating that God would not punish a people while its messenger is among them (8:33). The verses go on to describe the Sacred House and those who hinder others from it.

The parallel supplies what this verse leaves as pattern. The destructions are not merely a sequence of events; they are an example, and an example is something a reader is meant to apply rather than merely note.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn states the pattern and moves on to its next instance, and the reader is left holding a rule about communities that has been demonstrated four times and is currently being demonstrated a fifth. The fifth demonstration is not described in the past tense.

Al-Anfāl adds the condition that suspends it: the punishment does not fall while the messenger is still among them. The interval is therefore not merely fixed in length but protected in a specific way, and the protection ends when the messenger goes.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:45

> Then We sent Moses and his brother Aaron with Our signs and compelling proof

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Brothers Sent Together**

The verse names two messengers, and the naming of the second is the first thing to notice. Aaron is described as his brother, which establishes the relation before the mission, and the mission is given to both of them together rather than to one with the other attached as support.

The sūrah has so far described messengers sent singly, each to his own people. This verse changes the pattern, and the change is not incidental to the account that follows it.

A single messenger can be dismissed as an eccentric or a man with a grievance. Two men who agree with each other cannot be dismissed that way, and the objection recorded in the next verses takes a different form as a result. The brothers are named, and the relationship between them is stated, because the objection the court raises is precisely about who they are and where they come from.

**Two Men Against a State**

The scale of the mission deserves to be stated plainly. Two individuals, with no army, no institution and no standing of any kind, sent to the most powerful regime the audience knew of, whose ruler claimed divinity in the plainest terms available to him.

The sūrah does not soften the disproportion. It records the signs and the compelling proof, and then records the reply in the verses that follow — a question about whether the men are slaves. The contrast between what the two carried and what they were answered with is the whole content of the exchange.

Nothing in the account suggests the mission was expected to succeed on its own terms. It was expected to be delivered, and the delivery is what the verse describes. The verse ends at the point of delivery, before any response has been given, and the ending is exact.

The sūrah's first audience would have recognised the shape immediately. Their messenger had no army either, and the objection he faced was the same one — a man like us, eating what we eat. The disproportion is not an argument against the mission; it is what the mission has always looked like.

**Āyāt and Sulṭān Mubīn: Two Things, Not One**

The verse distinguishes between the signs and the compelling proof, and the distinction should be held on to. *Āyāt* are the signs — the things shown. *Sulṭān mubīn* is a clear authority, and the word *sulṭān* carries the sense of a warrant, a proof that establishes a claim.

The two together describe the messengers' equipment completely. The signs are what can be seen; the authority is what makes the seeing mean something.

A sign without authority is a marvel, and marvels can be explained away — which is exactly what the court attempts in the verses that follow, by treating the men as magicians performing craft. The verse gives the messengers both, and the pairing is the answer in advance to that charge.

**The Manner of the Address Was Specified Too**

The mission is described here by what the two men carried; Ṭā-Hā describes it by how they were told to speak. *"Go, both of you, to Pharaoh, for he has truly transgressed ˹all bounds˺. Speak to him gently, so perhaps he may be mindful ˹of Me˺ or fearful ˹of My punishment˺."* (20:43-44).

The instruction is worth holding beside the reply it received. Two men are sent with unmistakable signs and told to be gentle with a ruler who has already exceeded every limit, and the answer they get is a question about whether they are slaves.

The sūrah does not comment on the contrast between the gentleness commanded and the contempt returned. It reports the instruction in one place and the reply in another, and the distance between them is left for the reader to measure.

The gentleness is also a claim about the mission's purpose. Two men told to speak gently to a tyrant are not being sent to defeat him; they are being sent to give him an opportunity he can still take. The verse that follows records what he did with it.

**Nine Clear Signs**

The number is given in al-Isrāʾ: *"We surely gave Moses nine clear signs. ˹You, O Prophet, can˺ ask the Children of Israel. When Moses came to them, Pharaoh said to him, 'I really think that you, O Moses, are bewitched.'"* (17:101). The nine are listed across several sūrahs — the staff, the hand, the years of shortage, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, and the parting of the sea.

Al-Aʿrāf records the sending itself: *"Then after them We sent Moses with Our signs to Pharaoh and his chiefs, but they wrongfully rejected them. See what was the end of the corruptors!"* (7:103). The verse names the chiefs as addressees alongside Pharaoh, which is the same arrangement this sūrah gives in the verse that follows.

The enumeration matters for this verse because of what it excludes. The signs are specific, physical and repeatable, and they were performed in front of people who could have examined them.

The charge of bewitchment in 17:101 is not a claim that the events did not happen; it is a claim about their cause, made by a man who had every opportunity to establish the cause and did not. The word *sulṭān* in this verse is what answers that charge — a warrant, not a trick.

The distinction between a sign and a warrant is the distinction the court refused to make. A marvel can be attributed to craft; a warrant cannot, because a warrant is a claim about who sent the man, and that claim is either established or it is not.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse records the sending and the equipment, and stops. It does not say that Pharaoh believed, that the people followed, or that the mission succeeded in the ordinary sense of winning converts.

That economy is characteristic of the sūrah's treatment of the messengers. The sending is described; the refusal is described; the outcome is described; and the messenger's success or failure in persuasion is left aside, because it is not the measure the sūrah uses.

A messenger is evaluated by whether he delivered, and the verse establishes the delivery completely — two men, signs, compelling proof — before any of the objections have been raised. The objections come next, and they change nothing about what has already been established here.

That is the reason the verse stops where it does. Had it continued into the confrontation, the reader's attention would have moved to the outcome, and the outcome is not what the sūrah is measuring. The sending is complete in itself, and the verse treats it that way.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:46

> to Pharaoh and his chiefs, but they behaved arrogantly and were a tyrannical people

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chiefs, Not Only the King**

The verse names two addressees, and the second is the significant one. The mission went to Pharaoh and to his *malaʾ*, his chiefs — the council of notables around him, the men whose standing depended on the arrangement that was already in place.

The sūrah has used the same word for the chiefs of every previous community, and its use here is precise. A king alone can be persuaded, outmanoeuvred or overthrown. A council cannot, because its members have a collective interest in the arrangement that made them a council, and that interest is stronger than any individual conviction among them.

The verse identifies the actual site of the refusal. Pharaoh's claim to divinity is recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an, but it is the chiefs who speak in the next verse, and it is their argument — not the king's — that the sūrah quotes at length.

**Istakbarū: Arrogance as a Refusal to Look**

The verb is *istakbarū*, and it describes a specific behaviour rather than a general attitude. The root is k-b-r, greatness; the form means to make oneself great, to hold oneself above. The verse says the chiefs behaved arrogantly, and it does not say that they examined the signs and disagreed with them.

The distinction is the point. Arrogance in this vocabulary is a refusal to submit to an examination one might lose. It is a decision made before the evidence is weighed, and it has the advantage, for the person who makes it, that it never afterwards has to be defended.

The sūrah identifies it early in the account because it explains everything that follows. It explains why the signs are never discussed, why the objection that comes next is about status rather than evidence, and why the exchange ends in a diagnosis rather than a conclusion.

**ʿĀlūn: A People Who Exceed**

The second description is from a root meaning to rise above or to exceed, and it is used of people who are high-handed — who transgress against those beneath them. The verse pairs the arrogance with the tyranny, and the pairing is not a rhetorical doubling.

Arrogance describes the chiefs' posture toward the messengers. Tyranny describes their conduct toward everyone else. The two are the same disposition seen from two directions, and the sūrah gives both because the next verse depends on the second of them.

The objection the chiefs raise is about ownership of a population, and an objection of that kind is only available to people who have already been described as exceeding against those under them. The verse supplies the character before the speech, so that the speech needs no explanation when it arrives.

The pairing also closes off a charitable reading. Arrogance alone might be a manner; tyranny alone might be a policy. Together they describe a group whose posture toward the truth and whose conduct toward the weak come from the same place, which is why neither can be corrected without the other.

**A Moral Description Before a Quotation**

The order of the two verses should be noticed, because the sūrah could have arranged them the other way round. It could have quoted the chiefs and then described them, letting the reader judge the character from the words.

It does the opposite. The reader is told that the men behaved arrogantly and were tyrannical, and then he hears what they said. The effect is that the speech arrives already interpreted.

A reader who meets the objection cold might find it forceful. It is, after all, an observation about social fact — the messengers do belong to a subject population. Read after the description, the same observation is recognisable as what it is: a man's property invoked as a reason to dismiss him. The sūrah's arrangement is an argument in itself.

The method is used throughout the sūrah. The chiefs are never rebutted point by point; they are characterised first and quoted second. A reader who wants to evaluate the objection on its own terms has already been told what kind of men made it, and that information is part of the evidence.

**The Population Underneath**

The tyranny named in the verse had people underneath it, and the next verse names them in the chiefs' own words: their people are slaves to us. The two verses belong together as a single diagnosis of the regime.

A group that takes greatness to itself and exceeds against those beneath it will answer a messenger from the oppressed group with an objection about ownership. That is not a coincidence of history but a structure, and the sūrah has arranged its material to display the structure rather than merely to report it.

The verse describes the regime in two words; the following verse lets the regime describe itself in one sentence; and the reader who reads them in that order understands the second before it is spoken.

The arrangement also protects the messengers from the reading the chiefs intended. An objection about slavery works only if the reader accepts that ownership settles standing. The verse has already described the owners as people who exceed, and after that description the objection reads as a confession rather than an argument.

**Pharaoh's Command Followed**

The sūrah of al-Qaṣaṣ describes the same regime from the other side: *"Indeed, Pharaoh ˹arrogantly˺ elevated himself in the land and divided its people into ˹subservient˺ groups, one of which he persecuted, slaughtering their sons and keeping their women. He was truly one of the corruptors."* (28:4).

The division into graded groups is the mechanism of the tyranny this verse names, and the group singled out for persecution is the one Mūsā belongs to. The parallel supplies the social detail that this verse compresses into a single adjective.

The chiefs are not merely arrogant men; they are the beneficiaries of a system of graded oppression, and their objection in the next verse is a statement of their place inside that system. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives the character in a phrase and the argument in a sentence, and al-Qaṣaṣ fills in the arrangement that produced both.

The detail about the sons and the women matters for the objection that follows. A regime that kills the male children of one group has already treated that group as property, and the chiefs' question about slaves is not a new claim but a restatement of an arrangement already in force.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:47

> They argued, “Will we believe in two humans, like ourselves, whose people are slaves to us?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Does Not Expect an Answer**

The objection is framed as an interrogative — shall we believe? — and the form suggests a question while the content is not one. Nothing in the sentence could be answered by information, because nothing in it asks for information.

The rhetorical question is a device for closing a discussion in front of an audience. It invites the hearers to supply the obvious answer, and in supplying it they adopt the position without having examined it. The sūrah records the form because the form is the mechanism.

The chiefs are not persuading the crowd with reasons; they are recruiting it with a question that has only one socially available answer. A person in that hall who wanted to dissent would first have to answer the question, and answering it means engaging with a premise about slaves that the question has already settled.

**Two Humans, Like Ourselves**

The first half of the objection is the one the sūrah has already recorded twice. Nūḥ's chiefs said he wanted to be superior to them. The next generation said that obeying a human like themselves would make them losers.

The argument from the messenger's humanity recurs in every account, and it recurs in almost the same words. The recurrence is the sūrah's clearest statement about what the objection actually is.

It is not an argument about evidence, because it never engages with any. It is a claim about the messenger's category: a man who eats, works and stands in the market cannot be carrying a message. The objection is available to any audience at any period, which is why it appears in every account, and its availability is precisely what makes it useless as a reason.

The sūrah's own audience had made the same objection, and the verse records it here in the mouth of Pharaoh's court so that the reader meets it at its bluntest before he meets it in his own. Distance makes the shape visible.

**Whose People Are Slaves to Us**

The second half is new, and it is the bluntest statement in the sūrah. The chiefs do not dispute the messengers' sincerity, their evidence or their conduct. They state that the messengers' people belong to them.

The word for slaves here is *ʿabīd*, and the claim is one of ownership rather than of rank. It is the same objection in an unmuffled form: we own them, and therefore what they say has no standing before us.

The sūrah has recorded the argument from humanity twice before, each time with a layer of disguise — superiority first, then loss. This version drops the last layer and states the fact the earlier versions rested on. By the third account there is nothing left to answer, only something to recognise.

The progression across the three accounts is the sūrah's clearest demonstration of its method. It does not summarise the objection once and apply it three times. It records three versions, each blunter than the last, and lets the reader watch the disguise come off.

**Ownership Invoked as an Argument**

The reasoning deserves to be set out, because the verse does not set it out and the reader supplies it automatically. The chiefs' premise is that authority derives from position.

A man whose people are property cannot carry a message to the men who own them, because the direction of authority runs the other way. The premise is not stated as a premise. It is embedded in the question, which is what makes the question effective.

Nobody in the hall would have articulated it, and everybody would have assented to it. The sūrah's method throughout is to record the speeches of the chiefs without commenting on them, and the recording is sufficient: an argument that cannot be stated without collapsing does not need to be refuted, only quoted.

That is why the verse gives the objection in full and adds nothing to it. A commentator's gloss would invite the reader to consider the reasoning. The bare quotation invites him to notice that there is no reasoning to consider — only a fact about ownership, offered as though it settled a question about truth.

**The Charge in the Mouth of Pharaoh Himself**

Ash-Shuʿarāʾ records the same exchange with Pharaoh's own words and at greater length. Mūsā states that he is a messenger from the Lord of all worlds; Pharaoh turns to those around him and says, *"Did you hear ˹what he said˺?"* (26:25). Mūsā adds that He is their Lord and the Lord of their forefathers, and Pharaoh answers: *"Your messenger, who has been sent to you, must be insane."* (26:27).

The sequence shows the two objections in their working order. Pharaoh's first move is not to answer but to appeal to his audience — did you hear that? The second is the charge of insanity.

Neither move engages with what Mūsā said, and neither requires the signs to be examined. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives the ownership charge in a single sentence and reserves the madness charge for its Meccan section later, where the same pair appears in the same order.

**What God Did With the Oppressed**

The sūrah answers the objection not by argument but by the outcome it has already recorded. The people the chiefs described as their property were the people God delivered, and the deliverance follows immediately in the account.

Al-Qaṣaṣ states the principle in general terms: *"But it was Our Will to favour those who were oppressed in the land, making them models ˹of faith˺ as well as successors;"* (28:5).

The verse is a direct reversal of the chiefs' premise. They argued from who owns whom, and the answer is that the ownership they invoked was itself the thing being overturned. The sūrah does not refute the argument; it records what happened to the men who made it.

That method is worth naming, because it is how the sūrah answers every objection in this section. No speech is rebutted in its own terms. Each is followed by an outcome, and the outcome is the reply. The reader is given the argument and the result side by side, and asked to weigh them against each other.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:48

> So they rejected them both, and ˹so˺ were among those destroyed

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Rejection Recorded in the Dual**

The verb is dual: *kadhdhabūhumā*, they rejected the two of them. The sūrah has been careful with number throughout this account — two messengers, two brothers, one objection — and the dual form in the rejection closes the pattern.

The grammatical detail carries a substantive point. The chiefs' objection was about status, and it was raised against both men together. Neither Mūsā nor Hārūn was rejected separately or for a different reason.

The court dismissed them as a pair, on grounds that applied equally to both. The dual form records that fact precisely, and in doing so it confirms what the objection was: not a dispute about evidence, which would have differentiated between the two men, but a refusal of the category they both belonged to.

The dual also answers the objection by its own logic. Two men who agree are harder to dismiss than one, and the court's response was to dismiss them together rather than separately — which is what a refusal of the category looks like from the outside.

**The Punishment Named in a Participle**

The verse does not narrate the drowning. It uses a verbal form meaning they became, or they were, among the destroyed — a state rather than an event, expressed in a single word.

The compression is deliberate, and the audience explains it. The drowning of Pharaoh's court was the most familiar judgement in the scriptural tradition the Meccans were arguing about, and the sūrah does not need to retell it to a room that already knew it.

A punishment described at length is a spectacle. A punishment named in a participle is a fact. The verse puts the destruction of the greatest power of the age into the same grammatical form as every other entry in the sūrah's list of destroyed communities, and the flattening is the point: what remains worth remembering is the refusal, not the mechanism.

**Why the Sūrah Hurries Past the Greatest Story**

The choice to compress this narrative is the sūrah's clearest statement about what it is for. Al-Aʿrāf, Hūd, Ṭā-Hā, Yūnus, ash-Shuʿarāʾ and al-Qaṣaṣ all tell the story of Mūsā at length. This sūrah gives it four verses and moves on.

The reason is that this sūrah is building an argument about the messengers as a class rather than about Mūsā in particular. Length would have made the account stand apart from the others; compression brings it into line with them.

A reader who wants the details of the confrontation, the magicians, the plagues and the crossing can find them in six other places. What he gets here is the item in the series, and the series — a messenger, a refusal, a destruction — is the argument the sūrah is making.

The compression is also a claim about the audience. A room that already knows the story does not need it retold; it needs to be shown where the story sits in a pattern it has not been looking at. The sūrah gives the pattern and omits the story.

**"They Rejected" and the Three Earlier Refusals**

The verb is the same one the sūrah used in its summary verse, where every messenger's people denied him. The account of Mūsā is not an exception to that rule; it is an instance of it, and the verse places it inside the rule by using the rule's own verb.

That placement answers an objection the audience might have raised. Pharaoh's regime was greater than Nūḥ's village and better documented than the communities between, and a listener might have supposed that the difference in scale made the case different.

The verse removes the supposition in a single clause. The greatest power of the age is entered in the same list, with the same verb, and the entry is no longer than any other. Scale is not a variable in the sūrah's account of refusals.

That is the sharpest thing the verse does. A listener who had been impressed by the size of Pharaoh's regime — and the Qur'an elsewhere describes its wealth, its buildings and its reach — is told here that the size changed nothing about the shape of what happened.

**The Objection and the Outcome, Side by Side**

The arrangement of the four verses should be seen as a whole. Two men are sent with signs and a compelling proof. The chiefs behave arrogantly and are tyrannical. They ask whether they should believe in two humans whose people are their slaves. Then the verse records the rejection and the destruction in one breath.

Nothing intervenes. There is no debate, no examination of the signs, no attempt by the court to establish what the staff or the hand actually were. The sūrah moves from the objection to the outcome with no intervening argument, because there was none.

The speed of the transition is a description of the exchange, and it is the sūrah's answer to anyone who supposes that the chiefs had reasons they simply failed to state. The absence of a middle section is the middle section.

**Each Seized for Its Sin**

The sūrah's account of the destroyed communities ends with a statement about individual accountability, and the statement belongs beside this verse: every one of them was seized for its own sin.

That principle limits what the verse is saying. Pharaoh's court was destroyed because of what it did, not because of which regime it belonged to or which century it occupied.

The sūrah has now described four destructions with four different mechanisms, and the common element is not the mechanism but the refusal. The verse closes the fourth and longest of the four accounts in the shortest possible form, which is the sūrah's way of saying that the length of a narrative is not a measure of its importance.

The principle also cuts the other way, and the sūrah means it to. If each community is seized for its own sin, then the reader's own community will be judged on what it did and not on which story it resembles. The four narratives are precedents, and a precedent binds.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:49

> And We certainly gave Moses the Scripture, so perhaps his people would be ˹rightly˺ guided

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scripture Given After the Rescue**

The verse places the giving of the Scripture after the destruction of the court, and the sequence is the whole of its argument. The Book comes to a people who have already been delivered, not to a people still under the regime that oppressed them.

That ordering is not incidental. A community in slavery cannot be given a law to live by; it can only be given instructions for surviving under somebody else's rule. The Scripture arrives once the people are free to act on it, and the timing states a principle about what law presupposes.

The chiefs' objection in the earlier verses rested on ownership — these men's people are slaves to us — and the verse answers it by showing what the subject population became. A people described as property is described here as the recipients of a Book, and the two descriptions cannot both be final.

**Laʿallahum Yahtadūn: A Purpose That Can Fail**

The purpose clause deserves close attention, because it is qualified in a way that statements about revelation usually are not. *Laʿallahum yahtadūn* — so perhaps they would be guided. The particle carries expectation rather than certainty, and the sūrah uses it knowing what follows.

The qualification is not doubt about the Book. It is an accurate description of what happens when a Book is given to a community: some receive it and some do not, and the giving does not determine the receiving.

Most statements about revelation elsewhere describe it as guidance, light or mercy without a condition attached. This one attaches a condition, and the condition sits on the recipients' side of the transaction. A reader told that the Scripture was given so that perhaps they would be guided has not been given a guarantee; he has been told the outcome is still open.

**Kitāb: The Scripture as a Single Object**

The word is *al-kitāb*, the Scripture, with the definite article, and the sūrah treats it as one thing given at one time to one people. The Torah is not described here in terms of its contents, its laws or its history; it is described as a gift with a purpose.

That treatment matters for the verse's position in the sūrah. The account has moved from destruction to provision, and the provision is a Book rather than a territory or a victory.

The people delivered from the court are given something to live by, and the sūrah names it in the same vocabulary it used earlier for the provision of rain and gardens. What is given is described by what it is for, and what it is for is guidance — which is the same thing the messengers in the earlier accounts were sent for.

The parallel between a Book and a garden is not a loose one. Both are provisions; both require a recipient who can use them; and both can be wasted by people who have them and do not. The sūrah's later verses describe exactly that waste.

**A Gift to a Free People**

The connection between the deliverance and the Scripture is stated nowhere in the verse, and it does not need to be. The order of the verses supplies it, and the order is an argument.

The sūrah has spent its middle section on communities that refused their messengers, and each account ends with a destruction. This verse breaks the sequence in a specific way: it describes what was given rather than what was taken away.

The people of Mūsā are the first community in the sūrah whose story does not end in a punishment, and the difference is marked by the arrival of a Book. The reader is meant to notice the break in the pattern, and to notice what caused it.

The break is not presented as a reward for the deliverance. The deliverance came first, and the Book came after, to a people who had done nothing yet to earn either. The sūrah's sequence is provision, then obligation, and the obligation is the guidance the purpose clause names.

**The Same Purpose Elsewhere**

The Qur'an states the purpose of revelation in similar terms in several places, and the formulations vary in their confidence. Al-Baqarah describes the Book as guidance for the mindful (2:2), without a condition. Al-Aʿrāf says it was revealed so that the Prophet might warn by it (7:2), describing a function rather than an outcome.

This verse's formulation is the least assured of the three, and it is the most accurate to what the sūrah has just been describing. A reader who has read four accounts of refused messengers is in no position to accept an unqualified statement that revelation produces guidance.

The verse gives him the qualified one, and the qualification is what makes the earlier accounts intelligible rather than inexplicable.

The same qualification protects the reader from a second error. A person who believes that revelation guarantees guidance will read the history of his own community as evidence against the Book. A person who understands the purpose clause reads the same history as the thing the clause predicted.

**Given After the Earlier Nations Were Destroyed**

The verse belongs to the sequence the sūrah established two verses earlier: generations raised after generations destroyed. The Scripture arrives inside that sequence, at a specific point in it, to a specific people.

That placement is a claim about the nature of the gift. The Torah is not presented as a timeless object dropped into history from outside; it is presented as something given at a moment, to a people who had just been made capable of receiving it, for a purpose that might or might not be fulfilled.

The sūrah's treatment of revelation throughout is of this kind — concrete, dated and addressed — and this verse is its clearest instance. The reader is told when the Book came, to whom, and what it was for, and nothing in the account suggests that those details are incidental to what the Book is.

The same concreteness applies to the Book the sūrah's audience has just been given. It too arrived at a moment, to a particular people, for a purpose that may or may not be fulfilled, and the sūrah has spent its middle section showing what happens to the ones who do not.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:50

> And We made the son of Mary and his mother a sign, and gave them refuge on high ground—a ˹suitable˺ place for rest with flowing water

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Sign Standing for Two People**

The word for sign is singular — *āyah* — and it covers both of them. The son of Mary and his mother are made one sign together, not two signs separately, and the singular form is the verse's most consequential feature.

The commentators note the point directly: a single sign describing two people. The arrangement refuses to separate them, and it refuses to make either of them the sign on his or her own.

What is signified is the pair, and the pairing is what carries the meaning — a birth without a father, and a mother who carried it. The sūrah has used the same word for the signs given to Mūsā, where each sign was a separate event; here it gathers two human beings into one.

The gathering is the sign's content. Neither person taken alone would carry the same meaning, and the verse does not describe a miracle performed on one of them. It describes two people whose situation together is the thing being pointed at.

**Maryam Named, and Nobody Else**

The verse names the mother and identifies the son by her. There is no reference to a father, no mention of the household, and no naming of any other person in the account.

Sūrah Maryam gives the fuller narrative, and it too keeps the focus on her: the withdrawal, the announcement, the birth under the palm, and the return with the child in her arms. Neither account introduces a human father into the story at any point.

That economy is a choice. The Gospels name Joseph at length and give him a genealogy; this verse names one person and relates the other to her. The sūrah's account of the birth is complete without any other human agent, and the completeness is the point being made.

The naming of the mother also does something the alternative would not. A son identified by his mother is a son whose origin is stated rather than assumed, and the sūrah's audience knew why that identification was contested. The verse states it plainly and moves on.

**Rabwah: High Ground, and Why the Height Matters**

The refuge is a *rabwah*, an elevated place, and the verse adds two descriptions of it: a place suitable for settling, and one with flowing water. The commentators differ over where it is — some say Egypt, some Jerusalem, some a hill near a river — and the verse does not settle the question.

The height is doing specific work. A raised place is defensible, visible and separated from what surrounds it, and the verse uses it for a mother who needed all three properties at once.

The flowing water supplies the second requirement, and the two together describe a refuge that is both safe and habitable. The sūrah has used the same vocabulary earlier for the provision of water and settled places, and its use here places the refuge inside the same order as the gardens and the rain described in the sūrah's opening section.

**Maʿīn: Water That Flows**

The word for flowing is *maʿīn*, from a root meaning water that is visible and running — water one can see moving, as opposed to water drawn from a well or collected in a cistern. The verse specifies it, and the specification is not decorative.

Settled ground with visible running water describes a place where a person can live indefinitely without depending on anyone. That is what a refuge for a woman in her position requires, and the verse supplies it in two adjectives.

The sūrah's account of provision elsewhere is equally concrete: rain in due measure, gardens on hillsides, livestock with specific uses. The refuge is described in the same register, and the concreteness is what makes it a provision rather than a symbol.

That register is deliberate. A reader who is told about flowing water is being told about survival, not about a state of mind. The sūrah consistently describes God's care for people in the language of what they need in order to go on living.

**Provision Given at the Point of Rejection**

The verse's structure should be read against the story it summarises. Maryam was accused by her own people, and the verse describes her being given a place and water by God. The rejection and the provision are adjacent in the account, and the provision follows the rejection.

The arrangement answers a question the sūrah does not raise: what happens to a person whose community turns against him for a reason he cannot answer. The verse describes no vindication in front of the accusers and no change of heart among them.

It describes shelter. The provision is material, immediate and sufficient, and it comes from the one party in the story who is not confused about what happened.

The verse makes no promise that the accusers will be answered. It offers something narrower and more useful: that a person rejected for a reason he cannot explain is not therefore abandoned, and that the provision he needs will be arranged without his having to win the argument first.

**Mary Withdraws to a Remote Place**

Sūrah Maryam gives the sequence in full: *"So she conceived him, then withdrew with him to a remote place."* (19:22). The withdrawal comes before the birth, and the sūrah describes the labour, the palm tree, the stream beneath it, and the instruction to eat and drink and be at ease.

The parallel supplies the detail this verse compresses into two adjectives. The remote place of Maryam is the high ground of al-Muʾminūn; the stream beneath the palm is the flowing water.

Between the two sūrahs the refuge is described twice, in the same terms, and neither account mentions anyone who provided it except God. The verse in al-Muʾminūn states the provision in a single clause and calls the whole of it a sign, which is the largest claim the sūrah makes about the pair.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:51

> O messengers! Eat from what is good and lawful, and act righteously. Indeed, I fully know what you do

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Address in the Plural to Men Who Never Met**

The verse opens with a vocative plural — *yā ayyuhā ar-rusul*, O messengers — and the messengers of this sūrah lived in different centuries and never spoke to one another. The exegetes resolve this two ways. One reading holds that every messenger was given this instruction in his own time, and the plural gathers them. The other holds that the address is to the Prophet in the plural form, signalling that what he is being told is common to all of them.

Both readings produce the same result for the reader, and the difference between them is about the mechanics of revelation rather than the content. What the verse establishes is that the instruction is not local. It is not advice for a particular situation in seventh-century Arabia; it is the standing commission of every messenger, stated once and addressed to all of them together.

The address also flattens the line. No messenger is singled out as greater or lesser, and none is exempted from either command. The verse speaks to the whole company in one breath.

**Ṭayyibāt: The Good Things**

The word for what they are told to eat is *ṭayyibāt*, the plural of *ṭayyib* — good, wholesome, agreeable, clean. The Qur'an uses the word especially for provisions, and it uses it in commands: *"O believers! Eat from the good things We have provided for you. And give thanks to Allah if you ˹truly˺ worship Him ˹alone˺."* (2:172).

The word is not a legal category here. It is a description of what has been given, and the command is to take it. Al-Māʾidah answers a question about what is permitted with the same term: *"Say, 'What is good and lawful.'"* (5:4). The consistent use of *ṭayyibāt* across these verses makes the point that the provision is not a concession or a burden. It is good, and it is to be eaten.

The word also carries a sense of wholesomeness rather than mere permissibility. A thing can be lawful and still be disagreeable; *ṭayyib* describes what is good to take, and the command is framed around the quality of the provision rather than around a rule about it.

**Eat First, Then Act**

The order of the two commands is deliberate. Eating comes before righteous action, and the sequence is not accidental.

A person who will not take the provision he has been given is not in a position to do good with it, and the Qur'an is careful throughout its legal material to refuse the idea that abstention is itself a virtue. Al-Aʿrāf puts the refusal as a question: *"Who has forbidden the adornments and lawful provisions Allah has brought forth for His servants?"* (7:32). The verse here addresses the messengers first, and it tells them to eat before it tells them to act — which establishes that the righteous action is performed by a person who has accepted what he was given.

The sequence also answers a temptation the sūrah's audience would have recognised. A man refused by his neighbours can conclude that the world is hostile and withdraw from it. The verse tells him to eat first, and the eating is an act of acceptance rather than a concession to appetite.

**"Indeed, I Fully Know What You Do"**

The verse closes with a statement rather than a command, and the statement is about knowledge. Not "I will reward you" or "I am watching to punish". Simply: I know what you do.

The clause does specific work in this context. The messengers are the people least in need of a warning about hypocrisy, and the verse is not accusing them of anything. It is telling them that their work is seen, which for a man whose people have just called him a liar is not a threat but an assurance. The sūrah has recorded three communities refusing their messengers and calling them fabricators; the closing clause answers the isolation that produces.

It also removes any incentive to perform. A messenger who knows his work is seen by someone who sees the inside of it has no reason to make the outside impressive, and the sūrah's account of the messengers is consistently unimpressive in exactly that way — no armies, no wealth, no status among the men they were sent to.

**Why the Verse Sits Here**

The verse arrives between the account of the messengers and the account of the division. It is not part of either narrative, and its position is the point.

The messengers have been listed; the community has been described as one; and then the verse addresses the messengers directly and tells them to eat and act righteously. The instruction is the bridge between the unity of the message and the fragmentation that follows. What holds the line of messengers together is not an institution or a genealogy but a shared commission, and the commission is stated in two commands: take the good provision, and act rightly.

The placement also answers the objection the sūrah has just recorded three times. The chiefs refused their messengers because the men ate, worked and stood in the market. The verse between the messengers and the division tells the messengers to eat, and it tells them in God's own voice.


**The Messenger's Own Table**

The instruction has a practical dimension the sūrah's audience would have felt immediately. A man who tells his neighbours that there is no god but God, and who asks them for nothing, is a man whose household has to eat — and the verse tells him to eat, in a command addressed to every messenger who ever lived.

The Qur'an makes the same point in the mouth of the objectors, who treat it as a scandal: *"We never sent any messenger before you ˹O Prophet˺, who did not eat food and go about in market-places."* (25:20). The objection and the command are about the same fact. What the chiefs offered as a disqualification, this verse gives as an instruction. The messenger is not above the provision and not disqualified by needing it; he is told to take it, and then to act rightly.

The verse answers the scandal before it is raised. The objection treats the messenger's need as evidence against him; the command treats it as part of the commission, and it does so in the plural, for every messenger who ever came.

**The Command to Eat, With a Limit Attached**

Ṭā-Hā gives the same command to the Israelites with the boundary attached: *"˹saying,˺ 'Eat from the good things We have provided for you, but do not transgress in them, or My wrath will befall you. And whoever My wrath befalls is certainly doomed.'"* (20:81). An-Naḥl adds the response the provision calls for: *"So eat from the good, lawful things which Allah has provided for you, and be grateful for Allah's favours, if you ˹truly˺ worship Him ˹alone˺."* (16:114).

Read beside this verse, the two show the shape of the instruction. The provision is good and is to be taken; the taking has a limit, which is transgression; and the response required is gratitude. The verse in al-Muʾminūn gives the command in its simplest form, and the other two supply what surrounds it.

The limit in Ṭā-Hā is attached to the eating itself, not to something else. What makes the provision dangerous is not its quantity but the manner of taking it, and the warning is placed inside the command rather than after it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:52

> Surely this religion of yours is ˹only˺ one, and I am your Lord, so fear Me ˹alone˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Ummah**

The word is *ummah*, and the verse makes it singular: this community of yours is one community. The claim is not that all the messengers' followers have been one group in history. It is that what they were given is one thing.

The sūrah of al-Anbiyāʾ states the same fact with a nearly identical sentence: *"˹O prophets!˺ Indeed, this religion of yours is ˹only˺ one, and I am your Lord, so worship Me ˹alone˺."* (21:92). The two verses differ in one word, and the near-identity is itself the evidence for the claim: a sentence about the unity of the message appears twice in the Book in the same form, addressed both times to the messengers as a group.

The near-identity is itself evidence, and it is evidence of a particular kind. A claim stated once could be a local remark about one community; a claim stated twice in the same words, in two different sūrahs, is being presented as part of the structure of the message rather than as a comment on its history.

**Unity of Belief, Not of Law**

The commentators draw a distinction that the verse requires. The unity of the *ummah* is a unity of belief in one God; it does not exclude differences in *sharāʾiʿ*, the specific obligations given to particular communities — the fast, the direction of prayer, the details of the law.

That distinction is necessary to read the verse honestly. The Book plainly records different laws for different peoples, and a claim of total identity would contradict its own material. What the verse asserts is narrower and stronger: the object of worship has never changed. Every messenger called to the same God with the same exclusion of others, and the refrain the sūrah has quoted twice is the evidence. The laws varied; the Lord did not.

The distinction also protects the verse from being read as a claim that religious differences do not matter. They matter as differences of practice; they do not touch the object of worship, and the sūrah's charge against the sects is built on that gap.

**"I Am Your Lord" — The Basis of the Unity**

The middle clause is the reason for the first. The community is one because the Lord is one, and the order of the two clauses matters.

The verse does not begin with a demand for unity and then supply a reason. It states the unity as a fact, grounds it in the identity of the Lord, and then draws the consequence. That structure makes the unity descriptive rather than aspirational. The messengers' followers are not being asked to become one; they are being told that they already are, in the only respect that constitutes a religious community, and that the divisions described in the next verse are departures from a fact rather than alternatives to it.

The order also removes the most common argument for division. A group that splits usually claims to be preserving the original thing against corruption. The verse states the original thing first, in a single clause, and makes the preservation argument unnecessary before it can be made.

**"So Fear Me" Instead of "So Worship Me"**

The one word that differs between this verse and its parallel in al-Anbiyāʾ is the command at the end. There it is *faʿbudūn*, so worship Me; here it is *fattaqūn*, so fear Me, or be mindful of Me.

The variation is worth reading against the context. This verse is followed immediately by an account of division — people splitting the one religion into sects and each rejoicing in its own piece. Worship is the positive act; *taqwā* is the caution that keeps a person from adding to or subtracting from what has been given. The sūrah chooses the word that fits what comes next, and the choice suggests that the danger facing a community with one religion is not the absence of worship but the absence of care about what is done in its name.

The two sūrahs therefore give the same claim with a different emphasis, and the difference tracks the material that follows in each. Al-Anbiyāʾ goes on to describe the return; this one goes on to describe the sects.

**The Address Spans the Whole Line**

The verse is spoken to the messengers as a group, in the plural, across centuries. It is the second such address in two verses, and the two together form the sūrah's statement about what the prophetic line is.

The first verse told them what to do: eat and act righteously. This one tells them what they share: one religion, one Lord, one command. Between them the sūrah has described the commission and the content, and it has done so without reference to any particular people, land or period. The line of messengers is defined by these two verses alone, and the definition contains nothing that any one community could claim as its own.

That is the sharpest edge the verse has for the audience the sūrah addresses. A community that has split the religion into portions will want to claim the line as its inheritance. The definition given here contains no land, no language, no ancestry and no institution — nothing a faction could hold.

**What the Unity Does Not Claim**

It is worth stating what the verse is not saying, because the claim is easy to overread. It does not say that all religions are the same, and it does not say that the differences between the communities are unimportant. It says that the religion given to the messengers is one religion, and it grounds the oneness in the identity of the Lord.

The distinction matters for the verse that follows. The divisions described there are divisions *of it* — of the one thing, not of many things. A person who splits a single inheritance has done something different from a person who administers two separate estates. The sūrah's charge against the sects depends on the unity asserted here, and the unity is narrow enough to be defensible and specific enough to be breached.

The sūrah needs both properties. A unity so broad that nothing could break it would make the next verse meaningless; a unity so detailed that every community differed would make it false.

**One Lord, Many Laws, No Rival**

The verse's structure deserves one more look. It states a fact, gives its ground, and draws a consequence: the religion is one; the Lord is one; therefore fear Him. Nothing in the sequence depends on the followers being united, and nothing in it excuses them for not being.

That is the shape of the sūrah's whole argument about the messengers. The unity is on God's side of the transaction and has never varied. What varies is what people do with it, and the next verse describes the variation. The verse is therefore not a hope expressed about religious harmony. It is a statement of what has always been the case, made at the point where the reader needs to know that the fragmentation he is about to read about has not changed anything on the other side.

That is the comfort the verse offers and the warning it contains at once. Nothing the sects do alters the fact asserted here, and the fact is what they will be measured against.

**The Same Sentence in al-Anbiyāʾ, and What Follows It**

The parallel verse in al-Anbiyāʾ is followed by the same observation this sūrah makes: *"Yet the people have divided it into sects. But to Us they will all return."* (21:93). The two sūrahs give the unity, the division and the return in the same order, with almost the same words.

The parallel is close enough to establish that this is a fixed formulation rather than a local remark. The unity of the religion, the fragmentation of its adherents, and the return that will settle the matter: the three statements belong together, and both sūrahs present them as a unit. The verse here supplies the first of the three, and the reader who has read al-Anbiyāʾ already knows what comes next.

The two sūrahs are separated by two chapters, and the formulation is repeated almost word for word across the gap. That repetition is what the sūrah's argument about the messengers rests on: the message does not vary with the century, the people or the messenger.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:53

> Yet the people have divided it into different sects, each rejoicing in what they have

**Expanded Commentary**

**Zubur: Pieces, or Books**

The word translated here as sects or parties is *zubur*, and the exegetes read it two ways. One reading connects it to scriptures — the communities split into groups each holding its own book, so that the single revelation multiplied into many. Al-Rāzī notes that the root z-b-r also produces the image of pieces of iron, which fits the sense of a thing broken up. The other reading takes *zubur* simply as pieces, and the verse as describing a religion fractured into factions.

The two readings describe the same event from different angles. A community that splits over its scripture produces factions; a community that splits produces competing readings of its scripture. The verse does not force a choice, and both readings agree on the central fact: what was given as one thing is now held in pieces, and each piece is held by people who think it is the whole.

**The Division Follows the Gift**

The sequence is the verse's sharpest point. The people divided *it* — the religion just described as one — and the division happened after the giving, not before it.

The Book makes the same observation repeatedly and treats it as a pattern rather than an accident. Āl ʿImrān: *"Certainly, Allah's only Way is Islam. Those who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them."* (3:19). Ash-Shūrā gives the motive and the timing together: *"They did not split ˹into sects˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them."* (42:14). Al-Bayyinah says the People of the Book became divided only after the clear proof came to them (98:4). In every case the division is downstream of the revelation, and the cause named is not ignorance but rivalry.

The pattern removes the most sympathetic explanation available. A community that splits because it never received the message is a community to be instructed; a community that splits because it did is a community with a different problem.

**"Each Rejoicing in What They Have"**

The second clause describes the emotional state of the factions, and it is the diagnosis rather than the description. They are not merely divided; each party is pleased with its portion.

Ar-Rūm records the same condition with almost the same words: *"˹like˺ those who have divided their faith and split into sects, each rejoicing in what they have."* (30:32). The rejoicing is what makes the division stable. A split that produced grief would be a split people tried to repair; a split that produces satisfaction is a split that will last. The verse identifies the pleasure as the mechanism, and the pleasure is not in the truth of the portion but in having a portion that is one's own.

That is why the divisions are so stable. A person convinced of a position can be persuaded out of it by a better argument. A person attached to a portion cannot, because the argument is not what he is attached to, and the sūrah's refusal to argue with the sects follows directly from its diagnosis.

**The Rejoicing Is the Diagnosis**

The word for rejoicing here carries a sense of exultation — delight in something one possesses, with an implication of superiority over those who do not. That is a different state from conviction.

A person convinced of a position argues for it. A person exulting in a portion defends it, and the defence is about the portion rather than about the position. The verse is describing the psychology of sectarianism precisely: the object of attachment is not the truth but the distinction. The sūrah has just stated that the religion is one and the Lord is one; the division therefore cannot be a difference about the Lord, and the rejoicing reveals what it actually is.

The diagnosis has a practical consequence. A disagreement about God can be argued, because both sides are addressing the same question. A disagreement about whose portion is better cannot, because neither side is asking a question. The sūrah's refusal to enter the dispute follows from its account of what the dispute is.

**The Prophet's Distance From the Sects**

Al-Anʿām states the consequence for the messenger: *"Indeed, you ˹O Prophet˺ are not responsible whatsoever for those who have divided their faith and split into sects. Their judgment rests only with Allah. And He will inform them of what they used to do."* (6:159).

That verse belongs beside this one because it draws the line the sūrah implies. The messenger is not answerable for the fragmentation, and he is not authorised to resolve it. The verse in al-Muʾminūn describes the division without prescribing a remedy, and the reason is given in al-Anʿām: the matter has been referred. A reader looking for an instruction about how to reunite the sects will not find one here; he will find a description of what they have done and a statement of where the judgement sits.

The referral is not a dismissal of the divisions. It is a statement that the matter has been taken out of human hands, and that a person who tries to settle it by force or by argument is attempting something that has already been reserved. The verse leaves the description standing and the judgement elsewhere.

**The Portion Held as Property**

There is a further implication in the phrase "what they have". The portion is described as a possession, and possession is what makes it defensible against examination. A person who holds a reading of the religion as his own will defend it the way he defends anything else that is his.

That is the mechanism the verse is exposing. The sects did not divide because they disagreed about God — the verse before this one has just settled that question.
