They divided because each group came to hold something, and holding produces attachment, and attachment produces the rejoicing the verse names. The sūrah's list of the believers that follows describes people who hold nothing in that sense: they attend to their Lord, His signs, and the return, none of which can be owned.

The contrast is exact and the sūrah places the two lists close enough to be read together. One group is described by what it possesses; the other by what it pays attention to.

**Do Not Be Like Those Who Split**

Āl ʿImrān turns the observation into a prohibition addressed to the believers: *"And do not be like those who split ˹into sects˺ and differed after clear proofs had come to them. It is they who will suffer a tremendous punishment."* (3:105).

The parallel supplies what this verse leaves as description. The division is not a neutral historical fact about religious communities; it is a prohibited condition, and the prohibition is attached to the timing — after clear proofs had come. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn records the fact and moves on, and the following verses describe the state of those who are not in the factions, which is the sūrah's answer by contrast rather than by command.

The contrast is worth holding on to. The factions are described by what they hold; the believers by what they attend to. The sūrah never tells the reader which group to join. It describes both and leaves the choice where it belongs.

The method is used throughout the sūrah. The chiefs are never rebutted point by point; they are described and then left. A reader who wants a refutation is given something less convenient — an accurate account of the men who made the argument.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:54

> So leave them ˹O Prophet˺ in their heedlessness for a while

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ghamrah: Water Over the Head**

The word for heedlessness is *ghamrah*, and its literal sense is water deep enough to submerge a person — a flood one cannot stand in. The commentators take it as ignorance and confusion, and the image is exact.

A person in deep water is not merely mistaken about something. He is enclosed by the thing he is mistaken about, and he cannot see past it. The word describes a condition rather than an opinion, and it explains why argument has stopped working. The verse comes after three narratives in which messengers argued and were answered with accusations; the instruction to leave them assumes that the hearing is gone, not that the case is weak.

The image also explains the verse's gentleness. Nobody is described as wicked here; the people are described as submerged, which is a condition rather than a verdict. The instruction to leave them is addressed to a messenger, and it does not require him to think badly of the people he is leaving.

**An Instruction, Not an Abandonment**

The verse tells the Prophet to stop, and the stopping is a command rather than a concession. He is not permitted to withdraw out of exhaustion; he is instructed to withdraw at a specified point.

That distinction matters for what the verse is not saying. It is not a statement that the people are beyond reach in principle, and it is not a licence for indifference. It is a limit placed on the messenger's task, which the sūrah has already implied three times: the messenger delivers, and the outcome is not his. The instruction formalises what the narratives have shown — that there is a point at which further speech does not reach the hearer, and continuing past it serves nobody.

The sūrah has already shown a messenger at that point. Nūḥ stopped arguing and asked for help, and the verse here makes the same move available as a command rather than as a last resort. The difference is that the Prophet is told when to stop instead of having to work it out.

**"For a While"**

The instruction is bounded: *ḥattā ḥīn*, until a time. The duration is not specified, and the unspecified duration is doing work.

The reader is not told that the leaving is permanent, and the people addressed are not told that their position is final. The verse suspends the engagement without closing it, and the suspension has an end that the verse does not name. That arrangement leaves the hearers inside the interval — the same interval the sūrah described earlier as the only part of the arrangement a person controls — and it leaves the Prophet with a limit on his labour rather than a verdict on his people.

The unspecified duration also protects the reader from a comfortable reading. If the verse said how long, a listener could place himself outside the interval. Left open, it applies to whoever is inside it, and nobody inside it knows how much remains.

**Parallel Instructions Elsewhere**

The Book gives the same instruction in other places and with the same structure. Al-Anʿām records it twice: *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Allah ˹revealed it˺!' Then leave them to amuse themselves with falsehood."* (6:91), and, *"We turn their hearts and eyes away ˹from the truth˺ as they refused to believe at first, leaving them to wander blindly in their defiance."* (6:110).

The three verses share a pattern: a statement of the truth, then a withdrawal, then a description of what the withdrawn-from people do with the interval. The second of them supplies the mechanism this verse implies — the wandering is a consequence of the earlier refusal, not an independent condition. The instruction to leave them is therefore not arbitrary. It follows a decision they made, and it leaves them inside the consequences of it.

Al-Anʿām describes the wandering as a result rather than a punishment imposed from outside. The refusal produces the inability to hear, and the inability to hear makes the refusal permanent. The verse in al-Muʾminūn describes the end of that loop from the messenger's side.

**What the Prophet Is Relieved Of**

The verse answers a pressure the sūrah's audience would have recognised. A messenger whose people refuse him for centuries carries the refusal, and the narratives have shown three of them carrying it — one of whom prayed for help rather than continuing to argue.

Al-Anʿām states the relief explicitly: the Prophet is not responsible for those who divide their faith and split into sects (6:159). The instruction here is the same relief in a different form. The messenger's commission is to deliver, and once the delivery has been refused for long enough, the remaining part of the matter is not his. The verse gives him permission to stop, and the permission is a mercy to him rather than a judgement on them.

The sūrah has shown the cost of the labour. Nūḥ argued for centuries and ended by asking for help; the messengers after him were called fabricators and magicians. The instruction here sets a boundary around that cost, and the boundary is itself part of the commission.

**A Relief to the Messenger, a Warning to the Reader**

The verse has two audiences and does different work on each. For the Prophet it is a release from a labour that has stopped producing anything. For the reader it is the most uncomfortable sentence in the section, because the instruction to leave people alone is what God says when He has finished speaking to them.

The two readings cannot be separated, and the sūrah does not separate them. The same sentence that comforts the messenger warns the person who is being left. The verse does not say who is currently in the category, and the reader is left to ask whether the description of heedlessness — submerged, absorbed, unreachable — fits anyone he recognises, including himself. That is the question the following verses take up, in a description of people who are not in that condition.

The sūrah does not answer it directly, and the omission is deliberate. A verse that told the reader who was submerged would let him off; a verse that describes the condition and stops leaves the identification with him.

**The Interval That Remains**

The verse says "for a while", and the while is the same interval the sūrah has already described as the only thing a person controls. The heedless are not yet finished with; they are being left inside a period that has a limit neither they nor the reader can see.

That is what makes the instruction severe rather than dismissive. A person abandoned permanently has nothing left to lose. A person left for a while is still inside the time in which the outcome is open, and he does not know how much of it is left. The sūrah has made this point twice already — once through the fixed term that cannot be advanced or delayed, and once through the story of a people whose last morning looked like every other morning. The verse applies the same arrangement to the reader.

The interval is also the only part of the arrangement a person can do anything about, which is why the sūrah keeps returning to it. The term is fixed, the outcome is reserved, and what remains is the conduct inside the time that is left.

**Leave Them to Their Falsehood**

The parallel in al-Anʿām names what they are left with: *"Then leave them to amuse themselves with falsehood."* (6:91). The word for amuse is the significant one — the falsehood is not something they hold solemnly but something they play with.

That detail belongs beside the heedlessness in this verse. The people being left are not described as searching or struggling; they are described as occupied with something that entertains them. The verse in al-Muʾminūn says they are submerged, and al-Anʿām says what they are doing in the water. The two descriptions together are the sūrah's account of a community that has stopped being reachable: not hostile, not searching, but absorbed.

Absorption is the hardest state to address, and the sūrah names it rather than condemning it. A hostile audience can be answered; an absorbed one cannot, because it is not attending to anything the answer could reach.

The sūrah's own audience knew the type. The chiefs who called the Prophet a fabricator were not ignorant of the accounts he recited; they had heard them and gone back to what they were doing.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:55

> Do they think, since We provide them with wealth and children

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Left Hanging**

The verse is grammatically incomplete. It opens with a question — do they think — gives the grounds of the thought, and stops. The content of what they think arrives only in the next verse, and the reader is held in the gap between the two.

The suspension is deliberate. A reader who meets the first half is invited to complete the sentence himself, and what he supplies will be the obvious completion: that the provision is a sign of favour. The verse lets him construct the assumption before the next verse demolishes it, which is a more effective arrangement than a single sentence containing both halves. The assumption is the reader's own before it is the deniers'.

The sūrah uses the same device elsewhere — stating half of a proposition and letting the hearer complete it — and it always does so where the completion is something the hearer already believes. The verse is not testing knowledge. It is exposing a habit of inference.

**Wealth and Children: The Two Measures**

The two things named are the standard measures of a successful life in the society the sūrah addresses. Wealth is what a man holds; children are what continues after him. Between them they cover both dimensions of prosperity — the present and the future.

The pairing is used throughout the Book as a shorthand for worldly success, and it is consistently treated as a test rather than a verdict. The choice of exactly these two is precise. Wealth can be accumulated and is visible; children cannot be produced by effort and are the clearest evidence that a life has been given something. A person who has both has, by the ordinary reckoning of his society, been favoured, and the verse takes that reckoning as its subject.

The sūrah's audience lived inside exactly this reckoning. Standing in Mecca was measured by wealth and by sons, and the men who refused the Prophet were the men who had both. The verse names the measures and then removes their authority.

**A Verse That Deliberately Does Not Finish**

The incompleteness is unusual enough to be worth naming. Qur'anic verses routinely end mid-thought, but usually where a clause boundary falls naturally. Here the suspension is inside a single construction: the verb of thinking has no object.

The effect on the reciter is physical. The verse ends with the sentence unresolved, and the resolution requires the next verse. Anyone reading aloud is forced into the continuation, and anyone listening hears the assumption stated and then left in the air. The arrangement makes the reader a participant in the error before he is told that it is one.

That participation is the point of the structure. An assumption stated and refuted in one sentence can be dismissed as somebody else's mistake. An assumption the reader has just supplied himself is harder to put down, and the next verse's denial lands on his own reasoning rather than on a stranger's.

Qur'anic verses do end mid-thought elsewhere, but usually where a clause falls naturally. Here the suspension is inside a single construction, and the reader is made to finish a sentence that turns out to be the sentence he is being corrected for.

**The Assumption Behind the Question**

What the deniers think — and what the reader is invited to think with them — is that provision is evidence. If God has given a man wealth and children, God is pleased with him; and if God is pleased with him, his position on the disputed questions is likely to be right.

The verse does not state that reasoning, and it does not need to. It is the ordinary inference of every society in which material success is read as moral standing, and the sūrah's audience lived in one. The chiefs who refused the messengers were the wealthy men of their communities, and their standing was part of the authority their objection carried. The verse addresses the inference directly, and it addresses it in the form of a question because the inference is not usually stated aloud.

An inference nobody states cannot be examined, and an unexamined inference behaves like a fact. The verse's interrogative form is a way of making the hearer say the thing he has been assuming, and saying it is the first step toward seeing that it does not follow.

**Why These Two Things, Here**

The verse arrives immediately after the instruction to leave the heedless in their heedlessness. The connection is the reason the two things are named.

A community left to itself does not usually fall into visible misery. It prospers, and the prosperity is read as confirmation that the leaving was justified. The sūrah has described the mechanism before: warnings are ignored, then everything desired is given, and the recipients conclude that the silence of heaven is approval (6:44). The verse names the two items that make the conclusion feel inevitable, and it names them at the point where the reader is most likely to draw it.

Al-Anʿām describes the sequence in full: warnings ignored, then everything desired given, then pride, then seizure. The verse here names the middle stage, which is the stage that feels like evidence of favour.

The placement is deliberate and slightly unsettling. The verse comes immediately after the instruction to leave the heedless alone, which means the reader is told what prosperity looks like at the exact moment he is told that the prosperous are not being reached.

**Two Things Nobody Can Produce**

There is an asymmetry between the two items named that the verse does not comment on. Wealth is made; children are given. A man can acquire the first by effort and cannot acquire the second at all, and the verse pairs them as though they were the same kind of thing.

The pairing is the point. The society the sūrah addresses measured a man by both, and treated both as achievements. The verse takes the measurement apart by putting the unachievable item beside the achievable one, and then asking whether the combination is evidence of anything. A reader who cannot produce the second of the two has no grounds for reading either as a rating, and the verse leaves him with the question rather than the answer.

The sūrah uses the same pairing elsewhere to make the opposite point — that wealth and sons will not avail a person on the day the matter is settled. The two items are named together precisely because they are the ones people rely on.

**The Question Addressed to the Reader**

The verse is phrased as a question about other people, and it works on the person asking it. Do *they* think — the deniers, the ones being left in their heedlessness — that the provision means favour? The reader who agrees with the answer is not yet outside the assumption.

The sūrah arranges the question so that the reader has to check himself against it. Anyone who has looked at his own circumstances and drawn comfort from them has performed the inference the verse rejects, and the verse does not distinguish between the denier's version and the believer's. The distribution is by will and not by merit in both cases, and the reader is left holding the question rather than the answer.

Holding the question is the intended outcome. A person who has stopped reading his circumstances as a verdict has not acquired new information; he has lost a habit, and the loss is what the verse is after.

**Provision Given to Whoever He Wills**

Az-Zumar states the principle that answers the assumption: *"Do they not know that Allah gives abundant or limited provisions to whoever He wills? Surely in this are signs for people who believe."* (39:52). The same sūrah describes the misreading in a single scene: *"When one is touched with hardship, they cry out to Us ˹alone˺. Then when We shower Our blessings upon them, they say, 'I have been granted all this only because of ˹my˺ knowledge.' Not at all! It is ˹no more than˺ a test. But most of them do not know."* (39:49).

That verse supplies what this one leaves hanging. The provision is distributed by will, not by merit, and the person who receives it is being tested by it. The two verses belong together: the sūrah of az-Zumar gives the answer explicitly, and this one asks the question in a form that makes the reader hear his own assumption in it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:56

> that We hasten to ˹honour˺ them ˹with˺ all kinds of good? No! They are not aware

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer in One Word**

*Kallā* — no, not so, far from it. The particle is not a simple negation; it is a rejection of the premise, used where the statement being denied should not have been thinkable in the first place.

The verse completes the question the previous verse left open, and it answers with the shortest possible refusal. Nothing is argued. The reasoning that provision implies favour is not examined and shown to be flawed; it is dismissed, and the dismissal is the whole of the answer. The sūrah treats the inference the way it treats the chiefs' accusation of madness — as something beneath the level of a reply.

The particle also does something an argument cannot. An argument invites a counter-argument; a flat denial closes the exchange and moves on. The verse spends no words on the reasoning it rejects, and the economy is itself a judgement about the reasoning's weight.

**"They Are Not Aware"**

The closing clause identifies the defect, and it is not a defect of evidence. The deniers are not misinformed; they are unaware.

The word describes a failure of perception rather than of knowledge. The provision is in front of them, the messengers have come to them, and the pattern of destroyed communities has been recited to them at length in the preceding verses. What they lack is not information but the ability to see what the information means. The verse locates the problem in the seeing, which is why the instruction two verses earlier was to leave them: a person who cannot see cannot be shown.

That diagnosis is the sūrah's answer to a question the audience must have had. If the case against the chiefs is as strong as the narratives make it, why do they not accept it? The verse answers that the case is not what is failing. The faculty that would receive the case is what has gone.

**The Same Word Used for the Opposite Case**

The verb in the question — *nusāriʿu*, We hasten — is worth holding on to, because the sūrah uses the same idea for the believers a few verses later. There the haste is real: they race toward good deeds. Here the haste is imagined.

The contrast is precise. The deniers believe that good is being hurried to them, and it is not. The believers are actually hurrying toward good, and the sūrah says so in the same vocabulary. The two groups are described with one verb, and the difference between them is that one of the two is doing the thing and the other is receiving it. The verse's irony is built into the word choice.

The irony has a direction. The deniers are confident about a haste that is not happening; the believers are anxious about a return that certainly is. The sūrah places the confident people in the wrong and the anxious people in the right, and the reversal is the whole of its moral argument in this passage.

**Provision as a Test, Not a Verdict**

The Book states the principle that the verse assumes. Ash-Shūrā explains why provision is measured rather than abundant: *"Had Allah given abundant provisions to ˹all˺ His servants, they would have certainly transgressed throughout the land. But He sends down whatever He wills in perfect measure. He is truly All-Aware, All-Seeing of His servants."* (42:27).

That verse removes the inference at its root. Provision is calibrated, and the calibration is for the recipient's sake rather than as a measure of his worth. A reader who takes his circumstances as a rating has misread the arrangement: the amount given is what the person can carry without being destroyed by it, which is a statement about his condition and not about his standing.

The principle also runs the other way, and the sūrah means it to. A person given little has not been rated low; he has been given what he can carry. Both readings of circumstances as verdict are removed by the same sentence, and the verse removes the second only by implication.

**The Mechanism of Being Led On**

Al-Anʿām describes what the provision does to a people who refuse the warnings: *"When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise, then they instantly fell into despair!"* (6:44).

The sequence in that verse is the answer to the assumption in this one. The gifts arrive after the warnings are ignored, not before; they produce pride rather than gratitude; and the seizure follows the pride. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn asks whether the deniers suppose the gifts are an honour, and al-Anʿām supplies the description of what the gifts actually do — they close the interval in which the people could still have turned.

**The Haste That Is Real**

There is a haste in the sūrah that is not imagined, and the contrast between the two is the verse's quiet centre. The deniers believe good is being hurried to them; the believers described a few verses on are in fact hurrying toward good. One group receives and mistakes the receiving for approval. The other acts, and does not mistake the acting for anything.

The sūrah has already described the difference once, in its opening list. The people there are described by what they do — praying, giving, guarding, keeping — and the reward is attached at the end. The people here are described by what they have, and they read the having as the reward. The two descriptions are separated by fifty verses, and the verse's single word of denial is the hinge between them.

**A Measure, Not a Rating**

The principle runs through the sūrah's earlier material as well. The measured rain of verse eighteen is sent in proportion, and the verse that follows describes what it produces. The provision of a life is governed by the same rule, and the rule is the same in both cases: what is given is what the recipient can receive.

That is the sharpest form of the answer. The deniers read their wealth and children as a rating and were told it was a measure. The difference between the two is the whole distance between a person who thinks he has been approved and a person who knows he has been entrusted. The verse does not elaborate the distinction; it denies the assumption and names the unawareness, and leaves the reader to work out which of the two he has been doing.

The denial also protects the believers. A community with less wealth and fewer children than its opponents could easily conclude that the comparison is a verdict against it. The verse removes that inference from both sides at once, and it removes it in one word.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:57

> Surely those who tremble in awe of their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mushfiqūn: Fear Mixed With Tenderness**

The word is *mushfiqūn*, from a root whose sense is fear combined with weakness and tenderness — the anxiety of someone who cares about the outcome and is not certain of it. Al-Rāzī describes it as fear with softness in it, and records the reading that takes this awe as the highest form of the fear of God, because it is produced by vigilance rather than by terror.

The distinction is worth drawing carefully. Terror paralyses; *ishfāq* keeps a person attentive. The verse is not describing people who are frightened of God in the way a person is frightened of an enemy. It is describing people who are careful, in the specific sense of a person handling something valuable he cannot afford to break.

**The Fear That Comes From Vigilance**

The commentators' point about the origin of this awe is the key to the verse. It is described as the result of persistence in obedience — a fear that grows out of practice rather than out of a single overwhelming experience.

That reverses the ordinary assumption about religious fear. It is usually taken as the starting point: a person is frightened, and therefore obeys. The reading recorded here takes it as the product: a person obeys, and therefore becomes careful. The fear described in this verse is the fear of someone who has been doing the work long enough to understand what is at stake in it, and it is not available to anyone who has not begun.

That has a consequence for the reader. Awe of this kind cannot be acquired by reading about it or by being frightened once. It is the sediment of practice, and the sūrah's earlier list — prayer maintained, alms given, trusts kept — is the practice that produces it.

**Why Awe Comes First**

The verse opens a list of four descriptions, and awe is the first of them. The order is not arbitrary: belief in the signs, freedom from association and the fear of the return all follow, and all of them depend on the condition the first item names.

A person who is not careful will not examine the signs carefully, will not guard his sincerity, and will not perform his good deeds with attention to how they are received. The awe is the disposition that makes the other three possible, and the sūrah places it first for that reason. It is not the most impressive of the four items. It is the one the others are made of.

The ordering also makes the list testable. A person cannot inspect his own sincerity directly, but he can notice whether he is careful, and carefulness is the visible edge of the awe the verse names. The sūrah puts the observable disposition first and builds the rest on it.

**A List That Describes a Disposition, Not a Record**

None of the four items in the list is an achievement. There is no mention of wealth given, enemies faced, or years spent. The list describes a way of being attentive, and the sūrah's opening section — with its eight marks of the believers — is its closest parallel.

The difference between the two lists is instructive. The earlier one includes outward acts: prayer, alms, guarded desire, kept trusts. This one is almost entirely interior. The sūrah has moved from the observable shape of a believer's life to the condition underneath it, and the move comes at the point where the community has just been described as fragmented into self-satisfied sects. What distinguishes the people in this list is not their faction.

Nothing in the list could be claimed as a group marker. Awe cannot be worn, belief in the signs cannot be displayed, and a trembling heart cannot be shown to anybody. The sūrah answers the factions by describing a set of qualities that no faction can hold up as its own.

**The Same Word for the Angels**

The Qur'an uses the same word for the angels' relation to God: *"He ˹fully˺ knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them. They do not intercede except for whom He approves, and they tremble in awe of Him."* (21:28). The beings furthest from disobedience are described with the word for careful fear.

That usage settles what the word cannot mean. It cannot mean the fear of a person who expects punishment, since the angels have no such expectation. It means the reverence of a being who knows exactly who he is standing in front of, and whose knowledge produces care rather than collapse. The verse in al-Muʾminūn describes believers with the same word, and the description is a claim about how close the disposition is to the angelic one.

**The Opposite of the Sects' Certainty**

The verse is placed four verses after the description of the factions, and the contrast is exact. The sects are rejoicing; the believers are trembling. One group is satisfied with what it holds and the other is anxious about what it does.

The sūrah is making a claim about which of the two states is closer to the truth. Certainty that produces rejoicing in a portion is described as the condition of people who have stopped examining. Careful fear that produces continued attention is described as the condition of the people who will be rewarded. The verse does not argue the point; it puts the two states a few lines apart and lets the reader compare them.

The comparison has a practical edge. Certainty feels like strength and carefulness feels like weakness, and the sūrah reverses the valuation. The people who are sure of themselves are the ones described as lost; the people who are unsure of their own deeds are the ones described as racing toward good.

**Awe as the Condition of Everything Else**

Az-Zumar describes the physical effect of the same state: *"˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ has sent down the best message—a Book of perfect consistency and repeated lessons—which causes the skin ˹and hearts˺ of those who fear their Lord to tremble, then their skin and hearts soften at the mention of ˹the mercy of˺ Allah."* (39:23).

The verse is worth reading beside this one because it shows the awe in motion. It is not a fixed mood but a response: the skin trembles at the warning, then softens at the mercy. The people described in this sūrah are people to whom the Book does something, and the doing is the evidence that the awe is real. A person who is unmoved by both the warning and the mercy is not in the list, whatever else he believes.

The movement is also what keeps the awe from hardening. Fear that only tightens becomes despair, and despair stops producing obedience. The sequence in az-Zumar — trembling, then softening — is the shape of a fear that continues to work.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:58

> and who believe in the revelations of their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āyāt: The Word With Two Objects**

The word is *āyāt*, and it is one of the Qur'an's most consequential terms precisely because it has two referents. An *āyah* is a verse of the Book, and an *āyah* is a created thing that points to God. The same word covers both, and the commentators take it here in the second sense — the created signs, all of which indicate their Maker.

The double sense is not an ambiguity to be resolved but a claim being made. A revelation and a mountain are the same kind of thing in the Qur'an's vocabulary: both are signs, and both are read. The verse describes people who believe in the signs, and the description covers the Book and the world at once, because the Book is a sign about the world and the world is a sign of what the Book says.

**Created Things as Signs**

The reading that takes *āyāt* as created things is supported by the sūrah's own material. Verses twelve to twenty-two have been a catalogue of signs: the stages of a human being's making, the seven levels above, the measured rain, the gardens, the livestock, the ships.

The sūrah has been building the evidence before it names the people who accept it. A reader who arrives at this verse has already been shown twenty verses of signs, and the verse tells him what the response to them is supposed to be. The belief described here is not assent to a proposition. It is the ability to look at a camel or a rainstorm and see what it is evidence of.

The double sense of *āyah* makes that ability continuous with reading the Book. A verse of scripture and a fall of rain are the same kind of object, and the same faculty is required for both — which is why the sūrah can move between them without marking a change of subject.

**Belief in the Signs as a Separate Item**

The list could have gone from awe directly to freedom from association. It inserts this item between them, and the insertion is significant.

Awe is a disposition; freedom from association is a theological position. Between them the verse places the act of reading. A person may be careful and may hold the correct doctrine and still not see anything — he may live inside a world he does not read. The verse treats the reading as a distinct achievement, and it is the one the sūrah has spent its first twenty verses training.

The insertion also explains the sūrah's length. Twenty verses of rain, gardens, livestock and embryology would be padding if the signs required no reader. They are the syllabus for the item that appears here, and the item is what makes the syllabus worth reading.

**What the Item Adds to the List**

The four descriptions in this list are not a set of doctrines. They are a description of attention: awe, belief in the signs, refusal to associate, and the fear of the return. Each of them is a way of paying attention to something.

That is what makes the list answer the verse that precedes it. The sects are described as rejoicing in what they have — an attachment to a possession. The people in this list are described as attending to something outside themselves: their Lord, His signs, His uniqueness, and the return to Him. The contrast is between a person looking at his portion and a person looking at the evidence, and the sūrah places the two lists two verses apart.

The direction of attention is the sūrah's criterion here, and it is a criterion no reader can evade by claiming a better faction. A portion is looked at; the signs are looked through. The verse describes people doing the second.

**The Signs Already Listed in This Sūrah**

An-Naḥl closes its catalogue of provision with the same statement: *"And He has subjected for your benefit the day and the night, the sun and the moon. And the stars have been subjected by His command. Surely in this are signs for those who understand."* (16:12). And on the rain: *"And Allah sends down rain from the sky, giving life to the earth after its death. Surely in this is a sign for those who listen."* (16:65).

The two closing phrases name the audience for the signs — those who understand, those who listen — and this verse describes that audience. The signs are not self-evident; they require a reader, and the sūrah is describing the kind of person who can read them. The belief named here is the capacity the earlier verses presuppose.

An-Naḥl names the audience twice in as many verses — those who understand, those who listen — and both names describe attention rather than intelligence. The signs are available to anybody paying attention, which is why their absence is treated as a fault rather than as a limitation.

**Belief as Reading, Not Only Assenting**

The item deserves one more distinction. To believe in the signs is not the same as to believe that the signs exist. The deniers in this sūrah have seen the signs — the sūrah has listed twenty verses of them — and their position is not that the rain does not fall or that the cattle do not give milk.

Belief in the signs is the act of taking them as evidence of something. It is a reading, and a reading can be refused by a person who sees everything and concludes nothing. The verse describes the people who perform the reading, and the sūrah's whole first section has been the material they are reading. A person who has read those twenty verses and drawn no conclusion has not believed in the signs, whatever else he accepts.

**The Signs That Require a Reader**

Ar-Raʿd puts the same point as a series of questions: *"Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, 'Who is the Lord of the heavens and the earth?' Say, 'Allah!' Ask ˹them˺, 'Why ˹then˺ have you taken besides Him lords who cannot even benefit or protect themselves?' Say, 'Can the blind and the sighted be equal? Or can darkness and light be equal?'"* (13:16).

The questions assume that the signs are legible to anyone who looks, and that the failure to read them is not ignorance but blindness. That is the same claim this verse makes in positive form. The people described here are not people who have been given extra information. They are people who can see what is already there, and the sūrah's twenty verses of provision and origin are the test of whether a reader can.

The test is available to every reader of the sūrah, which is why the item is in the list at all. Belief in the signs is not a specialist attainment; it is the ordinary response to an ordinary world, and its absence is what the verse is describing.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:59

> and who associate none with their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Negation Stated Flatly**

The item is a simple negative: they do not associate partners with their Lord. No qualification, no explanation, no reference to the idols of any particular people. The verse states the condition in the barest form available.

That bareness is significant in a list this short. The other three items are descriptions of inner states — awe, belief, fear. This one is a statement about what a person does not do, and it is the only item in the list that could be checked from outside. The sūrah places the one externally verifiable condition in the middle of three that cannot be verified at all, and the placement suggests that the outer and the inner are not being treated as separate categories.

The bareness also makes the item portable. It names no idol, no practice and no people, so it applies to any reader in any century without adjustment. A list of the believers that began with a local prohibition would have dated itself; this one does not.

**Hidden Association**

The commentators extend the term beyond outright idolatry. Associating partners with God is understood to include the hidden form — a preoccupation with something other than God that prevents complete sincerity toward Him.

That extension is not a stretch of the word. The Qur'an's objection to *shirk* is not primarily about statues; it is about divided allegiance, and allegiance can be divided without any statue being involved. A person who performs the outward acts of worship while his actual trust sits elsewhere is in the condition the verse excludes, and the extension makes the item the hardest of the four rather than the easiest. The sūrah's audience included people who had no idols and plenty of preoccupations.

**Why This Item Comes Third**

The order of the list runs from disposition to perception to allegiance to performance. Awe, then belief in the signs, then refusal to associate, then the fear of the return.

The third position is the hinge. Awe and the reading of signs are capacities; the fear of the return is a consequence. What stands between them is the decision about where allegiance goes, and the decision is what makes the capacities operative. A person who reads the signs correctly and then directs his trust elsewhere has read them to no purpose. The verse places the refusal to associate at the point where the list turns from what a person perceives to what he does about it.

That is the hinge of the whole list, and the sūrah marks it with the plainest sentence in the passage. No adjective, no qualification, no elaboration — a flat negative, placed exactly where the reader moves from seeing to acting.

**Sincerity as the Condition of the Others**

The item also functions as a check on the other three. Awe that is partly directed at something other than God is not the awe the first verse describes; belief in signs that is held for the sake of a faction is the belief of the sects rather than of this list.

That is why the item is stated as a negative rather than as a positive virtue. It is not an additional achievement but a condition on the achievements already named. The sūrah has just described communities rejoicing in their portions, and a portion is a form of association — a piece of the religion held as property. The verse excludes it, and the exclusion is what separates the people in this list from the factions described two verses earlier.

The extension also widens the item's reach. Idolatry is a practice the sūrah's audience could identify in other people. Divided allegiance is a condition most of them would have recognised in themselves, and the verse is addressed to the second as much as to the first.

**A Verse Inside a List of Deeds**

The list that follows this item is about deeds: whatever good they do, they do with fearful hearts. The refusal to associate is placed immediately before it, which makes it the condition of the deeds rather than a separate category.

The arrangement is a claim about how deeds are evaluated. An act performed with divided allegiance is not counted the same way as the same act performed without division, and the sūrah states the requirement before it describes the performance. A reader who wants to know whether his deeds will be accepted has been given the criterion in the verse before the one that describes them.

The ordering also prevents a common misreading. A list that began with deeds would invite a person to count them. This one begins with a condition on the counting, and the condition concerns where his allegiance actually sits rather than how much he has done.

**Association in the Form of a Portion**

The verse before last described the sects as rejoicing in what they have, and the connection between that description and this one is closer than the intervening verse suggests. A portion of the religion held as property is a form of the thing this verse excludes.

The reasoning is not that holding a position is idolatry. It is that a person who treats his reading as his own has placed something beside God in the ordering of his allegiance — the reading, and the group that holds it, and the distinction from the other groups. The sūrah does not spell this out, and it does not need to. It describes the factions, then describes the believers, and the item that separates the two lists is the refusal to associate.

**The Command Not to Associate**

Al-Anʿām states the prohibition at the head of its list of commands: *"Say, ˹O Prophet˺, 'Come! Let me recite to you what your Lord has forbidden to you: do not associate others with Him ˹in worship˺.'"* (6:151). The prohibition comes first, before the honour owed to parents, before the prohibition on killing, before the rest.

That ordering matches the position of this item in the sūrah's list. The refusal to associate is not one command among many; it is the condition that makes the others meaningful. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn states it as a description of a kind of person, and al-Anʿām states it as the first thing God has forbidden. The two formulations are the same claim made in different registers.

The same chapter states the prohibition again at the end of its list of commands, closing with the path itself: *"Indeed, that is My Path—perfectly straight. So follow it and do not follow other ways, for they will lead you away from His Way."* (6:153). The plurality of ways is the danger named, and the plurality is what the sects have produced.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:60

> and who do whatever ˹good˺ they do with their hearts fearful, ˹knowing˺ that they will return to their Lord—

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deed and the Heart Named Together**

The verse describes a deed and the state of the person doing it in the same clause. Whatever they give — or whatever good they do — they do it while their hearts are trembling. The two halves are inseparable in the sentence.

That inseparability is the verse's claim. A deed is not evaluated on its size or its visibility alone; the condition of the doer is part of the deed. The sūrah has already made the point structurally, in the opening list where humility in prayer comes before the prayer's outward maintenance. Here it makes the point in a single phrase, and the phrase applies to every good act rather than to prayer alone.

The generality is what gives the verse its weight. A rule about prayer can be observed at fixed times; a rule about the condition of the heart during any good act applies at every moment a person does anything, including the moments he has stopped thinking about.

**Wajilah: Hearts Trembling**

The word for fearful is *wajilah*, from a root meaning to tremble, to be agitated, to shake. It is a physical word applied to the heart, and it describes a state rather than a thought.

The trembling is not the shaking of terror. It is the agitation of a person who is doing something that matters and is unsure whether he has done it well. The commentators connect it to the person who gives charity and fears it was not enough, or not sincere enough, or done for a reason he cannot fully see. The verse describes the state of someone taking his own good act seriously enough to be anxious about it.

The physical vocabulary is deliberate. The sūrah describes hearts elsewhere as hardened, sealed or covered; here the heart trembles, which is a movement rather than a blockage. A trembling heart is still working, and the sūrah treats that movement as evidence of life.

**Fear at the Moment of Doing Good**

The timing is the striking feature. The fear is not before the deed, when a person decides whether to act, and it is not after the reckoning. It is during — while the hands are giving.

That is a demanding description. The ordinary expectation is that a good deed produces satisfaction, and that satisfaction is treated as evidence that the deed was good. The verse describes the opposite: the person doing the good is afraid. The fear is not doubt about whether to act; it is the awareness that the act will be examined by someone who sees the inside of it, and that the doer cannot see the inside of it himself. The verse gives the reason in a clause rather than in a warning: they know that they are returning to their Lord. Knowledge, not fear of a sentence, is what produces the trembling, and the sūrah treats the knowledge as the more reliable of the two motives.

**"Knowing That They Will Return"**

The reason for the fear is given, and it is not the fear of punishment. It is the knowledge of the return — *ilā rabbihim rājiʿūn*, to their Lord they are returning.

The phrasing matters. A person who fears punishment fears an outcome; a person who knows he is returning fears an encounter. The verse describes the second. What makes the heart tremble is not the prospect of a penalty but the prospect of being fully known by the one the deed was done for. Al-Baqarah gives the same formula in the mouth of the patient at the moment of disaster: *"Surely to Allah we belong and to Him we will ˹all˺ return."* (2:156). The verse here applies it at the moment of a good deed, which is the harder application.

**The Fear That Does Not Paralyse**

The people described are doing. Whatever good they do, they do — the verse is about action, and the fear accompanies the action rather than replacing it.

That distinguishes this fear from the kind that stops a person. A man who is afraid of doing a thing badly and therefore does not do it is not in this list. The verse describes people whose fear is proportionate to their seriousness: they act, and they act carefully, and they are not certain of the result. The sūrah's description of the sects two verses earlier is the opposite condition — people who are certain, and whose certainty has made them stop examining anything.

The contrast is the sūrah's sharpest claim about religious temperament. Confidence is not treated as a virtue here, and anxiety is not treated as a defect. The people described are acting and unsure in the same breath, and the combination is what the sūrah recommends.

**A Deed Examined From the Inside**

The verse assumes something about how deeds are assessed that is worth stating. The heart's condition is part of the deed, which means the deed cannot be fully evaluated by anyone except the one who sees the inside of it. No witness can attest to the trembling, and no record can capture it.

That assumption explains the fear. A person whose act will be judged partly on something he cannot show anyone, and cannot fully see in himself, has reason to be anxious about it. The verse is not describing scrupulosity. It is describing the accurate response to being assessed on a dimension that is hidden from the assessor's eyes and partly hidden from one's own.

**The Return That Settles the Matter**

Al-Anbiyāʾ ends its account of the divided communities with the same statement: *"Yet the people have divided it into sects. But to Us they will all return."* (21:93). The return is offered there as the answer to the division, and here it is the reason for the trembling heart.

The two uses belong together. The factions rejoice in their portions because the portions look permanent; the believers tremble because they know the portions will be examined. The difference between the two groups is not what they have done but what they expect to happen next. The verse describes people who are living inside the expectation of the return, and the expectation is what shapes how they perform every good act they perform.

That is the whole content of the four-item list the sūrah has just given. Awe, belief in the signs, refusal to associate, and deeds done with a trembling heart — all four are descriptions of a person who has not forgotten where he is going. The verse supplies the reason for the other three, and the reason is not a threat but a fact about the itinerary.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:61

> it is they who race to do good deeds, always taking the lead

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Completes the List**

The verse finishes what the previous verse began. The four descriptions — awe, belief in the signs, refusal to associate, deeds done with trembling hearts — were left without a predicate, and this verse supplies it. Those people are the ones who race toward good deeds and are foremost in them.

The completion is worth noting because it is a verb of motion. The list did not describe a state of being; it described a direction. Awe, belief, sincerity and careful performance are not conditions a person settles into. They are what propels him, and the verse names the propulsion.

The sūrah has already described haste in this passage, and it described it as a delusion. The deniers believed good was being hurried to them. Here the haste is real, and it belongs to the people doing the hurrying.

**Sābiqūn: The Foremost**

The word is *sābiqūn*, from a root meaning to precede or to outstrip, and it describes people who are ahead in a race rather than people who are merely present in it. The verse pairs the racing with the leading, and the two are distinct claims.

Racing describes effort; leading describes position. A person can exert himself and still be behind, and the verse says these people are both exerting and ahead. The commentators connect the word to a rank rather than to a quantity of deeds — the *sābiqūn* are those at the highest level, and the term appears elsewhere with that sense.

Al-Wāqiʿah states the connection plainly: *"and the foremost ˹in faith˺ will be the foremost ˹in Paradise˺. They are the ones nearest ˹to Allah˺,"* (56:10-11). The precedence in this world is the precedence in the next, and the verse in al-Muʾminūn describes the first of the two.

**The Ḥadīth of ʿĀʾishah**

The commentary tradition records an exchange that fixes the meaning of the previous verse. ʿĀʾishah asked the Prophet whether the description of those who give while their hearts tremble refers to people who commit fornication, drink wine and steal while fearing God for it. He answered no: it is the man who prays, fasts and gives charity, and fears God on account of that.

The answer settles a real ambiguity. A reader could suppose the trembling describes a sinner afraid of being caught. The reply says it describes a person afraid about his obedience, not his disobedience — a man who has done the right things and is still unsure of them.

That is the harder state, and the sūrah recommends it. The verse that follows this exchange describes the same people as racing toward good, which is consistent with the reply: the fear is attached to the doing, and the doing continues.

**Racing, Not Merely Doing**

The verb matters more than the noun. The verse does not say they do good deeds; it says they race toward them, and the racing is the distinguishing feature.

The sūrah's earlier list described the believers by what they maintain — prayer kept, trusts honoured, alms given. This verse describes them by the speed of their movement toward the next good thing. Maintenance is steady; racing is urgent, and the two descriptions belong to the same people.

The urgency has a reason the sūrah has already given. The interval is fixed at both ends, and the people who understand that behave differently inside it from the people who do not. The sects rejoice in what they have; the believers race toward what they have not yet done.

The verb also removes the possibility of resting on the earlier list. A person who has maintained his prayer and kept his trusts has not finished; the verse describes him moving toward the next good thing, and the movement is the mark.

**The Haste Contrasted With the Earlier Haste**

Two verses earlier the sūrah described a haste that is imaginary. The deniers suppose that good is being hurried to them in the form of wealth and children, and the answer is a single word of denial. Here the haste is actual and belongs to the believers.

The contrast is built into the vocabulary. Both groups are described with words of speed, and the difference between them is that one is receiving and mistaking the receiving for approval while the other is acting. The verse's irony is quiet and exact, and it runs through the whole passage without ever being announced.

The sūrah has been building toward this contrast since the verse about provision. A reader who has followed the argument can now see the two postures side by side: the people who think they are being favoured, and the people who are running.

**The Foremost in al-Wāqiʿah**

At-Tawbah uses the same word for a specific group: *"As for the foremost—the first of the Emigrants and the Helpers—and those who follow them in goodness, Allah is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him."* (9:100). The term there names people, and it names them by their precedence in a particular event.

Al-Wāqiʿah uses it for a rank in the Hereafter, and the two uses agree on the underlying claim: precedence is a real category, and it is earned by what a person did when the matter was still open. The verse in al-Muʾminūn describes the earning, and it describes it as motion rather than as accumulation.

The difference between the two images is worth holding. Accumulation is something a person has; motion is something a person is doing. The sūrah's description belongs to the second, and the second is the only one available inside an interval whose end nobody knows.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:62

> We never require of any soul more than what it can afford. And with Us is a record which speaks the truth. None will be wronged

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wusʿ: The Capacity**

The word is *wusʿ*, capacity or scope — what a soul has room for. The verse states that no soul is tasked beyond it, and the statement is a limit on the demand rather than a concession to weakness.

The distinction matters. A concession would mean the requirement has been lowered for some people. A limit means the requirement was never framed in a way that exceeds the recipient. The verse describes the second: the demand is calibrated to the person it addresses, and it is calibrated by the one who made the person.

That is why the statement appears in legal contexts throughout the Qur'an rather than only in consolatory ones. It is a principle about how obligation works, and the sūrah places it immediately after a description of people racing toward good deeds — which is the context in which the limit is most needed.

**The Same Principle Across the Book**

The formula recurs, and its recurrence establishes it as a rule rather than a reassurance. Al-Baqarah: *"Allah does not burden any soul with more than it can bear."* (2:286). Al-Anʿām states it inside a list of commands about measure, weight and speech (6:152). Al-Aʿrāf states it inside a description of the residents of Paradise (7:42).

The three contexts are different — law, ethics, reward — and the formula is the same. That consistency is the point. The principle is not attached to a particular difficulty or a particular community; it is attached to obligation as such, and it travels with the obligation wherever it goes.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn states it after describing the believers' deeds, which is where a reader is most likely to wonder whether the standard can be met. The verse answers before the question is asked.

The recurrence also removes the suspicion that the principle is a softening applied to difficult cases. It appears in al-Baqarah's summary of belief, in al-Anʿām's commercial ethics and in al-Aʿrāf's description of Paradise, and it is the same sentence each time.

**A Record That Speaks the Truth**

The second clause introduces a book, and the description given to it is unusual: a record that speaks with truth, or by truth. The Book is not described as complete or as sealed but as truthful, which is a claim about its character rather than its contents.

Al-Jāthiyah uses almost the same phrase: *"This record of Ours speaks the truth about you. Indeed, We always had your deeds recorded ˹by the angels˺."* (45:29). The two verses agree that the record's distinguishing property is accuracy, and the accuracy is what makes it a record rather than an accusation.

The placement of the clause matters. It comes immediately after the statement about capacity, and the two together form a single assurance: the demand is within reach, and the accounting of it will be exact. Neither half works without the other.

Truthfulness is also the property a record most needs. A complete record could still be inaccurate; an accurate one that omits nothing is what the two verses describe between them, and the sūrah's emphasis falls on the accuracy.

**"None Will Be Wronged"**

The verse closes with the shortest possible statement of the outcome, and the statement is negative: they will not be wronged. Not rewarded, not forgiven — not wronged.

The negative is the right form for the claim. A promise of reward depends on what a person has done; a promise that no wrong will be done depends on the character of the one making it. The verse offers the second, which is the more secure of the two.

Al-Kahf states the same thing at the moment the record is opened, and it states it as the final clause of the scene: *"And your Lord will never wrong anyone."* (18:49). The two verses frame the accounting from opposite ends — this one before the deeds are described, that one after they have been read.

The negative form also answers a fear the trembling believers would have had. A person unsure of his own motives is not mainly afraid of being under-credited. He is afraid of being assessed by a standard he could not see, and the verse removes exactly that fear.

**The Two Clauses Belong Together**

The verse could have stated either half alone. A statement about capacity without a record would leave the accounting uncertain; a record without a capacity limit would leave the demand open-ended. The verse gives both, and the pairing is what makes it an assurance.

The sūrah has just described people trembling over their own good deeds. That trembling is not the anxiety of a person who fears an arbitrary verdict; it is the anxiety of a person who knows the accounting will be exact and knows he cannot see inside his own motives.

The verse answers that specific anxiety. The demand was within his capacity, and the record will not misstate what he did. What remains uncertain to him is not the fairness of the assessment but the quality of his own performance, and the verse does not pretend otherwise.

**The Record From Which They Read**

Al-Kahf describes the scene at length: *"And the record ˹of deeds˺ will be laid ˹open˺, and you will see the wicked in fear of what is ˹written˺ in it. They will cry, 'Woe to us! What kind of record is this that does not leave any sin, small or large, unlisted?' They will find whatever they did present ˹before them˺. And your Lord will never wrong anyone."* (18:49).

The parallel supplies the detail this verse compresses. The record is laid open; the wicked read it; their reaction is described in their own words; and the assurance about injustice closes the scene exactly as it closes the verse here. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives the principle, and al-Kahf gives the moment it is applied.

The two verses also agree on where the assurance sits. Al-Kahf places it after the wicked have read their record and cried out; this verse places it before any deed is mentioned. The same sentence does the same work at both ends of the accounting.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:63

> But the hearts of those ˹who disbelieve˺ are oblivious to ˹all of˺ this, and they have other ˹evil˺ deeds, opposite to this, in which they are engrossed

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ghamrah Again: The Submerged Heart**

The word is the same one the sūrah used for the heedlessness it told the Prophet to leave alone. There it described a people; here it describes hearts. The image is identical — water deep enough to cover a person — and the shift from the community to the organ is the verse's contribution.

A submerged people can still be addressed, at least in principle. A submerged heart cannot, because the organ that would receive the address is the thing that is under. The sūrah has moved from describing a social condition to describing an interior one, and the interior one is the reason the social one persists.

The verse also names whose hearts: the hearts of the disbelievers. The commentators take the reference as specific, and the specificity matters. This is not a general claim about human inattention; it is a diagnosis of a particular group the sūrah has been describing at length.

**"Other Deeds Besides"**

The second clause is the verse's sharper half. Their hearts are not merely submerged; they are occupied with something else. The verse describes other deeds — deeds other than the ones just listed, and the commentators take them as opposite in kind.

The contrast is exact. The believers were described by four qualities, all of them forms of attention. The disbelievers are described by other deeds, and the word for other carries the sense of things set alongside or in place of. The verse is not describing an absence of activity but a substitution.

That is a more serious diagnosis than idleness. A person doing nothing can be roused. A person busily doing the wrong things cannot, because the busyness is what keeps him from noticing that anything is missing.

The word for other also does quiet work. It does not say the deeds are evil in themselves; it says they are other — set beside, or in place of, the four qualities just listed. The substitution is the charge.

**The Future Tense Reading**

The commentators record a variant reading in which the second clause is future rather than present: other deeds which they will undertake. The difference is small in wording and large in implication.

The present tense describes a condition. The future tense describes an intention — a decision to continue. A person whose heart is submerged today might surface tomorrow; a person who intends to keep doing the other deeds has made the submersion durable.

The variant is worth preserving because the sūrah's argument elsewhere depends on the distinction. The interval is fixed and the conduct inside it is what varies, and a person who has decided to continue has settled the question about his own conduct. The reading makes explicit what the present tense leaves open.

Both readings are preserved in the commentary tradition, and the sūrah's argument does not depend on choosing between them. A heart submerged today and a decision to stay submerged tomorrow are the same condition seen from two moments.

**Engrossed: The Verb of Occupancy**

The verb translated as engrossed describes people working at something, occupied in it, actively engaged. It is not a word for distraction or for accident. The verse says they are busy.

The word choice answers an objection the audience might have raised. The chiefs were not idle men; they were the notables of a trading city, and their days were full. The verse does not accuse them of having nothing to do. It accuses them of having the wrong things to do, and of being thoroughly occupied with them.

Al-Kahf describes the same condition from the other side: *"We have certainly cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend this ˹Quran˺—and deafness in their ears."* (18:57). The veils and the deafness are what make the occupancy possible; the occupancy is what keeps the veils in place.

The two verses describe a closed circuit, and the circuit is the sūrah's account of how a person becomes unreachable. Nothing outside it can get in, because everything that would have carried meaning has been displaced by the other deeds.

**The Verse Placed Between Two Lists**

The verse's position is the sharpest thing about it. It comes immediately after the description of the racing believers and immediately before the account of the punishment that seizes the affluent.

The arrangement leaves no room for a neutral category. The sūrah has described the people who are racing, then the people whose hearts are elsewhere, then what happens to the second group. A reader looking for a middle position — someone neither racing nor engrossed — will not find one in this passage.

The absence of a middle category is the sūrah's answer to the sectarian picture two passages earlier. The factions were many and each was satisfied with itself. Here the division is into two, and the criterion is the direction of a person's attention.

The sūrah has used the same arrangement before, placing the chiefs' speeches beside the messengers' commissions and letting the outcome settle the comparison. Here it places two lists of people beside each other and lets the following verses describe the end of one of them.

**Hearts Covered Elsewhere**

Al-Jāthiyah describes the condition in its fullest form: *"Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ those who have taken their own desires as their god? ˹And so˺ Allah left them to stray knowingly, sealed their hearing and hearts, and placed a cover on their sight."* (45:23).

The verse supplies what this one leaves implicit. The other deeds are the desires taken as a god, and the sealing is the result rather than the cause. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn describes the occupancy; al-Jāthiyah describes what the occupancy eventually produces, and both agree that the person inside it does not notice.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:64

> But as soon as We seize their elite with torment, they start to cry for help

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mutrafūn: The Ones Given Ease**

The word is *mutrafūn*, from a root meaning to be given ease or luxury, and it describes people whose comfort has become their condition. The verse does not name the punishment's cause; it names the people it reaches first.

The choice is precise. The sūrah has described the chiefs' arrogance and the hearts' submersion, and this verse identifies the group in whom both are concentrated. The *mutraf* is not simply rich; he is a person whose life has been arranged so that he does not encounter difficulty, and the arrangement is what makes him unreachable.

Sabaʾ records the same group making the same refusal: *"Whenever We sent a warner to a society, its elite would say, 'We truly reject what you have been sent with.'"* (34:34). The verse there names the elite as the ones who speak, and this verse names them as the ones seized.

**"Until": The Delay Named**

The verse opens with *ḥattā*, until, and the particle carries the whole of the interval the sūrah has been describing. The seizure does not come immediately; it comes after a period, and the period is the same one whose length nobody inside it can know.

The sūrah has established the rule twice already: the term cannot be advanced or delayed, and the messengers are to be left in the people's heedlessness for a while. This verse shows the end of that while. The word *until* is where the interval terminates, and the verse gives no warning before it.

That is the verse's structural point. The reader has been told the interval exists and that its length is fixed. He has not been told when it ends, and neither has anyone inside it. The verse arrives at the ending without marking it in advance.

That is the sūrah's most consistent warning, and it is delivered structurally rather than rhetorically. The reader is never told when; he is told that there is a when, that it is fixed, and that it is not announced.

**The Cry for Help**

The response to the seizure is a cry — the same word the sūrah used for the sound that destroyed the earlier communities, here coming from the other direction. The people who were destroyed by a cry now make one.

The irony is exact and the sūrah does not comment on it. The *ṣayḥah* that overtook the people of the blast was a punishment arriving as a sound. The cry here is a plea departing as a sound, and the next verse refuses it in a single sentence.

The verse also notes that the cry comes after the seizure and not before. Nothing in the preceding verses suggests these people cried for help while the interval was running. The plea is a response to pain rather than a change of position, and the following verse treats it as such.

The sūrah distinguishes the two elsewhere. Nūḥ's cry came after centuries of refusal by others, and it was a request for a decision. This cry comes after the decision has been made, and it asks for the decision to be reversed.

**Badr as One Reading**

The commentators divide over the reference. The usual reading takes the punishment as that of the Hereafter. Some connect it with the defeat the idolaters suffered at Badr, which would make the verse a description of an event the audience had lived through or was about to.

The two readings are not incompatible, and the verse's wording allows both. It describes a seizure, a group seized, and a cry — none of which is tied to a particular time. The sūrah's habit throughout is to describe patterns that recur, and a verse that fits both Badr and the Hereafter is doing what the rest of the sūrah does.

The ambiguity also serves the audience. A reader who takes the verse as future has been warned; a reader who takes it as past has been shown that the warning was fulfilled. Both readings produce the same instruction.

**The Sequence the Sūrah Has Described**

The verse completes a sequence the sūrah has been assembling. The messengers came; the chiefs refused; the hearts became submerged; the interval ran; the seizure arrived; the cry went up.

Every step has been described before this verse, and the verse's contribution is the ordering. Nothing in it is new information. What is new is the demonstration that the steps follow one another, and that the cry comes at the end rather than at any point where it would have been heard.

The sūrah has described this sequence four times in its middle section, with four different peoples. This verse describes it a fifth time, and the fifth time it is the audience's own community that is inside it.

The sūrah has been careful not to name that community in the sequence. It describes the pattern in the past tense four times, and then describes the seizure without saying whose seizure it is. The identification is left to the reader.

**The Elite in Sabaʾ**

Sabaʾ gives the fullest account of the same group: the elite who refuse the warner, the people who follow them, and the destruction that comes to both. The sūrah there describes the gardens, the security and the ease of the people before the refusal, and the ease is what the refusal rests on.

The parallel supplies the social detail this verse compresses into a single word. The *mutrafūn* are not an abstract class; they are the men whose comfort made the messenger's message inconvenient, and whose inconvenience became the community's position. The verse in al-Muʾminūn names them and then describes what happens when the comfort stops.

Sabaʾ adds the detail that matters most: the elite speak first, and the community follows. The refusal is not a popular movement. It is a position taken by the men with the most to lose and adopted by everyone else.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:65

> ˹They will be told,˺ “Do not cry for help today. Surely you will never be saved from Us

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer in the Negative**

The verse answers the cry of the previous verse, and the answer is a refusal stated in the imperative. Do not cry. The form is a command, but its content is a statement of fact: the crying will accomplish nothing, so it may as well stop.

The sūrah has used imperatives of this kind before. The instruction to leave the heedless was a command whose content was a limit. This one is a command whose content is a closed door, and the difference between the two is the difference between an interval that is running and one that has ended.

The verse does not rebuke the crying. It does not say the cry is insincere or that the people are lying. It says the cry is useless, and the reason given is not their character but the situation they are in.

**"Today"**

The word does the most work in the sentence. Today — the day the cry is being made — is distinguished from the days before it, and the distinction is the whole of the verse's argument.

The sūrah has spent its length on the interval: the term that cannot be moved, the time the messengers were to be left, the period during which the hearts were submerged. All of that was "before today". The verse names the day on which the arrangement changes, and it names it from inside.

That is what makes the word severe. A reader outside the day can still act on it. A reader being addressed as living inside it has been told that the period in which his cry would have been heard has closed, and the telling comes too late to be acted on.

**"From Us"**

The second clause states what will not happen: they will not be helped, or saved, or given victory — the word carries all three. The preposition is significant. They will not be saved *from Us*, and the phrasing identifies the one they need saving from.

The sūrah has described the seizure as an act of God, and this verse makes the source explicit. There is no third party whose intervention is being ruled out; the help that will not come is help against the one who is seizing. The verse closes the possibility of appeal to anyone else by naming the only relevant party.

The formulation also removes the most common form of the plea. A person in that position would naturally ask for an intermediary, and the verse's grammar leaves no room for one.

The Meccan audience had intermediaries at the centre of its religion, and the verse's preposition is aimed at them. The help that will not come is help from the one who is seizing, and no third party is mentioned as available.

**The Reversal of the Cry**

The sūrah has recorded cries before, and the reversal here is exact. Nūḥ cried for help and was answered; the people destroyed by the blast were overtaken by a cry; the elite here cry and are told to stop.

The three cries mark three positions in the same arrangement. The messenger's cry comes from inside the interval and is heard. The destroyed people's cry is the punishment itself. The elite's cry comes after the interval and is refused. The verse supplies the third, and the sūrah has prepared the reader to recognise it.

The arrangement is not arbitrary. It tracks the timing, which is the sūrah's consistent concern. A cry's efficacy depends on when it is made, and the verse is about the when.

The sūrah makes the same point with the term that cannot be advanced or delayed. Timing is not a detail in this book; it is the variable that decides everything, and the verse shows what a cry made at the wrong moment is worth.

**What the Refusal Presupposes**

The verse does not argue with the people who are crying, and the absence of argument is itself a claim. Nothing is said about their deeds, their excuses or their beliefs. The refusal rests entirely on the timing.

That presupposes the whole of the sūrah's earlier argument. The interval was real; the messengers were delivered; the hearts were submerged by choice; the record speaks the truth. Given all of that, the cry at this point adds nothing, and the verse treats it as adding nothing.

A reader who has followed the sūrah will find the refusal intelligible rather than harsh. A reader who has not will find it severe, and the severity is the measure of what the earlier verses were for.

The sūrah has never threatened arbitrarily. It has described a term, a delivery, a refusal, an interval and a record. The verse arrives at the end of that chain, and its brevity assumes the reader has followed the chain.

**No Helper on That Day**

The Qur'an states the same closure in several places, and the statements agree on the point that matters. The day described is one in which the ordinary channels of help are unavailable, and the unavailability is not a punishment added to the situation but a description of the situation.

Al-Muʾminūn's version is the briefest of them. Two clauses, no elaboration, no description of the scene. The verse's economy is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the punishments throughout — the event is named rather than narrated, and the naming is what makes it a fact rather than a spectacle.

That economy also serves the reader. A described punishment can be watched from a distance; a named one cannot, because the naming is addressed to the people inside it. The verse speaks to them in the second person, and the address is the point.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:66
