> Indeed, My revelations were recited to you, but you used to back away ˹in disgust˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āyāt Recited, Not Given**

The verse specifies the medium. The revelations were *recited* — spoken aloud, in the hearing of the people addressed. They were not written on a tablet, not left in a place to be found, not delivered to someone else to summarise.

The specification is the ground of the charge that follows. A person who was never told cannot be accused of turning away; a person who heard and moved back can. The sūrah has used the same word for the signs given to Mūsā, where the signs were shown rather than spoken, and the difference is deliberate: this community's evidence was audible.

That has a consequence for the audience. Recitation can be repeated, quoted, checked against memory and discussed. The people addressed had access to the text in a form that allowed examination, and the verse's charge rests on their having had that access.

**Turning on the Heels**

The phrase describes a physical movement — turning back on one's heels, the motion of a person walking away from something he is facing. The commentators read it as distancing, and the image is more specific than the translation suggests.

A person who turns on his heels was facing the thing. He is not described as ignorant of it or as never having encountered it. He is described as having been in front of it and then moving backwards, which is a deliberate act performed by someone who knows what he is leaving.

The same image appears in the sūrah's account of the idolaters' three responses, and it is listed first there: distancing, then arrogance, then foolish talk at night. The sequence moves from withdrawal to contempt, and the verse here gives the first of the three.

**The Habitual Form**

The verb is in a form that indicates repeated or customary action. They *used to* turn back — not once, not occasionally, but as a settled practice.

The habitual aspect is the verse's sharpest detail. A single withdrawal could be a mistake or a moment of confusion. A repeated one is a policy, and the sūrah is describing a policy. The commentators note that the same people gathered nightly to discuss the Qur'an, which is the practice this verse names in its general form.

The habitual form also removes the most sympathetic explanation. A person who turns away once and then returns has not settled anything. A person who turns away as a matter of routine has, and the verse describes people in the second category.

The habitual form also explains the arrogance named in the next verse. A settled practice produces confidence, and confidence about a position never examined is what the sūrah has been describing since the chiefs of Pharaoh.

**The Three Ways Named**

The commentary tradition identifies three responses the idolaters made to the Qur'an, and the sūrah names all three across this verse and the next. First, distancing themselves from it. Second, arrogance on account of it. Third, foolish talk about it at night.

The three form a progression, and the progression is worth following. Distancing is passive; arrogance is a posture; the night talk is organised activity. The sūrah describes a community moving from avoidance to contempt to collective ridicule, and each step makes the next easier.

The verse gives the first step and the next verse gives the other two. Between them they describe not a disagreement but a social process, and the process is what the sūrah is accounting for.

Naming the process also removes the comfort of treating the refusal as intellectual. A disagreement can be answered with reasons. An institution that meets nightly to produce contempt has to be accounted for differently, and the sūrah accounts for it by describing it.

**Recitation as the Medium**

The sūrah returns repeatedly to the fact that its material was delivered by recitation. The Prophet recited; the people heard; the hearing is what the charge rests on. That is a claim about the nature of the revelation as much as about the audience's response.

Al-Isrāʾ describes what happens during the recitation: *"When you ˹O Prophet˺ recite the Quran, We put a hidden barrier between you and those who do not believe in the Hereafter. We have cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend it—and deafness in their ears."* (17:45-46).

The two descriptions belong together. The recitation is real and audible; the barrier is also real, and it is on the hearers' side. The verse in al-Muʾminūn describes the movement away; al-Isrāʾ describes what makes the movement possible.

Neither description excuses the movement. Al-Isrāʾ ties the barrier to disbelief in the Hereafter, which means the barrier follows the refusal rather than preceding it. The recitation was available, and the verse here holds the hearers to it.

**Back Away in Disgust**

The turning described here is not neutral retreat. The commentators read contempt into the motion, and the following verse confirms the reading by naming arrogance explicitly.

That distinguishes this response from the one the sūrah described earlier. The heedless people were submerged — unaware, absorbed, unreachable. These people are facing the revelation and moving away from it in disgust, which requires awareness. The two conditions are different, and the sūrah keeps them separate.

The verse is therefore addressed to a different group from the one the Prophet was told to leave. These are people who heard, understood enough to be repelled, and made the repulsion habitual.

The distinction has a consequence for the messenger. The submerged cannot be reached and the instruction was to stop trying. The repelled can be addressed, which is why the sūrah goes on to ask them questions rather than to leave them.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:67

> boasting of the Sacred House, and babbling ˹nonsense about the Quran˺ by night.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Boast About the House**

The verse names what the boasting was about, and the object is the Sacred House. The people addressed were the custodians of the Kaʿbah, and their standing in Arabia rested on that custody.

The boast is therefore not vanity in a general sense. It is a claim to religious authority drawn from a building — a claim that proximity to the House settled the question of who was right. The sūrah records the claim because the claim was the ground on which the refusal of the Qur'an stood.

The arrangement is the verse's sharpest point. A community whose authority comes from a place will reject a message whose authority comes from a recitation, and the rejection is not a disagreement about content. It is a defence of the basis on which the community's standing rests.

**Sāmir: The Night Gathering**

The word translated as babbling is *sāmir*, and it describes people gathered at night talking — a specific social practice, not a general habit of speech. The commentators identify the gathering precisely: the idolaters met in the environs of the Kaʿbah after dark.

The detail matters because it makes the charge concrete. This was not private doubt or individual mockery. It was an organised nightly assembly, held next to the sacred site, whose purpose was to characterise the Qur'an and its reciter. The verse names the practice, the place and the time.

The sūrah has used the same method throughout. It does not accuse the chiefs of being wicked in general; it records what they said, where and when, and lets the specificity carry the judgement.

The place is the sharpest detail. The assembly met beside the House they were boasting about, which means the contempt was produced at the site of the authority the contempt was defending. The verse names the irony by naming the location.

**What Was Said at Night**

The commentators record the content: the Qur'an called the product of magic or of poetry, and the Prophet insulted. The night talk was a working session in which the community's official account of the revelation was produced and maintained.

That function explains why the sūrah treats it as a charge rather than as background. A group that meets nightly to decide what a thing is has made a decision, and the decision is what its members then hold. The individual's contempt is downstream of the assembly's output.

That is why the sūrah treats the gathering rather than the individuals. A man who repeats what his assembly concluded is not independently convinced, and the sūrah's charge is aimed at the arrangement that produces the conviction.

The three responses named across these two verses now form a complete picture. Distancing, arrogance, and a nightly meeting that generates the language of contempt. The sūrah is describing an institution, not a mood.

**The Prophet's Practice at Night**

Al-Qurṭubī records, in connection with this verse, that the Prophet disliked sleeping before the night prayer and disliked conversation after it. His reason, as reported, was that the night prayer covers the sins of the day, while foolish talk at night seals the day with idleness and falsehood.

The report is worth setting beside the verse because it answers the charge with a practice. The same hours the idolaters spent in assembly were hours the Prophet kept free of talk, and the sūrah's audience would have known both facts.

The contrast is not presented as an argument in the text. It is available to anyone who knew the two habits, and the verse's mention of the night gathering invites the comparison without making it.

The report also gives the reader something to do with the verse. The charge is about how a community spends its evenings, and the answer recorded in the tradition is a practice rather than a rebuttal.

**Night as the Time of Sealing**

The reasoning recorded in the report gives the night a function. A day ends with whatever fills its last hours, and what fills them sets the character of the whole.

That is why the verse specifies the time. Foolish talk at noon is a remark; foolish talk as the day's final act is a conclusion. The sūrah's charge is not that these people said unkind things but that they arranged for their days to end with them, nightly, in a group, beside the House.

The sūrah has described the same mechanism from the other side in its account of the believers. Their deeds are done with trembling hearts, and the trembling continues into how the act is regarded afterwards. The two groups are separated by what they do with their attention at the end of the day.

The verse does not moralise about night as such. It names a specific gathering at a specific place with a specific purpose, and the timing matters because of what the gathering concluded the day with.

**Talk in Paradise**

At-Ṭūr describes the converse scene: *"They will pass around to each other a drink ˹of pure wine,˺ which leads to no idle talk or sinfulness."* (52:23). The absence of idle talk is named as a property of the reward, and its naming is a direct answer to this verse.

The parallel is exact in its structure. Here the night talk is a charge; there its absence is a feature of Paradise. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn describes a community organised around speech that produces nothing, and the Qur'an elsewhere describes a state defined by the removal of exactly that speech.

The parallel is also a promise rather than only a contrast. The drink in at-Ṭūr leads to no idle talk, which means the state described there includes conversation without the defect this verse names. The removal is of the falsity, not of the speaking.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:68

> Is it because they have never contemplated the Word ˹of Allah˺? Or ˹because˺ there has come to them something that did not come to their forefathers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word**

The verse calls the Qur'an *al-qawl*, the Word or the Saying, and the choice of word is worth holding. The sūrah has used *āyāt* for the revelations that were recited; here it uses a term whose ordinary sense is speech, something said by someone.

That ordinary sense is what the question depends on. A person does not contemplate a phenomenon; he contemplates a statement, and a statement can be examined for consistency, tested against what he already knows, and checked against itself.

An-Nisāʾ makes the same invitation and gives the test: *"Do they not then reflect on the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have certainly found in it many inconsistencies."* (4:82). The verse offers the Word as an object capable of failing an examination, which is what makes the invitation to examine it meaningful.

**Tadabbur: Contemplation**

The verb is from a root meaning to consider the end or the consequence of a thing — to follow it through. It is not the word for quick reading or for listening, and it is not the word for believing.

The distinction is the verse's point. The charge in the previous two verses was that the people turned away and talked foolishly. This verse asks whether they ever followed the Word through, and the question implies that they did not.

The sūrah has spent twenty verses giving the reader material for exactly this operation — rain, gardens, embryology, livestock, the stages of a human being's making. The verses are not decoration; they are the content a person would be following through if he were contemplating rather than talking.

The verb also implies time. Following a thing through to its end is not something done in a hearing. The charge against the night gathering is that it substituted talk for that work, and the verse asks whether the work was ever attempted.

**The Second Question: Two Readings**

The second half of the verse is a question about the forefathers, and the commentators give it two opposed senses. One reads it as a challenge: do they have some promise of immunity that their fathers did not have, and that nobody has? The other reads it as a rebuke: something has come to them that did not come to their fathers, and they should be grateful rather than dismissive.

The two readings differ in direction but agree on the fact. Either way, the audience has received something their ancestors did not. The first reading treats that as removing an excuse; the second treats it as creating an obligation.

The verse does not settle between them, and the ambiguity is productive. A reader who takes the first reading is told he has no special protection. A reader who takes the second is told he has been given something rare. Both conclusions bear on the refusal described in the preceding verses.

The commentators preserve both because both fit the question's grammar, and the sūrah gains by keeping them. The verse asks a question that can be answered two ways and closes the same escape route either way.

**"That Which Came Not to Their Forefathers"**

The phrase is often used by the idolaters as a reason for refusal — the message is new, and novelty is suspicious. The verse takes the same fact and asks a question about it instead of accepting it as an objection.

Yā-Sīn supplies the context: the Prophet was sent *"so that you may warn a people whose forefathers were not warned, and so are heedless."* (36:6). The absence of earlier warning is the reason the warning is being given now, and the verse there treats it as the ground of the mission rather than as an objection to it.

That reversal is what this verse is performing. The audience's argument from novelty is turned into the explanation for the message's arrival, and the question form leaves the audience to notice the turn.

**The Forefathers as an Authority**

The appeal to the forefathers runs through the Qur'an's accounts of refusal, and it is usually the strongest argument the objectors have. The sūrah has recorded it in the mouths of the chiefs earlier, and it appears in nearly every prophetic narrative.

The verse's question attacks the appeal at its weakest point. If the forefathers are the standard, then the forefathers received nothing, and a standard that received nothing cannot certify anything about what has now been received. The argument from the ancestors defeats itself the moment it is applied to revelation.

The sūrah does not spell this out. It asks the question and moves to the next one, leaving the collapse of the argument to the reader.

The collapse is complete. If the ancestors are the measure, then nothing new could ever be received, and the community that made the objection would have had no revelation of its own either. The argument proves too much, and the verse's question is enough to show it.

**Why the Denial?**

The heading the translation inserts at the end of this verse is a fair summary of the passage's method. The sūrah has described the refusal, the night talk, the turning away — and then it begins asking why.

The questions that follow are rhetorical in a specific sense. Each proposes an explanation and each is answered by the verse's own continuation, so that the reader is led through the available reasons and finds none of them sufficient. The denial is not explained by ignorance, by novelty, by unfamiliarity with the messenger, or by madness.

The sūrah's method here is diagnostic rather than accusatory. It does not assert that the chiefs are dishonest; it examines the explanations their position would require and finds them wanting.

The method is more effective than accusation, and deliberately so. A reader who is told the chiefs are liars can dismiss the claim as partisan. A reader who works through the four explanations himself arrives at the same conclusion on his own evidence.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:69

> Or ˹because˺ they failed to recognize their Messenger, and so they denied him

**Expanded Commentary**

**Not a Stranger**

The verse proposes an explanation for the denial and immediately makes it implausible. Did they not know their Messenger? The possessive is the point — *their* Messenger, a man who belonged to the community that was denying him.

The commentators are unanimous on the historical fact behind the question. The Prophet was not a marginal or unknown figure. He came from a noble lineage, though not a wealthy one, and he was famous in Mecca for honesty and trustworthiness before anything was revealed to him.

That reputation is what makes the verse's question rhetorical in the strict sense. A reader who knew the man could not answer yes. The denial therefore cannot be explained by unfamiliarity, and the verse removes that explanation before offering any other.

The possessive does further work. He is not a messenger who happened to arrive; he is their messenger, from their own lineage and known by their own naming. The denial is therefore not a judgement about a stranger.

**Al-Amīn**

The epithet recorded in the biographical tradition is *al-Amīn*, the Trustworthy, and it was given to him by the same people who later called him a fabricator and a madman. The title is not a Muslim attribution; it is what his own community called him.

The verse's force depends on that sequence. A man known as the Trustworthy for decades is not a plausible candidate for the charge of fabrication, and the charge was made anyway. The sūrah does not argue the point. It asks whether they knew him, and the answer supplies the argument.

The sūrah has recorded the fabrication charge twice already, in the mouths of two earlier peoples. In both of those cases the messenger was known to his people, and in both the charge was made by men who had dealings with him.

**Knowledge Without Recognition**

The verse distinguishes between knowing a person and recognising what he carries. The chiefs knew the man; the question is whether they recognised the message, and the verse's grammar connects the two with a consequence: they did not know him, *and so* they denied him.

The commentators read the connection as ironic. The men who denied him knew him better than anyone, and the denial was not a failure of information. The verse proposes ignorance as an explanation in order to close it off.

That is the same method the previous verse used with contemplation. Did they not contemplate the Word? The question is not asking for information; it is eliminating an excuse. Both verses work by proposing the most sympathetic available explanation and showing that it does not fit the facts.

The method leaves the audience with nothing to argue against. A charge can be denied; a question about what they already know cannot, and the sūrah's series of questions is built entirely out of the second kind.

**Noble but Not Wealthy**

The biographical detail about lineage matters for the objection recorded earlier in the sūrah. Pharaoh's chiefs asked whether they should believe in two humans whose people were slaves to them, and the Meccan chiefs made a version of the same objection about a man who ate food and walked in the markets.

The Prophet's position was between the two. He was of noble descent and modest means, which meant the objection from status was available but not decisive. He could not be dismissed as a slave, and he could not be honoured as a patron.

The verse's question therefore lands precisely. The man they denied was neither a stranger nor a superior — he was one of them, known by name, and the denial required no ignorance to perform.

That position also made the denial costlier. A messenger from outside can be dismissed as ignorant of local custom. A messenger from inside the same lineage has no such excuse, and the sūrah's question points at exactly that fact.

**The Question's Place in the Series**

This verse is the third in a sequence of questions, and the sequence is ordered. Did they not contemplate the Word? Has something come to them that did not come to their forefathers? Do they not know their Messenger?

Each question addresses a different possible ground for refusal: the content, its novelty, and the person carrying it. The sūrah works through them in order, and each is closed by the facts the audience already knows.

The ordering is deliberate. The content comes first because it is the ground a sincere objector would use. The messenger comes last because it is the ground the audience could check most easily, and the checking refutes it.

The series also moves from the hardest question to the easiest. Contemplating the Qur'an requires work; remembering a neighbour's reputation requires nothing. The sūrah ends where the answer is cheapest and the refusal least excusable.

**The Trustworthiness Recorded Elsewhere**

The Qur'an makes the same appeal through the mouths of earlier messengers. Hūd said to his people: *"I am truly a trustworthy messenger to you."* (26:107), and the same phrase appears in the accounts of Nūḥ, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ and Shuʿayb in that sūrah.

The repetition establishes that trustworthiness is part of the messenger's credentials, and that it is claimed publicly rather than assumed. The verse in al-Muʾminūn does not make the claim for the Prophet; it asks whether his people knew him, and leaves the claim to their own memory of what they used to call him.

The difference in method is the point. Ash-Shuʿarāʾ has the messenger assert his trustworthiness to people who dispute it. This verse asks a question whose answer the audience already knows, and the known answer does the work the assertion would have had to do.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:70

> Or ˹because˺ they say, “He is insane?” In fact, he has come to them with the truth, but most of them are resentful of the truth

**Expanded Commentary**

**Majnūn: The Fourth Explanation**

The verse gives the last of the four explanations the sūrah examines, and it is the bluntest. The charge is *majnūn* — possessed, mad, a man acted on by something other than himself.

The sūrah has already recorded the same charge twice in the mouths of earlier peoples. Nūḥ's chiefs said of him: *"This is only a human like you, who wants to be superior to you,"* (23:24), and then: *"He is simply insane, so bear with him for a while."* (23:25). Pharaoh said of Mūsā: *"Your messenger, who has been sent to you, must be insane."* (26:27).

The recurrence is the sūrah's clearest statement about what the charge is. It appears in every account, in the same word, made by men who knew the messenger personally. A diagnosis that recurs identically across unrelated centuries is not a diagnosis; it is a standard reply.

**What the Charge Does**

The charge of insanity is not a claim about the messenger's behaviour in any detail. Nobody in the accounts offers symptoms, episodes or evidence. The word is offered as a complete answer, and it functions by removing the need for one.

That is why the sūrah examines it last. A person who calls a messenger mad does not have to engage with what he said, because a madman's statements are not claims. The charge closes the discussion absolutely, and it does so without requiring the person making it to have read or heard anything.

The previous verse asked whether they knew the Messenger. The men who knew him best made this charge, and the sūrah places the two verses adjacent for that reason.

The adjacency is the argument. Familiarity is usually the strongest ground for trust, and here it produced the harshest charge available. The sūrah does not comment on the reversal; it sets the two verses side by side.

**"He Has Come to Them With the Truth"**

The verse answers the charge with a flat assertion rather than an argument. Rather — he has brought them the truth. No evidence is offered, no reasoning is set out, and none is needed at this point in the passage.

The sūrah has spent the preceding verses eliminating every alternative explanation. The Word was recited and not contemplated. The novelty was turned into the reason for the message. The messenger was known and trusted. What remains after those eliminations is the assertion, and the assertion stands on the elimination.

That method is unusual and worth naming. The verse does not prove the truth of the message; it removes the reasons for denying it, and then states the message's character as what is left.

The method also matches the audience's situation. These were people who had heard the recitation for years. They did not need evidence; they needed the excuses cleared away, and the four questions do exactly that.

**Kārihūn: Resentful of the Truth**

The closing clause names the actual reason, and the word is *kārihūn* — people who are averse, who find the thing distasteful. The commentators specify the causes: jealousy, and attachment to inherited custom.

The word is not about belief. A person can find something true and still hate it, and the verse describes people in that position. The objection is not that the message is false; it is that the message is unwelcome.

That diagnosis explains everything the sūrah has recorded. Men who find the truth distasteful will produce explanations for it, and the explanations will be the ones available: novelty, madness, magic, poetry. The sūrah has now listed four and closed all four.

The commentators' two causes — jealousy and attachment to custom — cover both directions. Jealousy is about the messenger; custom is about the community. Between them they account for the personal and the social halves of the refusal.

**Most of Them**

The verse says most of them are averse, not all. The qualifier is small and it matters. It leaves room for the people who were not, and the sūrah's audience included some of them.

The same qualifier appears throughout the Qur'an's statements about refusal, and it functions the same way each time. A blanket condemnation would let an individual reader place himself outside it; a statement about most people requires him to ask which group he is in.

The sūrah has used the device before. The believers were described as racing toward good; the disbelievers' hearts were described as submerged; and the verse here says most of them resent the truth. The reader is left to locate himself, and the locating is the verse's purpose.

The qualifier also keeps the passage honest. Some of the audience did accept the message, and the sūrah knows it. A statement about everyone would have been false as well as less useful.

**The Charge Elsewhere**

Sabaʾ puts the two charges together in a single question: *"Has he fabricated a lie against Allah or is he insane?"* (34:8). Ad-Dukhān records the same pair: *"then they turned away from him, saying, 'A madman, taught by others!'"* (44:14).

The three sūrahs give the same two accusations in the same order, and the pairing is instructive. Fabrication implies intent; insanity removes it. The two charges cannot both be true, and the objectors offer both, which is the clearest available evidence that neither is being put forward as a considered judgement.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn adds the third charge from the same repertoire — taught by others, or a fabricator — and the three together form a set. Each is available, none is evidenced, and any of them will do.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:71

> Had the truth followed their desires, the heavens, the earth, and all those in them would have certainly been corrupted. In fact, We have brought them ˹the means to˺ their glory, but they turn away from it

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Conditional That Reverses the Complaint**

The verse opens with a conditional whose premise is absurd, and the absurdity is the argument. Had the truth followed their desires — the corruption described would already have happened. The objectors had been treating the revelation as an imposition of someone's preference; the verse shows what would follow if preference were the measure.

The commentators give the conditional two closely related senses. Either: if the truth were in accord with their desires. Or: if the upholder of truth were to obey their desires. Both readings make the same point — that the revelation is not adjusted to what its hearers want, and that the absence of adjustment is not a defect in it.

The reversal is complete. The complaint was that the message did not suit them. The verse answers that a message suited to them would have destroyed the order they live inside.

**Desire as Chaos**

The reasoning behind the conditional is worth stating, because the verse does not state it. Human desires are not merely various; they conflict. What one person wants excludes what another wants, and a reality arranged around wanting would be arranged around contradiction.

Al-Anbiyāʾ makes the same argument about the divine order: *"Had there been other gods besides Allah in the heavens or the earth, both ˹realms˺ would have surely been corrupted."* (21:22). The structure of the two verses is identical — a plurality of wills produces corruption, and the existing order is evidence against it.

The verse extends the argument from gods to desires. A world governed by competing divinities and a world governed by competing appetites fail for the same reason, and the failure described in both cases is corruption rather than mere disagreement.

Corruption is the word both verses use, and it is stronger than disorder. Disorder can be rearranged; corruption is a thing losing the properties that made it what it was. The heavens and the earth in the conditional are not merely badly run — they are undone.

**"We Have Brought Them Their Reminder"**

The second half of the verse changes register. The reminder — *dhikr* — is given to them, and the commentators take it as the Qur'an and the truths it contains. The word carries the sense of something that recalls what is already known rather than something entirely new.

That sense matters for the verse's argument. A reminder is not an imposition of a foreign standard; it is a return to something the hearer already has. The verse therefore answers the complaint about novelty raised two verses earlier: what has come is not an alien thing but a recalling.

The phrase also names what has been done for them. The reminder was brought; the bringing is complete; and the turning away described in the clause that follows is theirs.

The sūrah keeps that division of responsibility throughout. The messenger delivers and the delivery is recorded as complete; the response is described separately and attributed to the responders. The verse states both halves in a single sentence.

**"Their Reminder"**

The possessive is the sharpest word in the sentence. It is not a reminder belonging to someone else, delivered across a distance. It is theirs, and the verse says they turned away from it.

The same possessive appears in the question about the Messenger two verses earlier — their Messenger, a man from their own lineage. The sūrah has been building a picture of a message that belongs to the people refusing it: their word, their messenger, their reminder.

That accumulation makes the turning away harder to explain, which is the sūrah's purpose. The four questions asked in the preceding verses eliminated the available explanations, and this verse supplies the fact that remains after the eliminations.

The possessive also removes the most comfortable reading available to the audience. A reminder belonging to somebody else can be set aside as not addressed to you. A reminder described as yours cannot, and the verse's wording leaves no room for the distinction.

**Corruption as the Alternative**

The verse's claim about what desire would produce is not hypothetical padding. It is the ground on which the whole passage stands, and it explains why the sūrah treats the objections so briefly.

If a truth adjusted to appetite would corrupt the order of things, then the objections raised against an unadjusted truth are not evidence against it. They are what an unadjusted truth should expect. The verse removes the force of the complaints by showing what their satisfaction would cost.

The sūrah has recorded the complaints at length precisely so that the reader can see them against this background. Novelty, madness, madness again, ownership, status — every one of them is a request that the truth be adjusted, and the verse states what adjustment produces.

That is why the sūrah does not rebut them individually. A complaint that asks for adjustment does not need answering; it needs to be shown what it is asking for. The first clause of the verse does exactly that, in one sentence.

**The Reminder Turned Away From**

The verse ends where the passage began. The reminder was brought; they turned away from it. The same verb of turning appears in the account of the recitation earlier in the passage, where the people were described as backing away in disgust.

The two verses describe the same movement from two sides. That one names the physical withdrawal; this one names what is withdrawn from. Between them they complete the sūrah's diagnosis: the message was delivered in an audible form, to a people who knew the messenger, and the response was movement away.

Nothing in the verse suggests the turning away was caused by ignorance, and the four questions have already closed that explanation. What remains is preference, and the first clause of the verse has described what a world built on preference would be.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:72

> Or ˹is it because˺ you ˹O Prophet˺ are asking them for tribute? But the reward of your Lord is best, for He is the Best Provider

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth Question**

The verse continues the series of questions, and it introduces a new kind of objection. The previous four concerned the message, its novelty, the messenger's identity and his sanity. This one concerns money.

The question is phrased as a possibility: is it because you ask them for a payment? The implied answer is no, and the audience knew it was no. The Prophet had not asked for anything, and the sūrah's audience had dealings with him over years in which no payment was requested.

That makes the question rhetorical in the strictest sense. It proposes an explanation that the people proposing it know to be false, and the proposing is a way of closing off the explanation before anyone can use it.

The sūrah has used the same device four times already, and each time the proposed explanation was one the audience had actually offered. This one is different: nobody in Mecca had claimed the Prophet was after their money, because he had never asked for any.

**No Payment: The Messengers' Standing Refusal**

The refusal of payment is one of the Qur'an's most repeated statements, and its repetition is the point. Hūd says to his people: *"O my people! I do not ask you for a payment for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from Allah."* (11:29). The same sentence appears in the mouths of Nūḥ, Ibrāhīm's people's messengers, Lūṭ and Shuʿayb in ash-Shuʿarāʾ (26:109 and four further places).

Sabaʾ puts it as a conditional: *"Say, 'If I had ever asked you for a reward, you could keep it. My reward is only from Allah. And He is a Witness over all things.'"* (34:47). Ṣād adds a second denial beside it: *"I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹Quran˺, nor do I pretend to be someone I am not."* (38:86).

The consistency across so many messengers establishes that the refusal is part of the office rather than a personal choice, and the verse here states it for the Prophet in the same terms.

**Why the Refusal Matters**

A messenger who is paid can be dismissed as an interested party. The refusal removes that dismissal, and the sūrah's question is aimed at exactly this.

Yā-Sīn turns the refusal into a criterion for the hearers: *"Follow those who ask no reward of you, and are ˹rightly˺ guided."* (36:21). The verse there makes the absence of payment a mark to look for, which means its presence would be a mark against.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn uses the same logic in the form of a question. It does not tell the audience to follow the unpaid messenger; it asks whether the demand for payment is the reason for the refusal, and the question cannot be answered yes.

Yā-Sīn's formulation is the sharpest version of the point. It makes the absence of payment something to look for in a guide, which means its presence would be a disqualification. The verse here relies on the same standard without stating it.

**"The Reward of Your Lord Is Best"**

The second clause states what the Prophet has instead of a payment, and the statement is comparative rather than absolute. His Lord's reward is *better* — better than what any audience could give.

The comparison is doing work. A man who refuses payment might be supposed to be working for nothing, and the verse corrects that reading. He is not unpaid; he is paid by someone whose payment exceeds what the audience could offer, which removes the suggestion of self-sacrifice without return.

The clause also answers the objection from the other direction. A man seeking wealth would seek it from the wealthy, and the Prophet's refusal of the wealthy men's money is evidence about where he expects his return to come from.

The comparison has a second edge. The men who might have paid him were the men refusing him, which means the payment was never available in the first place. The verse's comparison is not between two offers but between an offer and a source.

**"He Is the Best of Providers"**

The verse closes with a description of God as the best of providers, using the same vocabulary the sūrah used earlier for provision — the good things the messengers were told to eat.

The connection is exact. The messengers were commanded to take the good provision and act rightly; here the Prophet is told that his provision comes from the best of providers. The two verses describe the same arrangement from two sides: the provision is to be taken, and its source is named.

That naming also settles the question the verse raises. A man whose provision comes from God has no reason to ask his audience for anything, and the audience's suspicion that he is after their money is answered by the identity of his provider rather than by his denial.

**The Question's Place in the Series**

The five questions now cover the ground completely. The content of the message, its novelty, the messenger's identity, his sanity, and his motives. Each is proposed and each is closed by facts the audience already possesses.

The ordering is deliberate. The motive comes last because it is the most practical objection and the easiest to check. A man's financial arrangements are visible, and the Prophet's were visible to everyone in Mecca.

The sūrah ends the series where the answer is cheapest and most public, and the ending is the measure of how little the refusal had to work with.

The motive question also closes the series in the right direction. The four earlier questions were about the message and the man; this one is about money, which is the objection an audience would actually be embarrassed to make aloud. The sūrah makes it for them and answers it in the same breath.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:73

> And surely you are calling them to the Straight Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣirāṭ Mustaqīm: The Straight Path**

The phrase is the one every Muslim recites daily in the opening sūrah: *"Guide us along the Straight Path,"* (1:6). The verse here states that the Prophet is calling to it, which connects his mission to the prayer the community makes.

The word *ṣirāṭ* carries the sense of a wide, clear road — not a narrow track or a hidden way. The path being called to is not esoteric and not restricted to an initiated few. It is described as the ordinary route, and the adjective added to it is straightness rather than exclusivity.

That description answers an objection the sūrah has been addressing. A message requiring special knowledge or special status could be dismissed as belonging to somebody else. A straight, wide road is available to anyone standing on it or beside it.

**Calling, Not Compelling**

The verb is *tadʿū*, you call or invite. The verse does not say the Prophet brings them, drives them, or delivers them. It says he calls, and the calling is the whole of his part.

The distinction is the sūrah's consistent position on the messenger's function. He has been described throughout as a deliverer: the message is brought, the delivery is complete, and the response belongs to the hearer. The verb here is the same one used for the earlier messengers, and it carries the same limit.

That limit is also what makes the following verse possible. If the messenger compelled, nobody could deviate. The verse describes a call, and the next verse describes people leaving the path it leads to.

The limit is also what protects the messenger from the outcome. A man who calls has delivered when the call is heard, and the sūrah has described the delivery as complete before any response is recorded.

**The Verse's Position in the Series**

This verse follows the five questions and does not ask one. The series has closed the available explanations, and the verse states what remains: the call itself, and its character.

The shift from question to statement is significant. A question invites the hearer to supply an answer; a statement asks him to accept or reject one. The sūrah has finished eliminating the excuses and now describes the thing the excuses were being made about.

The description is brief and unqualified. The path is straight; the call is to it; nothing is added about difficulty, obscurity or cost. The plainness of the description is the answer to the objections.

That plainness is also what makes the following verse possible. A path described as narrow or hidden could not be left by accident, and the deviation the next verse describes would need a different explanation.

**Straightness as the Defining Property**

The adjective is what the verse insists on, and straightness has a specific implication. A straight path does not require interpretation to follow; it requires only that a person not leave it.

The following verse describes exactly that — people deviating from the path, using a verb of leaving rather than of misunderstanding. The deviation is not an intellectual failure. It is a movement off a road that remains visible.

Al-Fātiḥah's continuation describes the path by whose path it is: *"the Path of those You have blessed—not those You are displeased with, or those who are astray."* (1:7). The straight path is identified by the people on it, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn has spent its length describing both groups.

The identification by company rather than by rule is worth noting. The path is not defined by a list of requirements but by the people walking it, and the sūrah has described those people in detail — the ones who tremble, who read the signs, who race toward good.

**The Call and the Reminder**

The previous verse described the message as a reminder brought to them. This verse describes the same thing as a call to a path, and the two descriptions belong together.

A reminder recalls something known; a path is something walked. Between them they describe a message that is neither new information nor abstract doctrine. It is a return to something already recognised and a direction to move in, and the sūrah's audience had both available.

The verse's brevity is consistent with that. Nothing needs to be explained about a straight road. It needs to be pointed at, and the verse points.

The pairing also answers the charge of madness from the previous verses. A man calling people to a road they already know is not proposing anything that requires special insight, and the plainness of the call is what makes the charges against it implausible.

**The Path in Other Places**

Al-Anʿām states the same claim in the form of a command: the straight path is God's, and the Prophet is told to follow it and not to follow other ways, because they lead away from His (6:153). The plurality of other ways is contrasted with the singularity of the straight one.

That contrast is the background of the sūrah's account of the sects. The people who divided the religion into portions are described earlier as rejoicing in what they have, and the verse in al-Anʿām describes what divided ways do. The call recorded here is to the single road, and the sūrah has already described the alternative.

The verse in al-Anʿām also states that the other ways lead away from God's way, which is what the following verse in al-Muʾminūn describes as deviation. The two sūrahs give the same account in different registers — one as a command, the other as an observation.

The command form is worth noting for what it implies about the danger. A path that other ways lead away from is not merely one option among several; leaving it is a movement with a direction, and the sūrah names that direction in the verse that follows.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:74

> but those who disbelieve in the Hereafter are certainly deviating from that Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deviation Is Named, Not Explained**

The verse states a correlation and does not argue for it. Those who disbelieve in the Hereafter deviate from the path. No reason is given, no mechanism is described, and no evidence is offered.

That economy is deliberate. The sūrah has spent the preceding verses eliminating explanations for the refusal, and this verse supplies the one that remains. The deviation is not caused by the messenger, by the message's novelty, by unfamiliarity or by payment. It is caused by a disbelief stated in a single phrase.

The verse's flatness is its force. A reader who has followed the five questions arrives here with nothing left to object with, and the verse states the diagnosis without elaboration.

The absence of argument is also a claim. A correlation this direct does not need defending, and the sūrah's decision not to defend it suggests that the connection is one the audience already recognised.

**Disbelief in the Hereafter as the Cause**

The specific item named is disbelief in the Hereafter, and the choice is precise. Of all the things the audience disputed, this is the one the verse identifies as decisive.

The reasoning is not spelled out, and it does not need to be. A person who does not believe the matter will be settled has no reason to leave a comfortable position. The path is straight and visible, as the previous verse says; leaving it requires a reason, and the absence of a Hereafter supplies one.

The sūrah has described this disbelief elsewhere in the passage. The barrier placed during the recitation is tied to disbelief in the Hereafter in al-Isrāʾ (17:45), and Sabaʾ says of those who make the charges of fabrication and madness that they do not believe in the Hereafter (34:8).

**Jāʾirūn: Leaning Away**

The word for deviating comes from a root meaning to incline or to swerve — to move off a line while still able to see it. It is not the word for being lost or for ignorance.

The distinction matters. A person who is lost cannot be blamed for his position. A person who leans away from a visible road can, and the verse describes the second. The path was called straight in the previous verse precisely so that this verse could describe leaving it as a swerve.

The two verses form a pair: a straight road, and a movement off it. Nothing in the pair suggests the road was hard to find, and that absence is the verse's argument.

The verb also removes the excuse of confusion. A person who swerves from a visible line is not mistaken about where the line is, and the sūrah has spent five verses closing every other explanation for the movement.

**The Verse's Address to the Sects**

The passage began with a description of communities that divided the religion into portions, each rejoicing in what it had. The verse here describes deviation from a single path, and the two descriptions are related.

A path that can be left can also be replaced, and the sects are people who have replaced it. The sūrah does not make the connection explicitly, but the vocabulary of the passage — one religion, one path, portions, deviation — is consistent throughout.

The connection also explains the sūrah's insistence on straightness. A straight path has no portions. It cannot be divided without ceasing to be the path, and the division described earlier is therefore a departure from it rather than a variant of it.

The two accounts answer each other. The sects are described as rejoicing in what they have; the deviators are described as leaning away from a single road. Both descriptions are about people who have substituted something of their own for what was given.

**Disbelief in the Hereafter Elsewhere**

Sabaʾ links the same disbelief to the same charges: *"Has he fabricated a lie against Allah or is he insane?" In fact, those who do not believe in the Hereafter are bound for torment and have strayed farthest ˹from the truth˺."* (34:8).

The parallel is close enough to be a companion verse. It gives the charges this passage has just examined, and it names the same underlying disbelief as the cause. The two sūrahs agree that the accusations are downstream of a position about the Hereafter, and that the position is what produces them.

Al-Isrāʾ makes the same connection during the recitation itself, describing the barrier as falling between the Prophet and those who do not believe in the Hereafter (17:45). The three verses describe one condition from three angles.

The consistency across the three is what makes the diagnosis secure. A single verse could be read as a local remark about one group; three verses in three sūrahs, tying the same disbelief to the same turning away, describe something structural.

**What the Verse Leaves Open**

The verse describes a group and its condition, and it does not name anyone in it. The audience is left to ask the question the verse implies, and the asking is the verse's purpose.

The sūrah has used the same device repeatedly. Most of them resent the truth. Their hearts are submerged. They turn away from their reminder. Each statement describes a group without naming its members, and each leaves the identification with the reader.

The verse is therefore not a verdict on the audience but a description offered to it. A person who believes in the Hereafter has been told what follows; a person who does not has been told what his position produces.

The openness is what makes the verse usable. A named condemnation would let an unnamed reader stand outside it. A description of a condition leaves every reader to ask whether the condition is his.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:75

> ˹Even˺ if We had mercy on them and removed their affliction, they would still persist in their transgression, wandering blindly

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Mercy That Would Change Nothing**

The verse describes a hypothetical act of mercy and states its result in advance. The affliction is removed; the people persist. The mercy is real, the relief is real, and the outcome is unchanged.

That structure is the verse's argument. The audience had been treating hardship as the thing preventing belief — a position the sūrah examines here and rejects. The verse says the removal of hardship would not produce belief, and it says so as a counterfactual: even if.

The commentators take the affliction as hunger and worldly hardship, or as trials such as warfare. Both readings support the same point, and the verse's wording does not require a choice.

The counterfactual form is also worth holding on to. The verse does not say the mercy was withheld; it says that even if it had been given, the result would have been the same. The audience is being told something about itself rather than about God's dealings with it.

**Az-Zukhruf: The Same Sequence**

The Qur'an records the sequence as an event elsewhere: *"But as soon as We removed the torments from them, they broke their promise."* (43:50). The verse there is past tense and describes a people who were relieved and then reneged.

The two verses belong together. Az-Zukhruf supplies the precedent; this verse states what would happen again. A reader who has read both understands that the counterfactual is not speculative — it is a description of a pattern that has already been observed.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn has been describing patterns throughout its middle section, and this verse continues the method. The pattern is stated as a rule, and the reader is left to apply it.

Applying it requires no special knowledge. The audience had seen the affliction lifted and had watched what followed, which is exactly the sequence az-Zukhruf records for an earlier people.

**Ṭughyānihim: Persisting in Transgression**

The word for transgression is from the same root the sūrah used for Pharaoh in Ṭā-Hā — the one who exceeded all bounds. The verse describes persistence in that condition, and the persistence is the point.

A person who transgresses under hardship might be supposed to be reacting to it. A person who persists in transgression after the hardship is removed is not reacting to anything; he is continuing in a settled state. The verse removes the excuse that hardship supplies.

The sūrah has described the same settled state earlier, in the hearts that are submerged and occupied with other deeds. The persistence here is the outward form of that condition.

The word also connects this verse to the account of Pharaoh, where the same root described a ruler who had exceeded every limit. The audience's transgression is placed in the same category as his, and the category is defined by persistence rather than by scale.

**Wandering Blindly**

The closing phrase describes the manner of the persistence: wandering in confusion, or blind wandering. The commentators connect it to a state of spiritual blindness, and the phrase appears in several places with that sense.

Al-Baqarah uses it of the mockers: *"Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance."* (2:15). Al-Anʿām uses it of those whose hearts and eyes are turned away (6:110). In both cases the wandering follows a refusal rather than preceding it.

That sequence is what this verse describes. The wandering is not a search; it is what remains after a search has been abandoned. The people are moving, and the movement has no direction.

**Why the Verse Comes Here**

The verse follows the statement about the straight path and the deviation from it. A reader might suppose the deviation is caused by hardship, and that relief would end it. The verse closes that supposition.

The placement is therefore argumentative. The path is straight and visible; the deviation is a swerve; and the swerve is not caused by circumstances. The verse removes the last sympathetic explanation available to the deviators.

The sūrah has now eliminated every explanation for the refusal: the content, the novelty, the messenger, his sanity, his motives, and his audience's hardship. What remains is the position itself.

The elimination is cumulative, and the verse's position at the end of it is what gives it weight. A reader who has followed the passage arrives here having been shown that nothing outside the person explains the refusal, and the verse describes what is left inside him.

**Humility in Affliction**

Al-Anʿām describes the purpose affliction is meant to serve: *"Indeed, We have sent messengers before you ˹O Prophet˺ to other people who We put through suffering and adversity ˹for their denial˺, so perhaps they would be humbled. Why did they not humble themselves when We made them suffer? Instead, their hearts were hardened."* (6:42-43).

The two verses describe the same arrangement from opposite ends. Affliction is sent so that people may be humbled; this verse says the humility would not come, and that relief would not produce it either. Between them they describe a condition in which neither hardship nor ease changes the outcome.

That condition is the sūrah's hardest claim. Most accounts of refusal leave room for circumstance — if only things had been easier, if only the messenger had been different. The verse closes both openings at once.

It also removes the reader's own version of the excuse. Everyone has a circumstance that would make belief easier if only it were different, and the verse describes a people given exactly that circumstance and left unchanged by it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:76

> And We have already seized them with torment, but they never humbled themselves to their Lord, nor did they ˹submissively˺ appeal ˹to Him˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Torment Already Sent**

The verse is past tense, and the tense is the point. The affliction described in the previous verse as a hypothetical has already happened. The seizure is not a warning about the future; it is a record.

The commentators identify the torment as hunger and drought — the afflictions the Quraysh suffered in the years before the conquest. The identification matters because it makes the verse a statement about events the audience lived through rather than about a distant people.

That is the sūrah's method throughout its Meccan section. It describes the audience's own history in the same vocabulary it used for the destroyed communities, and the shared vocabulary is the argument.

The identification also makes the verse checkable. The audience knew when the hunger came and how long it lasted, and they knew what they did during it. The verse asks them to look at a period they lived through.

**They Did Not Become Humble**

The verb is *yataḍarraʿū*, from a root meaning to humble oneself, to make oneself small. The verse says they did not do it, and the negation is attached to a specific response rather than to belief in general.

Humility here is not a feeling. It is a posture toward God produced by hardship, and the sūrah has already described the purpose affliction serves: *"so perhaps they would be humbled"* (6:42). The verse reports that the purpose was not achieved.

Al-Anʿām continues with the consequence: *"Instead, their hearts were hardened, and Satan made their misdeeds appealing to them."* (6:43). The two verses describe the same failure and the same result, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn stops at the failure.

Stopping there is a choice. The sūrah does not explain why the humility did not come; it records that it did not, and leaves the next verse to describe what follows. The economy keeps the focus on the refusal rather than on its causes.

**Nor Did They Appeal**

The second clause adds a second thing they did not do, and the commentators read it as supplication or as a turning toward God in submission. The two are related but distinct: humility is a posture; appeal is an act.

The verse denies both. The people neither became small before God nor asked Him for anything. The absence of appeal is the sharper denial, because appeal is what a person in hardship naturally does, and the sūrah's audience had a well-developed practice of supplication.

The verse therefore describes something more specific than irreligion. It describes people who had the vocabulary and the practice and did not use them when the hardship came.

The two denials also cover the two directions a person in hardship can move. He can become small before God, or he can ask God for something. The verse closes both, which leaves only the third option — continuing as before.

**The Sequence the Sūrah Has Described**

The verse completes a sequence the passage has been assembling. The messenger called; the people turned away; the affliction came; they did not humble themselves; the next verse describes a gate of severe punishment opening.

Every step follows the previous one, and the sequence is the sūrah's argument. Nothing in it is arbitrary. The seizure in verse seventy-six comes after a refusal that was already complete, and the despair in verse seventy-seven comes after a humility that was never offered.

The sūrah has described the same sequence for four earlier peoples, with four different mechanisms. This verse describes it for the audience's own community, and the mechanism is hunger rather than a blast or a flood.

The change of mechanism is the point. The earlier accounts could be treated as belonging to a different order of events; hunger in Mecca could not, and the verse places it in the same sequence anyway.

**The Report About Thumāmah**

Some commentators connect the verse with a particular episode from the Prophet's own time. Thumāmah ibn Uthāl, the chief of Banū Ḥanīfah, accepted Islam and afterwards stopped the food supply reaching Mecca, which produced real hardship in the city. The Quraysh appealed to the Prophet to have the restriction ended, arguing that it breached the agreement made at Ḥudaybiyyah, and he agreed to end it.

The report matters for how the verse lands. The suffering was recent and the relief was granted, so the audience had seen both halves of the sequence the verse describes. What the verse says is that the second half did not produce what the first was meant to produce.

The sūrah names no event, and this identification is one reading among several. The verse states a pattern, and the episode is offered as an instance of it.

**Humility in Affliction Elsewhere**

Al-Aʿrāf states the principle in its general form: *"Whenever We sent a prophet to a society, We afflicted its ˹disbelieving˺ people with suffering and adversity, so perhaps they would be humbled."* (7:94).

The verse supplies the purpose this verse reports as unfulfilled. Affliction is sent with an aim, and the aim is humility rather than destruction. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn records that the aim was missed, and the next verse describes what follows a missed aim.

The two verses together describe an arrangement in which hardship is offered as an opportunity. The verse here says the opportunity was not taken, and the sūrah has already established that the interval in which it could be taken has a fixed length.

The fixed length is what makes the report serious. An opportunity inside an open-ended interval can be taken later; an opportunity inside a term that cannot be extended cannot, and the sūrah has stated the term twice before reaching this verse.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:77

> But as soon as We open for them a gate of severe punishment, they will be utterly desperate

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Gate, Not a Door**

The word is *bāb*, a gate, and the image is of something large and hinged — a thing that opens and cannot be shut again from the inside. The verse describes the severe punishment as arriving through a gate, and the gate belongs to God.

The commentators give the gate three referents: the gates of Hell, the conquest of Mecca, and the Muslim victory at Badr. The three differ in time and in kind, and the verse's wording allows all of them.

That openness is characteristic of the sūrah's eschatological language. It describes the shape of an event rather than its date, and the shape here is a closing — a point after which the interval described in the preceding verses is over.

The gate is also one-way. Nothing in the verse suggests it can be shut again from the inside, and the despair named in the second clause is what a person feels once he understands that.

**Mubsilūn: Utterly Desperate**

The word describes people who have given up, who have exhausted their options. It is not fear and not pain; it is the recognition that nothing further can be done.

The sūrah has already used the same word for the people destroyed by the blast, in al-Anʿām's account: they were seized by surprise and fell instantly into despair (6:44). The parallel is exact, and it places this verse inside the same sequence.

Despair is what remains when the interval closes. The verse describes it as the immediate and only response, and the absence of any other response is the point.

The sūrah has described crying for help earlier, and that cry was refused. Here there is no cry at all — only the recognition that nothing further can be done, which is a further stage than the refused plea.

**"Until": The Same Particle**

The verse opens with the same particle the sūrah used for the seizure of the affluent: *ḥattā*, until. The two verses mark the same kind of moment — the end of the period during which anything could still be done.

The sūrah has established the rule for that period twice: the term cannot be advanced or delayed, and the messengers are to be left for a while. This verse describes what the while ends in.

The particle also carries the reader across the gap. Nothing is narrated between the refusal and the gate. The verse moves from the people's condition to the opening without an intervening account, and the speed of the transition is the sūrah's description of how the ending arrives.

The same compression appears in the account of Pharaoh's court, where the rejection and the destruction occupy a single verse. The sūrah consistently refuses to narrate the gap, and the refusal is what makes the endings feel sudden.

**The Three Readings**

The commentators' three referents are worth setting out, because they cover the range of the verse's application. The gates of Hell place it in the Hereafter. The conquest of Mecca places it in the Prophet's lifetime, after the revelation. Badr places it inside the audience's own memory.

A reader in Mecca before Badr would take the verse as a warning. A reader after Badr would take it as a fulfilment. A reader at any time can take it as a description of the Hereafter. The verse is written so that all three readings produce the same instruction.

That is why the sūrah does not settle the reference. A dated warning can be survived by waiting; an undated one cannot, and the verse's undated form is what makes it apply to every reader.

**The Gate After the Refused Humility**

The verse follows immediately after the statement that the people did not humble themselves and did not appeal. The sequence is exact: the opportunity, the refusal of the opportunity, the gate.

Nothing intervenes. There is no second chance described, no further affliction sent for the same purpose. The sūrah has already established that affliction is sent so that people may be humbled; this verse describes what follows when they are not.

The arrangement answers a question the reader might raise. If hardship is meant to produce humility, why is more hardship sent? The verse's answer is that the further hardship is not sent for that purpose. It arrives through a gate.

The image of the gate makes the distinction plain. A gate is opened from one side only, and the person it opens onto does not control it. The earlier afflictions were sent with an aim; this one arrives as an ending.

**Despair Elsewhere**

Al-Anʿām describes the same ending for an earlier people: *"But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise, then they instantly fell into despair!"* (6:44).

The parallel supplies the detail this verse compresses. The pride comes first, the seizure follows, and despair is the immediate result. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives the sequence in two verses — the refused humility and the opened gate — and al-Anʿām gives it in one.

Both agree on the order, and the order is the sūrah's argument. The gate opens after the interval, and the interval ends because the humility was not offered.

The pride in al-Anʿām corresponds to the refused humility here. In both accounts the hardship arrives, the response is the opposite of the one intended, and the seizure follows immediately. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives the sequence in three verses; al-Anʿām gives it in one.

The compression in al-Anʿām makes the suddenness visible. Pride, seizure, despair — three states in a single sentence, with nothing narrated between them.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:78

> He is the One Who created for you hearing, sight, and intellect. ˹Yet˺ you hardly give any thanks

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Faculties, One Order**

The verse lists hearing, sight and hearts, and the order is not arbitrary. Hearing comes first, and the sūrah's audience had a specific reason to notice that: the revelation came to them by recitation, through the ear.

The three are named together in several places, and the grouping treats them as a single set of instruments. Al-Anʿām asks: *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Consider! If Allah were to take away your hearing and your sight and seal your hearts, which god other than Allah could restore them to you?'"* (6:46). As-Sajdah describes them as given: *"Then He proportioned him and breathed into him of His Spirit. And He gave you hearing, sight, and intellect—yet you hardly give any thanks."* (32:9).

The grouping matters for the verse's argument. The three are not three separate gifts but one apparatus for knowing, and the verse holds the whole of it against the response it received.

**"For You": The Faculties as Provision**

The preposition is significant. The faculties were made *for you*, and the phrasing treats them as provision rather than as equipment. The sūrah has used the same register throughout — rain in due measure, gardens, livestock, the refuge with flowing water.

That register places hearing, sight and intellect alongside food and water. They are things given for a purpose, and the purpose is not stated here because the sūrah has already stated it: the signs are there to be read, and reading requires a reader.

The verse therefore completes the sūrah's account of provision. The earlier verses described what sustains the body; this one describes what sustains the person's capacity to recognise the Giver.

The sūrah's list of the believers included belief in the signs, and the faculties named here are what that belief is performed with. The verse supplies the instruments for the item in the list.

**The Faculties Can Be Sealed**

The commentators note that the same three faculties are described elsewhere as subject to sealing, covering and blinding. Al-Isrāʾ describes it during the recitation: *"We have cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend it—and deafness in their ears."* (17:46).

The possibility of sealing is what gives the verse its edge. Faculties that cannot fail are not a charge against anyone. Faculties that can be lost, and that are lost through use, are — and the sūrah has described the loss repeatedly.

The connection between the two descriptions is the sūrah's argument about the refusal. The people who turned away from the recitation are the same people described as deaf in their ears, and the deafness is tied to the turning rather than preceding it.

That direction matters. A person born unable to hear is not at fault for not hearing. A person whose deafness follows his refusal to listen is described differently, and the sūrah's order of operations is what makes the charge fair.

**"Little Do You Give Thanks"**

The closing clause is short and quantitative. Not "you do not give thanks" but "little do you give thanks" — the gratitude exists and it is insufficient.

The sūrah has used the same device before. Most of them resent the truth. The qualifier keeps the statement accurate and keeps the reader inside it. A person who gives some thanks has been addressed; a person told he gives none can dismiss the sentence as describing somebody else.

The clause also names the response the provision calls for. The sūrah described the messengers as told to eat the good provision and act righteously, and an-Naḥl attaches gratitude to the eating: *"be grateful for Allah's favours, if you ˹truly˺ worship Him ˹alone˺"* (16:114). The verse here states that the gratitude has not kept pace with the gift.

**The Same Statement in al-Mulk**

Al-Mulk gives the verse in almost identical words: *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'He is the One Who brought you into being and gave you hearing, sight, and intellect. ˹Yet˺ you hardly give any thanks.'"* (67:23).

The near-identity across two sūrahs establishes the statement as a fixed formulation rather than a local remark. The faculties, the giving, and the insufficiency of the thanks: all three elements appear in both, in the same order.

Al-Mulk adds the bringing into being, which places the faculties inside the larger act of creation. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn begins with the same material in its opening verses, and the two sūrahs describe the same sequence from the same starting point.

The shared closing clause is the significant part. Both sūrahs end the statement with the same complaint about insufficient thanks, which suggests the formulation was fixed and that the complaint is the point the faculties are listed to support.

**The Verse After the Gate**

The verse's position is worth noticing. It follows the description of the opened gate and the despair, and it turns from the punishment to the provision.

The turn is the sūrah's method at this point. The warnings have been delivered, and the verse returns to what was given. The reader who has followed the passage is left not with the gate but with the faculties — hearing, sight and intellect — and with the statement that he hardly uses them in thanks.

The arrangement leaves the reader holding something he can act on. The gate is not his to open or shut; the faculties are his, and the verse describes what he has done with them.

The turn is also the sūrah's characteristic move at the end of a warning. It does not leave the reader inside the punishment; it returns him to what was given, and it states the shortfall. The next two verses continue the return.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:79

> And He is the One Who has dispersed you ˹all˺ over the earth, and to Him you will ˹all˺ be gathered

**Expanded Commentary**

**Dharaʾa: Scattered and Multiplied**

The verb is *dharaʾa*, and the commentators give it two related senses: to create, and to spread out or scatter. The verse uses a word that carries both, and the doubling is the point.

Humanity is not merely made; it is made in dispersion. The commentators draw the contrast explicitly — scattered across the earth in this life, gathered on the Day of Resurrection even from its farthest corners.

The pairing of the two senses in one verb is what makes the verse work. A word meaning only "created" would leave the scattering unaccounted for; a word meaning only "scattered" would leave the origin unnamed. The verse needs both, and the verb supplies both.

The commentators draw the contrast with the gathering explicitly, and the contrast gives the verse its shape. Scattered now, gathered then — the two halves of a single statement about how humanity exists and how it ends.

**Dispersion as a Fact to Explain**

The scattering is not presented as a problem or as a punishment. It is stated as a fact about how humanity exists, and the verse moves immediately to the gathering.

That arrangement answers a question the dispersion raises. A species spread across the whole earth, in separated groups with no common authority, looks permanent in its separation. The verse states the separation and then states its end, and the end is what makes the separation intelligible.

The sūrah has used the same method with the interval. The period is fixed and cannot be moved; what matters is what happens inside it. Here the dispersion is the condition and the gathering is the event that gives it a shape.

The same structure governs the verse's treatment of night and day in the next verse. A cycle is described, and then the governance of the cycle is named. The sūrah's method is consistent: state the arrangement, then state who holds it.

**"To Him You Will Be Gathered"**

The gathering is described with a direction: to Him. The verse does not describe a place or an assembly; it describes a movement toward a person.

That is the same formulation the sūrah used for the believers doing good with trembling hearts — they know they are returning to their Lord. The two verses describe the same destination from two sides: the believers know it and act on the knowledge; this verse states it for everyone.

The universality is marked by the pronoun. You will be gathered — all of you, the dispersed included. The scattering does not exempt anyone from the gathering, and the verse's structure makes the point by placing the two clauses side by side.

The destination is also personal rather than local. The gathering is not to a place on the map but to Him, which is what makes the far corners of the earth irrelevant to it.

**The Gathering Elsewhere**

The Qur'an states the gathering repeatedly, and the statements agree on its universality. Al-Anʿām: *"And to Him you will ˹all˺ be gathered."* The phrase appears as a closing formula across many sūrahs, and its function is always the same — to mark the end of the dispersion.

Al-Kahf describes the scene: *"And ˹remember˺ the Day We will cause the mountains to disappear, and you ˹O humanity˺ will see the earth exposed. And We will gather them all together, leaving none behind."* (18:47). The verse there supplies what this one compresses: the completeness of the gathering.

The two verses belong together. This one states the fact; al-Kahf describes it. Between them they establish that the dispersion has an end and that the end leaves nobody out.

**The Verse Inside the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse follows the statement about the faculties and precedes the statement about life and death. The three verses form a sequence: what was given, where humanity is, and what governs it.

The sequence is the sūrah's summary of its own material. The opening verses described creation in stages; the middle described provision and the messengers; the Meccan section described the refusal. These verses return to the beginning and state the arrangement in three clauses.
