The placement is deliberate. After the warnings and the questions, the sūrah restates the facts that make the warnings meaningful — the faculties, the dispersion, the gathering, the alternation of night and day.

None of the three statements is new information for the audience. The restatement is not teaching; it is assembling the material the closing question of the next verse will ask about.

**Scattered and Yet One**

The dispersion and the gathering together answer the sūrah's account of the sects. The communities that divided the religion into portions are scattered in their divisions, and the gathering is what will settle them.

The verse does not make the connection explicitly, but the passage supplies it. One religion, divided into portions; one humanity, scattered across the earth; one gathering, to which all of it returns. The sūrah has described division at both scales, and it describes a single end for both.

The reader is left with the same instruction the passage has been building toward. The divisions look permanent from inside them, and the verse describes the event at which they stop looking that way.

The verse also answers the sects' rejoicing. A portion held as property feels permanent because nothing inside the dispersion challenges it. The gathering is the challenge, and the verse names it in the same breath as the scattering.

The sūrah has kept the two facts adjacent for exactly that reason. Separated, they are two statements about humanity. Together they are an argument about how long a division can last.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:80

> And He is the One Who gives life and causes death, and to Him belongs the alternation of the day and night. Will you not then understand

**Expanded Commentary**

**Life and Death as One Act**

The verse pairs giving life and causing death in a single clause, and the pairing treats them as two halves of one operation rather than as opposites belonging to different orders.

The sūrah opened with the same material. Its first verses described the stages of a human being's making — the drop, the clot, the lump, the bones, the clothing of flesh — and it called the making a creation after another creation. This verse returns to the same subject and names the two ends of it.

The return is the verse's method. After the warnings, the questions and the gate, the sūrah goes back to what it began with, and the going back is an argument: the reader has been shown a great deal, and the verse asks him to look again at the thing he already knew.

**The Alternation Belonging to Him**

The second clause is about the night and the day, and the possession is stated flatly: to Him belongs their alternation. Not their creation only but their turning.

The word for alternation carries the sense of difference and succession — one following the other, differing from it. The verse describes a cycle and assigns its governance, and the assignment is what makes the cycle a sign rather than a fact.

The sūrah has used the same move with the measured rain and the seven levels above. The phenomena are described, and then their governance is named. The naming is what converts a description into an argument.

The alternation is also the most familiar of the signs the sūrah lists. Nobody has to be persuaded that night follows day, and that familiarity is what makes the ownership claim worth making.

**Night and Day in the Passage**

The verse's mention of the night is not incidental to the passage. Two verses earlier the sūrah described the idolaters gathering at night beside the Kaʿbah to produce their account of the Qur'an, and it treated the timing as part of the charge.

The alternation described here includes those nights. The cycle that belongs to God is the same cycle inside which the assembly met, and the verse's placement lets the reader make the connection.

The sūrah has described the believers' deeds as done with trembling hearts and the disbelievers' talk as done at night. The verse that governs both time-frames is this one, and it assigns both to the same owner.

The assignment is not a threat in itself. It is a statement about whose time it is, and the sūrah leaves the reader to notice that his nights are inside the same alternation.

**"Will You Not Then Understand?"**

The closing question is the sūrah's direct address, and it follows three statements rather than preceding them. The faculties were given; humanity is scattered and will be gathered; life and death and the alternation belong to Him. The question asks what the reader has made of all of it.

The particle *thumma* — then — marks a sequence. The understanding is not asked for in the abstract; it is asked for after the statements, as the next step. The verse treats understanding as something that follows from what has just been said.

The sūrah has used questions throughout this section, and most of them were rhetorical in the strict sense — proposing explanations and closing them. This one is different. It asks for something the reader can still do.

That difference is what makes the verse the right place to end the section. The questions before it were about people who had already refused; this one is put to a reader who has not, and the asking leaves the answer open.

**Understanding as the Response Asked For**

The verse does not ask for belief, for gratitude or for fear. It asks for understanding, and the choice is precise at this point in the sūrah.

The passage has spent its length eliminating explanations. The message was not novel, the messenger was not unknown, the charge of madness was not evidenced, no payment was demanded, and the hardship would not have changed the outcome. What remains after those eliminations is something to be understood rather than something to be believed on authority.

The sūrah has been building toward this question since the four questions began. Each closed an escape route, and the last verse of the sequence asks the reader to do the only thing left.

The difference between the two kinds of question is worth marking. The earlier ones were asked about people who had already refused; this one is asked of a reader who has not. The sūrah moves from diagnosis to address at exactly this point.

**The Three Verses as a Summary**

The verses about the faculties, the dispersion and the alternation form a unit, and the unit is the sūrah's summary of its own argument. What was given; where humanity stands; what governs it.

The three statements are all things the audience already knew. Nobody in Mecca disputed that they had hearing and sight, that people were spread across the earth, or that night followed day. The verse's question is therefore not about information.

It is about what the information means, and the sūrah has spent a hundred verses supplying the material for the meaning. The question arrives at the end of that supply, and it is addressed to a reader who has nothing left to plead.

The summary is also the sūrah's answer to the charge that the message was novel. Everything in the three verses was known before the revelation; what the revelation supplies is the reading of it, and the reading is what the question asks for.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:81

> But they ˹just˺ say what their predecessors said

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Refusal With a Precedent**

The verse's claim is short and devastating. The objection the Meccans are making is not new. It has been made before, in the same words, by people the sūrah has already described being destroyed for making it.

The sūrah has spent its middle section recording the speeches of the chiefs across four communities. The verse now says that the audience's own speech belongs in that list, and it does not have to argue the point because it has just spent fifty verses establishing the list.

That is the verse's method. It does not compare the objections; it states the identity. A reader who has followed the narratives can check the claim himself, and the checking is what the verse relies on.

The reliance on the reader is significant. A verse that argued for the identity would invite a counter-argument; a verse that states it and points back at fifty verses of evidence does not, because the evidence has already been read.

**"The Like of What Was Said"**

The phrase is *mithla mā qāla al-awwalūn* — the like of what the earlier ones said. The word for like allows some variation while insisting on the substance, and the sūrah has recorded enough variation to show what it means.

Nūḥ's chiefs said he wanted superiority. The next generation said obedience to a human like themselves would make them losers. Pharaoh's court said the messengers' people were their slaves. Three formulations, one objection.

The verse tells the audience that their own formulation is the fourth. It does not need to quote them, because the sūrah has already recorded their words earlier — the fables of the ancients, the madman, the man taught by others.

The three formulations differ in surface and agree in substance, which is exactly what the verse claims about the earlier peoples. The variety is not evidence of independent reasoning; it is evidence of one objection wearing different clothes.

**The Verse's Position After the Summary**

The verse follows the three summary verses about the faculties, the dispersion and the alternation, and it follows the question "will you not then understand?" The refusal comes immediately after the invitation.

That sequence is the passage's sharpest move. The sūrah has assembled everything the reader needs, asked him to understand, and then described what the audience did with the invitation. The refusal is not presented as a failure of the material; it is presented as a response to it.

The verse therefore closes the argumentative section of the sūrah. After this point the passage moves into the series of questions about ownership, which assume the answer has already been given.

The questions can only work if the audience shares the questioner's premises, and the verse establishes that they do. The refusal is not a disagreement about God; it is a refusal to act on an agreement.

**The Inheritance of an Objection**

The verse implies something about how objections travel. The Meccans did not invent their position; they inherited it, and the inheritance is what the verse names.

That has a consequence for the audience's self-understanding. A person who believes he has reasoned his way to a conclusion behaves differently from a person who knows he has repeated one. The verse tells the Meccans which of the two they are, and it tells them after listing the people they are repeating.

The sūrah has described the same inheritance elsewhere, in the appeal to the forefathers. The verse here turns that appeal around: the forefathers are the source of the objection, and the objection destroyed them.

That reversal is the verse's sharpest edge. The audience cites its ancestors as an authority, and the sūrah has spent fifty verses describing what happened to the ancestors who made the same citation.

**The Same Charge Elsewhere**

The accusation that the revelation is nothing but ancient fables appears repeatedly, and the repetitions are the evidence for this verse's claim. Al-Furqān: *"And they say, '˹These revelations are only˺ ancient fables which he has had written down, and they are rehearsed to him morning and evening.'"* (25:5). Al-Qalam: *"whenever Our revelations are recited to him, he says, 'Ancient fables!'"* (68:15).

The two verses give the charge in slightly different settings, and the wording varies. The substance does not. The verse in al-Muʾminūn says the Meccans are saying what the earlier peoples said, and the parallel verses show the charge circulating in the same form across the revelation.

The consistency is what makes the verse's claim checkable. A reader who has heard the charge before can recognise it in the earlier accounts, and the recognition is the verse's argument.

**What the Verse Leaves Unsaid**

The verse does not say the earlier peoples were destroyed for saying it, and it does not need to. The sūrah has just described four destructions, each preceded by a speech of exactly this kind.

The silence is the verse's economy. It states the identity between the speeches and leaves the reader to supply what the sūrah has already supplied four times. A reader who has read the middle section knows what happens to people who say this.

That is the verse's whole force. It is one sentence long, and everything that makes it serious has been established elsewhere in the sūrah.

The economy also makes the verse portable. A reader in any century who has read the middle section can apply it to any audience making the same objection, and the application requires no new argument.

That portability is the sūrah's reason for repeating the narratives at all. Four accounts of the same refusal, told in enough detail to be recognisable, produce a verse this short and this widely applicable.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:82

> They said, “Once we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Quoted in Full**

The verse quotes the objection rather than describing it, and the quotation is the point. The reader is given the actual words, with their incredulous tone intact, and is left to hear what they sound like.

The sūrah has used the same method throughout its account of the chiefs. Their speeches are recorded verbatim — shall we believe in two humans whose people are slaves to us, he is simply insane, these are fables of the ancients. The recording is the argument.

Here the quoted words are a question, and the question's form carries the disbelief. It is not asking for information. It is expressing astonishment that anyone could think so, and the astonishment is directed at the messenger.

The quotation also fixes the objection in a form that can be examined. A paraphrase could be softened; the recorded words keep their emphasis, and the emphasis is part of what the reader is asked to judge.

**Dust and Bones**

The two materials named are the end of the process the sūrah described in its opening verses. Those verses traced a human being from a drop to a clot to a lump to bones to flesh. This verse names the reverse journey: flesh gone, bones left, bones become dust.

The pairing is exact and the sūrah means the correspondence. The same material described as being assembled is described as being dispersed, and the objection is that the dispersal cannot be reversed.

The specificity is what makes the objection answerable. A general doubt about an afterlife has no purchase on evidence. A doubt about dust and bones can be met by pointing at how dust and bones came to be arranged in the first place, which is what the following verses do.

The sūrah's opening verses are the answer in waiting. A reader who has read them has already seen the assembly described stage by stage, and the objection assumes the assembly cannot be repeated by the one who performed it.

**The Same Objection Across the Book**

The denial appears in at least eight places, and the wording is remarkably consistent. Al-Isrāʾ: *"When we are reduced to bones and ashes, will we really be raised as a new creation?"* (17:49). Aṣ-Ṣāffāt: *"When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected? And our forefathers as well?"* (37:16-17).

Yā-Sīn puts it as a challenge: *"Who will give life to decayed bones?"* (36:78). Qāf states it as a verdict: *"˹Will we be returned to life,˺ when we are dead and reduced to dust? Such a return is impossible."* (50:3). An-Nāziʿāt: *"even after we have been reduced to decayed bones?"* (79:11).

The repetitions establish the objection as a fixed formula, and the formula's stability across unrelated contexts is what the sūrah's previous verse was claiming. The audience is saying what the earlier peoples said, and the parallel verses show the saying.

**The Objection's Actual Content**

Stripped of its tone, the objection is an argument from irreversibility. A body that has decomposed cannot be reassembled, and the objection treats the reassembly as the claim being made.

The sūrah answers it by redirecting. The following verses do not argue about decomposition; they ask who owns the earth and the heavens and the dominion of everything. The answer to the objection is not a mechanism but an ownership.

That redirection is the sūrah's method here. The objection assumes the question is about matter; the sūrah treats it as a question about power. A reader who follows the redirection finds the objection dissolving rather than being refuted.

The dissolution is more complete than a refutation would be. A refuted objection can be restated in a new form; an objection that turns out to rest on a false assumption about ownership cannot, because the assumption is what the following verses remove.

**"Will We Really Be Resurrected?"**

The particle of emphasis in the question — *a-innā la-mabʿūthūn* — carries the incredulity, and the sūrah preserves it. The objection is not stated flatly; it is stated with the stress that marks it as absurd in the speaker's own ears.

The preservation matters. A neutral statement of the objection would let the reader evaluate it. A quotation that keeps the scorn makes the reader hear the scorn, and the scorn is what the following questions answer.

The sūrah has preserved the tone of every objection it has recorded, and the consistency is deliberate. The chiefs are not summarised; they are quoted, and the quoting is what allows the reader to judge them.

The tone is also evidence. A person with a reasoned doubt states it plainly; a person expressing contempt stresses it. The verse preserves the stress, and the stress is what the following questions are aimed at.

**The Verse Inside the Series**

This verse and the next form a single unit, and the following four verses form another. The objection is stated; then the questions begin.

The arrangement is the sūrah's answer in structure rather than in argument. An objection about dust and bones is met with a series of questions about ownership, and the reader is left to see why the second is an answer to the first.

The sūrah never states the connection. It places the objection and the questions in sequence and lets the reader make the move, which is the same method it has used throughout.

The method has a practical advantage. A reader who assembles the argument himself holds it more firmly than one who is handed it, and the sūrah is writing for people who will have to defend the position afterwards.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:83

> We have already been promised this, as well as our forefathers earlier. This is nothing but ancient fables!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Appeal to the Forefathers, Reversed**

The objection has two halves, and the second is the significant one. The warning is not new; it was given to their fathers as well. The speakers present this as a reason to dismiss it.

The sūrah has already turned the same appeal around once. The verse about the message that did not come to the forefathers asked whether novelty was the objection, and it noted that a forefather-based standard cannot certify anything about a new revelation. This verse gives the audience's own version of the appeal.

The reversal is exact. The forefathers were warned — and the sūrah has spent its middle section describing what happened to the forefathers who were warned and refused. The appeal to them is an appeal to the people the warning destroyed.

**"We and Our Forefathers"**

The phrase joins the speakers to their ancestors in a single group, and the joining is what the verse is examining. The warning was given to both, and both are described as having received it.

That has a consequence the speakers do not seem to notice. If the warning was given to the forefathers, then the forefathers are inside the same situation as the speakers, and the sūrah has already described how that situation ended for them.

The verse therefore contains its own answer. The speakers appeal to a shared history with the warned forefathers, and the shared history is the history of the destroyed communities.

The sūrah does not point this out. It records the appeal and leaves the reader to notice that the people being appealed to are the same people whose stories occupy the middle of the sūrah.

**Asāṭīr: Legends of the Ancients**

The word is *asāṭīr*, and the commentators take it as tales or fables — stories written down and repeated, with no claim to truth. The charge is that the revelation is literature.

The word appears in several places with the same sense. Al-Furqān: *"˹These revelations are only˺ ancient fables which he has had written down, and they are rehearsed to him morning and evening."* (25:5). Al-Qalam: *"whenever Our revelations are recited to him, he says, 'Ancient fables!'"* (68:15).

The consistency across these verses is the evidence for the previous verse's claim. The charge circulates in the same form, and the sūrah says so because it has already shown the same charge in the mouths of the earlier chiefs.

The word's sense also matters. A legend is a story told for its own sake, and calling the revelation one is a claim about its purpose rather than its content. That is why the charge does not require reading, and why the sūrah treats it as avoidance.

**The Charge as a Way of Not Reading**

Calling something a legend does not require reading it. The charge is available to anyone who has heard of the text without engaging with it, and that availability is what makes it useful.

The sūrah has described the same avoidance earlier. The people turned away from the recitation; they gathered at night to produce an account of it; they did not contemplate the Word. The legend charge is the output of that process.

The verse's placement after the question about dust and bones is significant. The objection about resurrection could be examined; the charge of legendhood cannot, because it is not a claim about content.

The two verses therefore show the audience using both available moves. First an argument, however weak; then a label that makes argument unnecessary. The sūrah records the sequence because the sequence is the diagnosis.

**"Warned of This Before"**

The word for warned carries the sense of being promised or threatened — a message about what is coming. The speakers say the warning has been given before, and the sūrah agrees.

The agreement is the verse's sharpest point. The warning has been given before, to the peoples the sūrah has described, and in each case the warning was fulfilled. The speakers cite the repetition as a reason for dismissal and the sūrah has spent fifty verses showing that the repetition is a reason for alarm.

The verse does not make this point explicitly. It records the objection and lets the preceding narratives do the answering, which is the sūrah's consistent method.

The method also protects the argument from the charge of special pleading. The sūrah is not claiming that this particular warning will be fulfilled; it is noting that every warning it has described was fulfilled, and that the speakers have cited the fact themselves.

**The Two Halves of the Objection**

The verse's structure is worth seeing whole. The first half claims the warning is old; the second claims the warning is fiction. The two do not sit comfortably together.

If the warning is fiction, its age is irrelevant — a new story is as false as an old one. If the warning is old and was given to the forefathers, then the forefathers are witnesses to it rather than evidence against it.

The sūrah records both halves without commenting on the tension, and the recording is sufficient. An objection that undermines itself does not need refuting; it needs quoting, and the verse quotes it.

The tension also reveals the objection's function. It is not a position held for its consequences; it is a way of not engaging. Two incompatible claims held together are evidence that neither is being treated as a claim.

The sūrah has described the same avoidance three times already — turning away, night talk, and the refusal to contemplate. This verse adds the fourth, and the four together describe a community organised around not reading.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:84

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “To whom belong the earth and all those on it, if you ˹really˺ know?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Series Begins**

The verse opens a sequence of six, and the sequence is the sūrah's answer to the objection about dust and bones. No argument is offered; a question is put.

The shift from argument to question is the sūrah's method at this point. The preceding verses have eliminated every explanation for the refusal, and the sūrah now asks the audience something they can answer. The answer is not in dispute, and that is what makes the sequence work.

The verse begins with the earth and its inhabitants — the most immediate and least contestable of the three questions to come. The sūrah starts where agreement is certain and moves outward.

Starting with certainty is a deliberate choice. An argument that begins with a contested premise can be stopped at the first step; an argument that begins with an agreed one cannot, and the audience has to travel further before it can object.

**"If You Know"**

The conditional at the end is not a genuine condition. It is a formula that puts the burden on the person addressed, and the sūrah uses it where the answer is obvious.

The phrase appears again in the third question of the series, and both times it functions the same way. The questioner does not doubt that the answer is known; he is establishing that it is known before the answer is given.

That establishment is what the following verses build on. The audience answers, and the answer becomes the premise for the question that follows. The conditional is the sūrah's way of making the audience supply the premise itself.

The formula also carries a quiet challenge. To say "if you know" to people who are certain they know is to invite them to demonstrate it, and the demonstration turns out to be a single word they cannot refuse to say.

**The Earth and Whoever Is in It**

The question covers both the ground and its inhabitants, and the second half is the significant one. A person might grant ownership of the earth while disputing ownership of the people on it; the verse closes that gap in the question itself.

The inclusion of the inhabitants also connects the question to the objection it answers. The objection was about bodies returning to dust; the question is about who owns those bodies while they are arranged. The connection is not stated, and it does not need to be.

The sūrah has described the making of a human being in its opening verses, and the ownership question is the natural sequel to that description. A thing that was assembled belongs to someone, and the verse asks who.

**The Question's Audience**

The verse is addressed to the Prophet in the imperative: say. The question is his to put, and the audience is the same one that made the objection.

That arrangement matters. The sūrah is not delivering an argument to the reader; it is recording a question the Prophet was told to ask his own people, in their own assembly. The reader overhears an exchange rather than receiving a treatise.

The sūrah has used the same form throughout its Meccan section. The Prophet is told to say; the audience answers; the answer is used. The verse is the first of three such exchanges in this passage.

The form also protects the Prophet from the charge of inventing the premise. The question is given to him, not composed by him, and the answer comes from the people who are refusing him. Neither half of the exchange is his.

**The Answer the Verse Expects**

The verse does not record the answer; the next verse does. The suspension is deliberate, and it puts the reader in the position of the person being asked.

A reader who has followed the sūrah knows the answer before it is given, and the knowing is the point. The question is not a test of knowledge; it is a way of making the audience state a premise they will then have to live with.

The sūrah has used the same device earlier, in the verse that left the assumption about wealth and children hanging in the air. The reader supplies the missing half, and the supplying commits him.

The commitment is what the sequence runs on. Each answer becomes a premise the audience has stated rather than one it has been told, and a stated premise is far harder to abandon afterwards.

**Why the Earth Comes First**

The order of the three questions is worth noting. The earth and its inhabitants; the seven heavens and the Throne; the dominion of everything. The sequence moves from the near to the far to the total.

Starting with the earth is a rhetorical choice with a specific effect. The audience can verify the first question by looking down. Nobody in Mecca disputed that the earth existed or that they were on it, and the sūrah begins there so that the first answer is effortless.

The effortlessness is what makes the later questions work. Once the audience has said "God's" once, the following answers come more easily, and the final question asks about a dominion that includes the dust and bones the objection was about.

The order also mirrors the sūrah's own structure. It opened with the making of a human being, moved to the provision of the earth, and now asks who owns both. The question sequence retraces the sūrah's material in the form of an interrogation.

The retracing is what makes the sequence feel like a summary rather than a new argument. Nothing is introduced that the reader has not already met, and the familiarity is what allows the questions to be answered so quickly.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:85

> They will reply, “To Allah!” Say, “Why are you not then mindful?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Given Without Hesitation**

The verse records the answer in one word, and the absence of any qualification is the point. They will say: to Allah. No debate, no hedge, no request for clarification.

The sūrah has recorded the audience's answers elsewhere, and they were never this simple. The chiefs argued, objected, accused and quoted their forefathers. Here they answer a question about ownership in a single word, and the word is the one the verse expected.

That contrast is the verse's argument. The audience is articulate and resistant on every subject except this one, and on this one they agree immediately. The sūrah notes the agreement and then uses it.

The immediacy also removes the most common defence. A person who hesitates can claim the question was unclear; a person who answers at once has shown that it was not.

**"Will You Not Then Be Mindful?"**

The follow-up is not a new question but a challenge to draw a conclusion. *Afalā tadhakkarūn* — will you not take heed, or remember, or be mindful?

The verb carries the sense of recalling something already known rather than learning something new. The sūrah is not asking the audience to accept a new proposition; it is asking them to notice what follows from one they have just stated.

That is the sequence's method throughout. Each answer is followed by the same kind of challenge, and the challenge is never about information. It is about consistency between what the audience says and what the audience does.

The method also explains why the sequence has three rounds. A single challenge could be treated as rhetoric; three challenges following three identical answers establish a pattern, and the pattern is what the final round names.

**The Gap the Verse Exposes**

The verse's force lies in the distance between the answer and the behaviour. The audience agrees that the earth and everyone on it belong to God, and the audience is simultaneously refusing the message of God's messenger.

The sūrah does not spell out the inconsistency. It states the answer, then asks whether they will be mindful, and the asking leaves the gap for the audience to see. A reader who has followed the passage can see it immediately.

The gap is the same one the sūrah identified earlier. The people who turned away from the recitation were not ignorant of it; they knew it well enough to gather nightly and produce an account of it.

The gap is also what the challenge targets. A person who does not know a premise needs teaching; a person who states a premise and does not use it needs the challenge the verse issues. The sūrah has established which of the two it is dealing with.

**Tadhakkur: Remembering as a Religious Act**

The verb from the root dh-k-r appears throughout the Qur'an, and its sense is consistently active. Remembering is not a passive state; it is an operation performed on something already possessed.

That is why the verse uses it here. The audience has the premise. What they lack is the act of connecting it to anything, and the verse asks for the act rather than the premise.

The sūrah has described the same failure elsewhere, in the verse asking whether they had contemplated the Word. Contemplation and remembrance are the two operations the audience is described as not performing, and both are operations on material they already have.

That is why the verse asks for the operation rather than for belief. Belief in God's ownership is already present; what is missing is the act that connects it to anything, and the act is what the challenge names.

**The Verse Inside the Sequence**

This is the first of three answer-and-challenge pairs, and the three challenges differ. Be mindful; be reverent; how are you bewitched? The sequence moves from a gentle invitation to an accusation.

The progression is the sūrah's account of what happens when a premise is stated and not acted on. The first challenge assumes an oversight. The second assumes a failure of reverence. The third assumes that something has gone wrong in the person's reasoning.

The audience's single-word answer is the same in all three cases. What changes is the sūrah's assessment of why the answer has not produced anything, and the assessment hardens with each repetition.

The hardening is earned rather than imposed. Each round gives the audience a chance to act on what it has said, and each round records that it did not.

**The Answer's Implication for the Objection**

The verse follows the objection about dust and bones, and the answer to the ownership question bears directly on it. If the earth and everyone on it belong to God, then the bodies that return to dust are His property.

The sūrah does not make the connection explicit, and it does not need to. The sequence of verses places the objection and the ownership question adjacent, and the reader is left to see that the objection assumes the dust is nobody's.

That is the sūrah's answer to the resurrection question. Not a mechanism for reassembly, but an owner. The verse asks the audience to be mindful of something they have just said, and the something is the answer to their own objection.

The audience therefore answers itself, and the sūrah only has to ask it to notice. That is the economy of the whole sequence: no premise is supplied by the questioner, and no conclusion is imposed.

The economy also explains the sequence's brevity. Six verses do what a developed argument would need many more to do, because the argumentative work has already been done by the audience's own answers.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:86

> ˹And˺ ask ˹them˺, “Who is the Lord of the seven heavens and the Lord of the Mighty Throne?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Question**

The sequence moves from the earth to the heavens, and the shift is deliberate. The first question was about something the audience stood on; this one is about something they could see but not reach.

The sūrah has used the seven heavens before, in its account of the stages above the earth. The verse returns to the same image and asks about its Lord, which connects the ownership question to the cosmology the sūrah has already described.

The movement outward is the sequence's method. Each question covers more than the last, and the audience's answer stays the same. The expansion is what makes the final question about total dominion intelligible.

The expansion also does something else. Each wider question makes the previous one look smaller, and by the third round the earth the audience stands on is one item inside a dominion that includes everything.

**"Lord of the Great Throne"**

The Throne is named alongside the heavens, and the adjective given to it is *ʿaẓīm*, great or immense. The pairing places the Throne above or beyond the seven heavens, and the sūrah does not elaborate.

Al-Anbiyāʾ names the same Throne in its argument about the divine order: *"Had there been other gods besides Allah in the heavens or the earth, both ˹realms˺ would have surely been corrupted. So Glorified is Allah, Lord of the Throne, far above what they claim."* (21:22).

The parallel is worth holding beside this verse. Both name the Throne, both tie it to the lordship of the heavens, and both use the naming as part of an argument. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn turns the argument into a question.

A statement can be met with a counter-statement. A question has to be answered, and the answer the audience gives is the one the verse was after.

**Why the Throne Is Named**

The Throne functions in the Qur'an as the marker of sovereignty, and its naming here is not decoration. A question about the Lord of the heavens could be answered with a general assent; a question about the Lord of the Throne asks about rule.

That is the shift the sequence makes between the first and second questions. The first asked about ownership of a thing; this one asks about lordship, which is ownership plus authority. The audience's answer will be the same word, and the word will mean more.

The sūrah has been building toward this. The verses about the chiefs described people who claimed standing for themselves; the verse about the earth described property; this verse describes rule.

Rule is also what the audience's own chiefs claimed in a lesser form, and the question about the Lord of the Throne puts that claim in perspective. The sūrah has recorded the chiefs' arrogance at length, and this verse asks who the actual sovereign is.

**The Question's Form**

The verse uses *man*, who, rather than a possessive construction, and the change of form matters. The first question asked "to whom does the earth belong?" This one asks "who is the Lord?"

The commentators note the difference and its effect on the answer. A question beginning with "who" invites a name; a question about belonging invites a possessor. The audience answers both with the same word, and the sūrah records the answer in a form that covers both.

The variation in form across the three questions is characteristic of the sūrah's care with wording. The questions are not repeated mechanically; each is framed to elicit the same answer from a different angle.

The variation also prevents the sequence from becoming a formula the audience can anticipate. A repeated question invites a rehearsed answer; three differently framed questions each require the answer to be given afresh.

**The Audience's Knowledge**

The verse assumes the audience can answer, and the assumption is correct. The Meccans acknowledged God as the creator and the owner of the heavens; their dispute was about the messengers and the resurrection, not about the cosmos.

That shared premise is what the sequence exploits. The sūrah is not arguing with people who deny God's existence; it is arguing with people who affirm it and do not draw the consequences. The question is a way of making the affirmation explicit before the consequence is drawn.

The sūrah has described the same situation throughout. The audience knows the Prophet, has heard the recitation, and acknowledges God. What it does not do is connect those facts.

That is why the sequence takes the form it does. It is not persuading; it is connecting, and the connecting is done by asking the audience to say aloud what it already holds.

**The Question After the First Answer**

The verse follows the audience's answer to the first question and the challenge to be mindful. The second question comes immediately, without waiting for a response to the challenge.

That pacing is significant. The sūrah does not pause for the audience to act on the first answer; it moves to the next question. The sequence is designed to accumulate premises faster than the audience can respond to them.

By the third question the audience has said "God's" three times, and the final challenge asks how they are bewitched. The accumulation is what makes the last question possible.

The pacing also mirrors the sūrah's account of the messengers. A message delivered repeatedly at intervals is met with the same refusal each time, and the repetition is what eventually exposes the refusal for what it is.

The sequence performs in six verses what the sūrah's middle section performed across four narratives, and the performance is what makes the final challenge legitimate rather than merely severe.

The audience watches itself answer, and the watching is the whole of the argument.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:87

> They will reply, “Allah.” Say, “Will you not then fear ˹Him˺?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Answer, a Different Challenge**

The audience's reply is identical to the one in the earlier verse — to Allah — and the verse notes the identity by repeating the word. What changes is the follow-up.

The first challenge asked whether they would be mindful. This one asks whether they will be reverent, using a verb from the root w-q-y that carries the sense of guarding oneself, being careful, standing in awe.

The shift is a step up. Mindfulness is an act of attention; reverence is a posture toward what one has attended to. The sūrah moves from asking the audience to notice to asking them to be affected.

The order matters. A person cannot be reverent toward something he has not noticed, and the first challenge had to come before this one. The sequence is graded, and the grading is deliberate.

**Ittaqū: The Verb of the Believers' List**

The word used here is the same one the sūrah applied to the believers in its earlier list — those who tremble in awe of their Lord. The challenge is therefore not a general call to piety; it is a call to the specific disposition the sūrah has already described and recommended.

That connection is the verse's sharpest feature. The audience is being asked whether they will become the kind of people the sūrah described fifty verses earlier, and the asking follows immediately on their own admission about the Throne.

The sūrah has kept the two descriptions far apart in the text and close in the argument. The believers are described by their awe; the audience is asked whether it will have any.

**Reverence Begins With the Throne**

The question that produced this answer was about the Lord of the seven heavens and the Great Throne, and the challenge to reverence follows directly from it. The connection is not arbitrary.

A person who acknowledges a Lord of the Throne has acknowledged a ruler, and reverence is the appropriate response to a ruler one cannot oppose. The verse asks for the response that the answer implies.

The sūrah has described the opposite response at length. The chiefs behaved arrogantly; the audience turned away from the recitation in disgust. The challenge to reverence is aimed at exactly that behaviour.

Reverence is also the disposition the sūrah praised in its list of the believers, which makes the challenge specific rather than general. The audience is not being asked for vague piety; it is being asked for the exact quality the sūrah has already named as the first mark of the believers.

**The Progression of the Three Challenges**

The three challenges in this sequence form a progression, and the progression is the sūrah's diagnosis. Be mindful; be reverent; how are you bewitched?

The first assumes an oversight — the audience has the premise and has not connected it. The second assumes a failure of disposition — the audience has connected it and is not affected. The third assumes a defect — something has gone wrong in the audience's reasoning.

The audience's answer does not change across the three. What changes is the sūrah's assessment of why the answer produces nothing, and the assessment moves from charitable to severe.

The movement is the sequence's argument. A diagnosis that hardens across three identical answers is not becoming unfair; it is becoming accurate, and the accuracy is what the final verse states.

**One Answer Suffices for All Three**

The verse does not ask for more information, and the absence of a request is significant. The audience has said everything the sequence needs. What is missing is not knowledge but response.

That is the sūrah's consistent position on the refusal. The audience is not ignorant; it is unmoved. The verse asks for the movement rather than the knowledge, and the asking is the verse's whole content.

The sūrah has made the same point in the verse about contemplation. Did they not contemplate the Word? The question was not about whether they had heard it. This verse asks the same kind of question about a different faculty.

Both questions assume the material is present and the response is missing. The sūrah's diagnosis of the refusal is consistently a diagnosis of inattention rather than of ignorance.

**The Verse's Place in the Argument**

The sequence of questions is the sūrah's answer to the objection about dust and bones, and this verse is its middle term. The earth belongs to God; the heavens and the Throne belong to God; therefore the dust belongs to God.

The conclusion is not stated. The sūrah asks the questions, records the answers, and issues the challenges, and it leaves the reader to assemble the argument. The assembly is the reader's work, and the work is what makes the conclusion his.

That method is why the sequence is effective. An argument delivered to a hostile audience can be dismissed; a conclusion the audience reaches from its own answers cannot, and the sūrah has arranged the questions so that the audience supplies every premise.

The arrangement also protects the messenger. Nothing in the sequence is his composition; the questions are given to him and the answers come from the people refusing him. He is a conduit, and the verse's imperative form makes that visible.

The visibility matters for the charge the audience had been making. A man who invents an argument can be accused of inventing its conclusions; a man who relays a question and records the reply cannot, and the sūrah has arranged the exchange so that the distinction is plain.

The plainness is the sequence's defence of the messenger, and it costs him nothing to make.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:88

> Ask ˹them also,˺ “In Whose Hands is the authority over all things, protecting ˹all˺ while none can protect against Him, if you ˹really˺ know?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Malakūt: The Intensive Form**

The word for dominion is *malakūt*, and the commentators identify it as an intensive form of *mulk*, sovereignty. The intensification is not merely emphatic; it denotes the inner reality of the sovereignty rather than its outward appearance.

Al-Ṭabarī's reading takes it further, interpreting *malakūt* as the treasuries of things — the stores from which provision and events are issued. The interpretation connects the word to the sūrah's earlier material on measured provision, where rain was sent down in due measure.

Al-Mulk opens with the same vocabulary: *"Blessed is the One in Whose Hands rests all authority. And He is Most Capable of everything."* (67:1). The parallel establishes the word as a term for total sovereignty, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn uses it in the same sense.

**"In Whose Hand"**

The phrase places the dominion in a hand, and the image is one of holding rather than of distant rule. A king who rules from a throne is separate from what he rules; a holder is not.

The sūrah has used the same image elsewhere, and the Qur'an uses it consistently for immediate control. The dominion is not delegated or administered at a distance; it is held, and the holding is continuous.

The connection to the preceding question is exact. The Lord of the Throne is asked about, and then the hand that holds the dominion. The two images — enthroned rule and held dominion — describe the same sovereignty from two angles.

The second image is the closer of the two. A throne implies distance; a hand implies contact, and the verse moves from the first to the second at the point where the sequence reaches its widest scope.

**"He Protects, and None Is Protected Against Him"**

The second half of the question is the sharpest clause in the sequence, and it has two edges. God protects; and no one can be protected from God.

The commentators draw both senses out. None can hinder God, and none should expect to find security against Him, in this world or the next. The clause is not a threat so much as a statement about the absence of any alternative refuge.

That absence is what the verse is establishing. A person who acknowledges a protector he cannot be protected against has acknowledged that there is nowhere else to go, and the sūrah's audience had been relying on alternatives — the House, the forefathers, the chiefs.

The verse therefore answers the boast about the Sacred House recorded earlier in the passage. The audience claimed standing from a building; this verse asks who protects and who cannot be protected against, and the answer leaves the building out of it.

**The Third "If You Know"**

The conditional returns in this verse, and its return marks the end of the sequence. The first question used it; the second did not; the third does.

The formula's function is to establish that the audience possesses the knowledge before the answer is given. Its reappearance at the end signals that the sequence is complete and that the accumulated premises are now in place.

The sūrah has been careful with these formulas throughout. It does not repeat them mechanically; it places them where the audience's knowledge needs to be established before the answer is recorded.

Its return at the end also closes the sequence symmetrically. The first question opened with the formula, the second moved to a different form, and the third returns to it — which signals that the questioning is finished and the stating is about to begin.

**The Question's Scope**

This question covers everything — *kull shayʾ*. The earth and its inhabitants were the first; the heavens and the Throne the second; all things the third. The expansion is complete, and the final question includes the dust and bones the objection was about.

The sūrah does not point this out. It asks the question, records the answer, and leaves the reader to notice that the dominion of all things includes the bodies the objection claimed were beyond reassembly.

That is the sequence's answer to the resurrection question. Not a mechanism, but a scope. The dust is inside the dominion, and the verse has just had the audience say so.

The audience therefore answers its own objection in the act of answering the question, and the sūrah never has to point this out. The three answers contain the reply, and the reader who follows them finds it there.

**Why the Sequence Ends Here**

The three questions cover the ground, the heavens and everything, and the sequence stops. A fourth question would add nothing, and the sūrah knows it.

The stopping point is also where the challenge changes. The first two challenges asked for mindfulness and reverence; the third asks how the audience is bewitched. The expansion of the questions has produced a hardening of the challenge, and the verse's position at the end is what makes the hardening legitimate.

By the third answer the audience has stated the premise three times. The sūrah has given it every opportunity to draw the conclusion, and the final verse of the sequence asks why it has not.

The stopping point is also where the sūrah's tone changes. Questions invite; statements do not. The sequence ends at the exact point where further questioning would add nothing, and the next verse supplies the statement.

The precision of the stopping point is worth noting. The sūrah does not add a fourth question for emphasis or a summary of the three. It stops where the admissions are complete, and the completeness is what licenses what comes next.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:89

> They will reply, “Allah.” Say, “How are you then so deluded?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Answer and the Final Challenge**

The audience's reply is the same word for the third time, and the verse records it without comment. The challenge that follows is different in kind from the previous two.

Be mindful. Be reverent. How are you deluded? The first two are invitations; the third is a question about a defect. The sūrah has moved from asking the audience to do something to asking what has gone wrong with it.

The progression is the sequence's argument. Three identical answers, three escalating challenges, and the escalation is produced entirely by the repetition. Nothing new is added to the premises; the pressure comes from their accumulation.

That is why the final challenge can be as severe as it is. It is not an accusation imported from outside the exchange; it is what three unanswered invitations produce, and the audience has watched it arrive.

**"How Are You Deluded?"**

The word translated as deluded or bewitched is *tuʾfakūn*, from a root meaning to be turned away or diverted. The commentators take it as being misled or fooled, and connect it to Satan.

The verb is passive, and the passivity is significant. The audience is not described as choosing error; it is described as having been turned. The question asks how that has happened to people who have just stated the correct premise three times.

The sūrah has used the same charge elsewhere in this passage. The chiefs said the Prophet was possessed; this verse asks the audience how it is that they have been turned aside. The reversal is exact and deliberate.

The reversal also relocates the problem. The charge against the messenger placed the defect in him; the question places it in the people making the charge, and it places it there after they have stated the correct premise three times.

**The Reversal of the Madness Charge**

The audience has been calling the messenger mad, and the sūrah has recorded the charge twice. This verse turns the charge around and directs it at the people making it.

The reversal is not a rhetorical flourish. It is the sequence's conclusion. People who acknowledge God's dominion three times and then refuse His messenger are not describing a coherent position, and the verse asks what has produced the incoherence.

The sūrah does not answer the question. It puts it and stops, leaving the audience to supply the answer or to recognise that it cannot.

The silence after the question is the verse's sharpest feature. Every earlier challenge was followed by another question; this one is followed by a statement, and the change of register marks the end of the exchange.

The change is the sūrah's signal that the questioning has done its work. Nothing more is asked of the audience, because nothing more could usefully be answered.

**The Verse After Three Answers**

The position of the challenge matters as much as its content. It comes after three identical answers, not after one, and the accumulation is what licenses it.

A single answer followed by an accusation would be unfair; the audience might not have understood the question. Three answers followed by an accusation is a different matter, and the sūrah has arranged the sequence so that the audience has committed itself repeatedly before the challenge arrives.

The arrangement is the sūrah's method throughout. It does not accuse early. It establishes the premises, records the admissions, and then asks the question that the admissions make unavoidable.

The patience is also strategic. An accusation made before the admissions could be dismissed as hostile; the same accusation made after three admissions is the audience's own conclusion, restated.

**Delusion as the Sūrah's Diagnosis**

The verse supplies the diagnosis the passage has been building toward. The audience is not ignorant, not confused about the facts, not lacking evidence. It is turned aside, and the turning is what needs explaining.

The sūrah has eliminated every other explanation. The message was not novel; the messenger was known and trusted; no payment was demanded; the hardship would not have changed the outcome. What remains is a turning, and the verse names it.

The naming is also a mercy. A person who is turned aside can be turned back; a person who is simply wrong cannot. The verse's diagnosis leaves the possibility open, and the question form leaves it with the audience.

The sūrah has used the same diagnosis elsewhere, in the verses about hearts that are sealed and hearing that is covered. In each case the condition is described as produced rather than innate, which is what keeps the reversal available.

**The Sequence Complete**

The three questions and three challenges form a closed unit, and the verse is its conclusion. Nothing further is asked; the next verse states the truth and calls the audience liars.

The shift from question to statement marks the end of the sequence's method. Questions invite answers; statements do not. The sūrah has exhausted the questions because the audience has exhausted the answers, and the following verse says so.

The sequence is the sūrah's most sustained piece of argument, and it is built entirely out of the audience's own admissions. No premise is supplied by the questioner, and no conclusion is stated. The verse asks the question that the admissions produce, and leaves it standing.

Leaving it standing is the final move. A question answered by the questioner becomes his claim; a question left unanswered remains the audience's problem, and the sūrah has arranged the exchange so that the audience cannot hand it back.

The next verse does not answer it either. It states that the truth was brought and that the audience is lying, which is a description of the condition the question pointed at rather than an explanation of how the condition arose.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:90

> In fact, We have brought them the truth, and they are certainly liars

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Questions End, the Statement Begins**

The verse closes the sequence of questions and does so with a flat assertion. No further question is asked, no premise is established, no answer is invited. The truth was brought; they are liars.

The shift in register is the verse's first feature. Everything before it was interrogative — the sūrah asking, the audience answering, the sūrah challenging. This verse states, and the stating marks the end of the exchange.

The sūrah has used the same method elsewhere. The five questions about the refusal were followed by the statement that the Prophet calls to a straight path. Here the three questions about ownership are followed by the statement that the truth has been brought. In both cases the questions exhaust what can be asked and the statement supplies what remains.

**"We Brought Them the Truth"**

The verb is the same one used earlier for the reminder — brought. The truth was not offered at a distance or left to be found; it was brought to them, and the bringing is described as complete.

The commentators take the truth as the religion God sent through His Prophet. The definite article matters: not a truth among others but the truth, and the sūrah has been describing its delivery throughout.

The verse therefore restates what the passage has established. The recitation was audible; the messenger was known; the reminder was theirs. The bringing is the premise the sequence built, and the verse states it as a fact rather than as a claim.

The restatement also closes the question about the messenger's motives. A man who brought something and asked for nothing has been described completely, and the verse's verb covers the whole of his part.

**"They Are Liars"**

The second clause is the harshest statement in the passage, and it follows directly from the sequence. The audience said one thing three times and did another, and the verse names the discrepancy.

The charge is not that the audience speaks falsehoods about God. It is that its stated position and its actual position do not match, and the mismatch is what the three questions exposed. A person who says the dominion belongs to God and then refuses God's messenger is not speaking truly about either.

The sūrah does not elaborate the charge. It states it in three words and moves on, which is consistent with its treatment of the other accusations in the passage.

The charge is also the sequence's conclusion rather than an addition to it. Three admissions and three refusals to act on them is what lying looks like from the outside, and the verse names it.

**The Verse After the Delusion Question**

The previous verse asked how the audience is deluded, and this verse answers by implication. They are deluded because they are lying — and the lying is not to the messenger but to themselves.

The connection is not stated, and it does not need to be. A person who is turned aside has been given a false account of his own position, and the verse supplies the account: the truth was brought, and the refusal of it is a falsehood.

The sūrah has been building toward this statement since the objection about dust and bones. The objection, the legend charge, the appeal to the forefathers — all are described as false, and the verse names them collectively.

The collective naming is economical and complete. Three separate charges, each recorded earlier, are covered by one clause, and the clause rests on the three admissions the sequence has just extracted.

**The Truth and the Reminder**

The verse's word for truth should be read beside the earlier verse about the reminder. Both describe what was brought, and both use the same verb.

The reminder recalls what is known; the truth is what is recalled. Between the two verses the sūrah has described its message as neither novel nor foreign — a reminder brought to a people who already possess the material for it.

That description is what makes the charge of lying possible. A person who refuses something entirely new is not lying; he is declining. A person who refuses a reminder of what he already knows is saying one thing and holding another.

The sūrah has established both halves of that description at length. The reminder was brought, and the audience's own answers to the three questions show what it already knew. The charge rests on both, and both have been documented.

**The Verse's Position in the Sūrah**

The verse stands at the end of the argumentative section and before the sūrah's closing movement. What follows is description — the impossibility of God having a son, the account of the messengers, the final commands.

The position is significant. The argument is over, and the verse says so. There is nothing left to establish, nothing left to ask, and nothing left for the audience to supply. The truth has been brought and the refusal has been named.

The sūrah has spent ninety verses reaching this point, and the verse is three clauses long. The economy is the measure of how completely the argument has been made.

The brevity also does something a longer statement could not. It leaves nothing to argue with. A developed conclusion invites objections; a flat statement of what was brought and what was said about it invites only agreement or denial.

The sūrah moves on immediately, to the impossibility of God having a son and to the final commands. The argument is closed, and what follows assumes it.

That assumption is the verse's last service to the reader. Everything after it is instruction rather than persuasion.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:91

> Allah has never had ˹any˺ offspring, nor is there any god besides Him. Otherwise, each god would have taken away what he created, and they would have tried to dominate one another. Glorified is Allah above what they claim

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Denials in One Sentence**

The verse denies two things, and the two are connected. God has not taken a child; and there is no god alongside Him. The first answers a specific Meccan claim, and the second states the general principle behind the answer.

The commentators identify the claim being answered: the assertion that the angels were God's daughters. The charge appears in several sūrahs, and this verse is the sūrah's answer to it.

The two denials belong together because both concern plurality in the divine. A child is a second of the same kind; a partner god is a second of the same rank. The verse closes both openings in a single sentence.

The order is also significant. Offspring comes first, because that is the claim actually being made; the denial of partner gods follows as the general principle. The verse answers the specific before stating the universal.

**The Argument From Competition**

The verse then gives a reason, and the reason is a thought experiment. If there were multiple gods, each would take away what he created, and they would compete for dominance.

The reasoning is not about what gods would want. It is about what plurality implies. Two agents with independent power over the same domain cannot both be sovereign, and the verse describes what follows: partition and conflict.

The sūrah has used the same argument in different form earlier. The chiefs divided the religion into portions; the verse describes what a divided divinity would produce. The parallel is exact.

The parallel is also a warning rather than only a proof. A community that divides its religion is doing on a small scale what the verse describes at the divine level, and the description of the result applies to both.

**Al-Anbiyāʾ: The Same Argument**

The Qur'an gives the argument elsewhere in a form close enough to be a companion verse: *"Had there been other gods besides Allah in the heavens or the earth, both ˹realms˺ would have surely been corrupted. So Glorified is Allah, Lord of the Throne, far above what they claim."* (21:22).

The two verses share structure and conclusion. Both posit a plurality, both describe the corruption that would follow, and both close with a declaration of God's exaltation above the claim.

The parallel establishes the argument as a fixed formulation. The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives it in terms of competition between gods; al-Anbiyāʾ gives it in terms of the corruption of the realms. Both end at the same place.

The two formulations cover different grounds. One appeals to the behaviour of the gods; the other to the state of the world. A reader unmoved by the first can be met with the second, and the sūrah gives both across the revelation.

**A God Who Can Be Overcome**

The commentators draw out the logical point: a god who can be overcome by another could not properly be called a god. Sovereignty that can be defeated is not sovereignty.

That is the verse's sharpest claim, and it is aimed at the audience's own theology. A pantheon is by definition a set of powers that limit one another, and the verse says that such a set contains no gods — only competitors.

The sūrah has described the chiefs as people who claimed standing for themselves. The verse shows what a comparable claim looks like at the divine level, and what it produces.

The comparison also answers the audience's own theology on its own terms. A person who accepts a pantheon has already accepted that the gods limit one another, and the verse simply names what that acceptance implies.

**"Glorified Is Allah Above What They Claim"**

The verse closes with *taʿālā*, exalted or high above, and the object is what they claim. The declaration is not an argument but a refusal of the category.

The placement is significant. The argument has been made, and the verse does not rest on it. It ends by declaring God above the claim entirely, which means the argument is offered as a clarification rather than as a defence.

The sūrah uses the same closing formula in the next verse, and the repetition marks the end of the theological section. Both verses end with exaltation, and the doubled ending is the sūrah's way of closing the subject.

The formula also does something an argument cannot. A reader who disagrees with the reasoning can still hear the declaration, and the declaration does not depend on the reasoning being accepted.

**The Verse's Place in the Passage**

The verse follows the statement that the truth was brought and that the audience are liars. The sequence moves from the charge to the content of what was denied.

That placement tells the reader what the lying consisted in. Not a refusal of moral instruction, but a claim about God — that He has offspring, that there are gods beside Him. The verse supplies the specific content of the general charge.

The sūrah then moves to the Prophet's supplication, and the shift from doctrine to prayer marks the transition from what is true to what is to be done about it.

The transition is the sūrah's structure at its clearest. Argument establishes the facts; the charge names the response to them; and the prayer shows what a person who accepts both is left doing.

That the sūrah moves to prayer rather than to prediction is the verse's final claim. The theological section ends, and the next thing recorded is a request about standing, not an announcement about the audience's fate.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:92

> ˹He is the˺ Knower of the seen and unseen. Exalted is He above what they associate ˹with Him˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**ʿĀlim al-Ghayb wa-sh-Shahādah**

The verse gives God a title, and the title covers two domains: the unseen and the witnessed. The pairing is exhaustive, and the verse's brevity rests on that exhaustiveness.

The commentators gloss the two terms as what is perceivable by the senses and what is not. Between them they leave nothing out, and the title is therefore a claim about the completeness of God's knowledge rather than about its extent in any one area.

The title appears throughout the Qur'an — in al-Anʿām, at-Tawbah, ar-Raʿd, as-Sajdah, Sabaʾ, az-Zumar, al-Ḥashr, al-Jumuʿah and at-Taghābun. Its recurrence across so many sūrahs establishes it as a fixed designation.

The recurrence also means the audience had heard it before. A title repeated across the revelation is not a new claim needing defence; it is part of the shared vocabulary the sūrah is drawing on.

Drawing on that vocabulary is what allows the verse to be so short. Two clauses are enough because the title carries the weight of every other place it appears, and the sūrah is counting on the reader knowing them.

**Why the Title Comes Here**

The verse follows the denial of offspring and partner gods, and the title is the answer to both. A claim about what God has or has not is a claim about what is unseen, and the verse names the one who knows it.

The audience's assertion about the angels was a statement about a realm they could not see. The verse responds by naming the knower of that realm, and the naming is a refusal to argue on the audience's ground.

That refusal is the verse's method. The previous verse argued; this one does not. It states who knows, and leaves the audience's claim hanging without an examiner.

The refusal is also a claim about the argument's status. A proposition that has an examiner can be debated; a proposition about a realm only one being can see cannot, and the verse names the being rather than continuing the debate.

**The Title and the Sequence of Questions**

The verse also closes the passage that began with the three questions about ownership. Those questions established God's dominion over the earth, the heavens and everything; this verse establishes His knowledge of everything.

Dominion and knowledge are the two attributes the sequence has been building toward, and the verse supplies the second. A sovereign who does not know is not sovereign; the verse closes that gap.

The sūrah has described the same pairing elsewhere. The record that speaks the truth is an instrument of knowledge, and the dominion named in the questions is exercised through it.

The pairing also answers the audience's mockery about the resurrection. A question about dust and bones is a question about what is unseen, and the verse names the one who knows the unseen as thoroughly as the seen.

The answer is given without argument, and the absence of argument is the point. The sūrah does not demonstrate that God knows the unseen; it names Him by that knowledge at the close of the passage, and the naming assumes the demonstration is already finished.

**"Exalted Above What They Associate"**

The closing declaration is nearly identical to the one in the previous verse, and the near-identity marks the end of a unit. Both verses end with God exalted above a claim the audience has made.

The word for associate here is *yushrikūn*, and it names the act rather than the objects. The previous verse named the objects — offspring, partner gods. This verse names the doing.

The shift from objects to act is the sūrah's summary of the audience's position. What is being refused is not a particular theology but the act of association itself, and the verse closes the subject by declaring God above it.

Naming the act also widens the verse's reach. A reader with no angels to call daughters can still recognise association, and the declaration is addressed to the doing rather than to a particular set of beings.

**The Verse as a Full Stop**

The verse is short, and its shortness is functional. It ends the theological section of the sūrah, and it does so with a title and a declaration rather than an argument.

The sūrah has spent its length on the messengers, the refusals, the questions and the charge of lying. This verse closes all of it with a statement about who God is, and the statement is the ground everything else stood on.

What follows is supplication and instruction. The doctrine is settled, and the verse marks the settling.

The settling is also what makes the instructions practicable. A person who does not know where the matter stands cannot be told how to behave in it; the verse supplies the standing, and the commands that follow assume it.

The verse is therefore a hinge rather than an addition. Everything the sūrah argued for is compressed into a title and a declaration, and the sūrah moves on without restating any of it.

**The Title Elsewhere**

Al-Ḥashr gives the title in a list of the divine names: *"He is Allah—the Creator, the Inventor, the Shaper. He ˹alone˺ has the Most Beautiful Names. Whatever is in the heavens and the earth ˹all˺ glorifies Him. And He is the Almighty, All-Wise."* (59:24), with the knower of the unseen and the seen given earlier in the same passage (59:22).

The parallel places the title inside a fuller enumeration, and the enumeration shows what the title is doing. Knowledge of the unseen and the witnessed is not one attribute among many; it is the ground on which the others operate.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives the title alone, at the end of a passage about claims made without knowledge, and the placement is the point.

Giving it alone also makes it final. A title inside a list can be passed over; a title standing by itself at the close of a section cannot, and the sūrah uses the isolation deliberately.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:93

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “My Lord! Should You show me what they are threatened with

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet Told to Pray**

The verse opens with the imperative and then gives the words of a supplication. The Prophet is not composing this prayer; he is being given it, and the giving is recorded.

The sūrah has used the same form for the sequence of questions — say, and then the question. Here the form carries a prayer rather than an interrogation, and the change of content marks the change of register.

The recorded supplication is therefore not private devotion. It is instruction, given to the Prophet in the same way the questions were, and it is recorded for the same reason: so that the audience can hear it.

The recording also makes the words available to be used. A prayer given as instruction is a prayer intended to be said, and the verse supplies the words for anybody in the same position.

**The Conditional: "Should You Show Me"**

The prayer is framed as a conditional, and the condition is not doubted. The Prophet is not asking whether the punishment will come; he is asking what his position will be when it does.

The commentators take the threat as the punishment promised to the wrongdoers, and the supplication as a plea not to be counted among them when it arrives. The conditional is therefore about timing rather than about certainty.

That framing is significant. The sūrah has established that the term is fixed and that the interval has an end. The prayer assumes the end and asks about placement within it.

The assumption distinguishes the prayer from bargaining. A person who doubts the punishment would pray for its cancellation; a person who accepts it prays about his own position, and the verse records the second kind.

**"What They Are Threatened With"**

The phrase refers to the audience, not to the Prophet. The threat is theirs, and the prayer concerns the Prophet's relation to it.

The separation is exact. The verse does not ask that the punishment be withheld or that the audience be spared. It asks that the one making the prayer not be included in the group it falls on.

The sūrah has described the same separation throughout. The messenger delivers; the people respond; the outcome belongs to the responders. The prayer is the messenger's part in that arrangement, stated in his own words.

The separation also explains the prayer's form. A man who is not responsible for the outcome cannot pray about it; he can only pray about his own place, and that is exactly what the verse records.

The limitation is not a defect in the prayer. It is the shape a prayer takes when the person making it has done everything he was commissioned to do, and the verse gives that shape without apology.

**Why the Prayer Is Recorded**

The sūrah could have described the Prophet's position without quoting his prayer. It quotes it, and the quoting does specific work.

A reader who hears the messenger praying not to be counted among the wrongdoers learns something about the messenger's own seriousness. The sūrah has described the believers as trembling over their good deeds; this verse shows the messenger in the same posture.

The recording also gives the audience something to overhear. They have been addressed directly for many verses; here they listen to the Prophet speak to God about them, and the listening is a different kind of address.

The change of address is worth noticing. Direct address can be refused; overheard speech cannot, because it was not aimed at the listener. The verse changes the mode of speaking without changing the content.

**The Verse and the Next**

The verse is incomplete on its own. The supplication begins here and finishes in the next verse, and the two form a single sentence split across the division.
