The sūrah has used the same device earlier, in the verse about wealth and children that left its assumption hanging. Here the split serves a different purpose: the reader has to continue into the next verse to hear what is being asked for.

The continuation is short and specific, and the split makes the reader supply the expectation before hearing it.

What most readers supply is a request for safety, and the actual request is narrower than that. The gap between the expectation and the words is what the split produces, and the gap is instructive.

The instruction is about scope. A person praying inside a fixed interval cannot ask for the interval to change; he can ask where he will stand within it, and the next verse gives the words for exactly that asking.

**The Prayer Inside the Sūrah's Structure**

The verse stands between the theological declaration and the instruction to respond to evil with what is best. The sequence moves from what is true, to what the Prophet asks, to what he is to do.

That order is the sūrah's pattern at transitions. Doctrine, then supplication, then command. The prayer is the hinge, and its position tells the reader that what follows is grounded in it.

The sūrah ends with a prayer of the same shape, and the two supplications frame the closing section. The verse here is the first of the pair.

The framing is deliberate. The sūrah's closing movement — death, the barrier, the Trumpet, the scales, the Fire — is enclosed by two prayers, and the enclosure tells the reader what the descriptions are for.

They are for a person still inside the interval, and the prayers mark both ends of the enclosure so that the reader cannot mistake the descriptions for a report about somebody else.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:94

> then, my Lord, do not count me among the wrongdoing people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer Completed**

The verse finishes the supplication the previous verse began, and the completion is short and specific. Do not place me among the wrongdoing people. Nothing is asked beyond that.

The brevity is significant. The Prophet does not ask to be spared the sight of the punishment, or to be removed from the city, or to be given a sign. He asks not to be counted with a particular group, and the asking is about classification rather than comfort.

The sūrah has been concerned with classification throughout. The believers were listed; the sects were described; the audience was named as liars. The prayer asks where the one making it stands in the same accounting.

That the messenger asks about his own classification is the verse's most pointed feature. The accounting includes him, and he does not claim an exemption from it.

The absence of a claim is the verse's argument. A man who believed his commission guaranteed his standing would not have asked, and the asking is recorded so that the audience can see that he did not believe it.

**"Do Not Place Me"**

The verb is a request about placement, and the placement is God's to make. The Prophet does not claim to have secured his own position; he asks for it.

That is the verse's most striking feature. The messenger, who has delivered the message completely and been refused for years, still asks not to be counted among the wrongdoers. The asking implies that the position is not automatic.

The sūrah has described the believers as trembling over their good deeds, and this verse shows the messenger in the same condition. The prayer is the practical form of the trembling the sūrah recommended.

The verse also answers an unspoken question. A reader might suppose that the messenger's commission secures his standing; the prayer shows that he does not suppose so, and the showing is part of the instruction.

The instruction is addressed to anyone who has done what he was told to do and still cannot be certain of the outcome. The verse gives him the form of the asking, and the form is a request rather than a claim.

**"Among the Wrongdoing People"**

The phrase identifies the group by its characteristic rather than by its name. The wrongdoers are the people the sūrah has described at length — the chiefs who behaved arrogantly, the audience that turned away, the people who said the message was ancient fables.

The prayer therefore asks for separation from a specific, described group, and the sūrah's audience knows exactly which group is meant. The verse is addressed to them through the prayer.

The indirectness is the verse's force. The audience is not being told they are wrongdoers; they are listening to the Prophet ask not to be counted with them, and the asking tells them how the matter stands.

It also leaves them a way in. A direct accusation hardens a listener; a prayer overheard can be joined, and the sūrah's audience is hearing the words as they are given.

**The Prayer as a Model**

The verse records a prayer the Prophet was told to make, and the recording makes it available. A reader who hears the messenger asking for placement has been given the form of the request.

The sūrah ends with a prayer of the same kind — asking for forgiveness and mercy — and the two together give the reader the shape of what is to be asked for. Neither prayer asks for anything except a right relation to God.

The pairing is deliberate. The first prayer asks not to be counted with the wrongdoers; the last asks for mercy. Between them the sūrah has described everything the reader needs to know about why both are necessary.

Neither prayer asks for anything the sūrah has not already described. The accounting, the record, the interval and the barrier are all in place by the time the second prayer arrives.

**The Verse After the Charge of Lying**

The prayer follows the verse that called the audience liars, and the sequence is worth noting. A charge, then a prayer.

The sūrah does not follow the charge with a threat or a prediction. It follows it with the messenger asking God about his own standing, and the asking softens nothing while changing the register entirely.

The effect on the audience is specific. A man who has just been told he is a liar could respond with anger; a man who overhears the messenger praying about his own placement is given something different to attend to.

The difference is that the prayer cannot be answered by a counter-charge. An accusation invites a denial; a supplication invites nothing, and the audience is left with the sound of it.

That is the sūrah's method at its most economical. It does not argue the audience down; it lets them overhear a man asking God about his own standing, and the overhearing does the work an argument would have done more slowly.

**The Prayer and the Promise**

The next verse states that God is able to show the Prophet what the audience has been promised, and the statement follows the prayer directly. The sequence is: the prayer asks; the answer says the showing is possible.

The answer is not a grant. It is a statement of capability, and it leaves the timing open. The sūrah has established that the term is fixed and unknown, and the verse preserves that.

The prayer and the answer together form a complete exchange, and the exchange is the sūrah's transition from argument to instruction. What follows is a command about how to respond to the audience, and the command is grounded in the exchange.

The grounding is practical. A messenger who knows the punishment is God's to show does not have to bring it forward himself, and the freedom is what the next verse spends.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:95

> We are indeed able to show you what We have threatened them with

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Statement of Capability, Not a Promise of Timing**

The verse says God is able, and it does not say when. The capability is affirmed; the schedule is not given, and the withholding is the verse's most important feature.

The commentators differ over the reference. Some take it as the punishment God can deliver at any moment; some connect it with the sufferings the idolaters experienced on account of their refusal, in this world or the next.

The verse's wording allows all of these, and the openness is deliberate. A dated promise can be waited out; an undated statement of capability cannot, and the audience is left inside the uncertainty.

The uncertainty is also what makes the following instruction necessary. A person who knew the date could plan around it; a person who does not has to decide how to behave without that information.

The verse therefore prepares the ground for the command that follows. It removes the one piece of information that would have made the command optional, and the removal is deliberate.

**The Verse Answers the Prayer**

The previous two verses recorded a supplication, and this verse is the response. The Prophet asked not to be counted among the wrongdoers when the threat arrives; the verse says the showing of the threat is possible.

The response is not a grant of the request. It is an affirmation that the situation the prayer assumes is real, and the affirmation is what the prayer needed.

The sūrah has used the same structure elsewhere. A question is put, an answer is given, and the answer supplies what the question assumed. Here the prayer assumed a coming punishment, and the verse confirms the assumption.

The confirmation is also a rebuke to the audience in passing. The messenger's prayer presupposed the threat; the verse says the presupposition is correct, and the audience hears both halves of the exchange.

**"What We Have Threatened Them With"**

The phrase returns to the audience, and the possessive is significant. The threat is theirs, and the verse names it as something already given rather than something to be announced.

The sūrah has described the threat throughout its middle section — four destructions, each narrated in full. The audience has heard the accounts, and the verse refers to them as a known quantity.

That reference is the verse's economy. Nothing new is threatened. The verse points back at what has already been described and says that the showing of it is within reach.

The economy also removes the audience's room to object. A new threat could be questioned; a reference to four narrated precedents can only be accepted or dismissed, and the sūrah has already narrated what dismissal produced.

The possessive form reinforces the point. The threat is described as something already belonging to them rather than something newly announced, and the belonging is what the four narratives established.

**The Verse Inside the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse stands between the prayer and the command to respond to evil with what is best, and its position is argumentative.

A reader might suppose that a messenger praying about the coming punishment would respond to his opponents with hostility. The verse removes that supposition by establishing that the punishment is God's to show and not the messenger's to bring, and the next verse tells the messenger how to behave meanwhile.

The sequence is therefore: the threat is real, its timing is God's, and in the meantime the messenger is to respond to evil with good. The three statements form a complete instruction.

The instruction is also a description of the messenger's actual position. He is not waiting passively for the punishment; he is under a command that applies during the waiting, and the verse establishes the waiting before giving the command.

**Capability and the Fixed Term**

The sūrah has stated twice that the term cannot be advanced or delayed. This verse affirms God's ability to show the punishment at any moment, and the two statements are not in tension.

The capability is God's; the timing is fixed. The verse affirms the first without altering the second, and the combination leaves the audience in the position the sūrah has described throughout — inside an interval whose length is known only to God.

That position is what makes the following instruction necessary. If the timing were known, the messenger could wait; if the punishment were impossible, he could ignore the audience. Neither is the case, and the verse establishes both halves.

The combination is also what prevents the verse from becoming a threat with a date attached. It affirms that the showing can happen without saying when, and the withholding is the sūrah's consistent practice.

**The Showing as a Sign**

The verb for show is the same root the sūrah used for the signs given to the messengers. The punishment, when shown, is a sign — something that makes a thing visible.

The sūrah has described signs throughout: the stages of creation, the rain, the gardens, the nine signs given to Mūsā. The verse places the punishment in the same category, and the placement is a claim about what the punishment is for.

It is not merely a penalty. It is a showing, and the showing is what the sūrah's narratives have been describing four times over.

That is why the verse can affirm the capability without specifying the date. A sign's value lies in what it makes visible, and the making visible is God's to time.

The verse therefore does two things at once. It answers the prayer by confirming the threat, and it reframes the threat as a showing rather than merely a penalty, and the reframing is what the following command depends on.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:96

> Respond to evil with what is best. We know well what they claim

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command at the Point of Maximum Provocation**

The verse arrives immediately after the establishment that the punishment is real and that God is able to show it at any moment. The audience has been called liars; the threat has been confirmed; and the command given to the messenger is to respond to their evil with what is best.

The placement is the verse's sharpest feature. A command to respond well would be unremarkable in a passage about patience. Given here, after the charge and the confirmation, it is a demand made at the point where retaliation would be most intelligible.

The sūrah does not soften the demand or explain it. It states it in five words and moves to the second clause.

The lack of explanation is itself significant. A command that needed justifying would be given with reasons; this one is given plainly, which means the sūrah assumes the reader can see why it applies here.

**"With What Is Best"**

The phrase is *allatī hiya aḥsan*, that which is better or best, and the comparative form matters. The command is not to respond with good in the abstract; it is to respond with the better of the available options.

The commentators specify what the better response consists in: words of peace and greeting, reminders of God's blessings, and discourse based on evidence and persuasion. The specification turns a general principle into a practice.

The comparative also implies a choice. There is a worse response available, and the verse assumes the messenger can see it. The command is about selection rather than about feeling.

That distinction protects the command from being impossible. Nobody can be ordered to feel a particular way; everybody can be ordered to pick among the responses available to him, and the verse does the second.

The commentators' specification helps here, because it names the better options rather than leaving them to be guessed. Peaceful words, reminders of blessing, and discourse grounded in evidence are all available to a person under insult, and the verse tells him to use them.

**Fuṣṣilat: The Same Command**

The Qur'an gives the command elsewhere in nearly identical words: *"Good and evil cannot be equal. Respond ˹to evil˺ with what is best, then the one you are in conflict with will become a close friend."* (41:34).

The parallel supplies the outcome the verse here leaves unstated. Responding with what is best can change the relationship, and the sūrah of Fuṣṣilat says so explicitly.

Al-ʿAnkabūt gives the same instruction in a specific context: *"And do not dispute with the People of the Scripture except in the best manner,"* (29:46). The three verses establish the principle as general rather than situational.

The generality matters here, because the sūrah has been describing an audience that will not change. The command is not conditioned on the response it produces, and the verse does not promise that the hostility will stop.

The absence of a promise is what makes the command a command rather than a tactic. A tactic is dropped when it fails; a command is kept, and the verse gives it in the form that survives failure.

**"We Know Well What They Claim"**

The second clause is a statement about God's knowledge, and it is the verse's answer to the difficulty the command creates. A person told to respond well to people who are lying about him needs to know that the lying has been seen.

The verse supplies that knowledge without promising action. God knows what they claim; the timing remains His. The clause removes the messenger's need to establish the record himself, which is what retaliation would have been an attempt to do.

The sūrah has established the same principle throughout. The record speaks the truth; nobody will be wronged; the accounting is exact. The verse applies the principle to the messenger's immediate situation.

The application is what makes the principle usable. A general statement about the record is abstract; a statement that God knows what these particular people are claiming is addressed to a person under pressure from them.

**The Verse After the Prayer**

The command follows a prayer in which the messenger asked not to be counted among the wrongdoers, and the sequence is instructive. The prayer concerns the messenger's own standing; the command concerns his conduct toward the people he prayed about.

The two together describe a complete posture. A person who has asked God about his own classification is not in a position to classify others by his conduct, and the verse tells him how to behave accordingly.

The sūrah has described the believers as trembling over their good deeds. The command is the outward form of that trembling — a person unsure of his own standing responds carefully rather than forcefully.

The ordering of the two is also instructive. The prayer comes first, and the command follows it. Conduct is grounded in the asking, not the other way round.

**The Command's Universality**

The commentators describe this as a universal commandment, and the description is supported by the parallel verses. The instruction is not tied to the Meccan situation or to the Prophet alone.

That universality is what makes the verse portable. A reader in any century who is lied about has been given the same instruction, and the instruction comes after the same establishment — that the lying is known and that the accounting is God's.

The sūrah ends with a prayer for forgiveness and mercy, and the prayer is the same posture stated in the other direction. Respond with what is best; ask for mercy. The two commands frame the closing section.

Between them the sūrah describes the end of the interval, and the descriptions explain why the two commands are urgent rather than merely admirable.

The urgency is what the verse's placement establishes. A command given after the confirmation of the threat is not advice about temperament; it is instruction for the period that remains, and the period is shorter than the audience assumes.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:97

> And say, “My Lord! I seek refuge in You from the temptations of the devils

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Command to Pray**

The verse gives the Prophet another prayer to say, and the second prayer follows immediately on the command to respond to evil with what is best. The sequence is instructive: the messenger is told how to behave, and then told what to ask for while behaving that way.

The connection between the two is not stated, and it does not need to be. A person instructed to respond well to people who are lying about him is under pressure, and the pressure has a source. The verse names the source and gives the words for dealing with it.

The sūrah has used the same form twice already — say, and then the words. The repetition establishes the prayers as instruction rather than as recorded private devotion.

The instruction is also timed. The command about conduct comes first and the prayer second, which means the sūrah expects the difficulty to arise before the words are needed.

**Hamazāt: The Incitements**

The word is *hamazāt*, and the commentators give it two related senses: the whispering of the devils to the soul, and their goading of an enemy to cause harm. Some describe the incitements in terms of choking or throttling.

The two senses cover the inside and the outside. A person under pressure from his own thoughts and a person under pressure from hostile people are experiencing the same thing from two directions, and the word covers both.

The sense of constriction is worth holding. The commentators' image of choking describes what the incitement feels like — a narrowing of the field in which a person can think. The command to respond with what is best requires a wide field, and the prayer asks for it.

The two senses also cover the sūrah's description of the audience. A man under pressure from his own thoughts is distracted from remembrance; a man goaded by hostile people is pushed toward a response he would not otherwise make. The verse asks for protection from both at once.

**The Plural: Devils, Not the Devil**

The verse uses the plural — *ash-shayāṭīn* — and the plural matters. The incitements are not from a single source operating in a single way.

What has been described is a community organised around resistance: the chiefs, the nightly assembly, the appeal to the forefathers. The plural fits that description. A person facing an organised opposition is facing many incitements, and the prayer is framed accordingly.

The plural also generalises the prayer. A reader in any situation of sustained hostility has been given words that fit his situation rather than a single named adversary.

The generalisation is what makes the words usable. A prayer against one specific tempter would have to be adapted; a prayer against incitements in the plural can be said as it stands by anyone under pressure from any direction.

The sūrah has described pressure from many directions at once, and the plural fits that description exactly. The verse is not addressing a single temptation but a sustained campaign, and the words are shaped accordingly.

**Distraction From Remembrance**

The commentators connect the incitements to distraction from the remembrance of God, and the connection explains why the prayer is placed here.

Remembrance and contemplation are the two operations the audience failed to perform, and the sūrah named both. The incitements are what prevent those operations, and the prayer asks for protection from the prevention.

The verse therefore completes the command that precedes it. Responding with what is best requires attention; attention is what the incitements attack; the prayer asks for the attention to be protected.

The chain explains why the sūrah places a prayer here rather than an exhortation. A person cannot be told to be attentive; he can be given words that ask for attentiveness, and the verse gives them.

The verse also identifies the enemy of the sūrah's central recommendation. Contemplation was what the audience refused; distraction is what makes contemplation impossible; and the prayer is aimed at the distraction rather than at the people causing it.

**The Prayer's Structure**

The supplication is split across this verse and the next, and the split is deliberate. The first half asks for refuge from the incitements; the second asks that the devils not be present at all.

The progression is from protection against an action to protection against a presence. The first is defensive; the second is more complete, and the sūrah gives both.

The split also forces the reader into the next verse, and the next verse supplies the more demanding request. The sūrah has used the same device earlier, and it works the same way here.

The device has a further effect. A reader who hears the first half will supply an ending, and the ending given is stronger than most would supply. The gap is where the instruction sits.

The instruction is that the asking can go further than the first request. A person who has asked for protection from an action and found it continuing is told, by the shape of the prayer, to ask about the source next.

**Refuge as the Posture**

The word for seeking refuge carries the sense of taking hold of something for protection, and the posture is one of dependence rather than of effort.

That is the verse's claim about how the messenger is to meet the incitements. Not by strength of will or by argument, but by taking hold of God. The sūrah has described the same posture throughout — the believers tremble, the messenger prays, and neither relies on his own resources.

The posture also answers the command. A person who has taken refuge does not need to retaliate, because he is not the one holding the situation together.

The sūrah's realism about human capacity runs through the whole passage. The command is demanding; the prayer supplies what the command cannot, and the two are given together rather than separately.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:98

> And I seek refuge in You, my Lord, that they ˹even˺ come near me.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Prayer**

The verse completes the supplication, and the completion asks for more than the first half did. The first asked for refuge from the incitements; this one asks that the devils not be present at all.

The progression is from action to presence, and the difference is significant. An incitement can be resisted; a presence operates continuously, and the prayer asks for the presence to be removed rather than merely countered.

The sūrah gives both requests, and the giving of both tells the reader that the first is not sufficient. The prayer models an escalation in the asking, and the escalation is available to anyone using the words.

The escalation is also an honest account of how prayer works. A person who asks for protection from an action and finds the action continuing asks next about the source, and the verse records the second asking.

**Presence as the Ordinary Condition**

The commentators make a point about the verse that is easy to miss: the presence of a devil with a human being is not an extraordinary state of possession. It is the typical condition in which people find themselves.

That reading changes the prayer's meaning. The messenger is not asking for protection from a rare affliction; he is asking about the normal situation, and the asking implies that the normal situation requires prayer.

The implication is uncomfortable and useful. A person who assumes he is ordinarily unattended by incitement has no reason to pray the words; a person who assumes the opposite has a reason to say them daily, and the verse supports the second assumption.

Traditional Islamic psychology describes human beings as subject to both satanic and angelic influences while retaining the will to resist or conform to either. The prayer sits inside that description, and it asks for help with the first of the two influences.

**"Lest They Be Present With Me"**

The phrasing is worth holding. The request is not that the devils be destroyed or driven from the world; it is that they not be present with the one praying.

The limitation is exact and modest. The prayer does not ask for a change in the general order of things. It asks about a specific relation — their presence with him — and the modesty is characteristic of the sūrah's prayers.

The same modesty appears in the earlier supplication. Do not count me among the wrongdoers. Neither prayer asks for anything beyond the position of the one making it.

The modesty is also what makes the prayers sayable. A person cannot ask for the world to be reordered; he can ask about his own relation to it, and the verse gives the form of that asking.

The form is worth keeping. Both prayers in this passage are limited to the position of the speaker, and the limitation is what allows them to be used by anybody rather than only by a prophet facing a particular city.

**The Prayer After the Command**

The verse follows the command to respond to evil with what is best, and the two form a unit. The command says what to do; the prayer says what to ask for while doing it.

The unit answers an objection the command invites. A person told to respond well to sustained hostility will find that he cannot, and the sūrah anticipates the finding. The command is given, and immediately after it a prayer for the capacity to keep it.

The arrangement is the sūrah's realism about moral instruction. A command without a prayer is a demand on unaided will; the sūrah gives both, and the pairing is what makes the command practicable.

The pairing also describes the order correctly. The command is given first and the prayer second, and the sequence matches the experience: a person is told what to do, finds he cannot, and is then given the words.

**The Messenger Praying for Protection**

The verse records the Prophet asking for refuge, and the recording does something for the reader. The messenger is not presented as immune to the incitements; he is presented as needing protection from them.

That presentation matters for the sūrah's audience. A messenger who required refuge could not be accused of claiming a status beyond the human, and the charge against him had been that he was a man like them. The verse confirms the charge and turns it into a model.

The sūrah has made the same move earlier, in the verse telling the messengers to eat the good provision. What the chiefs offered as a disqualification, the sūrah presents as the condition of the commission.

The presentation also answers the charge in the sūrah's own way. The chiefs argued that a man like them could not carry a message; the verse shows a man like them needing refuge from incitements, and the needing is what makes the commission credible rather than implausible.

**The Prayer's Place in the Sūrah**

The two supplications stand between the command about responding to evil and the account of death and the barrier. The prayers close the instructional section, and what follows is description.

The placement tells the reader what the sūrah considers the messenger's equipment: a command about conduct and a prayer for the capacity to keep it. Nothing else is given, and nothing else is needed.

The sūrah ends with a third prayer, asking for forgiveness and mercy, and the three together frame the closing movement. This verse is the second of the three, and it is the one that asks for protection rather than for pardon.

The three cover the ground completely. A person's standing, his capacity to act, and his need for mercy are the three things the sūrah has been describing since the list of the believers.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:99

> When death approaches any of them, they cry, “My Lord! Let me go back

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Until": The Long Interval Closing**

The verse opens with a particle that the commentators connect back to the dismissive words of the disbelievers recorded much earlier: they will persist in that rejection until death comes. The interval between the two verses is the whole of the sūrah's middle section.

The connection is worth seeing, because it explains the verse's suddenness. The audience has been speaking for many verses; the verse describes the moment the speaking stops. Nothing is narrated between the last objection and the arrival of death.

The sūrah has used the same compression throughout. The refusal, then the outcome, with no intervening account. The verse applies the compression to the individual rather than to the community.

The compression is the verse's warning. A person's objections can occupy his whole life and still end in a single moment that the sūrah gives in half a line.

**The Cry at the Moment of Death**

The commentators differ over when the words are spoken. Some take the plea as uttered while the person is still alive, addressed to the angels who have come to take the soul. Others take it as spoken after death, after the Fire has been seen.

Both readings agree on the essential point: the request comes at the point where the interval has closed. Whether the speaker is dying or dead, the period in which the request could have been granted is over.

The difference between the readings is a difference in audience. The first addresses a person at the bedside; the second addresses a person who has already crossed. The verse keeps both, and each reader finds the one that applies to him.

The ambiguity is productive. A reader who takes the first reading hears the plea at the bedside; a reader who takes the second hears it afterwards. Both describe a request made too late, and the verse does not settle between them.

**"Let Me Go Back"**

The request is a return, and the word carries the sense of being sent back to where one was. The speaker is not asking for mercy or for forgiveness; he is asking for a second interval.

The sūrah has described the interval at length. It is fixed at both ends; it cannot be advanced or delayed; it is the only part of the arrangement a person controls. The request asks for the fixed thing to be reopened, and the next verse says it will not be.

The specificity of the request is what makes the refusal intelligible. A general plea for mercy could be answered; a plea for the interval to be reopened asks for something the sūrah has established as impossible.

The request also reveals what the speaker understands at the end. He does not ask for the punishment to be lessened or for a different destination. He asks for time, which means he has come to see that time was the thing he had and spent.

**The Verse After the Prayers**

The verse follows the two supplications of the Prophet, and the contrast is deliberate. The messenger asked not to be counted among the wrongdoers and asked for refuge from the devils; the person described here asks to go back.

The two postures are opposed. The messenger's prayers are made inside the interval and concern his standing; this cry is made at the end of the interval and concerns its length. The sūrah places them adjacent so that the difference is visible.

The arrangement is also an instruction. The prayers are available now; the cry is not. The verse describes what remains for a person who did not pray.

The adjacency is what gives the prayers their weight. Read alone, they are devotional; read next to the cry, they are the difference between two ways of meeting the end of the interval.

**The Cry Elsewhere**

The Qur'an records the same request in several places, and the recordings agree. Al-Munāfiqūn sets it at the moment of dying: a person is told to give from what he has been provided *"before death comes"* to him, and he then cries, *"My Lord! If only You delayed me for a short while"* so that he could give in charity and join the righteous (63:10).

As-Sajdah gives it after the Fire has been seen: *"Our Lord! We have now seen and heard, so send us back and we will do good."* (32:12). The parallel supplies what this verse leaves implicit — the seeing and the hearing that produce the request.

The repetitions establish the request as a fixed feature of the Qur'an's account of death, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives it in its shortest form.

The brevity here is deliberate. The other accounts give the reasoning and the setting; this one gives the words alone, which makes them available as something a reader can recognise in himself rather than study in another.

**The Verse as a Warning to the Living**

The verse describes someone else's death, and it is addressed to the living. A reader who is still inside the interval is being shown what the end of it sounds like.

That is the verse's function. It does not describe the punishment; it describes the request, and the request is what a person makes when the interval has closed. The reader is being given the words he will not be able to use.

The sūrah has used the same method throughout. It describes outcomes rather than threatening them, and the description is what makes the outcome intelligible.

The verse also closes the instructional section of the sūrah. The commands and prayers are finished, and what remains is description — the Trumpet, the scales, the Fire, and the final declaration.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:100

> so I may do good in what I left behind.” Never! It is only a ˹useless˺ appeal they make. And there is a barrier behind them until the Day they are resurrected

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reason Given, and the Refusal**

The verse completes the request and then refuses it in a single word. The speaker wants to return so that he may do good in what he left behind — and the answer is *kallā*, no, far from it.

The stated purpose is not implausible. The speaker is not asking to return for comfort or revenge; he is asking to do the good he did not do. The refusal is therefore not about the motive.

The commentators note a reading in which the phrase means "these are words he could not but speak" — that is, the request is involuntary, produced by the situation rather than by a settled intention. The reading makes the refusal easier to understand: a plea produced by sight of the Fire is not the same thing as a change of heart.

**Why the Request Cannot Be Granted**

The sūrah has established the reason across many verses. The term is fixed and cannot be advanced or delayed; the interval is the only part of the arrangement a person controls; the record speaks the truth and none will be wronged.

A return would reopen what has been closed, and the sūrah has described the closing as definitive. The refusal is not a punishment added to the situation; it is the situation.

The verse's single word of denial carries all of that. It does not argue, and it does not need to. The reader who has followed the sūrah already knows why the request cannot be granted.

A reader who has not followed it is told nothing, and the withholding is deliberate. The sūrah assumes the work has been done, and the assumption is a measure of how much the preceding verses were meant to establish.

**Barzakh: The Barrier**

The verse names what stands between the speaker and any return, and the word is *barzakh* — a barrier, or an isthmus between two things. The commentators take it as the truth that the dead do not return to the life of this world.

The word's sense of an isthmus is worth holding. A barzakh is not merely a wall; it is a strip of land between two bodies of water, a thing that separates by being between. The verse describes the state of the dead as an in-between, and the in-between lasts until the resurrection.

The sūrah has described intervals throughout — the fixed term, the time the messengers were to be left, the period inside which conduct matters. The barzakh is the interval after the interval, and it has a fixed end.

The fixed end matters. A barrier with no terminus would make the verse a statement about permanence; a barrier that lasts until a named day makes it a statement about waiting, and the sūrah has been about waiting throughout.

**"Until the Day They Are Resurrected"**

The barrier is not permanent, and the verse says so. It lasts until the day of resurrection, and the naming of the end connects the verse to the sūrah's earlier material.

The audience had been mocking the resurrection — when we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected? The verse answers by assuming it. The barrier lasts until the day they are resurrected, and the assumption is stated as a fact rather than argued for.

That is the sūrah's answer to the mockery. It does not defend the resurrection; it uses it as a fixed point in a statement about something else, and the using is the defence.

The method is worth noticing. A contested claim defended at length invites further contest; a contested claim used casually as a premise has already been granted by the sentence that contains it, and the verse takes the second route.

**The Verse After the Cry**

The two verses form a single unit: the request and the refusal. The sūrah gives the request in full, with its stated purpose, and then refuses it in a word and names what stands behind the refusal.

The completeness of the request is deliberate. A reader who hears the whole plea — let me go back, so I may do good in what I left behind — is being given the most sympathetic version of it, and the refusal follows the most sympathetic version.

The three statements are given in that order so that the last one carries the weight. A reader who disputes the first has still to face the third, and the third is not a judgement about the speaker but a description of the situation.

The arrangement removes the reader's ability to say that the speaker asked badly. The request was reasonable; the interval is closed; the barrier is real.

**The Warning to the Reader**

The verse is addressed to the living, and its function is to make the interval legible. A reader who knows that the interval ends in a barrier, and that the barrier lasts until the resurrection, has been told what his time is for.

The sūrah has been making the same point since the verse about the fixed term. What is new here is the naming of what comes after, and the naming closes the argument.

The verse is followed by the account of the Trumpet, the scales and the Fire, and the account assumes the barrier. The reader has been told where he is, and what follows describes where he is going.

The sūrah's closing movement is therefore addressed to somebody inside the interval. Every verse that follows is a description given to a person who still has time, and the giving is what makes the descriptions useful.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:101

> Then, when the Trumpet will be blown, there will be no kinship between them on that Day, nor will they ˹even care to˺ ask about one another

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Trumpet and the End of the Barrier**

The verse names the event that ends the barzakh described in the previous verse. The barrier lasted until the Day they are resurrected; this verse gives the signal that closes it.

The Trumpet appears throughout the Qur'an — in al-Kahf, Ṭā-Hā, an-Naml, Yā-Sīn, az-Zumar, Qāf, al-Ḥāqqah, al-Muddaththir and an-Nabaʾ. Its recurrence establishes it as a fixed feature of the Qur'an's account of the end.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives it in a single clause. Nothing is described about the blowing itself; the verse moves immediately to what follows, and the immediacy is characteristic.

The sūrah treats the signal as a transition rather than an event to be described. What matters is not the sound but what the sound ends, and the verse gives the ending in the same breath as the signal.

**Kinship Broken**

The verse states that there will be no kinship between people on that Day, and the statement is about the relations the sūrah's audience relied on.

The chiefs had appealed to their forefathers; the community had been organised around lineage and standing. The verse says that the structure will not hold, and the saying is a direct answer to the appeal.

Al-Ḥajj describes the same breaking: *"On the Day you see it, every nursing woman will forget what she nurses."* (22:2). ʿAbasa gives it more starkly: *"That Day a man will flee from his brother."* (80:34). The three verses agree that the closest bonds fail first.

The order is significant. Nursing and brotherhood are the two bonds a person does not choose, and the verses describe both failing. What is given rather than acquired is the first thing to go.

That is what makes the verse bear on the audience's theology. Their standing was inherited rather than earned, and the verse describes the inherited bond dissolving before the earned ones are even mentioned.

**"Nor Will They Ask One Another"**

The second clause is the verse's most discussed feature, because other verses describe people questioning one another on that Day — in al-Aʿrāf, aṣ-Ṣāffāt and aṭ-Ṭūr.

Al-Rāzī resolves the apparent contradiction by noting that the Day is a long period composed of many states. Some of those states are so overwhelming that people cannot attend to anyone else; others allow attention beyond one's own situation.

The resolution is worth holding, because it changes the verse from a flat statement into a description of one phase. The sūrah is describing what happens when the Trumpet is blown, not what happens throughout the whole Day.

The distinction also protects the other verses. Passages in which people address one another are not contradicted by this one; they describe a different state within the same long Day, and the sūrah's clause is limited to the moment of overwhelming.

**The Verse After the Barrier**

The verse follows the account of the request to return and the refusal, and the sequence is exact. The barrier lasts until the resurrection; the Trumpet signals the resurrection; the verse describes what the resurrection does to human relations.

The connection answers the audience's mockery about dust and bones. The verse does not argue that the resurrection will happen; it describes what happens when it does, and the description assumes the event.

That method runs through the whole sūrah. It does not defend contested claims; it narrates what follows from them, and the narrating establishes them.

The verse is the clearest instance of the method in the closing movement. The resurrection was mocked; the verse describes kinship failing on the Day of resurrection, and the description assumes the event it was mocked for.

**The Breaking as a Loss**

The verse describes the loss of kinship without comment, and the absence of comment is significant. The sūrah does not say that the breaking is a punishment or a relief; it states it.

The stating is enough, because the audience has been relying on those bonds. A community organised around lineage hears that the lineage will not hold, and the hearing is the verse's argument.

The chiefs' claim to standing, the appeal to the forefathers, the nightly assembly — all of it depends on relations the verse says will end. The verse therefore answers the audience's theology by removing its social foundation rather than by arguing with its content.

That is why no comment is needed. A reader who has been relying on his kin does not need to be told that the reliance was misplaced; he needs to be told that the kin will not be there, and the verse says exactly that.

**The Verse Inside the Closing Movement**

The verse stands between the barrier and the scales, and its position is structural. The sūrah's closing movement moves from the end of the interval to the resurrection to the weighing to the Fire, and the verse is the second stage.

Each stage is described briefly, and the brevity is consistent. The sūrah does not develop any of them at length; it gives each in a verse or two and moves on, and the cumulative effect is a sequence rather than a set of descriptions.

The sequence is addressed to a person inside the interval, and the address is what makes the brevity effective. A reader does not need elaboration; he needs the order of what is coming.

The order given here is Trumpet, weighing, Fire, dialogue, reward, and the measure of time. Each stage receives a verse or two, and the reader is carried through the whole of the Day in eighteen verses.

That compression is itself an instruction. A person who can hold the whole sequence in mind has a different relation to his own interval from a person who cannot, and the sūrah's brevity is what makes the holding possible.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:102

> As for those whose scale is heavy ˹with good deeds˺, it is they who will be successful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scales Introduced**

The verse introduces the weighing, and it does so by describing one of two outcomes. The scale is heavy, and the people with it are the successful.

The word for successful is *al-mufliḥūn*, the same word the sūrah used in its opening verse about the believers. The return of the word closes a circle that has run the length of the sūrah.

The opening described the believers as successful and then listed what they do. The closing describes the weighing and names the same people as successful, and the naming tells the reader what the list was for.

The list was not a description of a type of person; it was an account of what the success consists in. The verse here confirms that the account was accurate, and the confirming is the sūrah's way of closing its opening claim.

**Heaviness as the Measure**

The verse describes the scale as heavy, and the heaviness is the criterion. Nothing else is mentioned — not the number of deeds, not their visibility, not the standing of the person who performed them.

The sūrah has been concerned with hidden things throughout. The believers give what they give with hearts trembling; the record speaks the truth about what is forgotten; the hidden shirk of the heart is described. The weighing is where all of that is settled.

That the measure is weight rather than count is significant. A person with few heavy deeds and a person with many light ones are not described as equivalent, and the verse gives no way to convert between them.

The absence of a conversion is what makes the criterion ungameable. A person cannot compensate for lightness by increasing the number, and the verse refuses to say what makes a deed heavy.

The sūrah has already supplied the answer without stating it as one. The believers were described as trembling over their good deeds, and the trembling is the weight. A deed performed in that condition and a deed performed without it are not the same deed.

**Al-Qāriʿah: The Same Image**

The Qur'an gives the image at greater length elsewhere: *"As for those whose scale is heavy, they will be in a life of pleasure. But as for those whose scale is light, their home will be the pit."* (101:6-9).

The parallel supplies what this verse leaves unstated — the destination of each group. Al-Muʾminūn says the heavy are successful and the light have lost their souls; al-Qāriʿah names the pleasure and the pit.

The two accounts agree on the criterion and differ in what they add, and the difference is a difference of emphasis rather than of substance.

Al-Muʾminūn keeps the word for success because the sūrah opened with it; al-Qāriʿah names the destination because that sūrah is built around the weighing itself. Each account supplies what its own structure requires.

**The Verse's Position in the Sequence**

The verse follows the Trumpet and precedes the description of the light scale, and the order is deliberate. The successful are named first, and the loss follows.

The ordering matters for a reader inside the interval. The sūrah does not begin the account of the Day with the punishment; it begins with the success, and the success is described in the same word the opening verse used.

The arrangement tells the reader which outcome the sūrah is oriented toward. The description of the Fire that follows is given to people who have already been told what success looks like.

That ordering matters for the reader's response. A person who hears the punishment first may respond with fear alone; a person who has heard the success named first can hear the punishment as the loss of something specific, and the sūrah wants the second response.

**Success as the Sūrah's Subject**

The word *falāḥ* has been the sūrah's subject from the first verse. The believers were named as successful, and everything between that naming and this verse has been an account of what the success consists in and what stands in its way.

The verse at the end does not add anything to the opening claim. It confirms it, and the confirming is what the whole sūrah has been doing.

The return of the word is also a structural marker. A reader who hears *al-mufliḥūn* at the end of the sūrah is being told that the opening list has been completed, and the completion is the weighing.

The weighing is where the list is settled. Everything the opening verse described — humility in prayer, turning from idle talk, giving zakāt, guarding chastity, keeping trusts, maintaining prayer — is what the scale weighs, and the verse names the result without repeating the items.

Not repeating them is deliberate. By this point the reader has carried the list through a hundred verses of narrative and argument, and the sūrah trusts him to supply the contents of the scale himself.

**The Verse as an Answer to the Mockery**

The audience had been mocking the resurrection and the accounting. The verse answers by describing the accounting without reference to the mockery, and the absence of reference is the answer.

That method runs through the whole sūrah. It does not argue with the mockers; it describes what happens, and the description assumes that the event is real.

A reader who has followed the sūrah from the opening list to the weighing has been given a complete account, and the account does not pause to defend any of its premises. The mockery is answered by being left behind rather than by being refuted.

Arguing with it would have kept it in play. The weighing simply goes ahead, and the going ahead is the reply.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:103

> But those whose scale is light, they will have doomed themselves, staying in Hell forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Outcome**

The verse gives the other side of the weighing, and the structure mirrors the previous verse exactly. The scale is light; the people with it have lost themselves and will remain in Hell.

The mirroring is deliberate. The two verses are the same sentence with the criterion and the outcome reversed, and the symmetry makes the criterion the only variable.

That symmetry is the sūrah's method of teaching. The believers and the sects, the messengers and the chiefs, the trembling and the arrogance — each pair is given in parallel so that the reader can see what differs.

Here the parallel is at its tightest. Two adjacent verses, identical in structure, differing only in weight and outcome. Nothing else is introduced to explain the difference, and the absence of explanation is what makes the criterion unmistakable.

The reader is therefore left with a single variable. Everything about the two groups is the same except the weight of what they brought, and the verse refuses to say anything further about how the weight was made.

**"They Will Have Doomed Themselves"**

The phrase can be rendered "they will have lost their souls" or "lost themselves," and both readings carry the same claim. The loss is theirs, and it is described as something they did rather than something done to them.

The reflexive form is significant. The verse does not say they were destroyed; it says they destroyed themselves, and the saying places the agency with the people being described.

Everything in the sūrah has been building toward this claim. The record speaks the truth; nobody will be wronged; the deeds are what the person did. The verse states the conclusion of that argument in a single phrase.

Stating it at the weighing rather than earlier is deliberate. A reader who has just been told that the scale is heavy or light needs to know whose doing the weight was, and the verse supplies the answer in the same breath as the outcome.

**The Loss as the Outcome**

The verse describes the loss before describing the Fire, and the order matters. What the people lose is themselves, and the Fire is where the loss is located.

That ordering makes the punishment intelligible. A person who has lost himself has nothing to bring into the Fire, and the verse describes the state before describing the place.

The priority matches the way the sūrah has described failure throughout. The hidden shirk of the heart, the sealing of the faculties, the distraction from remembrance — all of these are states before they are locations, and the verse gives the state first.

The verse therefore does not introduce a new idea at the end of the sūrah. It applies the sūrah's existing account of failure to its final case, and the application is consistent with everything that preceded it.

**"Staying in Hell Forever"**

The verse adds permanence, and the addition is the second difference from the previous verse. The successful are not described as remaining anywhere; the lost are described as abiding.

The asymmetry is worth noting. The sūrah does not give a parallel permanence to the successful outcome in this verse, and the omission leaves the description of the loss more fully specified than the description of the success.

The fuller specification is the verse's warning. A reader is told exactly what the light scale produces, and the telling is unqualified.

The absence of qualification is deliberate. The sūrah does not soften the permanence or add a condition; it states the abiding and moves to the next verse, and the moving on is what makes the statement final.

The asymmetry with the previous verse is also deliberate. The successful are named and left; the lost are named, described as self-destroyed, and given a permanent location. The sūrah spends more words on the second outcome because the reader is being warned rather than congratulated.

**Al-Qāriʿah: The Pit**

The parallel verse in al-Qāriʿah names the destination differently: *"But as for those whose scale is light, their home will be the pit."* (101:9). The word for home is significant — the pit is described as a dwelling rather than a place of passage.

The two accounts agree on the permanence. Al-Muʾminūn says abiding forever; al-Qāriʿah says home. Both describe a settled state rather than a temporary one.

The sūrah of al-Muʾminūn adds the self-destruction, and the addition is what al-Qāriʿah leaves out. Between them the two accounts give both the agency and the destination.

The agency is what this sūrah needs, because it has spent its length describing choices. A reader who has followed the messengers and the refusals needs to know that the outcome was made and not merely suffered, and the verse supplies that.

**The Verse Inside the Sequence**

The verse stands between the weighing and the description of the Fire on the faces, and its position is transitional. The criterion is given, the outcome is stated, and the next verse describes what the outcome looks like.

The transition is from judgement to experience. The verse tells the reader what happens to the people with the light scale; the next verse tells him what they undergo, and the two together complete the account.

Stating an outcome and then describing it has been the sūrah's method throughout, and the describing is what makes the outcome intelligible to a reader inside the interval.

The reader is the one who needs the description. The people in the Fire do not need to be told what the Fire is like; a person who has not yet arrived does, and the verse is written for him.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:104

> The Fire will burn their faces, leaving them deformed

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Face as the Site of Punishment**

The verse specifies where the Fire touches, and the specification is the verse's whole content. Not the body in general, not the soul in abstraction — the face.

The commentators note that the verb can mean to burn or to scorch, and that the grimace results from the disfigurement of the lips by the flames. The description is physical and specific.

The specificity is what makes the verse effective. A general statement about punishment can be received abstractly; a statement about the face cannot, because the face is what a person is recognised by.

The sūrah has described the believers by what they do rather than by how they look. The verse here reverses the method for the other group, describing them by what is done to their appearance, and the reversal is the point.

**Faces Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

The Qur'an repeatedly locates punishment on the face. The commentators list al-Anfāl, Yūnus, Ibrāhīm, al-Isrāʾ, al-Kahf, al-Furqān, an-Naml, al-Aḥzāb, al-Qamar and al-Mulk as containing such descriptions.

The recurrence establishes the face as a fixed site in the Qur'an's account of the Fire, and the recurrence means the audience had heard it before. The verse is not introducing an image; it is applying a known one.

The application here is brief, and the brevity relies on the reader's familiarity. A verse that had to explain the image would be longer; this one assumes it.

The assumption is also a claim about the audience. A community that has heard the image across ten sūrahs cannot treat it as a novel invention, and the verse's brevity depends on that shared hearing.

The shared hearing is what the sūrah counts on throughout. It does not restate its premises because the audience already holds them, and the verse here is the clearest instance of the economy that habit produces.

**The Grimace as Disfigurement**

The verse says the Fire leaves them deformed, and the word carries the sense of a face drawn or twisted. The commentators connect it with the disfigurement of the lips.

The connection between the grimace and the face matters. A grimace is an expression — something a face does when a person is in pain. The verse describes a permanent expression produced by a permanent condition.

The contrast with the believers is the verse's point. They were described by their conduct and never by their appearance; the people here are described by nothing but what is done to their appearance.

That is the loss the verse names. A person reduced to a disfigured face has lost the conduct that the sūrah spent its opening verses praising, and the reduction is described in a single clause.

The clause is the sūrah's shortest description of the Fire and its most specific. What the believers were praised for is gone, and what remains is a face the Fire has made.

**The Verse After the Weighing**

The verse follows the account of the light scale and the self-destruction, and the sequence moves from judgement to experience. The criterion is given, the outcome stated, and now what the outcome looks like.

The movement is deliberate. A reader who has been told that a person lost himself has been given an abstraction; a reader who is told that the Fire burns his face has been given something he can picture.

Stating a principle and then describing its concrete form has been the sūrah's method throughout, and the describing is what makes the principle land.

The verse is the most concrete description in the sūrah's closing movement. Nothing else in these verses names a part of the body, and the naming is what prevents the account of the Fire from remaining general.

Generality would have allowed the reader to keep his distance. A named part of the body does not, and the verse spends its brevity on the naming rather than on the surrounding account.

**Recognition Survives Even the Face's Ruin**

The face is what makes a person recognisable to others, and the verse's choice of the face as the site of disfigurement is therefore significant. The people described will not be recognisable.

The sūrah has described the breaking of kinship in the previous verse — no relations between them, no questioning of one another. The disfigurement completes that account: people who cannot recognise one another and cannot ask.

The two verses together describe a total isolation. The bonds are broken and the faces are changed, and the combination leaves nothing by which one person could reach another.

That isolation answers the audience's social theology directly. Their standing rested on being recognised by a group; the verse removes both the group's willingness to look and the face that would be looked at.

The removal is total and it is described in two adjacent verses. Nothing is left by which the audience's system of standing could operate, and the sūrah does not comment on the fact.

**The Verse Inside the Closing Movement**

The verse stands between the weighing and the questioning that follows, and its position is transitional. The account of the Day moves from the criterion to the punishment to the dialogue, and the verse is the middle stage.

The dialogue that follows is addressed to the people described here, and the address assumes the punishment. They are asked whether the signs were not recited to them, and the asking happens while the Fire is on their faces.

The sequence is not softened. The punishment is described, the questioning follows immediately, and the reader is given both in adjacent verses.

Placing the questioning inside the punishment rather than before it is what distinguishes this reckoning from a trial. A trial precedes its sentence; this exchange happens after the sentence has begun, and the verse's position makes that plain.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:105

> ˹It will be said,˺ “Were My revelations not recited to you, but you used to deny them?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Put to the Punished**

The verse records a question addressed to the people in the Fire, and the question is about the revelations. Were they not recited to you? And you denied them.

The form is a rhetorical question expecting the answer yes. The recitation happened; the denial happened; the question establishes both before anything further is said.

The sūrah has been describing the recitation throughout. The signs were recited to the audience and they turned on their heels; the Word was given to them and they did not contemplate it. The verse returns to that history in the form of a question.

Returning to it as a question rather than a charge matters. The addressees are not being told what they did; they are being asked to confirm it, and the confirming is what makes the reckoning theirs rather than God's assertion alone.

The asking is the last use the sūrah makes of the recitation. Earlier the signs were described, quoted, and appealed to; here they are simply referred back to the people who heard them, and the reference requires nothing further.

That economy is what makes the question final. Nothing is added to the history and nothing is taken from it. The addressees are asked to hold what they already know, and the holding is the whole of the reckoning.

**The Question as an Accounting**

The verse is part of the reckoning, and the reckoning takes the form of a reminder rather than an accusation. The people are not told what they did; they are asked whether they remember.

The form matters. An accusation can be denied; a question about a shared history cannot, because the asker and the asked both know the answer. The verse uses the second form.

The sūrah has used the same method throughout. The three questions about ownership, the challenge to produce the like of the message, the questions about the heavens and the Throne — all of them are questions the audience answers itself.

**"But You Used to Deny Them"**

The second half of the question states the denial, and the stating is the verse's sharpest feature. The question does not ask whether they denied; it says they did.

The construction combines a question with an assertion, and the combination leaves the addressees nothing to answer. They cannot deny the recitation, and they cannot deny the denial.

The challenge about the message used the same structure — a question that contained its own answer. The verse here does the same thing at the reckoning, and the repetition shows that the method is not a rhetorical trick but the sūrah's way of settling a matter.

Settling it this way also removes the possibility of a defence. A person accused can dispute the charge; a person asked to confirm a shared history cannot, and the verse asks for confirmation.

The confirmation is therefore the verdict. Nothing further is pronounced on the addressees in this exchange; they confirm the recitation and the denial, and the confirming is the whole of the judgement.

**The Verse After the Punishment**

The verse follows the description of the Fire on the faces, and the sequence is unsoftened. The punishment is described, and the questioning happens immediately after, while the punishment is ongoing.

The placement tells the reader what the questioning is for. It is not a prelude to the punishment; it happens inside it. The people are being reminded of what they did while they undergo the consequence.

The sūrah has described the same arrangement in the earlier request to return. A person asks to go back at the moment the interval closes; the people here are questioned at the moment the punishment is described. Both sequences place the dialogue inside the outcome rather than before it.

**Recitation Stood Directly Against Their Denial**

The two halves of the question name the two things the sūrah has been describing. The recitation is God's part; the denial is theirs. The verse puts both in one sentence and asks the addressees to hold them together.

The holding is the reckoning. A person who can see that the recitation happened and that he denied it has been given the whole account in a single question, and the account is the judgement.

The same thing was said in different words earlier: the truth was brought and the audience are liars. The verse here is that statement put as a question to the people it concerns.

Putting it as a question transfers the work. The earlier statement was made about the audience; this one is made to them, and the difference is that they now have to hold the two halves together themselves.

**The Verse Inside the Dialogue**

The verse opens a short dialogue that runs through the next three verses. The question is put, the answer is given, a request is made, and the request is refused.

The dialogue is the sūrah's final account of the audience, and it is given in the form of a conversation rather than a description. The reader overhears it, as he overheard the Prophet's prayer earlier.

The overhearing is the verse's method. A description of the reckoning could be received as information; a recorded dialogue cannot, because the reader is placed inside the exchange.

The sūrah used the same device with the Prophet's prayer earlier. There the reader overheard a supplication; here he overhears a reckoning. Both placements make the reader a witness rather than a recipient of information.

Witnessing is what makes the dialogue usable. Information about the Day can be filed away; a recorded exchange between God and the people in the Fire cannot, because the reader is standing where the addressees stood.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:106

> They will cry, “Our Lord! Our ill-fate took hold of us, so we became a misguided people

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Given**

The verse records the reply to the question, and the reply is an admission. Their wretchedness overwhelmed them, and they became a people astray.

The admission is complete. The addressees do not dispute the recitation or the denial; they explain how the denial happened, and the explanation is that something took hold of them.

That condition has been described throughout the sūrah. The hearts were distracted, the faculties were sealed, the chiefs behaved arrogantly. The verse is the addressees' own account of what the sūrah has been describing.

**"Took Hold of Us"**

The verb carries the sense of taking possession or dominion over something, and the commentators note the strength of the word. The wretchedness did not influence them; it owned them.

The sense of ownership is significant. A person influenced can resist; a person owned cannot, and the addressees are describing the second condition.

The same possession appears elsewhere in the sūrah. The distraction from remembrance was described as something that made them forget; the sealing of the faculties was described as something done to them. The verse is the addressees naming what the sūrah has been describing.

Their naming it is significant. The sūrah's earlier descriptions could be resisted as a messenger's characterisation; the addressees' own words cannot, and the verse supplies those words.

The words are also the strongest available. Possession is not influence or pressure; it is ownership, and the addressees choose the strongest term to describe what happened to them.

**A Statement, Not a Plea**

The commentators read the verse as a report rather than a petition. The addressees are not asking for mercy; they are describing what happened.

The distinction matters. A plea can be answered; a statement of fact can only be acknowledged. The verse records an acknowledgment, and the acknowledgment is the whole of the reply. Nothing is asked for, nothing is disputed, and no alternative account is offered.

The absence of a plea is what distinguishes this verse from the request that follows. The addressees first describe their condition and then ask to be removed from it, and the sūrah gives the two speeches separately so that the difference is visible.

Separating them is deliberate. A single speech that described and then asked would blur the two; two speeches make the description stand on its own, and the standing is what gives the next request its context.

The context matters for the refusal that follows. A reader who has heard the description separately knows that the addressees understood their condition before they asked to be removed from it, and the knowing makes the refusal intelligible rather than harsh.

An earlier passage gives the contrast. The request to return at the moment of death was a plea; this statement is not. The difference is that the plea came at a point where something might still be asked for, and this comes after.

**Wretchedness as Destiny**

Some commentators read the verse as describing a divine decree recorded beforehand, so that the wretchedness was written rather than merely suffered. The reading connects the verse with the Qur'an's statements about what is inscribed.

The reading is not the only one available, and the sūrah does not settle the question. What the verse gives is the addressees' own account, and the account uses the language of being overwhelmed rather than of choosing.

Leaving the question unsettled is consistent with the rest of the sūrah. It describes distraction and sealing as things that happen to people while also holding them responsible, and the verse keeps both halves in the addressees' own mouth.

That ambiguity runs through the whole sūrah. The hearts are distracted and the distraction is described as something that happens to them; the faculties are sealed and the sealing is described as a consequence. The verse leaves both halves standing.

**The Verse After the Question**

The verse follows the question about the recitation, and the sequence is exact. The question establishes the facts; the reply explains them; and the explanation is an admission rather than a defence.

The arrangement leaves the addressees nothing to argue with. They have confirmed the recitation and the denial, and the explanation they offer does not dispute either.

That structure has been the sūrah's throughout. A question is put, the audience answers itself, and the answer establishes what the question was about. The verse here is the final instance, and it is the first in which the answer is given after the outcome has begun.

The timing changes what the answer does. An answer given inside the interval is a decision; an answer given after it is a description, and the verse records a description.

**"We Became a People Astray"**

The second half of the reply names the condition, and the naming is collective. They became a people astray, not individuals who went astray separately.

The collective form matters. The sūrah has described a community organised around resistance — the chiefs, the nightly assembly, the appeal to the forefathers. The verse is that community describing itself.

The self-description is the verse's final feature. The addressees are not being told what they were; they are saying it, and the saying is the reckoning.

The collective form also matches the sūrah's account of how the refusal happened. It was not a set of individual errors but a shared posture — the chiefs, the assembly, the appeal to the forefathers — and the addressees describe themselves in the same collective terms they lived in.

The match is the verse's quiet point. The community that organised itself around shared standing describes its ruin in the same collective voice, and the voice is the only thing that survives unchanged.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:107

> Our Lord! Take us out of this ˹Fire˺. Then if we ever return ˹to denial˺, we will truly be wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Request Made to Be Sent Back**

The verse records a second speech from the people in the Fire, and this one is a request. Take us out, and if we return to denial, we will truly be wrongdoers.

The request differs from the admission in the previous verse. That was a statement of fact; this is an appeal with a promise attached, and the promise is what distinguishes it.

An earlier passage gives the same plea. The request to return at the moment of death asked for a second interval; this request asks for the same thing from inside the Fire rather than at its edge.

The repetition of the plea across two different points is the sūrah's way of showing that the request does not change with the situation. The words are nearly the same, and the nearness is what makes the second refusal intelligible.

A different plea might have warranted a different answer. The same plea repeated after the barrier has closed cannot, and the sūrah gives the repetition so that the reader sees the sameness himself.

**The Promise and Its Structure**

The addressees offer a conditional promise: if we ever return to denial, we will truly be wrongdoers. The promise accepts the classification the sūrah has been applying throughout.

The acceptance is significant. The addressees do not dispute the term *wrongdoers*; they use it themselves, and the using is an admission that the classification is correct.

The previous verse gives the same acceptance. The addressees said they became a people astray; here they say they would be wrongdoers if they returned. Both statements use the sūrah's own vocabulary against themselves.

That is the sūrah's aim throughout. It supplies the terms — believers, wrongdoers, the successful, the distracted — and waits for the audience to apply them. The verse records the application being made by the people it concerns.

**The Promise as Evidence Against Itself**

The commentators note that the request is to return to the world to perform good works, with the attestation that a second failure would make them wrongdoers. The attestation is the verse's sharpest feature.

A promise that names its own violation is a promise the speaker expects to be tested. The addressees are not claiming they would certainly succeed; they are saying that failure would confirm the judgement already passed on them.

The interval was given and not used; the record speaks the truth; the accounting is exact. The promise assumes all of that and asks anyway.

Asking anyway is what makes the request a request rather than an argument. The addressees have no case to make and know it, and the verse records an appeal made without grounds.
