Recording it is what makes the refusal that follows comprehensible. A reader who has heard an appeal with no grounds behind it understands why the answer is short, and the shortness is the sūrah's next verse.

**The Verse After the Admission**

The verse follows the statement about wretchedness, and the sequence moves from description to appeal. The addressees have said what happened; now they ask for something to be done about it.

The movement is natural and the sūrah records it without comment. A person who has admitted a condition will ask for relief from it, and the verse gives the asking in the addressees' own words.

The sūrah does not say that the request is futile; it records the request and lets the next verse answer it. The restraint is what makes the answer land, because the reader has heard the whole of the asking before hearing the refusal.

Recording the asking in full also prevents the refusal from seeming arbitrary. A reader who has heard the promise and the conditional knows exactly what is being refused, and the knowing is the verse's contribution to the sequence.

**Requests Like It Appear Elsewhere**

The Qur'an records the same request in several places. As-Sajdah: *"Our Lord! We have seen and we have heard; so send us back that we may work righteousness."* (32:12). Al-Munāfiqūn gives it at the moment of death rather than from the Fire (63:10).

The parallel in this sūrah is the earlier request to go back, and the two are the same plea made at different points. The sūrah gives both, and the giving of both shows that the plea does not change with the situation.

The repetitions establish the request as a fixed feature of the Qur'an's account, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives it twice — once at the barrier and once from the Fire.

Giving it twice inside one sūrah is unusual, and the doubling is deliberate. The first refusal came with a barrier; the second comes with a command not to speak. The escalation between the two is what the repetition makes visible.

**The Verse Inside the Dialogue**

The verse is the third stage of the short dialogue that began with the question about the recitation. The question was put, the admission was made, the request is now made, and the next verse answers it.

The dialogue is the sūrah's final account of the audience, and it is complete. Nothing further is asked or said by the addressees after this verse, and the next verse closes the exchange.

The closure is abrupt, and the abruptness is the sūrah's method. The dialogue is given in full and then ended without elaboration, and the ending is what the reader is left with.

The sūrah then turns to the other group, and the turning is the point of the abruptness. The exchange with the mockers is finished in a sentence; the account of the servants who prayed is given in full, and the difference in length is itself a judgement.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:108

> Allah will respond, “Be despised in there! Do not ˹ever˺ plead with Me ˹again˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Refusal Given Without Appeal**

The verse records the answer to the request, and the answer is a refusal in two parts. Be gone in there; do not speak to Me.

The first part dismisses; the second closes the channel. The addressees are not merely refused; they are told that the asking itself is over, and the telling is the verse's sharpest feature.

Earlier refusals in the sūrah were refusals of requests that could not be granted. This one is different in kind: it closes the possibility of asking, and the closing is permanent rather than situational.

The difference matters for the reader. A request refused on its merits might succeed on different terms; a channel closed cannot be reopened, and the verse gives the closing without qualification.

The lack of qualification is what makes the verse the sūrah's severest statement. Nothing else in the closing movement removes a capacity; everything else describes a condition, and this verse removes the capacity to ask about the condition.

**"Do Not Speak to Me"**

The commentators connect the phrase with the Qur'an's statements about God not speaking to certain people, understood as both a sign of disapproval and a punishment in itself. Al-Baqarah gives the idea elsewhere.

The double character of the phrase matters. The refusal is not only a denial of the request; it is the withdrawal of the relation that made the request possible. The addressees lose the capacity to address God, and the loss is itself the punishment.

The same withdrawal appears earlier. The sealing of the faculties was described as something done to people who refused to use them; the verse here describes the closing of the channel of prayer.

The parallel is exact. What was not used is taken away, and the taking away is not an additional penalty but the completion of the refusal. The verse gives the completion in four words.

**The Verse After the Promise**

The verse follows the conditional promise, and the refusal does not engage with the promise. The addressees offered to be wrongdoers if they returned; the verse does not test the offer.

The non-engagement is significant. A promise is tested by being accepted; a promise that is not accepted has not been tested, and the verse leaves the offer standing without answering it.

The arrangement has been the sūrah's throughout. The interval was given and not used; the request to return comes after the interval has closed. The verse here is the final statement of that arrangement.

Leaving the promise untested is also the verse's answer to it. A person who would have kept the promise did not need to make it inside the interval; the verse treats the offer as evidence about the past rather than as a proposal about the future.

**Such Abruptness Belongs to This Refusal**

The verse is short, and the shortness is deliberate. The dialogue that began with the question about the recitation ends in a single sentence, and the sentence does not elaborate.

The absence of elaboration is the verse's method. A longer refusal would invite further argument; a short one closes the exchange, and the sūrah closes it.

The sūrah has used the same economy elsewhere. The statement that the truth was brought and the audience are liars was given without development; the refusal here is given the same way.

Both statements are verdicts rather than arguments, and neither is developed. The sūrah reserves development for the descriptions of what happens; the verdicts are given in a clause and left standing.

The division of labour is exact, and it tells the reader where the sūrah puts its weight. Length is spent on making the situation visible; brevity is spent on settling it.

The verse is the shortest verdict in the sūrah and the last one addressed to the mockers. After it they are not spoken to again, and the sūrah turns to the group they laughed at.

**The Verse Inside the Sequence**

The verse stands between the request and the account of the servants who prayed, and the contrast is deliberate. The addressees are told not to speak to God; the next verse describes a group who spoke to Him and were answered.

The juxtaposition is the sūrah's final contrast. Two groups are described in adjacent verses — one that mocked the other, and one that prayed. The refusal and the reward are given in sequence, and the sequence is the sūrah's last judgement.

The arrangement tells the reader which group the sūrah has been describing all along. The believers were listed at the opening; the mockers have been described throughout; the closing verses give both their outcomes.

**The Refusal and the Interval**

The verse closes the dialogue, and the closing is permanent. The addressees are not told to wait or to ask again later; they are told not to ask at all.

The permanence answers the request's implicit assumption. The addressees asked as though the situation could be revisited; the verse says it cannot, and the saying is the final word on the interval.

Everything since the verse about the fixed term has been describing that interval. This verse closes it, and the closing is what the whole account has been moving toward.

The sūrah then describes a group for whom the channel was open, and the contrast gives the closing its full weight. The refusal is not a general statement about prayer; it is a statement about what happens when the interval has been spent.

The contrast is the verse's final service to the reader. He is shown both the closing and the openness, and the showing is what makes the instruction that follows the sūrah's last one.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:109

> Indeed, there was a group of My servants who used to pray, ‘Our Lord! We have believed, so forgive us and have mercy on us, for You are the best of those who show mercy,’

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Group Named**

The verse introduces a second group, and the introduction follows immediately on the refusal given to the first. The addressees were told not to speak to God; this verse describes people who spoke to Him.

The juxtaposition is the verse's whole force. Two groups are set side by side — one that mocked and was refused, one that prayed and was answered — and the sūrah gives both outcomes without comment.

The reader is given the two accounts and left to see the contrast, and the seeing is the verse's argument. No verdict is pronounced on either group in this verse; the verdict emerges from the adjacency.

The adjacency is also the sūrah's last contrast. It has paired believers with sects, messengers with chiefs, trembling with arrogance. This final pairing is the tightest, because the two groups are described in consecutive verses and in God's own voice.

**The Prayer Quoted**

The verse records the words of the group, and the words are a prayer. Our Lord, we have believed; forgive us; have mercy on us; You are the best of those who show mercy.

The prayer is complete in a single verse. It states belief, asks for forgiveness, asks for mercy, and names God as the best of the merciful. Nothing is left out, and nothing is added.

That posture has been described throughout. The believers give with hearts trembling; they are humble in their prayers; they guard what they have been entrusted with. The verse gives the words that correspond to the posture.

The correspondence is what makes the verse the sūrah's summary of prayer. Everything the opening list described about the believers' inward condition is gathered into one sentence, and the sentence is given as something said rather than something felt.

**"We Have Believed, So Forgive Us"**

The sequence in the prayer is significant. Belief is stated first, and the request for forgiveness follows it. The group does not ask for belief; it states it and then asks for what belief requires.

The arrangement tells the reader what the group understands about belief. It is not a finished state; it is a condition that makes forgiveness necessary, and the prayer is shaped accordingly.

The believers elsewhere in the sūrah tremble over their good deeds, and the record speaks the truth about what is forgotten. The prayer assumes both and asks for mercy on that basis.

That basis is what distinguishes this prayer from the plea in the previous verses. The addressees asked for a change in their situation; the group asks for mercy within a situation it accepts, and the difference is the sūrah's final contrast.

**"The Best of Those Who Show Mercy"**

The phrase appears elsewhere in the Qur'an — in al-Aʿrāf, Yūsuf, al-Anbiyāʾ, and at the end of this sūrah. Its recurrence establishes it as a fixed form of address.

The form is a superlative, and the superlative does specific work. It does not merely call God merciful; it places His mercy above every other instance of mercy, and the placing is what the prayer needs.

The sūrah ends with the same phrase, and the repetition frames the closing movement. The group's prayer and the Prophet's prayer use the same words, and the sharing is deliberate.

Sharing the phrase also levels the two. The messenger does not pray in a register above the believers; he closes the sūrah with their formula, and the closing places him among them rather than above them.

The placement is the sūrah's answer to the charge that he was only a man like them. He is a man like them, and the verse shows him praying the same prayer they prayed, in the same words, for the same thing.

**The Verse After the Refusal**

The verse follows the command not to speak to God, and the sequence is exact. The channel is closed for one group; the next verse describes a group for whom it was open.

The arrangement answers an unspoken question. A reader who has just heard the refusal might suppose that prayer has become impossible; the verse shows that it was available, and that the group used it.

The prayers of the Prophet were recorded earlier; the believers were described as humble in their prayers. The verse here gives the words of the group that used what was available.

Supplying the actual words is what makes the verse usable. A reader is not told that a group prayed; he is given the sentence they said, and the sentence is available to him.

The availability is the verse's purpose. A description of prayer would inform; a quotation of prayer equips, and the sūrah ends by equipping rather than informing.

**The Group and the Mockers**

The verse describes the group as My servants, and the possessive is significant. The mockers were not addressed in those terms; the group is.

The next verse will describe what the mockers did to this group — they mocked them until the mockery made them forget the remembrance of God. The verse here establishes the group before describing the treatment.

The order makes the mockery intelligible as a response to something specific rather than as general hostility. The reader hears the prayer first, and the mockery then has an object.

That ordering also protects the group's prayer from being read as a reaction. It was not provoked; it was already being said when the mockery began, and the verse's placement shows that.

The placement also gives the mockery its full character. Laughter directed at a prayer is different from laughter directed at a claim, and the sūrah makes sure the reader hears the prayer before hearing about the laughter.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:110

> but you were ˹so busy˺ making fun of them that it made you forget My remembrance. And you used to laugh at them

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mockery Named**

The verse describes what the addressees did to the group named in the previous verse, and the description is specific. They mocked them, and the mockery made them forget the remembrance of God.

The specificity matters. The verse does not describe general hostility; it names mockery and laughter, and it describes the effect the mockery had on the mockers rather than on the mocked.

That behaviour has been described throughout. The chiefs laughed; the nightly assembly talked; the audience turned on its heels. The verse here names the behaviour and its consequence in a single sentence.

Naming the consequence is what makes the verse more than a complaint. The mockery is not presented as an injury done to the group but as a harm the mockers did to themselves, and the harm is specified as forgetting.

Forgetting is the precise loss. The sūrah has described remembrance as the practice the audience abandoned, and the verse identifies mockery as one of the things that produced the abandonment.

**The Effect on the Mockers**

The verse says the mockery made the addressees forget God's remembrance, and the direction of the effect is the verse's sharpest feature. The mockery did not harm the group; it harmed the people doing it.

The commentators connect the verse with the Qur'an's statements about forgetting to remember God, and the connection is significant. Forgetting is described as something that happens to a person through his own actions.

The sūrah has described the same mechanism elsewhere. The distraction from remembrance was described as something that happens; the sealing of the faculties was described as a consequence. The verse here gives the mockery as the cause and the forgetting as the result.

**Mockery as a Recurring Trait**

The Qur'an repeatedly describes mockery as a trait of those who refuse the messengers. The commentators list al-Baqarah, an-Nisāʾ, al-Māʾidah, al-Anʿām, Luqmān and al-Jāthiyah as containing such descriptions.

The recurrence establishes mockery as a fixed feature of the Qur'an's account of refusal, and the recurrence means the audience had heard it before. The verse is not introducing a behaviour; it is naming one the audience recognises.

A reader who has been mocking can hear himself described, and the description is given without elaboration. No motive is supplied and no excuse is recorded, which leaves the behaviour standing on its own.

The absence of a motive is deliberate. Mockery explained would become understandable; mockery named remains what it is, and the verse chooses the second.

The choice also refuses the reader an exit. A person who is given a motive can consider whether it applies to him; a person who is simply shown the behaviour has to recognise it or not, and the verse leaves the recognition to him.

**The Verse After the Prayer**

The verse follows the group's prayer, and the juxtaposition is deliberate. The group prayed; the addressees mocked. The two actions are described in adjacent verses, and the contrast is the verse's argument.

The arrangement also explains the mockery. The addressees were not responding to a threat or an accusation; they were responding to a prayer. The mockery was directed at people asking God for forgiveness, and the direction is what the verse establishes.

Elsewhere in the sūrah the chiefs laughed at the messengers and the audience turned away from the signs. The verse here gives the laughter a specific object, and the object is the least threatening thing imaginable.

That is what makes the verse severe. The mockery was not a response to power or to accusation; it was a response to humility, and the sūrah records it as the thing that cost the mockers their remembrance.

**"And You Used to Laugh at Them"**

The verse ends with a second description of the same behaviour, and the repetition is deliberate. The mockery is named, its effect is described, and then the laughter is named again.

The repetition makes the behaviour inescapable. A reader cannot say that the verse describes something other than laughter; the word is given twice, and the giving is the point.

The same method appears elsewhere in the sūrah. The charge of lying was stated twice; the questions about ownership were repeated in different forms. Repetition is how the sūrah closes a description.

Here the repetition closes the account of the mockers. After this verse they are described no further; the sūrah turns to the reward of the group they laughed at, and the turning is the last word about them.

The turning is also the sūrah's final reversal. The people who were laughed at are given the last description, and the people who laughed are given the last silence.

**The Verse Inside the Closing Movement**

The verse stands between the group's prayer and the statement of their reward, and its position is transitional. The group prayed, the addressees mocked, and the next verse gives the outcome for the group.

The arrangement completes the contrast. Two groups are described, their actions are named, and their outcomes are given. The sūrah does not add a judgement; the sequence is the judgement.

The sequence is also the sūrah's final word on the audience. The mockers have been described throughout; the group has been described from the opening verse; the closing verses give both their ends.

Everything the sūrah has been moving toward is contained in that giving. The list of qualities, the messengers, the refusals, the interval, the weighing — all of it resolves into two groups and two outcomes, and the verse supplies the last description of the first group.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:111

> Today I have indeed rewarded them for their perseverance: they are certainly the triumphant.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reward Stated**

The verse gives the outcome for the group named two verses earlier, and the outcome is stated in God's own voice. Today I have rewarded them for their patience; they are the triumphant.

The direct speech is significant. The sūrah has been describing the Day in the third person; this verse switches to the first, and the switch marks the moment as God's own statement rather than a description of it.

The refusal to the people in the Fire was given in the same voice. The reward here matches it, and the parallel makes the contrast complete.

Both verdicts are therefore spoken rather than reported, and the speaking is what gives them finality. A description can be questioned; a statement in the first person cannot, and the sūrah reserves that voice for the two outcomes.

Reserving it is what marks these two verses as the sūrah's judgement. Everything else is narration; these two are speech, and the difference tells the reader where the sūrah's weight lies.

**"For Their Perseverance"**

The verse names what the reward is for, and the naming is *ṣabr* — patience, steadfastness, perseverance. The group is rewarded not for their belief alone but for having held it under mockery.

The specification is the verse's sharpest feature. The previous verse described the mockery and the laughter; this verse names patience as the thing that was exercised in response, and the naming connects the two.

The same virtue underlies everything the sūrah praised. The believers are humble in their prayers; they turn away from idle talk; they give with hearts trembling. Patience is the condition that made all of those possible under opposition.

Naming it as the ground of the reward also tells the reader what to cultivate. The qualities listed at the opening are achievable only by someone who can hold them while being laughed at, and the verse identifies the holding as the thing rewarded.

**"They Are the Triumphant"**

The word is *al-fāʾizūn*, and the commentators connect it with *fawz* — attainment of felicity in the Hereafter as well as victory over the world in a spiritual sense. The word carries both senses at once.

The double sense matters here. The group was mocked and laughed at; the verse calls them triumphant. The triumph is not a reversal of their worldly position but a statement about what that position turned out to be worth.

The same reversal runs through the sūrah. The chiefs claimed standing and were destroyed; the messengers were refused and were saved. The verse gives the reversal its name.

Naming it matters, because the group could not have seen it. While the laughter was happening, the triumph was invisible; the verse supplies the word that makes the invisible outcome speakable.

The word is also the sūrah's own. Success was claimed at the opening and confirmed at the weighing; triumph is the same reality given its final name, and the naming is spoken rather than reported.

**The Verse After the Mockery**

The verse follows the description of the mockery and the laughter, and the sequence is exact. The group prayed, the addressees mocked, and now the reward is given.

The arrangement leaves the mockery as the last thing described before the reward, and the placement is deliberate. The reader is given the hostility in full and then the outcome, and the outcome does not erase the hostility.

The messengers elsewhere in the sūrah were refused and then saved, and the refusal is not forgotten in the telling. The verse here gives the group's reward after their mockery for the same reason.

Keeping the mockery in view is what makes the reward meaningful. A reward given without reference to what was endured would be a statement about destination; a reward given immediately after the laughter is a statement about what the endurance was worth.

The worth is the sūrah's answer to the mockers. They treated the group's prayer as absurd; the verse records the prayer as the thing that was rewarded, and the recording reverses the judgement without arguing with it.

**The Verse Inside the Closing Movement**

The verse stands between the account of the two groups and the dialogue about the length of time, and its position closes the contrast. Both groups have been described and both outcomes have been given.

The sūrah then moves to a new dialogue — the question about how many years the addressees remained on earth — and the movement is a shift from outcome to perspective. The reward is settled; what follows is about how the time will look from the other side.

The shift is the sūrah's final method. It does not elaborate on the reward; it moves to the question that makes the reward intelligible, and the question is about duration.

Duration is the right subject because the reward was for patience, and patience is measured in time. A reader who knows how short the time was knows how small the cost of the patience turned out to be.

**The Word Returns**

The word *fāʾiz* belongs to the same root as *falāḥ*, the success named in the sūrah's opening verse and at the weighing. The group described as triumphant here is the group described as successful there.

The return of the root closes the sūrah's central claim. The believers were named as successful at the opening; the weighing confirmed it; the verse here gives it its final form.

The closing is deliberate. The sūrah began with a list of qualities and a claim about success; it ends with the same claim stated by God about a specific group, and the stating is the sūrah's last word on the matter.

The change of speaker is what closes it. The opening claim was made in the third person about the believers; the closing claim is made in the first person to them, and the change marks the difference between a description and a verdict.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:112

> He will ask ˹them˺, “How many years did you remain on earth?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A New Dialogue Opens**

The verse begins a short exchange about duration, and the exchange follows immediately on the statement of the reward. The outcomes are settled; the question that follows is about time.

The shift is deliberate. The sūrah has described what happens to each group; the dialogue now describes how the time will look from the other side, and the looking is the verse's subject.

That perspective is what makes the sūrah's instructions urgent. A reader told only that the interval is fixed can still treat it as long; a reader told how short it will seem afterwards cannot.

The verse therefore does not add new information about the Day. It changes the reader's relation to the time he is in, and the changing is its whole purpose.

That is what makes the verse an instruction rather than a description. Everything the sūrah has said about the Day has been aimed at the interval, and the question about duration is the last and most direct of those aims.

The directness is deliberate. After the reward and the refusal, the reader needs one further fact — how short the time was — and the verse obtains it from the people who have already used theirs.

**The Question Put**

The verse records the question in God's voice, and the question is about number. How many years did you remain on earth?

The form expects an answer, and the answer given in the next verse is wrong. The question is therefore not a test of arithmetic; it is a setup for the comparison that follows.

The three questions about ownership worked the same way — they expected answers the audience could give, and the giving established what the questions were about. This question expects an answer that will be corrected.

The correction is the point. A reader who hears the estimate and then the correction learns something he could not have learned from either statement alone, and the sūrah needs both halves.

The two halves together give the measure of the interval. The addressees supply the confusion; the correction supplies the smallness, and the reader is given both because neither is sufficient on its own.

**"On Earth"**

The phrase specifies the location, and the specification matters. The question is not about time in general; it is about time on earth, in the interval the sūrah has been describing.

The interval is what the verse is measuring. The sūrah has described it throughout — the fixed term, the period inside which conduct matters, the time the messengers were to be left. The question asks how long it seemed.

The asking is the verse's method. A person inside the interval experiences it as long; a person outside it will describe it differently, and the verse sets up the difference.

Setting it up as a question rather than a statement matters. The addressees will supply the answer themselves, and an answer supplied by the speaker carries more weight than a figure given to him.

**The Verse After the Reward**

The verse follows the statement that the group has been rewarded, and the sequence moves from outcome to perspective. The reward is given; the question that follows makes it intelligible by showing what the time was worth.

The arrangement is deliberate. A reader who has been told that patience was rewarded needs to know what the patience cost, and the cost is measured in time. The question sets up the measurement.

The sūrah therefore makes the addressees its witnesses against themselves one final time. They supplied the answers about ownership; they supply the estimate about duration, and the estimate is the last thing they contribute.

The contribution is also the least useful thing they could have offered. A number would have settled the question; an estimate followed by a deflection does not, and the sūrah records both halves of the failure.

That concern runs through the whole sūrah. The interval is the only part of the arrangement a person controls; the record speaks the truth about what was done in it. The verse here asks how long the interval was.

**The Question and the Mockers**

The verse is addressed to the people who were refused two verses earlier, and the address continues the dialogue. The refusal closed their requests; the question opens a new exchange.

The continuation is significant. The addressees were told not to plead with God; they are now being asked a question by Him. The channel is not entirely closed — it is closed in one direction and open in the other.

That asymmetry appears earlier in the sūrah. The audience could not change the outcome; God could still address them. The verse here gives the addressing a specific content.

**The Verse Inside the Sūrah's Account of Time**

The verse stands inside the sūrah's sustained account of time and duration. The fixed term was described early; the interval was described throughout; the barrier was described as lasting until the resurrection.

The question about the number of years is the final stage of that account. Everything so far has described the interval from inside; the question asks how it looks from outside, and the answer will be given in the next two verses.

The account is the sūrah's way of making the interval legible. A reader who knows how long the interval seemed to those who have left it has been given a measure of what his own time is worth.

The measure is not a number but a comparison, and the comparison is what the sūrah needs. Nobody can be told how many years he has; everybody can be told how short they will seem, and the verse supplies the second.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:113

> They will reply, “We remained ˹only˺ a day or part of a day. But ask those who kept count.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Given**

The verse records the reply, and the reply is an estimate. A day, or part of a day. The addressees do not give a number of years; they give a fraction of a day.

The estimate is the verse's whole content. The sūrah does not correct it here; the correction comes in the next verse. The verse gives the answer and leaves it standing.

The arrangement is deliberate. A reader who hears the estimate before the correction is given the addressees' own perspective first, and the perspective is what the verse is about.

Giving the perspective first is what makes the correction intelligible. A reader who is simply told that the interval was short learns a fact; a reader who hears the addressees groping for a number learns what the shortness feels like.

The groping is the verse's contribution. It is not a statement about duration but a record of confusion, and the confusion is what the sūrah wants the reader to recognise.

**"A Day or Part of a Day"**

The phrase is an idiom for a very short time, and the sūrah uses it without explanation. The addressees are not describing a literal day; they are saying that the whole of their earthly life now seems brief.

The Qur'an uses the same idiom elsewhere. The commentators connect it with passages in Yūnus, al-Isrāʾ, al-Kahf, ar-Rūm, al-Aḥqāf and an-Nāziʿāt, all of which describe the life of this world as seeming short from the perspective of the Hereafter.

The recurrence establishes the idiom as a fixed feature of the Qur'an's account, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn gives it in the form of a reply to a question.

The reply form is the sūrah's variation. Elsewhere the shortness is stated; here it is estimated by people who have lived it, and the estimating is less precise and more convincing.

**"But Ask Those Who Kept Count"**

The second half of the reply deflects, and the deflection is significant. The addressees do not stand behind their estimate; they refer the question to people who kept count.

The deflection reveals their uncertainty. They have given an estimate and immediately said that someone else would know better, and the saying is an admission that they do not trust their own answer.

That ignorance is the verse's sharpest feature. The addressees did not know the length of the interval while they were in it; they do not know it now. Both halves of the ignorance are given in one sentence.

The second half is the more severe. Losing track of time inside the interval is ordinary; being unable to reconstruct it afterwards means the time has no handle left, and the verse describes exactly that.

**The Verse After the Question**

The verse follows the question about the number of years, and the sequence is exact. The question asked for a number; the answer gives a fraction of a day and then deflects.

The arrangement sets up the correction. A reader who has heard the estimate and the deflection is prepared for the next verse to say that the estimate was wrong, and the correction is given without surprise.

The same method appeared with the three questions about ownership. The answers were given and then used; here the answer is given and then corrected, and the correcting is the verse's purpose.

The difference between using and correcting is worth noting. The ownership answers were right and were built on; this answer is wrong and is replaced, and the replacing is what the verse exists to make possible.

**The Deflection as a Description**

The deflection is also a description of the addressees' condition. They cannot answer a question about their own duration, and they know they cannot.

The sūrah has described the same condition throughout. The hearts were distracted; the faculties were sealed; the record speaks the truth about what they forgot. The verse gives the ignorance its final form — they do not know how long they were.

The ignorance is the verse's sharpest feature. A person who does not know how long he lived has lost the measure of the only thing he controlled, and the loss is described without comment.

The absence of comment is deliberate. The sūrah does not say that the ignorance is a punishment; it records it, and the recording leaves the reader to see what has been lost.

What has been lost is the interval itself. The sūrah spent its length describing the interval as the only part of the arrangement a person controls, and the verse shows people who can no longer say how much of it they had.

**The Verse Inside the Sūrah's Account of Time**

The verse stands between the question and the correction, and its position is transitional. The estimate is given, the deflection is made, and the next verse states the truth.

The arrangement completes the sūrah's account of the interval. The fixed term was described; the interval was described as the only part a person controls; the barrier was described as lasting until the resurrection. The verse here gives the addressees' own estimate of the whole, and the estimate is a fraction of a day.

The estimate is what the sūrah has been moving toward. A reader who hears it has been given the measure of the interval from the other side, and the measure is what makes the sūrah's instructions urgent.

Urgency is the verse's contribution to the closing movement. The reward has been given and the refusal has been given; what remains is to tell the reader how much time the difference between them took, and the answer is part of a day.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:114

> He will say, “You only remained for a little while, if only you knew

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Correction Given to the Dead**

The verse records the correction, and the correction is brief. You remained only a little while — if only you had known.

The correction does not give a number. The question asked for years; the answer gives a little while, and the vagueness is deliberate. The point is not the quantity but the comparison.

That method is consistent across the sūrah. It does not supply figures; it describes relations, and the relation here is between the length of the interval and how it now seems.

A figure would have invited argument about whether the interval was long or short. A comparison settles the matter, because the addressees have already given their own estimate and the correction simply confirms how small it was.

The correction is therefore not a new claim but a confirmation of what the addressees groped toward. They said a day or part of a day; the verse says a little while, and the nearness of the two statements is what makes the correction final.

**"If Only You Knew"**

The phrase is the verse's sharpest feature. It is addressed to people who no longer have the capacity to know, and the addressing is what makes it a rebuke rather than a statement.

The conditional implies that the knowing was available earlier. The addressees are not being told something they could not have known; they are being told something they did not know when knowing it mattered.

That availability is what the whole sūrah has described. The signs were recited; the interval was given; the record speaks the truth. The verse here names the failure to know as the addressees' own.

Naming it as theirs is what makes the phrase a rebuke rather than a remark. A person who could not have known is not rebuked for not knowing; a person who could have is, and the conditional does that work in three words.

**The Verse After the Deflection**

The verse follows the reply that deflected to those who kept count, and the correction does not engage with the deflection. The addressees referred the question to others; the verse answers it directly.

The non-engagement is significant. The deflection was an admission of ignorance; the correction confirms the ignorance without commenting on it. The sūrah gives both and moves on.

An answer is given, a deflection is made, and a correction is stated in a single sentence. Nothing is elaborated, and the lack of elaboration is the sūrah's method at its most economical.

The economy also refuses the addressees a way back into the exchange. A comment on their deflection would have invited a reply; a bare correction closes the matter, and the closing is immediate.

After this verse the addressees are not quoted again. The sūrah moves to the question about purpose and then to its declarations, and the silence is what marks the end of their part.

**The Comparison and the Interval**

The verse completes the sūrah's account of the interval. The fixed term was described early; the interval was described as the only part a person controls; the barrier lasts until the resurrection; and now the whole of it is described as a little while.

The description is the verse's final claim. The interval that seemed long from inside is short from outside, and the shortness is what makes the sūrah's instructions urgent.

Everything since the verse about the fixed term has been describing that interval. This verse gives its measure, and the measure is what the whole account has been moving toward.

The measure is also the sūrah's answer to the audience's delay. They treated the interval as though there were more of it; the verse says there was less than a day, and the saying is what the instructions have been building toward.

**This Verse Speaks to Its Reader**

The verse is addressed to the addressees, but it is heard by a reader inside the interval. The hearing is the verse's purpose.

A reader who is told that the interval is a little while has been given a measure of his own time, and the measure is not a number but a comparison. The time he is in will seem short when he is out of it.

The interval is the only part a person controls, and the record speaks the truth about what was done in it. The verse here tells the reader how long he has, and the telling is the sūrah's final instruction.

It is an instruction rather than information because it changes what the reader does with the time. Knowing that the interval will seem short is not a fact to be filed; it is a reason to act inside it, and the verse gives it for that purpose.

**The Verse Inside the Closing Movement**

The verse stands between the dialogue about duration and the question about purpose, and its position is transitional. The time has been measured; the next verse asks what it was for.

The arrangement completes the sūrah's account. The interval is short; the question that follows asks whether it was purposeless. The two together give the reader both the measure and the meaning of his time.

Neither half is sufficient alone. A short purposeless interval would be nothing; a long purposeful one could be deferred. Short and purposeful together is the combination that requires action now, and the sūrah gives both in adjacent verses.

What follows is the sūrah's last word — the exaltation of God, the statement about calling on other gods, and the closing prayer. The account of time is finished, and the declarations assume it.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:115

> Did you then think that We had created you without purpose, and that you would never be returned to Us?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question About Purpose**

The verse puts a question to the addressees, and the question is about the purpose of their creation. Did you suppose We created you frivolously, and that you would not be returned to Us?

The form is rhetorical and expects the answer no. The addressees are not being asked whether they held the supposition; they are being shown that the supposition is what their behaviour implied.

That method runs through the sūrah. The three questions about ownership expected answers the audience could give; this question expects a denial that the addressees' conduct contradicts.

The contradiction is the verse's force. A person who denies that creation was purposeless while having lived as though it were is caught between his answer and his conduct, and the verse leaves him there.

Leaving him there is the sūrah's method at its most economical. No further argument is offered, because the contradiction does not need one; it needs only to be seen, and the question makes it visible.

**"Without Purpose"**

The word carries the sense of play or frivolity, and the Qur'an repeatedly denies that creation was made in play. The commentators connect it with passages in al-Anbiyāʾ, ad-Dukhān, al-ʿAnkabūt, ar-Rūm, az-Zumar and Āl ʿImrān, all of which assert that the world was created in truth rather than in vain.

The recurrence establishes the denial as a fixed claim of the Qur'an, and the sūrah of al-Muʾminūn puts it as a question rather than a statement. The form makes the addressees answer it themselves.

The question form is also more effective than a statement. A reader who denies that creation was purposeless has committed himself to the return, and the verse makes him say so.

Binding the two claims together is the verse's real work. Purpose and return are asserted separately elsewhere in the Qur'an; here they are made inseparable, so that conceding one concedes the other.

**"And That You Would Not Be Returned to Us"**

The second half of the question names the return, and the naming is the sūrah's answer to the mockery about dust and bones. The audience asked whether they would really be resurrected; the verse assumes the return and asks whether they supposed otherwise.

The assumption is the verse's method. The sūrah does not argue for the resurrection; it uses it as a premise in a question about purpose, and the using is the argument.

The Qur'an repeatedly asserts that human beings will return to God, and that all things return to Him. The verse here gives the return in the form of a question the addressees cannot answer otherwise.

The earlier mockery about dust and bones is answered by being bypassed. The verse does not revisit the objection; it asks a question in which the return is already assumed, and the assumption is more difficult to dispute than a direct claim would have been.

**The Verse After the Measure of Time**

The verse follows the statement that the interval was a little while, and the sequence is exact. The time has been measured; the question asks what it was for.

The arrangement completes the sūrah's account. A short interval with no purpose would be trivial; a short interval with a purpose and a return is urgent, and the verse establishes both halves.

The fixed term, the conduct that matters, the record that speaks the truth — all of it assumes a purpose. The verse names the assumption and asks the addressees to deny it.

Putting the two halves in one question is what makes the urgency land. Shortness alone would be a consolation; shortness combined with purpose and return is the opposite, and the verse holds them together.

Holding them together is also the sūrah's summary of its own argument. Every verse about the interval, the record and the reckoning has been describing a short period with consequences, and the question states that description in a single sentence.

**The Question as the Sūrah's Central Claim**

The verse states what the whole sūrah has been arguing. Creation is purposeful; the interval matters; the return is real. The three claims are given in a single question.

The question form makes the claims the addressees' own. A person who answers no has accepted all three, and the sūrah has been leading him to that answer since the opening verse.

Everything the sūrah described points the same way. The believers were listed; the messengers were sent; the signs were given; the interval was fixed. The verse asks whether any of it was purposeless, and the question answers itself.

Asking it at the end is what makes it a summary. By this point the reader has seen the messengers, the refusals, the destructions, the weighing and the Fire, and the question gathers all of it into a single denial.

**The Verse Inside the Closing Movement**

The verse stands between the dialogue about duration and the final declarations, and its position is transitional. The account of the Day is finished; what follows is the sūrah's last word about God and about worship.

The arrangement is deliberate. The sūrah does not end with the Fire or the reward; it ends with a declaration about God's sovereignty and a prayer. The question about purpose is the hinge between the account and the declaration.

A reader who has accepted that creation is purposeful and that the return is real is prepared for the declaration that follows, and the declaration assumes both.

The hinge also reverses the direction of the sūrah. Everything before it described what happens to people; the declaration describes who God is, and the question is what allows the turn.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:116

> Exalted is Allah, the True King! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him, the Lord of the Honourable Throne

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Declaration Opens**

The verse begins with an exaltation, and the exaltation follows immediately on the question about purpose. The addressees were asked whether they supposed creation was frivolous; the verse declares God exalted above that supposition.

The connection is exact. The question implied that a purposeless creation would be unworthy of God; the verse states the exaltation that makes the implication explicit.

The same formula appeared earlier, at the end of the passage about offspring and partner gods. The repetition marks the verse as a closing declaration rather than a new argument.

Reusing the formula also tells the reader what kind of statement this is. The earlier instance closed the theological section; this one closes the sūrah's account of the Day, and the shared wording links the two closings.

Linking them is what makes the sūrah cohere. The denial of offspring and partner gods and the declaration of the True Sovereign are the same claim made at the beginning and the end of the sūrah's argument, and the verse is the second making.

**"The True King"**

The phrase can be read as the Sovereign who is Truth, or the True Sovereign, and both readings carry the same claim. The kingship is real, and it is identical with truth.

The word *malik* connects directly with *mulk* (sovereignty) and *malakūt* (dominion), the latter of which the sūrah used in the question about who holds the dominion of everything. The verse returns to that question and answers it with a title.

The connection is deliberate. The audience was asked who holds the dominion and answered God; the verse here names Him as the True King, and the naming confirms the answer.

Confirming it at the end is what closes the sequence of questions. The audience supplied the answer under questioning; the verse restates it as a title, and the restatement turns an admission into a confession.

**"There Is No God but He"**

The verse states the central declaration, and the statement follows the title rather than preceding it. The kingship is named first; the uniqueness follows.

The order is significant. A reader is told what God is before being told that there is no other, and the telling makes the uniqueness intelligible rather than merely asserted.

Everything before this verse has been building toward the statement. The questions about ownership, the denial of offspring and partner gods, the challenge about the heavens and the Throne — all of it leads here, and the verse gives it plainly.

Giving it plainly at the end is deliberate. The sūrah has argued at length; the declaration is not another argument but the sentence the arguments were arranged to make sayable.

The arrangement is what makes the sūrah an argument rather than a list of claims. Every question, narrative and description has been positioning the reader to say this sentence, and the verse lets him say it.

**"Lord of the Honourable Throne"**

The verse ends with a title, and the title returns to the Throne mentioned in the question about the seven heavens. The audience was asked who is the Lord of the seven heavens and the Lord of the Great Throne; the verse names Him as the Lord of the Honourable Throne.

The change from Great to Honourable is worth noting. The question used the adjective of magnitude; the declaration uses the adjective of nobility. The sūrah gives both, and the giving is deliberate.

Magnitude is what an outsider can be asked to acknowledge; nobility is what a worshipper confesses. The question could use the first because it was being put to people who conceded nothing else.

The Throne has been the sūrah's highest image throughout. It appeared in the question, in the parallel verse in al-Anbiyāʾ, and now in the closing declaration, and the verse places it at the end as the final image of the sūrah.

**The Verse After the Question**

The verse follows the question about purpose, and the sequence is exact. The addressees were asked whether they supposed creation was frivolous; the verse declares God exalted above the supposition and names Him as the True King.

The arrangement completes the sūrah's argument. The purpose of creation has been established; the return has been assumed; the sovereignty has been named. The verse gives all three in a single declaration.

The dominion of everything was asked about and answered; the Lord of the heavens and the Throne was named. The verse here is the final statement of what the questions established.

Giving it as a declaration rather than a conclusion is the sūrah's last move. A conclusion summarises an argument; a declaration praises, and the sūrah ends by praising rather than by summarising.

**The Verse Inside the Sūrah's Structure**

The verse stands between the question about purpose and the final statement about calling on other gods, and its position is structural. The declaration is given, and the verse that follows applies it.

The application is the sūrah's last argument. Whoever calls on another god has no proof; the disbelievers will not succeed. The declaration establishes the sovereignty; the application states what follows from it.

The sūrah then ends with a prayer, and the prayer assumes both. The reader has been given the declaration and the application, and the prayer is what remains for him to do.

Leaving the prayer as what remains is deliberate. A sūrah that ended with the declaration would close the matter; a sūrah that ends with a prayer leaves the reader with something to say, and the saying is the sūrah's last demand on him.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:117

> Whoever invokes, besides Allah, another god—for which they can have no proof—they will surely find their penalty with their Lord. Indeed, the disbelievers will never succeed

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Condition Stated**

The verse gives a conditional statement, and the condition is about calling on another god alongside God. Whoever does so has no proof for it.

The structure is worth holding. The verse does not say that calling on another god is forbidden and then give a reason; it says that whoever does so has no proof, and the absence of proof is the verse's claim.

The same method ran through the sūrah. The challenge about the message asked the audience to produce something; the questions about ownership expected answers the audience could give. The verse here states that the audience has nothing to produce.

Stating it as a fact rather than a demand is the verse's economy. Nobody is asked to produce the proof; the verse simply records that it does not exist, and the recording closes the matter without an exchange.

**Burhān: The Proof**

The word is *burhān*, and the commentators gloss it as a demonstration resting on firm foundations — evidence too clear to be argued away. The same term appears in al-Baqarah, al-Anbiyāʾ, an-Naml and al-Qaṣaṣ.

The sense of firmness matters. The verse does not say the audience has weak evidence; it says they have none. The absence is total, and the totality is what makes the verse a statement rather than an invitation to argue.

The same absence runs through the sūrah. The chiefs had no proof for their standing; the nightly assembly had no proof for its conclusions; the appeal to the forefathers had no proof behind it. The verse names the absence.

Naming it at the end gathers every instance into one statement. The audience's whole position — its standing, its tradition, its nightly reasoning — rests on nothing demonstrable, and the verse says so in a single clause.

**"His Reckoning Is with God"**

The phrase states that the accounting belongs to God rather than to any human being. Al-Ghāshiyah puts the same point in the divine voice (88:26), and Nūḥ says the equivalent in ash-Shuʿarāʾ when he hands the accounting of his people back to his Lord (26:113).

The recurrence establishes the principle as fixed. The reckoning is not a human task; the messenger does not carry it out; the verse states that it belongs to God alone.

That principle has run through the sūrah. The messenger delivers; the people respond; the outcome belongs to God. The verse here gives the principle its final form, at the point where the reader might most expect the messenger to be given a role in the settling.

Denying him that role is also a protection. A messenger who carried out the reckoning would be a judge with an interest in the outcome; the verse removes the interest by removing the role.

**"The Disbelievers Will Not Succeed"**

The verse ends with a statement about success, and the word is the same root the sūrah used for the believers at the opening and at the weighing. The disbelievers will not attain *falāḥ*.

The return of the root closes the sūrah's central contrast. The believers were named as successful; the disbelievers are named as not successful. The two statements frame the whole sūrah.

The framing is deliberate. The sūrah began with a claim about who succeeds and ends with a statement about who does not, and the two statements are given in the same vocabulary.

Using the same root for both is what makes the framing exact. The reader is not given two different measures; he is given one measure applied to two groups, and the verse supplies the negative application.

The negative application is also the sūrah's last word about the audience. They were called liars, refused, dismissed, and now named as people who will not succeed, and the naming closes their account without further argument.

**The Verse After the Declaration**

The verse follows the declaration of God's sovereignty, and the sequence is exact. The True King has been named; the verse states what follows for anyone who calls on another.

The arrangement applies the declaration rather than repeating it. The sūrah does not restate the sovereignty; it gives its consequence, and the consequence is stated as a fact rather than a threat.

That method runs through the sūrah. A principle is stated and then its consequence is given, and the giving is what makes the principle operative.

The application here is the last one the sūrah makes. After it there is no further argument, only a prayer, and the prayer assumes that the consequence has been understood.

Understanding the consequence is what makes the prayer appropriate. A person who does not know that calling on another god leaves him with no proof and no success has no reason to ask for forgiveness; the verse supplies the reason and the next verse supplies the asking.

**The Verse Inside the Sūrah's Structure**

The verse stands between the declaration and the closing prayer, and its position is final. The argument is complete; the declaration has been given; the consequence has been stated.

What follows is a prayer, and the prayer assumes everything the verse has established. A reader who knows that the reckoning is God's and that the disbelievers will not succeed is prepared to ask for forgiveness and mercy.

The sūrah ends with that prayer, and the ending is the sūrah's last instruction. The argument is finished; what remains is the asking.

Making the asking explicit in the form of a prayer is the sūrah's final move. The demand is not a command to stop doing something; it is a command to ask for forgiveness, and the asking assumes the stopping.

## Sūrah al-Muʾminūn 23:118

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “My Lord! Forgive and have mercy, for You are the best of those who show mercy.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Final Command to the Prophet**

The verse ends the sūrah with an imperative and a prayer, and the form is the same one the sūrah used for the sequence of questions and for the two earlier supplications. Say — and then the words.

The placement is significant. The sūrah does not end with a declaration or a threat; it ends with a command to pray, and the command is the last thing the reader is given.

That method runs through the whole sūrah. It gives instructions rather than conclusions, and the final instruction is a prayer.

**The Prayer's Content**

The prayer asks for two things: forgiveness and mercy. Nothing else is requested, and the brevity is deliberate.

The sūrah has described the same economy in the earlier prayers. Do not count me among the wrongdoers; I seek refuge from the incitements. Each prayer asks for one or two specific things, and the asking is limited to the position of the one praying.

The limitation is what makes the prayers usable. A person cannot ask for the world to be reordered; he can ask for forgiveness and mercy, and the verse gives the words.

The two requests also cover the ground completely. Forgiveness deals with what has been done; mercy deals with what remains to be endured. Between them there is nothing left to ask for, and the verse asks for nothing else.

**"The Best of Those Who Show Mercy"**

The phrase returns, and it is the same phrase the group of servants used two verses earlier. The Prophet's prayer and the group's prayer end with the same words.

The sharing is deliberate. The sūrah has described two groups — the mockers and the servants who prayed. The final verse gives the Prophet the words the servants used, and the giving places him with them.

The placement is the sūrah's answer to the charge that he was only a man like them. He is a man like them, and the verse shows him praying the same prayer they prayed, in the same words, for the same thing.

The phrase also appears in al-Aʿrāf, Yūsuf and al-Anbiyāʾ, and its recurrence establishes it as a fixed form of address. The sūrah ends with a phrase the audience had heard before, and the familiarity is the point.

The sharing is what makes the phrase usable. A reader who has heard it in three other sūrahs does not need it explained; he needs it given, and the verse gives it as the sūrah's last instruction.

**After the Warning Comes the Prayer**

The verse follows the statement that the disbelievers will not succeed, and the sequence is exact. The warning is given; the prayer follows it.

The arrangement is the sūrah's final method. It does not end with the warning; it ends with what a person who has heard the warning is to do. The doing is a prayer, and the prayer is given in full.

That pattern appears throughout. A statement is made and an instruction follows; the instruction is what the reader is left with. The final instruction is the final prayer.

That the prayer follows the warning rather than the reward is worth noting. The reader is not left with the comfort of the group's triumph; he is left with a request for forgiveness, and the request is the appropriate response to both outcomes.

The appropriateness is what makes the verse the sūrah's conclusion. A person who has heard both outcomes knows that he needs forgiveness rather than congratulation, and the verse gives him the words for the asking.

**The Prayer and the Sūrah's Opening**

The verse echoes the prayer of the believers recorded earlier in the sūrah, and the echo closes a circle. The group prayed for forgiveness and mercy; the sūrah ends with the same prayer given as a command.

The circle is the sūrah's structure. It opened with a list of the believers' qualities and a claim about their success; it ends with the prayer those believers made and a command to make it.

The arrangement tells the reader what the sūrah has been for. Not an argument to be won but a prayer to be said, and the saying is what the whole sūrah has been leading toward.

Everything between the opening list and this verse has been preparation for the saying. The messengers, the refusals, the interval, the weighing, the Fire and the reward are all reasons to ask for forgiveness, and the verse supplies the asking.

The arrangement also explains the sūrah's length. Everything between the opening list and this verse has been preparation for the saying, and the preparation is what makes the prayer appropriate rather than merely conventional.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Last Word**

The verse is the final verse of the sūrah, and its content is a prayer rather than a conclusion. The sūrah does not summarise its argument or restate its claims.

The absence of a summary is deliberate. The believers have been described, and the messengers, the refusals, the questions, the interval, the Day, the weighing, the Fire and the reward. The final verse gives none of that; it gives a prayer.

The prayer is what remains for a reader who has followed the whole account. The argument is finished; the interval is short; the return is real. What is left is the asking, and the verse gives the words.

Giving the words is the sūrah's final act of generosity. It does not leave the reader to compose a prayer under the weight of everything he has just read; it supplies the sentence, and the sentence is the one the believers used.
